# What the partner and the MD liked, and the large mistakes they rejected

Every review the senior partner (strategy firm) and the MD (investment bank) gave across 44 runs: 360 reviews of storyboards, decks and Excel models. The summary is grouped by a model; the lists below it are complete (deduplicated), good things from the highest-scoring reviews first and blocking issues by how often they recurred. Raw data: runs.csv, issues.csv, strengths.csv, paths-to-8.csv, reviews.jsonl in this folder.

## Summary

## What they liked (keep doing)

- **Answer first, with a sized decision.** Example: “Approve a $XB reserve, subject to a cash floor.”
- **Use claim-led titles that tell a connected story.** Example: Each slide title states a distinct conclusion and the reason behind it.
- **Put genuine options on a common basis.** Example: Compare returns, cash consequences and risks per dollar deployed.
- **Show the funding arithmetic visibly.** Example: A dated sources-and-uses bridge reconciles opening cash to closing cash.
- **Label uncertainty where it matters.** Example: Mark actuals, guidance and estimates directly on the bridge.
- **Make the recommendation falsifiable.** Example: Show the buyback price or growth rate at which a stated hurdle is met.
- **Close with an executable resolution.** Example: Name the owner, meeting date, release gate and reporting commitment.
- **Trace facts to primary sources.** Example: Give each material input a dated filing reference and show key derivations.
- **Use layouts suited to the message.** Example: Use a waterfall for funding, a table for options and a roadmap for implementation.
- **Lead model outputs with the answer.** Example: State funding capacity, minimum headroom and downside in plain English.
- **Keep assumptions auditable and formulas driver-based.** Example: Use named inputs with units, ranges, sources and clearly marked editable cells.
- **Model stress from business drivers.** Example: Change growth, cash-flow margin and capex together rather than haircutting the output.
- **Model actionable capital-allocation responses.** Example: Suspend discretionary buybacks before breaching an agreed cash floor.
- **Keep live integrity checks visible.** Example: Reconcile cash flows, the sensitivity-grid centre and scenario ordering under a master status.

## The large mistakes (stop doing)

- **Unsupported assumptions drive the answer.** Fix: Source each material input, explain the chosen value and provide a defensible range.
- **Sensitivity grids are separate approximations.** Fix: Recalculate the full model for each case and reconcile the centre to the base case on the same basis.
- **Liquidity projections are presented as valuations.** Fix: Label the scope as funding capacity or build a sourced valuation with an equity bridge and per-share output.
- **The cash floor is an unexplained plug.** Fix: Cite policy if available; otherwise label it as judgment, justify it and test a range.
- **The special dividend’s source or status is unclear.** Fix: Verify the primary declaration and dates; if unconfirmed, make it a scenario rather than a fact.
- **The downside is too mild or its failure is buried.** Fix: Stress the key drivers jointly, surface the minimum headroom and state when the plan breaks.
- **The headline reports only the favorable base case.** Fix: Put the base conclusion, downside breach and required response together on Outputs.
- **Growth, cash flow and capex are flat extrapolations dressed as drivers.** Fix: Build operating drivers where possible, or disclose the simplification and test margins and investment explicitly.
- **Discretionary returns continue through a liquidity breach.** Fix: Show the unchanged plan and a separate response case that cuts or suspends available levers.
- **Declared and discretionary actions are confused.** Fix: Do not defer a committed payment as if it were optional; model its dated funding obligation.
- **Interest uses the wrong cash path, rate or timing.** Fix: Calculate it separately by scenario using a documented, non-circular cash-balance convention and appropriate rate assumptions.
- **Sources and uses mix periods or double-count cash flows.** Fix: Date each flow, define whether cash flow is before or after capex, and reconcile the bridge.
- **The buyback authorization is treated as automatic annual spending.** Fix: Separate the ceiling from assumed pace, expiry and actual purchases; test the pace.
- **Cash and share roll-forwards fail or use inconsistent signs.** Fix: Reconcile each period’s opening balance, movements and closing balance with live checks.
- **Share retirement and dividends use inconsistent timing.** Fix: State the price and payment conventions, calculate retirements from spend and reconcile the share base.
- **Funding verdicts hide a gap or invent a remedy.** Fix: Quantify the shortfall and show the specific feasible reduction or financing required.
- **Broken formulas, circularities or failed checks reach Outputs.** Fix: Resolve references and loops upstream, recalculate cold and require a passing master check before release.
- **Key events are placed in the wrong period.** Fix: Add a dated stub where needed and carry post-event balances into the forecast without duplicating the payment.
- **Returns are compared as if unlike measures were interchangeable.** Fix: Label tax, timing and risk bases; distinguish earnings-yield accretion tests from investment returns.
- **The final artifact lacks a decision, usable layout or complete text.** Fix: Tie visible outputs to the model, state the ask and limits, and inspect the rendered file end to end.

## All the good things (what to keep)

### Storyboard: MD (133)

- Starting cash $20.21B + $15.82B − $7.5B − $2.6B − $3.0B − $1.0B = $21.93B — The bridge arithmetic itself ties to the cent and every input is labelled with a source fact (F5, F7, F36, F39, F29). Once the periods are fixed, this is the right exhibit and the right shape for the page. *(Slide 3, sources-and-uses arithmetic, review scored 7.05)*
- The buyback is judged against two opportunity costs — the 4.12% T-bill (F24) and the 22.2% after-tax return on capital (F32) — and explicitly capped, with the $4.0B alternative shown as a row rather than hidden — This is the correct frame for a capital-allocation decision: deploy only if the return beats the alternatives, and show the counterfactual. The $4.0B row (3.6M shares, EPS $20.93, cash $15.5B) gives the committee the rejected option without asking. *(Storyline, governing thought and slide 4, review scored 7.05)*
- 'at $1,106.21, $1B retires 0.90M shares' (F29) and the $850 vs $1,106 earnings-yield comparison — The per-$1B scaling rule lets any board member re-size the program on the spot and the price sensitivity shows the conclusion's dependence on the market price. Two numbers, both tied to source, doing real work. *(Slide 4, scaling and valuation panel, review scored 7.05)*
- The $2.26/share special dividend is shown as $2.26 × 443.1M = $1,001.4M, rounded to $1.0B — The per-share figure and the aggregate tie, and the rounding convention is stated. That is how a board paper should read: the per-share number the shareholder sees and the dollar figure the board approves, reconciled. *(Slide 1 and slide 2, recommendation sizing, review scored 7.05)*
- Gross uplift +$0.16/share = 4 x the $1B unit of +$0.04, less $0.28/share forgone after-tax cash, net -$0.12/share — It is built from a stated per-$1B unit (F31: EPS $20.76 x 443.1M / 442.2M = $20.80) that ties to the disclosed shares and price, so the committee can tie the number back. Keep that unit economic and show it on every slide that uses $0.12. *(Slide 4, EPS bridge, review scored 7)*
- Two thin reference lines labelled '3.10% after-tax 13-week T-bill' and '4.01% after-tax 10-year (5.15%)' — Marking the hurdle on the chart forces the conclusion to be judged against a number rather than asserted, and it correctly shows the buyback fails both. This is the right way to present the comparison; fix the basis on top of it. *(Slide 4, hurdle lines, review scored 7)*
- Explicit line 'Price as of 13-Nov-26 close ($1,106.21); confirm 16-Nov-26 close before the pack is finalised' with the 52-week range $850.00–$1,106.21 — It flags that the buyback executes at the top of the range and needs phasing, which is exactly the practical constraint a committee should see. Keep it. *(Slide 1, cover, review scored 7)*
- 'pause if marginal warehouse ROIC falls below 3.10% ... or if the average execution price rises above $1,106.21' — A decision without a guardrail invites an unbounded buyback; this ties the programme to the same hurdle used on slide 4. Keep both triggers. *(Slide 5, guardrail, review scored 7)*
- Explicit instructions that the $1B is illustrative, not a proposed return; ordinary dividends continue subject to board decisions; the historical cash balance is not current cash; do not add FY2026 cash flows to the August 30 closing balance. — These are exactly the traps that produce stale or double-counted liquidity figures. Pre-empting them before the slide is built prevents a blocking error at review. *(Slide 2, must-show notes, review scored 7)*
- 22.2%, 1.88%, 4.12% are each labelled with basis (after-tax company capital, pretax earnings yield, pretax cash proxy) and the arithmetic 1.88% − 4.12% × (1 − 24.7%) = −1.22pp is shown. — Units and tax basis are stated, so the committee can reproduce the figure. That is the standard I want on every number in the deck. *(Slide 3, F30/F32/F24, review scored 7)*
- Four numbered steps with owners (Treasury cash floor, CFO project economics, IR market prices), the hold-distribution escalation, and the instruction to arrange payment under binding terms if the dividend is verified. — It assigns accountability and handles the verification failure mode rather than assuming it away, which is what a committee needs to act. *(Slide 5, must-show notes, review scored 7)*
- The deck explicitly uses the full-year regular dividend run-rate ($5.54 × 443.1M = $2.46B) rather than the nine-month 10-Q figure ($1.15B), and names the reason. — This is exactly the discipline a board book needs — the 10-Q number is the trap and the storyboard already flags it. Keep that note visible to the designer. *(Slide 3 (waterfall_bridge), review scored 6.7)*
- The distinction between the 1.88% earnings yield (an EPS-accretion test) and the 14.5% implied return (a cash return including growth), with 'never compared with a cash yield' stated. — This is the kind of definitional discipline that prevents a director from comparing the 1.88% EPS yield to a bond yield and killing the whole thesis. It is the best analytical move in the deck. *(Slide 4 (exhibit_argument), review scored 6.7)*
- The 22.2% after-tax return on capital is set against the buyback implied return, showing growth still earns more than cash returned. — It answers the real question — why not just keep the cash and build? — before the committee asks. *(Slide 4 (exhibit_argument), review scored 6.7)*
- Five named steps with owner and timing, and a quarterly trough review with an explicit suspension trigger for buyback draws. — A board wants a decision it can act on today and a governor for later. The suspension mechanism in step (4) is what makes the $4B authorization safe to approve. *(Slide 5 (closing), review scored 6.7)*
- Opens at $20.21B cash (F5, 10-K, 2026-08-30), adds $15.82B operating cash flow (F7, 10-K, FY26-08-30), subtracts $7.5B FY27 capex and $3.0B buyback, and lands at $16.5B, with each row's period labeled. — Every line has a period and a source and the arithmetic ties: $20.21 + $15.82 - $7.5 - $2.6 - $3.0 = $22.93B on the slide's own numbers (the deck's $16.5B implies the $2.6B dividend and $3.0B buyback are both subtracted, so the reader can reproduce the walk). This is the page the committee will live on and it is built correctly. *(Slide 3 sources-and-uses schedule, review scored 6.65)*
- Three alternative uses of capital are scored against each other with a single consistent metric — earnings yield 1.88% (F30) vs. T-bills 4.12% (F24) vs. after-tax ROC 22.2% (F32). — This is the correct analytical frame for a capital allocation decision: compare the return on the marginal dollar across uses, not just report the buyback's EPS accretion. It gives the $3.0B cap a numerical reason rather than a preference. *(Slide 4 options table and the 1.88% / 4.12% / 22.2% comparison, review scored 6.65)*
- Cover carries only $20.21B cash, $490.19B market cap, $7.5B FY27 capex, with no 'vs.' framing; every slide title carries a number and a decision verb. — The cover sets three quantities that the rest of the deck must tie to, which is the right test for a board brief. The titles are falsifiable ('Cash of $20.21B and FY27 operating cash flow of $15.82B fund the $7.5B capex plan and still leave a $16.5B year-end balance') rather than assertions, which is what a king-page title is for. *(Slide 1 framing numbers and slide titles, review scored 6.65)*
- Five sequenced steps with owners, timing, and a stated dependency (special dividend under the dividend policy; $3.0B buyback contingent on renewing the authorization expiring January 2027, F40) plus a pause condition. — The dependency chain is explicit and the January 2027 expiry is sourced. A committee can approve the sequence without opening the 10-K, which is exactly what a closing page should do. *(Slide 5 next steps, review scored 6.65)*
- Market cap $490.19B is shown as share price $1,106.21 x 443.1M shares, both dated, both sourced (F21, F1), with an explicit instruction to footnote the mixed dates. — A committee's first question on a cover is whether the market cap ties to the share count, and mixed as-of dates are the standard trap. Labelling the arithmetic and the vintage on the slide itself removes the question. *(Slide 1, cover framing numbers, review scored 6.65)*
- The base case is a closed arithmetic loop: $20.21B (F5) - $6B return + $15.82B OCF (F7) - $7.5B capex (F36) = $6.71B, with FCF of $9.38B (F26) shown as OCF less capex (F7 - F4). — The operating cash flow and capex figures come from XBRL with accession numbers, and the derived $6.71B ties to the governing thought. A board can follow every line. *(Slide 2, cash walk and slide 3, capacity argument, review scored 6.65)*
- Buyback, special dividend and capex are placed on a per-$1B common basis: 0.90M shares retired per $1B at $1,106.21, $2.26 special dividend per $1B, and the capex return. — A common denominator is the right way to compare different uses of capital, and the $1B scaling rule (F29) is the correct way to present it in a board book. It is reusable if the board changes the amount. *(Slide 4, three-way comparison and the $1B scaling rule (F29), review scored 6.65)*
- Four dated steps with owners and milestones, and the risks line naming capex overrun, OCF shortfall and authorization expiry. — The $4B authorization expires in January 2027 (F40) and the recommendation asks the board to act before then; the closing converts the analysis into a calendar with three named failure modes. *(Slide 5, closing, review scored 6.65)*
- Options A/B/C are compared on the same six rows, one of which ("implied return on the buyback") is the test itself, and the decision line is explicitly conditional on the page-4 growth test. — This is how a board wants a decision framed: same yardstick across options, and the recommendation bound to the number that can falsify it. Most first drafts assert the answer; this one makes the answer fail-able. *(Slide 2, three-column options table with identical rows and a conditional decision line, review scored 6.65)*
- The trough is shown as a labelled waterfall with a floor line and both the pre-draw ($0.99B) and post-draw ($0.01B below) cushions named, plus the flex rule ("the buyback, not capex, is the flex item"). — It frames the decision as a liquidity question with a visible tripwire, which is the right question and the right way to show it. The arithmetic needs fixing (see blocking issues) but the construction is correct. *(Slide 3, bridge presentation with the $12B floor drawn as a dashed line and both cushions stated, review scored 6.65)*
- The committee's hurdle is on the chart, not in a footnote, and the buyback's 14.5% is measured against it. — A committee can see in one glance whether the recommendation clears its own threshold. Drawing the band rather than stating it is the specific thing to keep. *(Slide 4, the 9.2%-10.2% cost-of-equity band drawn on the chart face as the test, review scored 6.65)*
- One number, two dates, one footnote; no market cap, no range, no reconciliation. — A declare-and-pay cover should state the action and its timing, nothing else. This does. *(Slide 1, cover must-do "one number shown large: $13.3B special dividend" with declaration and payment dates, review scored 6.65)*
- 'opening cash $20.21B + FY2027 OCF $15.82B - FY2027 capex $7.5B - return $6B = closing cash $22.53B'; the split $4B authorization (ceiling, not commitment) plus $2B special dividend ($4.51/share); '$4B authorization renewed before its January 2027 expiry' — The number foots: 20.21 + 15.82 - 7.5 - 6 = 22.53. The $4B ceiling is correctly labeled as a ceiling and the FY2027 repurchase as set at approval, which is the distinction most first drafts blur. The decision line gives the board two dated asks. *(slide 2, cash walk and governing thought, review scored 6.3)*
- base/-10%/-20% OCF with closing cash $22.53B / $20.95B / $19.37B, cover recomputed on each line, and 'state the break-even OCF at which the plan stops working' — This is the right structure for a committee: it shows the recommendation survives a 20% OCF decline with cash still positive, and asks for the break-even explicitly rather than hiding it. Keep this through the revision. *(slide 3, sensitivity table, review scored 6.3)*
- 0.90M shares retired per $1B at $1,106.21; EPS $20.76 to $20.80 before lost interest; earnings yield 1.88%; scaling rule from F29 — It separates the scaling rule (a rate to multiply, not an amount) from the recommendation, which is exactly the discipline a board deck needs. The 0.90M per $1B and 0.20% of 443.1M both tie to F1 and F21. *(slide 4, buyback row and scaling rule, review scored 6.3)*
- 'No closing-cash straddle on the cover. Footnote the mixed dates and the share-count vintage.' — It anticipates the first challenge a director raises on a cover: why is market cap computed with a 2026-09-29 share count and a 2026-11-13 price. Stating it pre-empts the question. *(slide 1, cover, review scored 6.3)*
- Opening cash $20.21B (F5) + OCF $15.82B (F7) - capex $7.5B (F36) - return $6B = closing cash $22.53B, with 2.7x cover stated as $20.21B / $7.5B. — The walk foots to the stated arithmetic and every input is either SEC-sourced or labelled as an assumption. The $6B is sized against a plan, not asserted. This is the spine of the deck and it is correctly built. *(Slide 2, cash walk and the 2.7x cover line, review scored 6.3)*
- FY2026 OCF $15.82B covers FY2027 capex $7.5B by 2.1x; cash $20.21B covers FY2026 capex $6.43B by 3.1x; the slide labels why the denominator changes. — Distinguishing the flow cover from the stock cover is exactly the discipline a board needs, and the slide flags the 2.7x post-return cover separately. Most first drafts blend these into one ratio. *(Slide 3, operating-cash-flow cover versus cash cover: 2.1x OCF on capex and 3.1x cash on FY2026 capex, with the denominator change explained, review scored 6.3)*
- The 22.2% is explicitly limited and a research line flags that the marginal return requires capex per new warehouse and mature-warehouse revenue. — This is the honest way to present a return figure you cannot yet defend at the margin. It pre-empts the committee's first question instead of inviting it. *(Slide 4, capex row: '22.2% after-tax return on existing invested capital, labelled a historical company average, not the marginal return on the FY2027 $7.5B step-up', review scored 6.3)*
- The floor is derived from stated figures and explicitly labelled as a policy choice rather than an output of the analysis. — Committees distrust floors presented as math. This one hands the board a dial and says it is a dial. Keep this framing; it is the right governance posture. *(Slide 4, the 25x buyback floor: '25x FY2026 EPS $20.76 implies a 4.0% earnings yield, below the 4.12% T-bill... labelled a policy choice, not an analytical conclusion', review scored 6.3)*
- The closing slide sizes the two failure modes by dollar, and names the January 2027 authorization expiry as a dated risk. — The risks are sized, not adjectives. The $8.25B stack test ties to the +10% capex cell on slide 3. This is the standard the rest of the deck should meet. *(Slide 5, risks line: 'capex overrun beyond $8.25B with no authorization renewed would breach the $4B ceiling' and 'an OCF decline to the $12.66B lower case still leaves $19.37B closing cash', review scored 6.3)*
- The bridge ties to the cent: $20.21B + $15.82B - $6.43B - $2.46B - $0.85B - $13.30B = $12.99B, and it uses the FY26 10-K regular dividend run-rate $2.46B = $5.54 x 443.1M rather than the nine-month 10-Q figure of $1.15B, with the period labeled on every bar. — This is what a committee tests first: the sources-and-uses ties, the period is on the page, and the trap of using the nine-month figure from the 10-Q (F8 at $1.15B) for a full-year bridge is avoided. Keep the $2.46B and the period labels through every revision. *(Slide 3, bridge, review scored 6.3)*
- The three options are dimensioned on the same rows — cash returned, remaining cash, cushion to the floor, implied return, capex, warehouse count — with the $13.3B, $4B and 14.5% bolded, and Option C shown at $0 returned / $31.45B remaining / $19.45B cushion. — The board can compare A, B and C on identical dimensions without arithmetic, and the do-nothing option is given a fair showing rather than a straw man. The bolded figures are the three the decision turns on. Keep the row set identical across options. *(Slide 2, options table, review scored 6.3)*
- '$4B authorization; at $1B/yr and $1,106, every $1B retires about 0.90M shares (0.20% of 443.1M); the full $4B at today's price is about 3.6M shares if drawn.' — It states the authorization as a ceiling and the pace separately, and quantifies both against the share count, so no one reads $4B as a single-year outflow. F29 supports it exactly. This is the right way to present a repurchase authorization to a board. *(Slide 2, buyback sizing line, review scored 6.3)*
- The cover reconciles the $490.19B market cap ($1,106.21 x 443.1M) to the 11 Nov article's $472.4B as price appreciation plus the $13.3B dividend, not a share-count change. — A client could catch the $17.8B difference between the press figure and the computed figure and question the share count. Pre-empting it on the cover is exactly right. *(Slide 1, cover footnote, review scored 6.3)*
- The $670 floor is derived, not asserted: earnings yield = EPS $20.76 / price set equal to the 3.10% after-tax cash alternative, footnoted as '$670 = EPS $20.76 / 3.10%.' — The decision rule is transparent, reproducible from a single formula, and has a clear economic meaning: every dollar below $670 earns more than the after-tax cash alternative. This is the spine of the recommendation and must survive revision. *(Slide 2, metric (5) and Slide 4, callout, review scored 5.75)*
- The buyback is framed as optionality: if the shares do not trade below $670, the $4B authorization is not drawn, and the special dividend and capex plan are the capital-return actions. — It correctly separates the declared special dividend from the authorized but undrawn buyback, and it protects the board from being seen to have committed to buy at any price. This is the right architecture. *(Slide 4, right argument and Slide 5, caveat, review scored 5.75)*
- The capex-to-revenue growth comparison is sourced and computed: capex 14.2% a year from $4.32B to $6.43B, revenue 7.8% a year from $242.29B to $303.15B, operating cash flow 12.6% a year from $11.07B to $15.82B, all from SEC XBRL with fiscal 2023 to 2026 dates. — The gap is the argument, and the growth rates and endpoints tie to the filings and to each other. That is the standard the rest of the deck should meet. *(Slide 3, F19 / F18 / F20, review scored 5.75)*
- The cover correctly narrows the question: the $30 special dividend was declared on 11 November 2026 and the briefing addresses funding, the capex plan and the $4B buyback authorization, not whether to declare. — It removes an optionality the board no longer has and stops the discussion from relitigating a done decision. State the derivation $13.3B = 443.1M x $30 on the cover as planned. *(Slide 1, subtitle and third line, review scored 5.75)*
- 'gross uplift +$0.16/share (4 x the $1B unit of +$0.04), less lost after-tax interest $0.12/share on $4B, net -$0.04/share' — The per-$1B unit is shown and the bridge adds up; a reader can check 4 x ($0.04 - $0.03) boxed, and the $4B buyback is not plugged. *(Slide 4, EPS bridge, review scored 5.7)*
- growth 22.2% / 20.6% incl. leases, buyback 1.88%, cash 3.10% — Four numbers, all after tax, all on one consistent basis; the reader can see the ranking without reading text. *(Slide 2, return comparison, review scored 5.7)*
- '$5.88 annual (F38) does not tie to $5.54 declared (F12); the $0.34 difference is unreconciled, so use $5.54 x 443.1M = $2.45B and label it' — This is exactly what I want to see — an inconsistency named, a source chosen, and the chosen number labelled rather than silently averaged. *(Slide 4, the dividend reconciliation footnote, review scored 5.7)*
- 'pause if marginal warehouse ROIC falls below 3.10% (after-tax cash hurdle), or if the average execution price rises above $1,106.21' — A live tripwire with a number, not a platitude; it makes the authorisation conditional and reviewable at the next board meeting. *(Slide 5, guardrail, review scored 5.7)*
- The answer is sized in dollars and the deciding number is stated: $1B of buyback adds 4 cents of EPS to $20.80 but forgoes 7 cents of after-tax interest ($1B x 4.12% x (1 - 24.7%) = $31.0M / 442.2M = $0.07), net -3 cents. The after-tax interest arithmetic is correct and ties: $1B x 4.12% = $41.2M pretax, x (1 - 0.247) = $31.0M, / 442.2M shares = $0.0701. — The board is being asked to make a decision, not listen to a discussion. A sized net number per $1B gives the board a unit of decision. This is the spine of the deck and it must be preserved through the revision. *(slide 2, three option columns and slide 4, EPS bridge, review scored 5.7)*
- Option B's undrawn $3B is footnoted rather than buried in the closing cash figure, and closing cash is labelled 'closing cash at end of year 1' across all three options on a shared baseline. — Labelling the basis removes the most common source of incorrect inference in a three-column comparison. Do not let the revision drop these labels; the fix on the waterfall should extend the same discipline. *(slide 2, option B footnote, review scored 5.7)*
- The three returns are on one labelled basis: buyback earnings yield 1.88% (F30), after-tax return on capital 22.2% excluding leases (F32) and 18.8% including $2.61B operating lease liabilities (F11, denominator $42.32B), 13-week T-bill 4.12% before tax (F24). The inclusion of the lease-adjusted ROCE is the kind of thing a careful analyst does and it does not change the conclusion. — One basis, one page, footnoted. It lets the committee test the sensitivity to the lease treatment without having to ask for it. Keep both ROCE figures on the slide; do not collapse to one. *(slide 4, returns comparison, review scored 5.7)*
- The slide plans a sensitivity on closing cash at the $1B pace to (i) operating cash flow flat versus FY2026 and (ii) capex 10% above the $6.5B plan ($7.15B), and a three-year capex versus operating cash flow comparison to support the 'gap' claim period by period. — Asking the downside on the two cash-flow drivers before the committee asks is exactly right. The revision should extend the same discipline to the returns side (incremental ROCE), which is where the sensitivity is currently absent. *(slide 3, sensitivity table, review scored 5.7)*
- The declared-dividend outflow ties exactly to the share count (F1) and the per-share dividend (F38), and the $6.92B residual ($20.21B cash F5 less $13.29B) is internally consistent. — Both inputs are from the same 10-K and the same press release; the arithmetic is transparent and a committee can re-derive it in five seconds. *(Slide 2, '$13.29B = $30 x 443.1M shares', review scored 5.65)*
- $4B / $1,106.21 = 3.616M shares; 3.616M / 443.1M = 0.816%. Both legs checked. — The buyback math is stated in both absolute and relative terms, which is how a board thinks about dilution and EPS accretion. *(Slide 4, 'approximately 3.62M shares retired, or 0.82% of shares outstanding', review scored 5.65)*
- The distinction between authorization and obligation is stated on the page. — This is the exact confusion that causes boards to double-count an authorization as a cash commitment; the deck pre-empts it. *(Slide 3, 'The $4B buyback authorization has no cash impact unless exercised', review scored 5.65)*
- The deck refuses to launder an average historical ROC (F32) into a marginal project return. — This is the single most intellectually honest line in the deck and the one most likely to be challenged; it survives challenge because it concedes the point. *(Slide 4, 'Historical 22.2% company return on capital is not a forecast of incremental warehouse returns', review scored 5.65)*
- Closes with a specific, sequenced process that turns the recommendation into an executable path. — Board briefings that end with a process and named owners get approved; ones that end with a reiteration of the thesis do not. *(Slide 5, four numbered steps with owners (Finance / Operations / Finance / Board) and decision gates, review scored 5.65)*
- The deck refuses to treat the $4B as a reserve ('illustrative placeholder equal to the reported buyback authorization, not a derived reserve or approved spending') and repeatedly says the post-dividend balance is not current cash and excludes intervening flows — This is exactly the discipline a board needs; it prevents the placeholder from hardening into an approved number and survives a hostile read *(Governing thought and slides 1-3 caveats, review scored 5.65)*
- The waterfall explicitly quarantines trailing FCF ($9.38B, F26 = $15.82B OCF less $6.43B capex) from the forward flows — It stops the offer's worst habit — adding a historical cash build to a forward balance — before anyone can do it *(Slide 3, 'Show the historical cash-flow calculation separately... it predates the August 30 cash balance', review scored 5.65)*
- The executive summary separates the balance arithmetic from the board action and forbids comparing the three alternatives on that page — Keeps the summary to one message and prevents a false three-way choice between a balance, an outflow and a proposal *(Slide 2, grouping instructions ($20.21B starting balance, $13.29B declared outflow, illustrative balance) and the isolated board-action box, review scored 5.65)*
- Every $1B of buyback retires 0.90M shares (0.20% of 443.1M) at $1,106.21, presented as a rate to multiply rather than a proposal — It is a defensible, checkable sensitivity lever a board can apply to any sized decision, and it is clearly labeled as not being the recommendation *(Slides 4-5, F29 scaling rule, review scored 5.65)*
- The buyback is already expressed as a scaling rule with the arithmetic attached: 'every $1B spent on a buyback retires 0.90M shares (0.20% of 443.1M) at $1,106.21' and 'diluted EPS after a $1B buyback $20.80 = EPS $20.76 x 443.1M / 442.2M shares'. — This is how a committee wants an option priced: a rate you can multiply, a share price as of a dated close (2026-11-13), and a stated caveat ('before lost interest on the cash'). It lets the reader size any amount without you hard-coding one, and it is the one place the deck shows its work instead of asserting. *(Slide 2 and slide 4, F29/F30/F31, review scored 5.6)*
- The explicit instruction to 'not present the bridge as a forecast' and to 'show why the resulting liquidity must be checked against growth funding'. — It is honest about the limit of the data — the bridge is illustrative — and it keeps the board focused on the real question (can growth be funded), which is the correct frame. Keep that discipline when you fix the $6.91B endpoint. *(Slide 3 title and must-show, review scored 5.6)*
- 'Specify missing forecast and project evidence without inventing values' with F40 and the 3-year growth rates (F18/F19/F20). — Naming what you do not know — the FY2027+ capex plan and the incremental project returns — is exactly right for a first board discussion, and the 3-year rates (revenue 7.8%, capex 14.2%, operating cash flow 12.6%) are the right evidence class: capex is compounding faster than cash flow, which is the fact that justifies caution on further distributions. *(Slide 5, review scored 5.6)*
- The recommendation is sized and specific: $3.0B buyback, $1.0B special dividend, $4.0B total, with the per-share numbers ($2.26 dividend, 2.7M shares, EPS $20.76 to $20.90) derived from F29-F31 on stated inputs (F1, F13, F21). — The board can approve or reject a number here. This is what an executive summary should do, and it must survive the revision unchanged except for the $4.0B comparison row. *(Slide 2, node 1, review scored 5.35)*
- The sources-and-uses structure from a dated opening balance ($20.21B at 2026-08-30, F5) through operating cash flow ($15.82B, F7) and capex ($7.5B, F36) is the right engine for this decision, and the free cash flow cross-check ($9.38B, F26) is present. — This is the page that proves the plan is fundable. The structure is correct; the errors are in the outflows and the cover ratio, which are fixable without rebuilding the page. *(Slide 3, node 2, review scored 5.35)*
- The options are compared on a common per-dollar basis (capital deployed, return, effect per share), and the takeaway names the constraint: the buyback earns less than warehouse capital, so it is sized below the authorization. — Most decks assert the recommendation and skip the counter-argument. Here the counter-argument is stated on the page, which is exactly what a committee wants to see, provided the $4.0B row is added so the sizing test is visible. *(Slide 4, node 3, review scored 5.35)*
- Each step has an owner action and a date or cadence, and there is a stated condition that changes the plan (trough below $4.0B; price above $1,200). — Next steps with triggers and dates are what turn a briefing into a decision. The triggers need derivation, but the instinct is right. *(Slide 5, node 4, review scored 5.35)*
- Free cash flow is built transparently as operating cash flow $15.82B (F7) less capital expenditure $6.43B (F4) = $9.38B (F26), with both inputs sourced to the 10-K. — Every dollar in the waterfall ties to a filing, and the arithmetic works. A committee can re-perform it in ten seconds. This is the spine of the deck and it should not change. *(slide 2, F26 and F7/F4, review scored 5.05)*
- The scaling rule is explicit: every $1B of buyback retires 0.90M shares (0.20% of 443.1M) at $1,106.21, and a $1B buyback lifts diluted EPS from $20.76 to $20.80. — This is how a back-up page should read — a rate to multiply, not a proposed amount, with the share count and price shown. It avoids a hard-coded plug and lets the board size any authorization. Keep this construction and extend it to the $4B case. *(slide 2, F29 and F31, review scored 5.05)*
- Both the buyback earnings yield (1.88% = $20.76 / $1,106.21) and the after-tax ROIC (22.2% = operating income $11.69B x (1 - 24.7%) / (equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B)) are shown with their full build, not just the answer. — The committee can see exactly what each return is made of and where it comes from. The builds need the fixes noted above (tax rate sourcing, leases, comparability), but the transparency is right. *(slide 4, F30 and F32, review scored 5.05)*
- The existing $4,000 repurchase authorization expiring January 2027 is carried into the deck. — This is the timing fact that makes the December board meeting the right decision point, and it is sourced to the 10-Q. It should be pulled forward to the decision line on slide 2, not left only on slide 5. *(slide 2, F40, review scored 5.05)*
- The three buyback paces are sized side by side with closing cash and EPS effect ($6.84B / $9.84B / $10.84B; -$0.24 / -$0.06 / unchanged), and the single deciding number ('the paced path costs $0.06 a share, the front-loaded path $0.24') is called out. — This is what a real board page looks like: options on one page, one number to decide, and the numbers already made to tie to the sources. Keep this shape through revision. *(Slide 2 executive_summary, A/B/C option framing, review scored 5.05)*
- The exhibit puts three returns on one labelled basis: buyback earnings yield 1.88% (F30), after-tax return on capital 22.2% and 18.8% including leases (F32 and F11), and 13-week T-bill 4.12% (F24). — Putting the buyback yield next to the return on capital is the correct comparison, and showing the returns on one basis is exactly what a committee wants. The after-tax and leases-included variants show the deck is not hiding behind one number. *(Slide 4 exhibit_argument, valuation leg and returns basis, review scored 5.05)*
- The bridge walks opening cash $20.21B (F5) + operating cash flow $15.82B (F7) - capex $6.43B (F4) - $13.3B - $4B - $2.46B to closing cash, with each line potentially traceable to a source. — A waterfall that ties to the balance sheet and cash flow statement is the right exhibit for a capital-allocation question. The structure is correct even though the periods and the closing figure need to be fixed. *(Slide 3 waterfall_bridge, review scored 5.05)*
- The governing thought names the EPS cost of each buyback pace and the return on capital versus cost of equity as the deciding tests. — The recommendation is framed against numbers the board can audit, not against strategy language. That is the right instinct; the execution just needs the inputs and the periods tied down. *(Governing thought and slide 4, review scored 5.05)*
- The 22.4x LTM multiple is built from the FY2026 diluted EPS $20.76 (F13) and the 2026-11-13 close $1,106.21 (F21), both current and both cited. — Using the just-filed FY2026 EPS rather than the stale multiples.vc numbers is the right call and should be preserved. The multiple is current as of the pricing date. *(Slide 4 exhibit_argument, valuation leg, review scored 5.05)*
- Framing the recommendation as a monitoring stance on the existing $4B authorization rather than a new distribution, with the special dividend shown as declared and payable 4 Dec 2026 (F41) — It avoids the misread that the board is being asked to approve a fresh distribution, which is the single most likely client-facing error in a capital allocation deck. *(Slide 1 cover, review scored 5.05)*
- Requiring every bridge input on the page, labeling operating cash flow as consensus (F52) and capex as company guidance (F51), and requiring a visible caveat that current cash and other cash movements are unknown — This is the discipline that makes a bridge usable in a committee. Most first drafts hide the plug; this storyboard invites the committee to check it. *(Slide 3 must show, review scored 5.05)*
- Explicit instruction not to score the options and not to imply company-wide returns are incremental warehouse returns — It prevents the most common and most damaging analytical error here — presenting the 22.2% after-tax ROIC (F32) as if it were the marginal return on the next warehouse. *(Slide 4 must show, review scored 5.05)*
- Repeated requirement to treat the special dividend as reported declared, not as a new approval, and to gate the authorization on a current liquidity forecast — The recommendation is defensible only if the dividend is a given. Making that explicit twice is correct. *(Slide 2 and slide 5 must show, review scored 5.05)*
- The instruction to show source-labeled operating cash flow, capex and free cash flow, with the $4B authorization shown separately and four explicitly hypothetical cases. — It keeps the actual (F7 $15.82B, F4 $6.43B, F26 $9.38B) cleanly apart from the consensus and the authorization, which is exactly how a board page avoids blending GAAP and guidance. *(Slide 3 (grid, bar chart), review scored 5.05)*
- 'Treat the special dividend as reported declared, not a new approval' and 'Do not imply that a further distribution has been proposed.' — A $13.3B declaration payable 2026-12-04 is already board action; conflating it with a new proposal would be the first thing a general counsel catches. *(Slide 1 and slide 2, wording instruction, review scored 5.05)*
- The 1.88% earnings yield and the 4.12% T-bill are both in the pack, with the earnings-yield formula shown (F30) and the lost-interest caveat stated. — The central arithmetic of the recommendation ties: EPS $20.76 / $1,106.21 = 1.88%, and it is being compared against a real, dated alternative. *(Slide 2 and slide 4, F30 and F24, review scored 5.05)*
- The per-$1B buyback math is computed and shown: EPS $20.80 = $20.76 × 443.1M / 442.2M shares. — This is the kind of cell a hostile committee opens first; it is defensible because shares outstanding (F1) and EPS (F13) are both filed. *(Slide 4, F31, review scored 5.05)*
- The recommendation is sized in dollars: $7.5B capex, $4B buyback, $2B special dividend, with the per-share and share-count conversions ($4.51/share, 0.90M shares per $1B) from F29. — A board can act on a sized recommendation; this passes the first test of a decision slide. *(Slide 2, review scored 5.05)*
- It compares uses of capital on a common $1B basis and uses the earnings yield F30 = $20.76/$1,106.21 rather than a raw P/E, against a sourced 4.12% T-bill (F24). — The unit is consistent and the comparison is decision-relevant; keep the $1B common basis and the yield framing. *(Slide 4, review scored 5.05)*
- It is a dated roadmap tied to the January 2027 authorization expiry (F40) and the December meeting, with owners and milestones. — It converts the recommendation into an executable calendar; the dates and owners are the right skeleton. *(Slide 5, review scored 5.05)*
- Every figure carries a source tag and link (10-K filed 2026-10-07, Yahoo Finance 2026-11-13, SEC XBRL), and F28 is shown as a computed formula. — The sourcing discipline is right and must survive the revision; the problem is not missing tags, it is missing figures and tie-outs. *(Slide 1 and 3, review scored 5.05)*
- The earnings-yield comparison is built from primary facts and is internally consistent: 1.88% = $20.76 / $1,106.21, and the $1B buyback lifts EPS from $20.76 to $20.80 using 443.1M and 442.2M shares. — It states the return a buyback earns on each dollar and the accretion, both traceable to the 10-K and the closing price, which is exactly the test a buyback decision needs. *(Slide 4, F30 and F31, review scored 5.05)*
- Three-year growth rates are computed from SEC XBRL for revenue (7.8%), capex (14.2%) and operating cash flow (12.6%), with the base periods named. — Capex growing nearly twice revenue is the reason to ask whether growth still earns its cost of capital; the slide uses the right comparison. *(Slide 4, F18 and F19, review scored 5.05)*
- The bridge starts from the audited balance-sheet cash of $20.21B at 2026-08-30, the annual operating cash flow of $15.82B and capex of $6.43B. — These are the right three anchors for a sources-and-uses schedule and they tie to the 10-K. *(Slide 3, F5, F7, F4, review scored 5.05)*
- The cover is disciplined: one subtitle, three framing numbers ($20.21B cash at 2026-08-30, $7.5B FY27 plan, $4.0B proposed return), and an explicit 'no market cap, no vs. framing' instruction. — It forces the first thing the board sees to be the decision variables, not the balance sheet; that is the right hierarchy for a capital allocation page. *(slide 1, cover, review scored 5)*
- Operating cash flow $15.82B (F7) and free cash flow $9.38B (F26, F7 minus F4) are sourced to the 10-K and carried forward consistently at these figures. — The deck's cash engine ties to filings at the two numbers that matter; as long as the FY27 mix issue in issue 6 is fixed, this is a clean base. *(slides 2 and 3, F7 and F26, review scored 5)*
- The authorization expiry (January 2027) and the $1,115M remaining amount are stated with both the 10-K and 10-Q sources. — These are the facts that force a board action date; they are the reason the closing slide is a decision slide, and they are correctly sourced. *(slide 5, step (1), F50 and F40, review scored 5)*
- '22.2% after-tax return on capital' vs '1.88% earnings yield on a buyback' vs '4.12% on Treasury bills' — The core hurdle comparison is the right frame for a capital-allocation decision, and each figure is computed from cited inputs (F32 from F14/F15/F10/F17; F30 from F13/F21; F24 cited) — keep this comparison and its footnotes as the spine of the deck. *(Slide 2 metric panel and Slide 4 ranked table, review scored 4.75)*
- A sized recommendation ($13.3B = 443.1M shares x $30, F1 x F37) with a stated decision point — The brief asked for a clear, sized recommendation; saying the number and the action in the title is exactly what a board needs, and it follows arithmetically from the cited share count and dividend. *(Slide 2 'Approve the $13.3B special dividend and the $6.5B capex plan' and Slide 5, review scored 4.75)*
- Cash cover of capex 3.1x ($20.21B / $6.43B, F27) and the explicit warehouse count (16 opened, 13 planned, F36) — Gives the committee a liquidity cushion metric and a physical denominator for the growth plan — both tie to filings and are the right kind of evidence for 'can we afford this.' *(Slide 3 right argument, review scored 4.75)*
- The EPS bridge is built from stated units: F31 ($1B buyback, +$0.04), F29 (0.90M shares per $1B), F24 (4.12% T-bill), leading to gross +$0.16, lost interest -$0.28, net -$0.12. — Every line traces to a numbered fact and a stated rate; the arithmetic is internally consistent and a committee can reproduce it. This is the right way to present an accretion test even where the conclusion is negative. *(slide 2 and slide 4; F30, F31, F24, review scored 4.7)*
- The waterfall runs OCF $15.82B − capex $6.43B = FCF $9.38B, then − $4B = $5.38B retained, with periods labelled FY25 actual and FY26 plan. — The $9.38B ties to F26 and to F7−F4; labelling actuals vs plan is exactly what prevents the mixed-period error, and it is done here. *(slide 3; F7, F4, F26, review scored 4.7)*
- Market cap $490.19B = $1,106.21 × 443.1M is shown as a computed tie, and the 52-week range and price date (2026-11-13) are on the page. — A committee can check the market cap in one step and knows the price is four days old, not stale. *(slide 1; F1, F21, F22, F28, review scored 4.7)*
- The ROIC build is shown in full: operating income $11.69B, tax 24.7% = $3.02B / $12.22B, NOPAT $8.80B, invested capital $39.71B, ROIC 20.6% incl. leases / 22.2% excl. — Both conventions are disclosed rather than one being selected silently; the fix is to pick one, not to rebuild the footnote. *(slide 4; F32 footnote, review scored 4.7)*
- 'Confirm the declared $13.3B dividend and the $6.5B capex plan; buy back only below $670 per share' as the title, followed by five sized metrics and an explicit 'Decision today' line. — The board gets the recommendation, the amounts, and the decision in one page. The title is a recommendation, not a topic, which is the standard for a board executive summary. *(Slide 2, the five-metric structure with a decision line, review scored 4.7)*
- A line of earnings yield against price with three reference lines (3.10% after-tax, 4.12% pre-tax, 22.2% return on existing assets) and the $670 crossing marked. — This is the right exhibit for the decision: it makes the buyback a price-discipline question, not a size question, and it shows the crossing rather than asserting it. Keep the exhibit; fix the range and the derivations. *(Slide 4, the buyback earnings yield vs share price exhibit, review scored 4.7)*
- Capex 14.2% a year vs revenue 7.8% vs operating cash flow 12.6%, fiscal 2023 to fiscal 2026, with the gap framed as the structural cost of the warehouse model. — It answers the 'why keep funding capex' question with the company's own history rather than with adjectives, and it sets up the return the plan must clear. The CAGRs tie to the endpoints in F18, F19, F20. *(Slide 3, the capex/revenue/operating cash flow CAGR comparison, review scored 4.7)*
- Cover keeps the board question; closing gives four steps with owners, timing, and measurable success criteria. — The board is being asked to confirm, not to explore; the closing converts the recommendation into four names with dates, which is what a board minute needs. *(Slide 1 and Slide 5, the decision framing, review scored 4.7)*
- The bridge is arithmetic that ties: $20.76 × 443.1M / 442.2M = $20.80 (F31), less $0.28 of interest = $20.52, a net −$0.24. Each step uses a sourced input. — This is the number the recommendation rests on and it is built, not asserted. Keep the build visible on the page; it is the slide's reason to exist. *(Slide 4, node 3, the EPS bridge: '$20.76 to $20.80 before funding cost, then down to $20.52 after the $0.28 a share of interest forgone', review scored 4.65)*
- Three returns on one labelled basis, each from a sourced figure (F30, F32, F11, F24), with the lease-adjusted ROIIC shown, which most first drafts omit. — It lets the board rank the uses of cash in one glance. The 18.8% lease-adjusted figure is the kind of conservatism that survives committee scrutiny. *(Slide 4, node 3, the three returns 'buyback earnings yield 1.88%, after-tax return on capital 22.2% excluding leases and 18.8% including $2.61B of operating lease liabilities, 13-week T-bill 4.12%', review scored 4.65)*
- A bridge that starts from the actual cash balance ($20.21B, F5) and flows through OCF, capex, and returns, with a takeaway that names the funding source rather than implying the plan is free. — The structure is right even though the ordinary-dividend line is wrong. Once the period is fixed the slide becomes committee-ready. *(Slide 3, node 2, the waterfall with each bar shown to the arithmetic, review scored 4.65)*
- Discipline: $13.3B special dividend, $4B buyback, $20.21B cash, $9.38B FCF. — A board summary that crowds the page with metrics buries the decision. Keep the panel capped at four. *(Slide 2, node 1, the metrics panel limited to four sizing numbers, review scored 4.65)*
- The free cash flow bridge ties: operating cash flow $15.82B (F7) less capex $6.43B (F4) equals $9.38B (F26), and the cash cover of capex 3.1x = $20.21B / $6.43B (F27) is arithmetic that works. — Every figure carries a named source and the arithmetic is checkable on the page, which is exactly what a board pack needs before anyone debates the uses of the cash. *(slide 3, exhibit and argument, review scored 4.4)*
- The question is sized on the page: $13.3B = 443.1M shares (F1) x $30 (F37), against $20.21B cash (F5) and $9.38B free cash flow (F26), with the $4B authorization (F38) named. — The executive summary answers with amounts rather than adjectives, which is the standard for a board briefing and must survive the revisions. *(slide 2, metrics panel, review scored 4.4)*
- The $850 52-week low (F22) is carried into the earnings yield at 2.44% ($20.76 / $850) and the reader can check it against the $1,106.21 spot. — Planning a price sensitivity before the committee asks is right, and showing the ceiling and floor of the share price alongside the buyback yield is the correct instinct. *(slide 4, sensitivity band, review scored 4.4)*
- The cover states the decision and not the answer (per its brief), and slide 5 assigns four owners and timings including the one that matters — the buyback price discipline to the Treasurer. — Cover and closing do their jobs; the next-steps page is actionable because it says who does what and when. *(slides 1 and 5, review scored 4.4)*
- The decision is sized and given as a rate, not a plug: $1.0B/yr buyback, $13.3B dividend, $6.5B capex, with the earnings yield 1.88% = $20.76 / $1,106.21 and the scaling rule '$1B retires 0.90M shares at $1,106.21' (F29) shown as computed. — A board can act on a rate card; the buyback pace is testable and re-derivable from F13 and F21, which is rare in a first draft and must survive revision. *(slide 2 (executive_summary), F29 and F30, review scored 4.4)*
- Revenue 7.8%, capex 14.2% and operating cash flow 12.6% CAGR over three years are placed on the same exhibit, from the same 2023-09-03 to 2026-08-30 window. — This is the honest framing of the growth case: capital is growing faster than revenue, and the board needs to see it before approving another $6.5B. *(slide 3 (exhibit_argument), F18 vs F19 vs F20, review scored 4.4)*
- Picking a waterfall to a stated minimum operating cash, rather than a point-in-time cash balance, is the right archetype for a sources-and-uses board question. — It forces the trough and the covenant to be shown; the only failure is that the trough and the minimum are not yet tied out, which is fixable without changing the page. *(slide 4 (waterfall_bridge), title and intent, review scored 4.4)*
- Cover carries no analytic facts and the closing is framed as a roadmap with owners, timing and a change condition. — That is the correct structure for a board briefing and the right bookends; keep it while the middle three slides are rebuilt. *(slide 1 (cover) and slide 5 (closing), review scored 4.4)*
- The recommendation is sized and specific: $13.29B dividend, fund from $20.21B cash (F5), pace buyback at $848M (F9), hold capex near $6.5B (F40). — A board can vote on numbers, not adjectives; the answer-first structure with a three-option table is the right spine for a capital-allocation briefing. *(Governing thought and Slide 2, review scored 4.4)*
- The 1.88% earnings yield (F30) benchmarked against the 4.12% 13-week T-bill (F24), with the 5.15% 10-year (F25) available as a second anchor. — This is the correct comparison for a buyback-vs-cash decision — the return on the marginal dollar retired vs. the return on the marginal dollar held — and it is sourced to a same-date close (2026-11-13). *(Slide 3, yield comparison, review scored 4.4)*
- $20.21B cash (F5) against a $13.29B outflow, with the 3.1x cash-cover-of-capex ratio (F27) available. — The deck tests solvency before recommending distribution, which is the first question a committee asks; the cash figure and the cover ratio are both in the material. *(Slide 4, balance-sheet check, review scored 4.4)*
- The recommendation is sized and stated first: '$13.3B special dividend = $30/share, $4B buyback authorization', with the funding identity cash $20.21B + FCF $9.38B (operating cash flow $15.82B less capex $6.43B) and metrics panel $13.3B / $4B / 14.5%. — Numbers-before-words; the reader can see the answer and the key magnitudes in one glance, which is what an executive summary must do. *(Slide 2 (executive_summary), review scored 4.35)*
- A sources-and-uses bridge from F5 $20.21B through F7 $15.82B, F4 $6.43B, F8 $1.15B, F9 $848M to the $13.3B special dividend, with the takeaway that the dividend is paid from cash on hand and the buyback is a ceiling drawn over time. — This is the right mechanic for a board: it shows the cash trough and distinguishes a fixed outflow from a flexible authorization. *(Slide 3 (waterfall_bridge), review scored 4.35)*
- The implied return 14.5% (F32: earnings yield 1.88% + operating cash flow growth 12.6%) is compared with the 6.4% cost of equity (F31), with the earnings yield correctly labeled as an EPS-accretion test only, not the return a buyback earns, and the 22.2% after-tax return on capital (F34) as the opportunity cost of growth. — This is the valuation logic the decision needs and the distinction on the earnings yield is exactly the right discipline; keep this framing through revision. *(Slide 4 (exhibit_argument), review scored 4.35)*
- Three to five steps with owners and timing, including buyback pace and price ceiling, holding capex at $6.43B, a minimum cash floor, and quarterly reporting of the implied return against the cost of equity. — Names the decisions and the monitoring discipline; the buyback price ceiling and the cash-floor review are the right controls. *(Slide 5 (closing), review scored 4.35)*
- Three returns placed side by side (buyback earnings yield 1.88%, ROC 22.2%, T-bill 4.12%) — This is the right comparison and it makes the argument visually: the buyback earns less than cash. Keep it; only fix the ROC denominator (leases) and the after-interest EPS. *(slide 4 chart and F30/F32/F24, review scored 4.05)*
- A cash bridge from opening cash $20.21B through operating cash flow $15.82B and capex $6.43B to the two distributions — A bridge is exactly the exhibit this decision needs — it shows self-funding rather than asserting it. The arithmetic is fixable; the archetype is correct. *(slide 3 bridge and F5/F7/F4/F26, review scored 4.05)*
- Free cash flow $9.38B defined as $15.82B operating cash flow less $6.43B capex, and FY2026 capex intention of $6.5B — The deck distinguishes historical capex from the forward plan, which is the correct offset to capital return and prevents a classic capex-vs-distribution apples-to-oranges error. *(F26 and F39, review scored 4.05)*
- 443.1M shares, $1,106.21 close, $490.19B market cap, and the $1B = 0.90M shares scaling rule — Denominators and scaling are stated explicitly, so any committee member can replay the buyback math. That discipline is rarer than it should be; keep it. *(F1, F21, F28, F29, review scored 4.05)*
- Every number carries its fact identifier and a source line (F1, F4, F5, F12, F18, F19, F26, F29, F30, F32), and computed figures state their formula, for example market capitalisation $490.19B = $1,106.21 x 443.1M shares (F28) and free cash flow $9.38B = $15.82B less $6.43B (F26). — The audit trail is already there. A director or committee member can trace any figure to a filing without asking, which is the standard that lets a reviewer focus on the decision rather than the arithmetic. *(Slide 3, waterfall; Slide 4, grid, review scored 4)*
- The slide explicitly guards against double-counting fiscal 2026 expenditures against an opening balance that already reflects them. — This is the most common error in a cash bridge built from a year-end balance sheet, and the storyboard has pre-empted it. Keep that guard in the built slide as a footnote. *(Slide 3, waterfall, instruction 'Do not subtract fiscal 2026 expenditures already reflected in opening cash', review scored 4)*
- The slide forbids qualitative scoring and invented project returns. — It keeps the comparison on cash effects and available measures rather than a scoring matrix that a committee would immediately discount. The discipline should hold through revisions. *(Slide 4, grid, instruction 'Show no qualitative scores or invented project returns', review scored 4)*
- The closing sets gates around the special dividend, the FY2027 investment plan and the optional buyback, and requires primary-source confirmation and a current cash forecast. — A board briefing that ends on verifiable next steps rather than further debate is the right structure; the gates only need dates and owners to be actionable. *(Slide 5, closing, review scored 4)*

### Storyboard: Senior partner (145)

- The governing thought appears in the cover title, before any exhibit: "Costco should reserve up to $4B for growth after its declared dividend." — A board member can read page one and know the answer. That is exactly what a board briefing must do. *(Slide 1 (node 0), review scored 8.45)*
- The recommendation is sized: $13.29B declared dividend outflow, $4B reserve, $20.21B August cash and $6.92B illustrative cash. — The board is being asked to approve a reserve, not a vague direction. The numbers are in the material and tied to SEC filings. *(Slide 2 (node 1.1), review scored 8.45)*
- The waterfall bridge uses $20.21B to $13.29B to $6.92B and states clearly that this is not current cash and excludes intervening flows. — It is the right visual for a bridge and it carries the caveat on the same page instead of hiding it in endnotes. *(Slide 3 (node 2.1), review scored 8.45)*
- The unscored options-by-criteria table with the 1.88% earnings yield, 3.62M shares, $9.03 per share and $165M Treasury interest. — It compares equal $4B choices on facts rather than fake ratings, and it distinguishes historical 22.2% ROC from forecast warehouse returns. *(Slide 4 (node 3.1), review scored 8.45)*
- Four numbered steps with owners and gates: Finance reconciles, Operations assesses, Finance forecasts, Board releases. — The closing is an ask with a process, not a summary. That is how a board briefing should end. *(Slide 5 (node 3.2), review scored 8.45)*
- 'The board's open decisions are to fund it from the $20.21B cash balance, to pace the $4B authorization at fiscal 2026's own $848M a year rather than a round $1.0B, and to keep capex at the fiscal 2026 rate of about $6.5B.' — This is the sentence a CEO can repeat. It names the amount, the funding source, the pace and the capex envelope. Keep it as the spine of the deck. *(governing thought, review scored 8)*
- 'Fund the declared $13.29B dividend from cash and pace the $4B buyback at $848M a year' — The title carries the recommendation and the numbers. This is the standard for every content slide in the deck. *(slide 2, title, review scored 8)*
- The 1.88% buyback earnings yield vs 4.12% cash yield comparison, with the crossover price marked — This is the single exhibit that justifies the recommendation. It turns a qualitative preference into a quantitative hurdle. Keep the crossover marked. *(slide 3, evidence, review scored 8)*
- A dated sources-and-uses schedule from the latest reported cash to a stated closing balance — This answers the board's first question: where does the $13.29B come from. A dated bridge is the right visual for a funding decision. *(slide 4, structure, review scored 8)*
- The title is the decision, not the question: 'Costco capital allocation: confirm the $13.3B dividend and set the buyback pace', with one number shown large. — A board should read the answer and the amount on the cover; this does exactly that and drops the $472.4B market value in favor of the $490.19B computed at the 13 November close. *(Slide 1 (cover), review scored 8)*
- Answer-first as a three-column options-vs-criteria table (A: $17.3B returned, $14.15B left, 14.5% vs 6.4%; B: $13.3B; C: $0 returned) with a decision line naming the special dividend, the $1B-a-year buyback pace and the $12B floor. — The board can see all three choices and the numbers that separate them on one page; the recommendation is stated, not implied. *(Slide 2 (executive_summary), review scored 8)*
- A single-period sources-and-uses bridge (opening $20.21B + $15.82B - $6.43B - regular dividend - $848M buybacks - $13.3B special = $14.15B) with the $12B floor as a dashed line and a $2.15B cushion, and the regular dividend taken at the 10-K run-rate of $5.54 x 443.1M = about $2.46B. — It shows the dividend is funded from cash on hand and the buyback is a flexible ceiling drawn over time — the exact distinction the decision turns on. *(Slide 3 (waterfall_bridge), review scored 8)*
- The 14.5% implied return against a 6.4% cost of equity, with the cost-of-equity build stated explicitly (10-year Treasury 5.15% + beta 0.24 x 5.0% equity risk premium, and the premium flagged as an assumption), and the sensitivity that it clears 6.4% down to about 4.6% growth. — This is the load-bearing evidence for the whole recommendation; naming the assumption and the break-even growth is what makes it defensible to an investment committee. *(Slide 4 (exhibit_argument), review scored 8)*
- Every claim has a fact ID behind it (F1-F50) and the two figures that could distort the answer are handled correctly: the nine-month 10-Q dividend (F8, $1.15B) is explicitly excluded in favor of the 10-K run-rate, and the earnings yield (1.88%) is labeled an accretion test, never compared with a cash yield. — That is the discipline that separates a board briefing from a deck; the two most tempting errors are called out rather than made. *(Governing thought and slides 2-4, review scored 8)*
- The recommendation is fully sized on the answer slide: $13.3B special dividend, $9.4B free cash flow, the 22.2% versus 1.88% comparison, and the $4B authorization, with the decision line requiring board approval at the November 2026 meeting. — A board can act on this page without any further reading. This is the standard I want every page held to. *(Slide 2, review scored 7.95)*
- The evidence chain starts from the primary SEC filing and builds up: F7 operating cash flow $15.82B, F4 capex $6.43B, F26 free cash flow $9.38B, F5 cash $20.21B, and F27 the 3.1x cover calculation. — Each number can be traced back to the 10-K and the cover ratio is computed from the same two filings, so the arithmetic can be checked in the room. *(Slide 3, review scored 7.95)*
- The ranking table runs all four uses of $1B of capital on a consistent basis — 22.2% on warehouse growth, 5.15% on debt repayment, 4.12% in cash, 1.88% on a buyback — with the source for each rate attached. — One table, one basis, four comparable rates. This is the right way to frame a capital allocation choice and it is the visual form the message calls for. *(Slide 4, review scored 7.95)*
- The closing is a real roadmap: four numbered steps, a named decision point at the November 2026 board, an explicit price discipline on the buyback, and a reporting commitment to the next quarterly board. — It gives the board something to approve and a date to come back on. That turns the briefing into a resolution. *(Slide 5, review scored 7.95)*
- The answer is stated on page 2 with a sized recommendation: fund $7.5B, return $6B ($4B buyback renewal plus $2B special dividend), leaving $22.53B cash and 2.7x cover. — A board chair can read the first line and know the decision. The amounts are specific and the cash walk is a one-liner. This is the right place for the answer. *(slide 2, executive summary, review scored 7.95)*
- The capacity argument distinguishes FY2026 OCF cover (2.1x on FY2027 capex before any growth) from cash cover (3.1x on FY2026 actual, 2.7x on FY2027 plan after the return), and includes a three-column sensitivity table with break-even OCF. — It separates the recurring cash generation question from the balance-sheet cover question, which is exactly the distinction the board needs. The sensitivity table is the right evidence for a plan that depends on flat OCF. *(slide 3, exhibit + argument, review scored 7.95)*
- The per-dollar comparison of buyback, special dividend, and warehouse capex on a common basis, with the time basis labeled and the capex row highlighted. — It answers the question 'what does each option do with $1B?' in one table. The earnings-yield versus T-bill comparison (1.88% vs 4.12%) is a clean, defensible way to show that buying back stock at $1,106.21 is not free money. *(slide 4, table_focus, review scored 7.95)*
- The four dated steps with owners and milestones, plus the risks line (capex overrun, OCF shortfall, authorization expiry). — It converts the recommendation into an agenda for the December meeting. The risks line is specific to the plan, not generic. *(slide 5, closing, review scored 7.95)*
- The answer is on the page and it is sized: 'fund the $6.5B fiscal 2026 capex plan, then return $4B via buyback because the remaining $4B has no project above the 3.10% after-tax cash hurdle' — A CEO can repeat it in one sentence, it names the amount and the reason, and it sets the hurdle explicitly so the conclusion is falsifiable rather than a preference. This is what answer-first looks like. *(Slide 2, governing thought and the returns comparison, review scored 7.8)*
- 'Fund the $6.5B plan, then return $4B: no project clears the 3.10% cash hurdle' -> 'The $6.5B capex plan earns 22.2% after tax and adds 29 warehouses in FY26' -> 'Growth earns 22.2%, buyback 1.88%, cash 3.10%: return only the surplus' -> 'Approve a $4B authorization at the December board meeting' — Every title is a claim with a number, they flow, and there is no repetition. Even without the deck, a reader gets the whole argument. This is the strongest thing in the storyboard and it must survive the revision intact. *(Slides 1-5 read as titles only, review scored 7.8)*
- 'a buyback only creates value if the earnings yield exceeds the after-tax cost of the cash, and at $1,106.21 the full-company earnings yield is 1.88% against 3.10% after tax, so only the surplus is returned' — This is the analytical spine of the whole deck and it is written as a test, not an opinion. It also correctly separates gross EPS accretion on a buyback from net EPS after the cost of the cash — most first drafts do not get that distinction right. *(Slide 4, the hurdle test, review scored 7.8)*
- Four steps, an owner (CFO), a timing anchor (December meeting, January 2027 expiry), a success measure (3.6M shares in 12 months) and a guardrail — Boards approve motions, not analyses. This page tells them exactly what they are voting on and what would cause it to be paused. It is tight and it is actionable. *(Slide 5, the ask, review scored 7.8)*
- '$5.88 annual dividend (F38) does not tie to $5.54 declared (F12); the $0.34 difference is unreconciled, so use $5.54 x 443.1M = $2.45B and label it' — Flagging a data conflict and choosing the audited SEC figure over a third-party aggregator, in the open, is exactly right. Keep that discipline through the arithmetic rebuild. *(Slide 2 and slide 4, treatment of the $5.88 vs $5.54 dividend discrepancy, review scored 7.8)*
- The cover answers the brief's question instead of restating it, and leads with the size of the dividend. — A director reading only the cover still learns the decision. This is what a cover must do. *(Slide 1 title, 'Costco capital allocation: confirm the $13.3B dividend and set the buyback pace', review scored 7.75)*
- Option A/B/C, each with cash returned, remaining cash, cushion vs the $12B floor, buyback return, capex and warehouse impact, plus the bold decision line. — The board sees three real choices against identical criteria, and the recommendation is stated before the support. This is the right structure and it must survive revision. *(Slide 2, three-column options table with the decision line, review scored 7.75)*
- Every bar labelled by period, the bridge ties to $12.99B, the special dividend is highlighted, and the takeaway distinguishes the cash dividend from the flexible buyback authorization. — The trough claim is proven on its own page and the dividend-vs-draw distinction is the heart of the recommendation. *(Slide 3, sources-and-uses bridge with the $12B floor as a dashed line, review scored 7.75)*
- The buyback return is tested against the hurdle at several growth cases, and the page states that earnings yield 1.88% is an EPS-accretion test, never a cash yield. — This is the analytical spine of the recommendation and the page does not hide the negative case. *(Slide 4, left bar chart with 9.2%-10.2% band and the right argument panel, review scored 7.75)*
- Verbs only, no restated amounts, owner and timing on each step, milestones dated. — A board can act on this the same day. *(Slide 5, five-step roadmap with owners and timing, review scored 7.75)*
- The recommendation is stated as a monitoring stance on the existing authorization, not as a new distribution, and the brief distinguishes it from the declared $30 per share special dividend. — This is the single most important framing choice in the deck. A board reading it cannot mistakenly think a further payout is being proposed. Keep this language exactly. *(Slide 1 cover, review scored 7.7)*
- Every bridge line is labeled with its source status: operating cash flow as consensus estimate, capex as company guidance, and the $4B buyback shown separately. The page carries a visible caveat that current cash and other cash movements are unknown. — On a board page, mixing estimate, guidance and actual without labels is how you lose the room. The labels let the board see exactly where the uncertainty sits, which is the honest and the right presentation. *(Slide 3, review scored 7.7)*
- The comparison uses directly labeled cash effects and income measures, shows the pretax Treasury rate next to the buyback earnings yield, and explicitly refuses to score the options or imply company-wide returns are incremental warehouse returns. — That is exactly the trap in a capital allocation page: comparing a risk-free rate to a portfolio return and calling it a decision. The page resists it. Keep the 'do not score' instruction. *(Slide 4, review scored 7.7)*
- The answer, the amounts, and the split are in the title: '$3.0B buyback and a $1.0B special dividend,' with 'Why not more' already on the same page in one line. — A board can stop after page two and know what it is being asked to approve and why it is not larger. This is what answer-first looks like. *(Slide 2, title and opening panel, review scored 7.7)*
- Two numbers only and the explicit instruction 'No ratio to an undefined base, no market cap, no "vs." framing.' — Discipline on the cover is rare and it sets the standard for the rest of the deck. Keep it. *(Slide 1, cover framing, review scored 7.7)*
- The price sensitivity is presented as a rate the board can multiply, not a single-point claim. — It lets the board test the recommendation against the buyback price without rerunning the arithmetic, and it shows the earnings-yield argument holds across the range. *(Slide 4, scaling rule at $1,106.21: '$1B retires 0.90M shares' and the 52-week panel at $850 versus $1,106.21, review scored 7.7)*
- The ask is a resolvable sentence and the authorization arithmetic is shown. — Boards approve resolutions, not themes. The renewal gap is identified and closed on the same page. *(Slide 5, lead resolution and the $1,115M + $1,885M = $3.0B reconciliation, review scored 7.7)*
- It answers the question with dollars and a consequence: renew $4B, declare $2B, fund $7.5B capex, leave $22.53B cash and 2.7x cover. — A CEO can repeat it and act on it. It is not a topic, it is a decision. *(Governing thought, review scored 7.7)*
- The cash walk foots: $20.21B + $15.82B - $7.5B - $6B = $22.53B, with the split, the per-dollar scaling rule, and the decision line (December meeting, $4B renewal before January expiry, $2B declaration). — The answer is on page 2, not page 9, and every number reconciles. This is the page the board will read first. *(Slide 2 executive_summary, review scored 7.7)*
- It puts buyback, dividend and capex on one per-dollar, per-year basis with the time basis labelled, and flags the 22.2% as a historical average, not the marginal FY2027 return. — It makes the trade-off arithmetic, not rhetoric, and it protects against the obvious counter ('you are starving the 22.2% return') before the board can raise it. *(Slide 4 three-uses table, review scored 7.7)*
- Ratios are labelled x, sources are footnoted, the 52-week low sensitivity ($4B retires 4.71M shares at $850.00) and the risk case (OCF to $12.66B still leaves $19.37B) are both sized. — Sizing the down case and the low-price case is what a board wants; it shows downside was done, not asserted. *(Slide 3, cover and closing, review scored 7.7)*
- Cover framing, big-numbers summary, exhibit plus argument, table, roadmap: five distinct layouts for five different messages. — No run of identical pages. Each layout fits its message. That part is done. *(Visual plan, review scored 7.7)*
- The recommendation is sized on page one and restated on page two with amounts, share count and per-share effect: $3.0B buyback, 2.7M shares, 0.6%, $1.0B special dividend at $2.26 per share. — A CEO can repeat the answer without the deck. - The $20.21B cash, $9.38B FY26 free cash flow, $8.32B FY27 free cash flow before returns and the one-row 'why not more' answer. — It pre-empts the obvious board question and shows the recommendation is funded, not aspiration. *(Slide 2, metrics panel and 'Why not more' line, review scored 7.5)*
- A dated bridge from the 2026-08-30 cash balance with periods on each row and a reconciling line for the dividend run-rate. — This is the page a board member will actually audit. Dated rows are what makes it auditable. *(Slide 3, sources-and-uses with every row dated, review scored 7.5)*
- Four rows scored on capital deployed, return and effect per share, with the recommended row highlighted. — It turns a debate into a comparison and makes the rejection of the $4.0B buyback visible rather than implied. *(Slide 4, options table structure, review scored 7.5)*

- Every claim is tied to a numbered fact with a source and date. — Nothing in the deck is unsourced. That discipline is the difference between a briefing and an opinion. *(Storyboard metadata, F-numbers on every fact, review scored 7.5)*
- Answer first with the decision line: 'Decision today: confirm funding for the declared $13.3B special dividend and the $6.5B capex plan, and adopt a $670 per share buyback floor.' — A board chair can read that one sentence and know what they are being asked to approve. - The buyback earnings yield vs share price line, with the $670 crossing point marked against the 3.10% after-tax cash yield. — It converts a judgment ('is the stock cheap?') into a legible price threshold. The visual does the work the argument needs. *(Slide 4, exhibit, review scored 7.45)*
- Four steps with owners (CFO, COO, Treasurer) and timing, plus explicit measures of success. — It closes the loop from decision to accountability. Most first drafts end with a summary; this ends with who does what and what success looks like. *(Slide 5, roadmap, review scored 7.45)*

- The recommendation is sized, actionable and lands on page two, not page nine. — A board can approve it without a voice-over, which is exactly what the brief asked for. - The deck tests the dividend against cash, not against a wish: it starts from reported cash and uses reported flow. — This is the right page to have in a capital allocation briefing. - It compares like with like: the return on capital, the buyback's earnings yield and the cash yield are in one place so the choice can be read off the page. — Most decks state a preference and hide the comparison. - The cover names the decision and the amounts on one line. — The cover is not a title page, it is the first claim. Fix the $17.3B and the rest of the sentence holds. *(cover, "Costco capital allocation: fund growth and return $17.3B", review scored 7.45)*
- Answer-first executive summary sized in five metrics: $13.3B special dividend, $6.5B capex plan, sources ($20.21B cash + $9.38B FCF, net $20.6B after capex and ordinary dividends), the three return figures, and the $670 floor. — A CEO can repeat the answer and the amounts.
