- The cover correctly narrows the brief: the $30 special dividend was declared on 11 November 2026 and this briefing addresses funding, capex and the $4B authorization, not whether to declare. — It prevents the board from relitigating a decision already taken and focuses the meeting on the two live choices, funding and buyback discipline. *(Slide 1, F37/F38 and the 'declared on 11 November 2026' line, review scored 7.4)*

- The bridge from $20.21B opening cash through operating cash flow, capex, special dividend and ordinary dividends to $4.15B ending cash, plus the share-retirement math ($4B at $1,106.21 retires 3.62M shares, 0.82%; at $670 it retires 5.97M, 1.35%). — Two decisions, funding and execution, are shown in the arithmetic that settles them. - 'Historical capital earned 22.2% after tax; buyback earnings yield is 1.88%' puts two numbers in the title and signals a comparison. — Action titles with figures are the standard; it tells the reader the point before they read the chart. *(slide 3, title, review scored 7.25)*
- The same illustrative $1B is applied across all three options as an apples-to-apples comparator, with explicit labels that it is neither a proposed return nor a reserved growth budget. — This is exactly how a fair options-vs-criteria page should be built; it prevents the classic 'you sized the buyback but not the dividend' attack. *(slide 2 'must show', review scored 7.25)*
- 'Retain the $0 policy until liquidity and project returns are tested' with named owners (Treasury, CFO, IR) and a clear statement that no WACC or FY2027 cash-flow forecast is available. — Flags the gaps rather than hallucinating them — a partner or committee will trust the analysis more, not less, for that candour. *(slide 4 title and 'must show', review scored 7.25)*

- The recommendation is on page 1 and restated with an amount on page 2: "Protect warehouse funding by deferring an additional $4B distribution." — A CEO can repeat it and a board can vote on it; this is the correct answer-first structure and must survive every revision. *(Slides 1 and 2, review scored 7.2)*
- Waterfall bridge of FY26 year-end cash through the declared dividend and labelled FY27 scenario flows, with the optional $4B buyback shown separately. — The right visual for a change-over-time question, it isolates the decision from the already-declared outflow, and it is the only slide that makes the cash constraint tangible. *(Slide 3, review scored 7.2)*
- "Holding $4B preserves cash while the buyback offers a 1.88% earnings yield" uses a real, sourced measure (F30) rather than a qualitative score. — This is the kind of quantified trade-off a board can interrogate; fix the warehouse side and this page works. *(Slide 4 title, review scored 7.2)*
- Three CFO-owned gates with primary-source confirmation and a current cash forecast. — It converts the recommendation into an ask with accountability, which is what a closing page is for. *(Slide 5, review scored 7.2)*

- One sentence a CEO could repeat: 'Fund the $6.5B warehouse plan first, then return $4B of surplus cash as a buyback, because growth earns 22.2% while a buyback earns 1.88%'. — It is a decision with a stated mechanism, not a topic. - The exhibit does the real work: 22.2% vs 1.88% vs 4.12% on the left, the buyback math and the below-T-bill conclusion on the right. — It is the only page that argues rather than confirms, and it is correctly built as options against criteria. Keep this structure. *(slide 4, exhibit_argument, review scored 7.15)*
- A waterfall for the cash bridge and a two-panel return comparison for the trade-off — the visual follows the message on both pages. — No run of identical layouts; a reader knows what to look at before reading the words. *(slides 3-4, visual plan, review scored 7.15)*
- 'Fund $7.5B of FY2027 capex and return $6B: $4B buyback plus $2B special dividend' — a sized, dated, actionable recommendation on the second slide, not the ninth. — This is what a board briefing is for. - 'Warehouse capex returns 22.2% on existing capital; a buyback returns 1.88% on each dollar' — two levels compared on one page, with the punchline in the title. — The title is a claim, not a topic. - Four dated steps with owners, milestones and a risks line. — Most briefings end with 'we recommend' and no plan. This one tells the board what happens Monday. Keep the dated steps; fix the buyback floor rationale. *(Slide 5, closing, roadmap, review scored 7)*
- Every number carries a source: SEC XBRL tags with URLs, Yahoo Finance for market data, as-of dates on each (shares 2026-09-29, cash 2026-08-30, price 2026-11-13). — A committee can check any figure without asking. - The recommendation is stated in one sentence with the two amounts, and it is on the executive summary page, not buried at the bottom of the deck. — Answer first, with numbers, is what the brief asked for and it is the hardest thing to get right. - The title is a claim with two numbers, and it is the right claim for this board. — This is the one page where the story goes from accounting to judgement. - This is exactly the right instrument for the question, and the sources are SEC XBRL with filing dates, not press coverage. — A board asking about a $13.3B distribution will ask whether the company can afford it. - These two facts make the buyback talk concrete rather than rhetorical, and they are already surfaced. — They let slide 2 give a real consequence to the buyback option instead of a gesture. - Title states the recommendation with both amounts and both uses: 'Fund $7.5B of FY2027 capex and return $6B: $4B buyback plus $2B special dividend.' — A board can repeat it back after one read. That is exactly what an answer-first page must do, and it is the hardest thing to get right in a ghost deck. *(slide 2, executive summary, review scored 6.95)*
- F29 gives per-$1B scaling: 0.90M shares retired (0.20% of shares) and the per-share special dividend, and F31 gives EPS $20.76 to $20.80 after a $1B buyback. — The recommendation is sized, not directional. Numbers a committee can multiply are worth more than adjectives, and these two facts carry the whole sizing argument. *(slides 2 and 4, scaling rules, review scored 6.95)*
- The page pairs a cash-flow chart with the capacity argument that $9.38B FCF covers the $7.5B step-up. — Chart plus takeaway on one page is the right shape and the right density for a board. Keep that pairing when the data gaps are fixed. *(slide 3, exhibit, review scored 6.95)*
- The three-column idea — buyback, special dividend, warehouse capex — is the right comparison for the question the board actually asked. — It forces the alternative into the open instead of arguing only one side. The execution needs the sources above, but the concept stays. *(slide 4, table focus, review scored 6.95)*
- The recommendation names the size and the mechanism: '$4B via buyback,' 'retires 3.6M shares,' 'lifts gross EPS +$0.16,' 'cash to $16.21B from $20.21B.' — A board can act on this sentence without a voice-over. Sizing the recommendation on page 2 is exactly what the brief asked for. *(Slide 2, 'must show', review scored 6.9)*
- The cover stat line carries the three numbers a director needs to frame the decision: $490.19B market cap, $9.38B FCF, decision in December 2026. — Board covers are usually wasted. This one does the framing work in a single line and sets up the sizing on slide 2 without repeating it. *(Slide 1, cover, review scored 6.9)*
- The side-by-side fully-loaded EPS math: gross uplift +$0.16, lost after-tax interest, net −$0.12. — This is the honest presentation. It concedes the buyback is not accretive in the near term and shifts the case to surplus capital. A weaker deck would have shown only the +$0.16. *(Slide 4, exhibit_argument, review scored 6.9)*
- Linking the $4B to the $1.99B actually returned in FY26 (the $1.15B dividends + $848.0M repurchases) and adding the ordinary dividend line ($5.88/share × 443.1M = $2.61B, F38). — This is the reader's calibration. Without it, $4B looks arbitrary; with it, the board sees a 2x step up and can judge whether the increase is warranted. *(Slide 3, waterfall_bridge, review scored 6.9)*
- The governing thought and recommendation sit on page 1, not page 9: 'Protect growth funding after the $13.3B special dividend' appears on both the cover and the executive summary. — The board's question is answered before any support, which is the single most valuable property of a board deck. *(Storyboard, slides 1 and 2, review scored 6.75)*
- The visual choice fits the message: a bridge from cash through the dividend is exactly the right form for a liquidity question. — The visual plan is not decorative; it does the explaining. Keep the waterfall, fix the arithmetic and the omitted capex bar. *(Slide 3, waterfall visual, review scored 6.75)*
- The page is scoped to one common amount of $4B across the options rather than mixing a $13.3B dividend with a $4B authorization. — Like-for-like comparison is what makes an options page defensible, and it is what the board will use to choose. *(Slide 4, the $4B comparison, review scored 6.75)*
- The close correctly refuses to invent forecast or project-return values and asks for them instead. — Intellectual honesty on a page like this is a strength; it just needs dates and owners, not new numbers. *(Slide 5, the closing, review scored 6.75)*
- The title is the answer: 'Approve $4.0B of returns in fiscal 2027: $3.0B buyback and a $1.0B special dividend' and the decision line names the amount and the timing. — A CEO can repeat it and the board knows what is being asked before the evidence. This is the standard the other pages should meet. *(Slide 2, executive summary title and decision line, review scored 6.75)*
- The bridge starts from '$20.21B cash at 2026-08-30,' adds '$15.82B operating cash flow,' subtracts '$7.5B capex,' '$2.6B dividends,' and '$3.0B buyback,' leaving '$16.5B.' — It answers 'can we afford it' on one page and every input is traceable to a fact in hand. This is the evidence the rest of the deck needs. *(Slide 3, date-stamped cash schedule, review scored 6.75)*
- Fund warehouse growth, $3.0B buyback, $4.0B buyback, and $1.0B special dividend are compared on capital deployed, return, and effect per share, with the $3.0B row highlighted. — It makes the choice explicit and defensible rather than presenting a single option as inevitable. The 1.88% earnings yield versus 4.12% on T-bills is the right comparison. *(Slide 4, options table with four rows, review scored 6.75)*
- Pause buybacks if year-end cash falls below $4.0B or the share price exceeds $1,200, with the arithmetic at $1,200 ($20.76/$1,200 = 1.73% earnings yield, 2.5M shares retired rather than 2.7M). — A board can approve with a governor attached. It converts a recommendation into a policy the board can actually adopt. *(Slide 5, conditions that change the plan, review scored 6.75)*
- The $4.0B total ($3.0B buyback + $1.0B special dividend) and the $7.5B plan appear on slides 1, 2, 3, and 5 with the same figures. — Internal consistency is the first thing a committee checks and the easiest thing to get wrong. Keep it through revision. *(Storyboard overall, consistency of the recommendation, review scored 6.75)*
- Gross uplift +$0.16/share (4 x the $1B unit of +$0.04), less forgone after-tax cash $124.0M = $0.28/share on 443.1M shares, net -$0.12/share, and the incremental test $4B buys 3.62M shares at $1,106.21 = $75.1M of earnings against $124.0M of forgone cash, net -$48.9M. — The bridge is walkable in the room. Every line is a number with a source, and the net -$0.12 matches the governing thought exactly. This is the strongest piece of analysis in the pack — do not let the rewrite bury it. *(Slide 4, EPS bridge and incremental test, review scored 6.7)*
- Four sequenced steps with owners (CFO, company secretary), guardrail (pause if marginal warehouse ROIC falls below 3.10% or average execution price rises above $1,106.21), timing anchored to the December meeting and the January 2027 expiry, and a cost-of-doing-nothing line. — This is what a board ask should look like: what to approve, who owns it, when it starts, when it stops, what happens if nothing is done. The guardrail is the right one — it ties the recommendation to the same hurdle used on slide 4. *(Slide 5, closing, review scored 6.7)*
- Replaces the current $4,000M program with $1,115M remaining at end-2026 (10-K, 2026-08-30), closing the capacity gap before the January 2027 expiry. — This is the detail that turns a recommendation into a resolution. It tells the board exactly what it is signing, what it is superseding, and why it cannot wait for the February meeting. Keep it verbatim. *(Slide 5, step 1, review scored 6.7)*
- Fund the full $6.5B fiscal 2026 warehouse plan, then return the $4B that has no project clearing the after-tax cash hurdle (3.10% short-duration, 4.01% on the 10-year) as a buyback, accepting a net -$0.12 EPS. — One sentence, a real answer, a number the CEO can repeat. It states the amount, the instrument, the hurdle and the cost — all four. This is the standard the rest of the pack should be rewritten to. *(Governing thought, review scored 6.7)*
- Title is the open decision question: 'Costco capital allocation: $13.3B of declared returns against a $6.5B capex plan — fund, pace, or hold?' — The board sees the three choices immediately. A cover that frames the decision rather than the topic is exactly right for a board briefing. *(Slide 1 (cover), review scored 6.7)*
- States the recommendation first and sizes all three options with amounts: (A) $13.3B dividend plus $4B buyback, closing cash $12.30B; (B) $17.3B into capex at 22.2% return; (C) retain $30.4B at 4.12% for +$1.05 EPS. — Answer-first with sized options is what a board expects. The single deciding number (-$0.24 EPS on the buyback) is called out clearly. *(Slide 2 (executive summary), review scored 6.7)*
- Bridge from $20.21B opening cash through $15.82B operating cash flow, $6.43B capex, $13.3B dividend, $4B buyback to $12.30B closing cash, with each bar shown to the arithmetic. — A waterfall is the correct visual for a cash bridge. The numbers reconcile and the board can follow every step. *(Slide 3 (waterfall), review scored 6.7)*
- The cover answers the question in the title and names the three items in the order they are decided. — A board member reads one line and knows what is being asked of them. Keep this exact construction through revision; it is the standard the content titles should meet. *(Slide 1 title, 'fund the declared $13.3B dividend, pace the $4B buyback, hold the $6.5B capex plan', review scored 6.7)*
- Action title with the deciding number in it, and the three options are built as columns on closing cash at end of year 1 with the baseline $10.84B stated first. — This is answer-first done properly. The recommendation, the size and the alternative are on one page. Slide 3's closing-cash reconciliation to the same $9.84B is the discipline that makes it credible. *(Slide 2, 'Pace the $4B buyback at $1B a year: each $1B adds 4 cents of EPS but forgoes 7 cents of interest', review scored 6.7)*
- The returns comparison is placed on one labelled basis and the funding cost is shown explicitly at $31.0M after tax rather than asserted. — Showing the arithmetic invites the board to check it, which is what you want on the one page that carries the decision. Keep the explicit derivation; cut the duplicates around it. *(Slide 4, 'the buyback should be paced, not front-loaded, and that the special dividend returns cash the balance sheet does not need', review scored 6.7)*
- 'Approve $4.0B of returns in fiscal 2027: $3.0B buyback and a $1.0B special dividend' — Answer-first, sized, and the amounts are in the title. A CEO can read this slide alone and know what is being asked. This is the spine of the deck and it is right. *(Slide 2, title, review scored 6.7)*
- Dated sources-and-uses schedule from $20.21B cash to the trough — The board decision is a liquidity decision and this is exactly the right exhibit for it. It shows the math rather than asserting it. Keep the schedule; fix the trough labels. *(Slide 3, left side, review scored 6.7)*
- Buyback scored against the alternatives: 1.88% earnings yield versus 4.12% on cash and 22.2% on warehouse capital — This is the argument that determines the size of the buyback, not just its existence. Most decks would simply recommend the full authorization; this one explains why $3.0B and not $4.0B. That is board-grade reasoning. *(Slide 4, title and row logic, review scored 6.7)*
- Owners, timing, $250M/month pace, pause above $1,200, and the condition that changes the plan — Execution-ready. The pause trigger and the trough trigger give the board a monitoring rule, not just an approval. This is the page that makes the recommendation safe to approve. *(Slide 5, roadmap, review scored 6.7)*
- One sentence a CEO could repeat: fund the declared $13.3B special dividend and $4B buyback from $20.2B cash and $9.4B free cash flow while holding the $6.5B capex plan, because a buyback earns only 1.9% against 22.2% return on capital and 4.1% on idle cash. — It takes a position, sizes it, and gives the reason. That is the hardest part of a board briefing and it is done. Every downstream page should now serve this sentence. *(Governing thought, review scored 6.65)*
- A bridge from opening cash $20.21B through operating cash flow $15.82B, capex $6.43B, the $13.3B special dividend and $4B buyback to closing cash, with a stated takeaway that the plan is self-funded. — This is the page that proves the recommendation is financeable rather than aspirational. A board that sees the cash walk will approve; a board that only sees a return comparison will ask where the money comes from. Keep this page. *(Slide 3 (waterfall_bridge), review scored 6.65)*
- A single exhibit that puts 1.88% buyback earnings yield next to 22.2% return on capital and 4.12% on cash. — The right instinct: one chart, one argument, a number in the title. The comparison needs to be put on a consistent basis (see issue), but the structure — exhibit on the left, argument on the right — is the page that will actually change a mind. *(Slide 4 (exhibit_argument), review scored 6.65)*
- Cover, big numbers, waterfall, exhibit plus argument, roadmap. — Five slides, five different layouts, each matched to its message. No run of identical pages. This is the part of the storyboard that is already at standard and should survive the rewrite untouched. *(Visual plan across the five slides, review scored 6.65)*
- The recommendation appears up front and is sized: '$13.3B special dividend', '$4B buyback', '$1B a year', '$6.84B closing cash', '$0.06 and $0.24 per share'. — A CEO can repeat the answer after two minutes. The answer is stated, sized, and mapped to three named options. *(Slides 1 and 2, review scored 6.5)*
- The waterfall uses only facts already in hand (F5, F7, F4, F40, F41, F12, F1) and reconciles opening cash to closing cash to the dollar. — This is the slide a CFO will audit first. The arithmetic closes, and the source for each bar is named. *(Slide 3, review scored 6.5)*
- The slide names the four facts that matter: EPS after $1B buyback $20.80, interest forgone at 4.12%, buyback earnings yield 1.88%, return on capital 22.2%. — The comparison is the core of the decision: the buyback earns less than the 13-week bill, while the capex plan earns more than both. *(Slide 4, review scored 6.5)*
- The recommendation is stated up front and is consistent across cover and executive summary: pause the $4B buyback, treat the $13.3B dividend as already declared. — Answer-first is the hardest thing to get right in a board deck. This one does it. The consistency between slide 1 and slide 2 means the board reads the same claim twice and remembers it. *(Slide 1 and slide 2, titles, review scored 6.45)*
- The title carries two numbers (3.1% and 1.88%) and names the comparison before the body. — This is the shape every content title should have. The board can read the title alone and know the conclusion of the page. *(Slide 4, title, review scored 6.45)*
- The storyboard explicitly guards against overclaiming — 'Make clear that a pause preserves optionality rather than proving warehouse investment earns a superior incremental return.' — Intellectual honesty is worth more than a strong-looking claim in a board room. This is the right instinct and it must survive revision. *(Slide 2, must show, review scored 6.45)*
- The four cash-flow cases are labeled explicitly hypothetical, not forecast. — A board member will forgive a hypothetical; they will not forgive an unlabeled projection. This protects the deck's credibility. *(Slide 3, must show, review scored 6.45)*
- Three dated, document-based gates with explicit document requirements (current liquidity forecast, capital plan, incremental warehouse returns). — Gates are actionable. The CFO leaves knowing exactly what to produce and by when. *(Slide 5, must show, review scored 6.45)*
- "Fund the declared $13.3B dividend from cash and pace the $4B buyback at $1.0B a year" — It is an action title that answers the question on page two, names the instrument, the size and the pace. Keep that structure through the revision; only the arithmetic needs fixing. *(Slide 2, title, review scored 6.45)*
- "Buybacks earn 1.88% at today's price, below the 4.12% cash yield" — It states a claim with both numbers in the title and the comparison is the right one for the decision. This is the strongest page in the deck once the stale 52.0x P/E is removed. *(Slide 3, title, review scored 6.45)*
- Neutral title, subtitle, date November 2026, no analytic facts. — The cover brief is followed correctly — a board cover should not leak the recommendation. Only the title wording needs tightening. *(Slide 1, must show, review scored 6.45)*
- A dated sources-and-uses schedule from the latest reported cash to the trough, against a stated minimum operating cash. — This is exactly the right page to have in a five-page board briefing: the cash test is what makes the recommendation credible. The plan is right; the sourcing is the problem. *(Slide 4, must show, review scored 6.45)*
- The call to action with owners, timing and the condition that would change it. — A closing with a named trigger to revisit is the right instinct for a board page and rare in first drafts. Keep the structure, fix the ask from 'approve' to 'ratify execution'. *(Slide 5, must show, review scored 6.45)*
- The executive summary does what an executive summary should: it gives the answer first, with the amounts ($13.3B, $6.5B, $4B) and the return comparison (22.2% vs. 1.88% vs. 4.12%). — A board can read this page and know the recommendation without reading the rest of the deck. That's the standard. *(slide 2, review scored 6.25)*
- The cash bridge (operating cash flow $15.82B less capex $6.43B to FCF $9.38B) and the cash sources bar ($20.21B + $9.38B = $29.6B against $13.3B) are the right exhibits for a funding page. — The page answers the question 'can we afford it?' with two bars and one number. That's efficient. *(slide 3, review scored 6.25)*
- The ranked table of uses of capital is the right structure for a capital-allocation page: it forces a comparison and makes the recommendation visual. — The board sees the answer (22.2% is the highest) without reading the prose. *(slide 4, review scored 6.25)*
- The closing roadmap has owners, timing, and a measure of success. That's what a board wants from a closing page. — It converts the recommendation into actions and accountability. *(slide 5, review scored 6.25)*
- Options-vs-criteria format with an unscored comparison and the incremental-return caveat spelled out — This is the one page that actually frames a board choice. The caveat that the $4B is incremental to the $13.29B declared dividend is exactly the right anchor for the discussion. *(slide 4 (grid), review scored 6.1)*
- Every claim is tied to a numbered fact (F1, F5, F13, F21, F29, F32) with a primary source URL — A committee can audit the arithmetic line by line. That is the standard for fairness-book evidence and it is the strongest element of this draft. *(slides 2, 3, 4, review scored 6.1)*
- The 0.90M shares per $1B buyback and the 1.88% earnings yield are both shown, with the 'before lost interest' caveat — They correctly avoid the classic buyback slide error of presenting EPS accretion without the lost-interest offset. Keep this, even after the revisions. *(slide 4 (grid), review scored 6.1)*
- Clear definition of the $30 special dividend outflow as a calculation, not a forecast; explicitly notes it excludes intervening flows. — This is the kind of intellectual honesty that keeps the board from treating a press-report number as a committed cash outflow. *(Slide 2, review scored 5.7)*
- The 22.2% after-tax return on capital versus the 1.88% earnings yield is the strongest single comparison in the deck. — It gives the board a return-based reason to favor growth over buybacks, which is the governing thought. This comparison should be the spine of the storyline. *(Slide 3, review scored 5.7)*
- F26 free cash flow $9.38B and F27 cash cover of capex 3.1x are solid, computable evidence. — They demonstrate Costco can fund $6.43B capex from cash on hand plus operating cash flow without incremental returns, which directly supports the recommendation. *(Slide 2, review scored 5.7)*
- The sources-and-uses structure (opening cash $20.21B, operating cash flow $15.82B, capex $6.43B, dividends $1.15B, buybacks $848M, then the special dividend and buyback authorization) is the right way to show fundability. The takeaway 'the dividend is paid from cash on hand, the buyback is a ceiling drawn over time' is exactly the distinction the board needs. — It separates a committed outflow (the dividend) from an authorized ceiling (the buyback), which is the single most important framing for the decision. Keep this structure. *(Slide 3 waterfall_bridge, review scored 5.5)*
- The 14.5% implied return vs. 6.4% cost of equity is the correct decision test. The slide also correctly distinguishes the 1.88% earnings yield as an EPS-accretion test only, not a return, and brings in the 22.2% return on capital as evidence growth still earns more than cash. — This is the analytical core of the recommendation. Most decks would compare the earnings yield to the cost of equity and get the answer wrong. This one does not. Keep the 14.5% vs. 6.4% bar and the 1.88% clarification. *(Slide 4 exhibit_argument, review scored 5.5)*
- The five steps have owners and timing (approve dividend today, set buyback pace, hold capex at $6.43B and 30 net new warehouses, set cash floor, report quarterly). That is what a board asks for at the end of a briefing. — It converts the recommendation into an executable sequence. The quarterly reporting of implied return against cost of equity is a strong governance touch. Keep the owners and timing columns. *(Slide 5 closing, review scored 5.5)*

### Workbook: MD (55)

- Master status OK, closing cash ties to flows (difference 0), grid centre equals closing cash less interest (difference 0), and downside cash is not above base (difference 0) — The three ties test the two failure modes that kill a capital-allocation model — a cash roll that does not foot and a downside that is not symmetric — and they pass at zero, computed, not hard-coded. *(Checks!F3 MasterCheck = OK; the three checks with difference 0, review scored 7.55)*
- revenue_base=303,150 ($m, 303,150-303,150, F2 10-K), ocf_margin=5.2% (4.5%-5.5%, F7/F2), capex_base=6,500 ($m, 6,000-7,000, F35 10-Q), cash=20,210 ($m), shares=443.1m (F1), dps=5.88 ($, F33/F37), cash_yield=4.1% (2.0%-5.0%, F24 13-week T-bill) — Every input is sourced to a filing, guidance, market data or a labeled assumption, with a range and a named cell. That is what a back-up page should look like and it is what makes the model defensible. *(Inputs tab, rows 4-25: every assumption carries unit, range and source, review scored 7.55)*
- The downside is driver-based: 2.0% growth, 1.0% margin cut, 15% capex overrun, each separately ranged in Inputs — The downside is built from the same drivers as the base, so the grid and the downside scenario move together when an input changes; not a single blended 'stress' number. *(Model!B32:F32 downside FCF = revenue*(ocf_margin - down_margin_cut) + capex*(1+capex_overrun), review scored 7.55)*
- Special dividend deferred if cash breaks the floor (B33), buyback suspended if the year would breach the floor (B34), with COUNTIFS output 'Years the buyback is suspended' = 1 and special dividend deferred = 0 — The model does not just show a constrained cash balance; it models the board's actual levers and reports how many years each lever is pulled, which is the answer to 'funded in the downside?'. *(Model!B33:F33 / B38:B40 downside protection logic, review scored 7.55)*
- Outputs pull named ranges (ca_fcf_total=58,639.41, ca_uses_total=47,666.46, ca_cash_end=35,085.54, ca_min_headroom=5,956.87, ca_down_cash_end=7,048.46, ca_down_min_headroom=528.34) — The summary is linked to the named results, not retyped, so the answer text and the tables cannot drift from the Model. *(Outputs rows 12-26, B4 answer text, review scored 7.55)*
- Every check ties to zero difference and the master status is OK — This is the standard I expect — a single master check that goes red if any of the components fail, so a reviewer can see the workbook's self-assessment in one cell. *(Checks!F3 MasterCheck = OK; the three checks show difference 0, review scored 7.35)*
- Sign convention is consistent (outflows negative, inflows positive) and closing cash explicitly ties to the flows — No plug, no hard-code — every closing cash balance is the sum of its components, which is what makes the Checks tab credible. *(Model!B25 = -SUM(B14:F16) = 32,901.64; Model!B8 = B6+B7; Model!B17 = B10+B8+B11+B14+B15+B16, review scored 7.35)*
- Every input carries an SEC or market-data citation with the exact document and period — F2 10-K FY2026 for revenue, F1 for shares outstanding, F39/F40/F41 for the special dividend — this is the sourcing discipline that lets a committee trust the number before they read the story. *(Inputs tab — named cells with unit, range and source for revenue_base, cash, shares, share_price, dps, special_dps, buyback, ocf_margin, capex_base, down_growth, down_margin_cut, capex_overrun, review scored 7.35)*
- The downside explicitly models the special dividend being deferred and the buyback being suspended when the floor is breached — This is the right behaviour for a capital allocation model — the equity return is subordinated to the cash floor, and the model takes the action rather than assuming it away. *(Model!B33-B40 — downside special dividend conditional on cash floor breach, review scored 7.35)*
- The model reports how many years the buyback is suspended and how much special dividend is deferred — Quantifying the deferral, not just flagging it, is what a board needs to decide whether the downside is tolerable. *(Model!B38 = COUNTIFS(B34:F34,0,B16:F16,"<>0") = 1; Model!B39 = SUM(B33:F33)-SUM(B15:F15) = 13,293, review scored 7.35)*
- MasterCheck = OK, all three checks pass, closing cash ties to flows with difference 0, grid centre ties to closing cash less interest with difference 0. — Basic accounting identity is enforced, no plug, no residual imbalance. The grid centre reconciliation is exactly what a committee wants to see: the grid is not a separate model, it is the same model. *(Checks tab, review scored 7.3)*
- Formulas are consistent across the row: B10 = cash, F10 = E17; B17 = B10+B8+B11+B14+B15+B16, F17 = F10+F8+F11+F14+F15+F16; B12 = shares, F12 = E19. No hard-coded numbers inside formulas. — The cash flow recirculates a single flow (closing -> opening, closing shares -> opening shares). That is the discipline that makes a cash model auditable. Do not change the pattern in the next revision. *(Model rows 10, 12, 14, 16, 17, 32, 35, review scored 7.3)*
- Downside special dividend and buyback are dynamically suspended: B33 = IF(cash+B32+B11+B14+B15<min_cash,0,B15) and B34 = IF(...<min_cash,0,B16). The check ca_down_suspended = 3 is computed, not hard-coded. — This is a policy rule embedded in the model, not a plug. A committee can see exactly what causes the suspension. Keep this structure and extend it to the base case if the answer is 'raise the dividend'. *(Model rows 33-34, review scored 7.3)*
- Every input carries a source URL, and 6 have a range distinct from the source (rev_growth 5.0%-9.0%, ocf_margin 4.8%-5.6%, capex_growth 0.0%-6.0%, min_cash 4,000-10,000, buyback 0-1,000, cash_yield 2.0%-6.0%). — Ranges on the drivers that matter are what makes the sensitivity grid meaningful. The grid is built off rev_growth and capex_growth, not off growth rates alone, which is the right way round. *(Inputs header row, review scored 7.3)*
- The closing cash ties to its flows (difference 0), the grid centre ties to closing cash less interest (difference 0), and the downside is not above the base (difference 0). — A capital-allocation model's first job is to tie, and this one ties on the three identities that matter. Keep these checks through every revision. *(Checks tab (Checks!F3 MasterCheck = OK; three check rows, 0 not OK), review scored 7.2)*
- Inputs, Model, Outputs and Checks are separated; the assumption book carries value, unit, range and source for each named driver, including ranges on the drivers that matter. — This is the structure a reviewer wants: one place to change assumptions and a stated range on each, so a stress test is a range edit rather than a formula edit. *(Inputs tab (single assumptions tab; blue cells the only cells to change), review scored 7.2)*
- B11 = AVERAGE(B10+B15, B10+B15+B8+B14+B16) * cash_yield — average cash over the year, explicitly documented as 'no circularity: yield x average'. — Circularity is the standard first flaw in cash waterfall models and this one is deliberately linearized. Keep the note and keep the convention. *(Model!B11 (interest income), review scored 7.2)*
- The downside flexes three separate drivers (revenue growth to 2.0%, OCF margin cut 1.0%, capex overrun 15.0%), keeps regular dividends and defers the special / suspends the buyback with an IF that fires only when the floor is breached, and reports the number of suspended years (ca_down_suspended = 1) and the deferred special (ca_down_special_deferred = 0). — The downside is not just a lower growth rate; it changes the capital-allocation decision, which is the point of the model. This is the right shape of a stress case. *(Model rows 31-40 (downside), review scored 7.2)*
- Cash cover of capex in x, with a divide-by-zero guard. — Small thing, but it is the kind of guardrail most first drafts lack and it is the reason there are no #DIV/0 cells in the digest. *(Model!F21 = IF(F7=0,"NM",F17/-F7), review scored 7.2)*
- Balance sheet/cash ties check is live and shows OK with a zero difference, and the downside check confirms closing cash does not exceed the base. — This is the single most important control in a capital-allocation workbook. It is present, it is named, and it is green. Keep it through every revision. *(Checks!F3 (MasterCheck = OK) and Checks rows, review scored 7.05)*
- All assumptions are in a single input area, each with a unit, a range and a source (10-K, 10-Q, Yahoo Finance, US Treasury). — The model passes the hard-coded-number audit: no formulas outside the input tabs contain hard-coded numbers. This is the discipline that makes the rest fixable. *(Inputs tab (rows 3-25 with 69 named cells), review scored 7.05)*
- A simple readability metric and a yes/no funding answer are exposed at the top of the model. — The committee can see the answer before the arithmetic. Keep both, but fix the special-dividend treatment so the answer is still Yes after the corrections. *(Model!B21 (Cash cover of capex, 2.16x) and Outputs!B17 (Funded without new debt? = Yes), review scored 7.05)*
- The grid varies two drivers and shows the highest and lowest closing cash (33,635 to 59,126). — Range on the answer rather than a point estimate is exactly right. Extend it to include the OCF margin rather than replacing it. *(Model!B43:F48 (grid), review scored 7.05)*
- The capital-allocation identity ties: closing cash ties to flows (difference 0), grid centre ties to closing cash less interest (difference 0), and downside closing cash is not above base (difference 0). — The three ties that break most cash-flow models are the three that are checked here, and they are checked with an explicit difference rather than a boolean. That is the right way. *(Checks tab, Checks!F3 MasterCheck = OK; three checks with 0 not OK, review scored 7.05)*
- Each driver carries a source citation (10-K FY2026, 10-Q FY2026, Yahoo Finance) with an explicit range, including rev_growth (4.0%–8.0%), ocf_margin (4.5%–5.5%), capex_growth (0.0%–10.0%), cash_yield (2.0%–5.0%). — This is what a sourced assumption book looks like. The range columns on the operating drivers are the thing to keep through the next revision. *(Inputs tab, 16 named assumptions with unit, range and source on every row, review scored 7.05)*
- The downside case is built on drivers — lower growth, a margin cut, a capex overrun — rather than a haircut to the answer. — A downside built from drivers is testable and can be explained in the room. A downside built as a % of base FCF is neither. *(Model!B32: "=B31*(ocf_margin-down_margin_cut)+B7*(1+capex_overrun)", review scored 7.05)*
- In the downside, the model turns capital returns off when the cash floor would be breached, and reports the cost: ca_down_suspended = 1 year, ca_down_special_deferred = $0. — The model does not pretend the board can keep paying a dividend and buying back stock through a downside. The suspension logic is exactly what a capital-allocation case needs. *(Model!B33 and B34: special dividend deferred and buyback suspended conditionally on the cash floor, review scored 7.05)*
- Three checks — closing cash ties to flows (diff 0), grid centre = closing cash less interest (diff 0), downside closing cash not above base (diff 0) — all OK. — This is the discipline a board paper needs. Each check has a stated difference of 0 and a status. The tie-out on the grid centre is exactly the kind of internal consistency test that catches plug errors before the committee does. *(Checks tab (Checks!F3 = OK) and the three specific checks, review scored 6.95)*
- Every formula references named inputs (revenue_base, rev_growth, ocf_margin, capex_base, cash, min_cash, shares, share_price, dps, dps_growth, buyback, cash_yield); the digest confirms no hard-coded numbers inside formulas and no #REF!/#DIV/0. — The base case is a clean, named-driver build. A reviewer can trace revenue → OCF → capex → FCF → dividends → buyback → closing cash without hunting for a plug. This is the backbone of the workbook and it should not change. *(Model rows 5–19 (base case flow), review scored 6.95)*
- B11 = AVERAGE(B10+B15, B10+B15+B8+B14+B16) * cash_yield, which is a non-circular average-cash balance approach. cash_yield is sourced to the 13-week T-bill close of 4.12% on 2026-11-13 (F24). — Avoiding circularity on interest income while still earning a market yield is the right call — many first drafts either drop interest entirely or create a circular reference that breaks in Excel. The sourcing is also live: a dated market observation, not a stale year-end figure. *(Model row 11 (interest income) and Inputs cash_yield sourcing, review scored 6.95)*
- The downside case explicitly gates the special dividend and buyback on the cash floor, and the three downside drivers are individually ranged (0–4%, 0.5–2.0%, 5–25%). — The instinct to protect the floor before returning cash in a downside is exactly what a board wants to see. The driver choices (growth, margin, capex overrun) are the three the case actually depends on. *(Model rows 33–34 (downside dividend/buyback gating) and Inputs down_growth/down_margin_cut/capex_overrun, review scored 6.95)*
- Built on average cash balances with an explicit no-circularity note: B11 =AVERAGE(B10+B15,B10+B15+B8+B14+B16)*cash_yield — This is the correct way to handle interest on cash without creating a circular reference — no iterative calculation, no plug. Keep this structure. *(Model!B11:F11 — interest income, review scored 6.85)*
- Three checks tie with difference 0: closing cash ties to flows, grid centre ties to closing cash less interest, downside closing cash is below base. — The file's own arithmetic is internally consistent on the cases and there are no #REF!/#DIV/0 cells. A committee can trust the mechanics it can see. *(Checks!F3 — MasterCheck = OK, with 0 not-OK and differences of 0, review scored 6.85)*
- Downside is built from the drivers (growth - down_margin_cut, capex x (1+capex_overrun)), not from a single lower output number. — Driver-based stress, not a haircut. That is what lets the reader argue with the assumptions rather than with the arithmetic. *(Model!B32:F32 — downside FCF, review scored 6.85)*
- The cash floor is a named input (min_cash = $6,430m) and headroom over the floor is computed, not assumed. — Gives a concrete threshold for the 'funded?' answer — a board can debate the floor, which is the right place for the debate. *(Model row 17 and Outputs rows 8-9, review scored 6.85)*
- Closing cash ties to flows, grid centre ties to closing cash less interest, and downside does not exceed base — each with a zero difference. — Three independent ties caught before the file left the desk; this is the control standard I would hold the next revision to. *(Checks tab: MasterCheck = OK, all three checks difference 0, review scored 6.8)*
- Revenue, OCF and capex are driven from base × (1+growth) and margin/capex inputs, with the downside cutting the margin (ocf_margin-down_margin_cut = 4.2%) and overrunning capex (+15%). — Driver-based and shocked explicitly rather than a single growth-rate plug; the downside is a real margin/capex stress, not a revenue haircut alone. *(Model rows 5-7 and 31-32, review scored 6.8)*
- The downside suspends the buyback and defers the special dividend before breaching the floor, and counts the suspensions. — Shows the protective waterfall — cash returns flex before liquidity — which is exactly the mechanics a board wants to see in a stress. *(Model rows 38-39 and 33-34 (ca_down_suspended = 0, ca_down_special_deferred = 0), review scored 6.8)*
- Blue input cells, one assumptions area with unit/range/source per line, and calculations confined to Model/Outputs. — Nothing hard-coded inside formulas and no #REF/#DIV/0: the structural discipline is there, and it must survive the rebuild of the grid. *(Inputs tab, named cells (49 named ranges) and clean separation, review scored 6.8)*
- Cash roll-forward ties (difference 0), grid centre ties to closing cash less interest (difference 0), and downside is not above base (difference 0). — Every other number in the file could be wrong and I still could not publish without this. It is the single best feature of this model. *(Checks!F3 = OK; Checks rows show 'difference 0' three times, review scored 6.75)*
- Special dividend modelled as a one-time first-year outflow, deferred in the downside when it breaches the floor. — Treating the special as a discrete, testable event rather than smoothing it into cash returned is the right convention — it makes the downside bite visible. *(Model!B15 = -13,293 and F15 = 0; Outputs!B22 tied to Model!B39, review scored 6.75)*
- Downside flexes both the OCF margin (down 3.0pp) and capex (up 35%) simultaneously, and shows a loss of -$3,731m in 2027 widening to -$6,188m by 2031. — Many 'downside' cases flex one driver; the compound here is the right shape for a stress case and the arithmetic is transparent in the formula. *(Model r32: B32 = B31*(ocf_margin-down_margin_cut)+B7*(1+capex_overrun), review scored 6.75)*
- Grid corners span $34.6bn-$62.1bn closing cash across growth ±2pp and investment ±20%. — The grid is the only place in the file where the answer is shown as a distribution rather than a point — that is the correct instinct; it just needs range labels and a stated base-case position on the grid. *(Model!B50 = 34,564 and B51 = 62,146 (2-D grid on growth × investment), review scored 6.75)*
- revenue_base, ocf_margin, cash, shares, share_price, min_cash and others all cite a filing or market source. — Most first drafts have unnamed inputs; here I can walk every source. The gaps are in the £ranges” and the downside drivers, not in the base case. *(Assumption book — every input has a source URL (SEC 10-K, Yahoo Finance, special-dividend news article, IRX), review scored 6.75)*
- MasterCheck = OK; three checks pass with 0 not OK: closing cash ties to its flows (difference 0), grid centre = closing cash less interest (difference 0), downside closing cash is not above the base (difference 0). — The two plumbing tests that matter — flows tie to balances, and the downside is genuinely worse than the base — are live and clean. That is the minimum and it is met. *(Checks!F3 and Checks rows, review scored 6.45)*
- Every driver has a unit, a range and a named source with an SEC filing reference and URL: revenue_base, rev_growth, ocf_margin, capex_base, capex_growth, cash, min_cash, shares, share_price, dps, dps_growth, special_dps, buyback, cash_yield, down_growth, down_margin_cut, capex_overrun. — Nothing is unsourced. Each input points to a 10-K, 10-Q or a dated quote. That is the standard I hold the desk to and it is already there. *(Inputs tab, assumption book, review scored 6.45)*
- Base closing cash '=B10+B8+B11+B14+B15+B16' -> 14,010.53 and downside '=cash+B32+B11+B14+B33+B34' -> 8,538.82 use the same flow structure with case-specific drivers substituted. — The downside is not a separate calculation bolted on; it reuses the base architecture with down-case drivers. That is the right way to build it and it keeps the checks honest. *(Model row 17 vs row 35, review scored 6.45)*
- No #REF!, no #DIV/0!, no hard-coded numbers inside formulas outside the input tabs. — The X0-X9 and X11-X14 rules pass and the hard-code scan is clean. A committee can open any cell and trace it. *(Workbook-wide, review scored 6.45)*
- The three integrity checks are live and tie exactly (difference 0 on each), the checks tab is dedicated and MasterCheck = OK, and no #REF!/#DIV/0 cells exist anywhere in the recalculated workbook. — The mechanical spine is sound. A reviewer can trust that Model!B17, B23, B25 and the named outputs are not plugged — the model's own arithmetic is closed. *(Checks tab: Master status OK; three checks (closing cash ties, grid centre ties, downside not above base) all OK with difference 0, review scored 6.25)*
- Every driver has an explicit range and will not silently accept a point estimate. Named cells (not hard-coded numbers) are used in every Model formula I can see. — This is the discipline most capital-allocation models skip. It makes the sensitivities and grid possible and lets a committee ask 'what range?' and get an answer. *(Inputs tab: 18 named drivers, all with unit ($m, %, $, m shares), range and a filing / dated source URL, review scored 6.25)*
- The workbook goes beyond 'how much cash' to 'how many years of coverage' and 'when is the pinch point' — and puts the year on the Outputs page. — Those are the two questions an IC actually asks. INDEX/MATCH back to the year row is the right mechanism; it will survive when inputs change. *(Model!B21 cash-cover-of-capex and Model!B36/B40 lowest-headroom output; Outputs!B25/B26 year-of-lowest-headroom via INDEX/MATCH, review scored 6.25)*
- One formula per row across the timeline, no hard-coded plugs inside calculations, consistent sign convention (capex, dividends, buybacks negative; FCF positive). — This is what makes the model auditable and reusable. Do not break this in the next revision. *(Formula consistency: Model!B5:F5 through Model!B20:F20 use the same formula pattern left to right; downside rows B31:F40 mirror; no hard-coded numbers inside formulas, review scored 6.25)*
- All three checks tie with difference 0 and MasterCheck shows OK — A cash-flow identity check plus a downside-vs-base integrity check is exactly the minimum bar, and it is passing. *(Checks!F3 = OK, and the three checks (closing cash ties to flows; grid centre = closing cash less interest; downside closing cash ≤ base), review scored 5.9)*
- Anchored to the FY2026 10-K with an SEC Archives URL — The base is tied to a filing, not a memory, and that is the discipline that should hold across every input. *(Inputs!B2 'Revenue, last full fiscal year ($m)' = 303,150, review scored 5.9)*
- Every calculation row pulls from a named input — This is what allows an inputs-only flex, and it should be preserved as the sensitivity and downside logic are extended. *(No hard-coded numbers inside formulas outside Inputs, and 55 named cells, review scored 5.9)*

### Workbook: Senior partner (55)

- Closing cash ties to its flows; grid centre ties to closing cash less interest; downside closing cash is not above base — all pass. — This is what lets a reviewer trust every number downstream. Keep it and keep the MasterCheck visible on Outputs, not buried. *(Checks tab (Checks!F3 = OK, three ties at difference 0), review scored 7.75)*
- One sentence in plain English: 'Over the forecast the business generates $58,639m of free cash flow and returns $32,902m to shareholders; cash ends at...' — The answer is up front and quantified. Most models don't do this. This is the single best thing in the workbook. *(Outputs!B4 (Answer cell), review scored 7.75)*
- Every assumption carries a named range, value, unit, range and an SEC/Yahoo source with a URL. — A client can trace any number to a filing. This is audit-ready. Do not let the next revision loosen it. *(Inputs tab (header row 3, sourced assumption book), review scored 7.75)*
- Clean driver chain: revenue_base*(1+rev_growth), times ocf_margin, plus capex. No plugs, no hard-coded numbers inside formulas. — A client can follow the logic in three lines. Preserve the driver discipline when you split the downside cases. *(Model rows 5-8 (revenue → OCF → FCF), review scored 7.75)*
- The base case rolls revenue -> OCF -> FCF -> cash with named inputs only, no hard-coded numbers, and three tie-out checks all reconcile (closing cash to flows, grid centre to closing cash less interest, downside not above base). — A reviewer can audit the roll-forward in ten minutes and trusts the arithmetic. That is the backbone the rest has to protect. *(Model rows 5-17 and Check Tab (Checks!F3 = OK), review scored 7.75)*
- Every driver has a stated source and, where relevant, a range (rev_growth 5.0%-9.0%, ocf_margin 4.8%-5.6%, cash_yield 2.0%-6.0%, min_cash 4,000-10,000). — It shows what is fact and what is judgment, which is exactly what an IC wants to see before it argues with a number. *(Inputs tab (blue-cell convention, range and source columns), review scored 7.75)*
- The interest formula averages opening and closing cash to avoid a circular reference, and dividends run on average shares across the buyback. — These are the two places most models blow up or hide a plug. Solving them cleanly and documenting it is real craft. *(Model!B11 (interest on average cash, no circularity) and Model!B14 (dividends on average shares), review scored 7.75)*
- Closing cash ties to its flows, the grid tie is tested, and the downside is not above the base — all green. — A committee can go straight to Outputs because the arithmetic is already verified. This is the discipline that separates a model from a spreadsheet. *(Checks tab, 3 checks, 0 not OK, MasterCheck = OK, review scored 7.65)*
- Every driver has a value, a range and a source; only blue cells are meant to be touched. — A reviewer can change one cell and see the answer move, and can challenge any number back to the filing. Keep this exactly as-is. *(Inputs tab, blue-cell convention with ranges and sources on every assumption, review scored 7.65)*
- The downside flexes the three things that would actually hurt Costco — growth to 2.0%, margin down 1.0pp, capex overrun 15% — and suspends the buyback before breaching the floor rather than plugging. — This is the logic of the business, not a plug. The buyback-suspension row (ca_down_suspended = 1, in 2028) is the single most useful number in the workbook because it tells the board what they would actually give up. *(Model rows 31-40 (downside block), review scored 7.65)*
- Growth +/-2pp across five columns against investment-plan change across five rows, giving a low of 18,523 and a high of 43,971. — The decision turns on whether a weaker revenue environment can still fund the returns. A two-way grid is the right tool; it just needs to reconcile to the base case (see issue). *(Model rows 43-48 (sensitivity grid), review scored 7.65)*
- The answer is stated on page one as a sentence: 'Over the forecast the business generates $58,639m of free cash flow and returns $32,902m to shareholders; cash ends at $51,458m.' — That is exactly the discipline a client deck needs — the question answered before the supporting pages. Keep this pattern. *(Outputs!B4 (the Answer cell), review scored 7.4)*
- Clean five-line bridge: opening cash + FCF + interest + dividends + special + buyback = closing cash, with the cash floor check one row below. — A client can follow the capital allocation without a voice-over. This is the backbone of the model — do not complicate it. *(Model!B10:B19 (base cash roll-forward), review scored 7.4)*
- Three structural ties all reconcile: cash ties to flows (diff 0), grid centre ties to cash (diff 0), downside ≤ base (diff 0). — Models that don't tie don't ship. This one ties and says so. *(Checks!F3 = OK with 0 not OK, review scored 7.4)*
- Every significant assumption carries a source key (F-tag to the filings) and a range. — This is what lets the client challenge an assumption rather than the model. Ranges with sources are how you earn the room's trust. *(Inputs!cash = 20,210, source F5 10-K FY2026; Inputs!revenue_base = 303,150, source F2 10-K FY2026, review scored 7.4)*
- The downside has an actual capital-allocation response — the model defers/suspends rather than pretending the downside cash can absorb everything. — Most first drafts would just let cash go negative. Building the response into the downside is the right instinct; it just needs to be more aggressive and better labelled (see issues). *(Model!B33:B34 (special dividend deferral, buyback suspension in downside), review scored 7.4)*
- The single-cell answer string ('Over the forecast the business generates $54,511m of free cash flow and returns $34,091m to shareholders; cash ends at ...') is followed by the components on consecutive rows. — This is the pattern that gets a committee to the point in fifteen seconds. Keep it — it is the entire reason this workbook is readable. *(Outputs!B4 and Outputs!B12-B19, review scored 7.4)*
- Three independent ties: closing cash to flows, grid centre to cash less interest, downside closing cash not above base — with zero not-OK statuses. — These are the right checks for this model, not cosmetic: they make the downside arithmetic defensible and they caught the interest-vs-cash tie. Do not remove any. *(Checks!B3:F5 and Checks!F3 = OK, review scored 7.4)*
- Every assumption carries a value, a range, and a source line that points at a 10-K, 10-Q, or market quote with a URL. No hard-coded numbers inside formulas. — This is the standard a committee will test you on. A reviewer can trace rev_growth back to the three-year 7.8% calculation and the 6.0% choice is visibly inside the computed range. *(Inputs!B3:B25, review scored 7.4)*
- The downside is built from the same three drivers (growth, OCF margin, capex overrun) rather than a plug adjustment, and the drivers have ranges tied to the historicals. — The downside is legible. The weakness is only that it is not flexed far enough — the structure is right. *(Model!B32:F32 (downside FCF), review scored 7.4)*
- The Outputs tab leads with the answer string and the seven decision numbers (FCF 53,907; cash returned 14,423; closing cash 68,138; min headroom 7,345; downside closing 22,482; downside min headroom 1,601; closing shares 441.8) — The question a client committee asks — does the plan fit inside the cash the business throws off — is answered on one page without opening the engine. That is the discipline most models lack. *(Outputs!B4, B12, B14, B15, B18, B19, B24, review scored 7.25)*
- A single master status plus three named tie checks with a stated sign/direction — It tells the reviewer the binder is closed and the downside cannot be flattering the base. The 'grid centre = closing cash less interest' check is the right one to have caught the grid problem in any future edit. *(Checks!F3 MasterCheck = OK, and the three tie checks (closing cash ties to flows diff 0; grid centre = closing cash less interest diff 0; downside not above base diff 0), review scored 7.25)*
- The base case is a driver tree a CFO can walk line by line, ending in 'Cash cover of capex (x)' 3.7x to 7.8x — No plugs, no hard-coded numbers inside formulas, and the coverage ratio is exactly the line that answers 'can they keep reinvesting'. Keep this layout through any refactor. *(Model rows 5-21 structure: revenue -> ocf -> capex -> fcf -> cash returned -> opening/closing cash -> headroom -> cash cover of capex, review scored 7.25)*
- Closing cash ties to flows (diff 0), grid centre equals closing cash less interest (diff 0), and downside closing cash is not above base (diff 0). — Every model that goes to an IC needs a tie-out the reader can trust without recomputing. Three checks, all passing, with explicit difference = 0 is exactly right. *(Checks tab (3 checks, MasterCheck = OK at Checks!F3), review scored 7.15)*
- Interest is computed off average cash including the opening and closing balance, with a note 'no circularity' and no hard-coded interest. — Interest-on-cash is the classic hidden plug in these models. Computing it from average balances and flagging the circularity choice is craft. *(Model!B11/F11 (interest income), review scored 7.15)*
- Special dividend deferred and buyback suspended via IF against the floor, and the file reports years suspended (Model!B38 = 3) and dividend deferred (B39 = 0). — The downside isn't just 'cash is negative' — it shows WHICH capital action breaks and when. That is the decision a board makes, and it is already in the workbook. *(Model!B33/B34 (downside special dividend and buyback), review scored 7.15)*
- Closing cash across growth and investment step changes, bounded at 47,830 and 75,409. — Quantifying the range around the base case is what a committee asks for. It exists; it just isn't surfaced as a headline. *(Model!B44:F48 (grid), review scored 7.15)*
- Revenue from the 10-K SEC XBRL, buyback from F9/F40, dps from the annual report — each with a URL. — Nothing is a plug. Every driver traces to a filing. Keep this through the next revision. *(Inputs tab (49 named cells, every input has a source URL), review scored 7.15)*
- Cash flow tie-out, grid-centre tie, and downside<=base monotonicity are all checked and green. — Every number on Outputs can be traced and the model never disagrees with itself. That is the discipline that lets a reviewer trust the arithmetic and spend time on the assumptions. *(Checks tab, Checks!F3 MasterCheck = OK, three checks all OK, review scored 7.1)*
- Every assumption has a value, a range, and a source (10-K / 10-Q / Yahoo, with URLs). 69 named cells; no hard-coded numbers inside formulas. — This is the way a model should be built. Any partner can walk the assumption list top to bottom and challenge any one input without touching formulas. *(Inputs tab structure (blue-cell-only convention, source column), review scored 7.1)*
- The downside actually suspends the buyback and defers the special dividend when the floor is breached, rather than pretending they still happen. — Most first drafts let the discretionary flows sail through a downside. This one behaves like a treasurer would. - Growth and capex flexed together across a +/- 2pp / +/- 20% range, bounding closing cash from $19.7B to $44.9B. — Bracketing the answer is exactly what an investment committee wants to see. - Drivers are explicit and formula-driven: revenue is revenue_base*(1+rev_growth), OCF is revenue*ocf_margin, FCF is OCF + capex. Downside is the same structure with three separate dials (down_growth, down_margin_cut, capex_overrun). No hard-coded numbers inside formulas (X9 pass). — A failure at any single driver is traceable in one step; a client can explain the model to another client without the modeler present. *(Model!B5:F8, B31:F32, review scored 6.85)*
- Closing cash is a clean sum of opening cash + FCF + interest - dividends - buyback, and the downside version uses the same line items with different values. Checks!F3 = OK; the closing-cash-ties-to-flows check returns 0 difference. — The balance-sheet identity holds and the waterfall is self-evident. This is the part of the workbook to preserve through the restructure. *(Model!B17 and B35, review scored 6.85)*
- A 5x5 sensitivity grid on growth (+/-2pp) x investment plan (+/-20% in 10% steps) gives a $34,564m to $62,146m range on closing cash. That is the right set of variables for a capital allocation decision. — Few first drafts include a proper two-way grid. The client sees the answer is robust across the central band; the failure is in not explaining where in the grid it breaks. *(Model!B44:F48 and Outputs!B23:B24, review scored 6.85)*
- Headroom over the $6,430m board cash floor is tracked year by year, base and downside. ca_min_headroom = 7,005.36 and ca_down_min_headroom = -20,714.36. — The floor is the right anchor. - Closing cash ties to flows, grid centre ties to closing cash less interest, and downside is forced below base. — Three genuine controls, no fake 'OK' cells — a reviewer can trust the arithmetic before reading the narrative. *(Checks tab (MasterCheck Checks!F3 = OK, three ties at 0 diff), review scored 6.75)*
- Every assumption carries a source tag (F2 10-K FY2026, F37 10-Q 2026-05-10, F21 Yahoo Finance 2026-11-13, etc.) with a stated range. — This is the difference between a model and a guess — the reviewer can challenge a driver against the document. Keep this discipline. *(Inputs tab (blue cells only, source column for every driver), review scored 6.75)*
- The special dividend is booked as a discrete event, not spread as a run-rate dividend, and the source correctly flags it as non-recurring. — The most common error on a $30/share special is to treat it as recurring and overstate the capital return. Keep it isolated in every scenario. *(Inputs!A12 special_dps = $30 with source 'F39/F40/F41 special cash dividend $30 per share declared 2026-11-11/12, payable 2026-12-04; F50 one-time, not a commitment', review scored 6.75)*
- The workbook ties: closing cash to flows (difference 0), grid centre to closing cash less interest (difference 0), downside cash not above base (difference 0). — A capital allocation model that does not tie is worthless in the room; this one does, and it says so in one cell a partner can find. *(Checks!F3 MasterCheck = OK, with three named checks and 0 failures, review scored 6.75)*
- Every assumption carries a named range (revenue_base, rev_growth, ocf_margin, capex_base, dps, buyback, cash_yield, down_growth) and a sourced note pointing at 10-K/10-Q/page references. — A client can trace every number back to a filing, and the model can be re-pointed without hunting through formulas. This is the house standard. *(Inputs, blue-cell convention with named ranges and a source on every line, review scored 6.75)*
- The downside defers the special (B33) and suspends the buyback (B34) automatically when the cash floor is breached; ca_down_suspended = 1. — The model shows what management would actually do under stress, not just a lower cash number. That is the right logic for a capital allocation page. *(Model row 33/34, IF-based special dividend and buyback suspension in the downside, review scored 6.75)*
- The answer is stated on the front page in one sentence: FCF of $59,350m, cash returned of $47,666m, ending cash of $35,840m. — The answer is not on page 9. Many first drafts hide it; this one does not. Keep that structure. *(Outputs!B4, review scored 6.75)*
- Closing cash ties to its flows (difference 0), grid centre ties to closing cash less interest (difference 0), and the downside is bounded below the base (difference 0). — A capital-allocation model that does not tie is worthless to a committee. These three checks cover the flow-to-stock tie, the grid anchor and the scenario ordering — the exact places such a model breaks. It is clean and it is the right set. *(Checks tab, Checks!F3 MasterCheck = OK; three checks all OK, zero differences, review scored 6.75)*
- B11 = AVERAGE(B10+B15, B10+B15+B8+B14+B16) * cash_yield, with the comment 'no circularity'. — Interest on average cash is the right convention and avoiding the circularity by ordering the average explicitly is the kind of detail that saves a rebuild later. Keep this if you restructure. *(Model row 11, Interest income formula uses AVERAGE of opening and closing cash, review scored 6.75)*
- Every assumption has a unit, a source URL and most have a stated range (rev_growth 3.0-8.0%, ocf_margin 4.0-6.0%, capex_growth -3.0-8.0%, cash_yield 2.5-5.5%, min_cash 10,105-20,210). — This is the difference between a model and a guess. The ranges in particular tell the reader which levers you consider uncertain and let him test the grid against them. Do not lose these when you rebuild the scenarios. *(Inputs tab, all 18 assumptions, review scored 6.75)*
- B33 = IF(cash+B32+B11+B14+B15<min_cash,0,B15); B34 = IF(...,0,B16); B38 counts suspensions; B39 sums the deferred special. — Cash-protective cutbacks in a downside are exactly what a board wants modelled. The logic is in there and it works (0 and 0 in this run). Preserve it — it becomes informative the moment the buyback is non-zero. *(Model rows 33-34 and 38-39, the downside buyback/special-dividend suspension logic, review scored 6.75)*
- A single convention: blue cells are the inputs. — Simple, standard and it lets a reviewer find the dials in seconds. Keep it when the model grows. *(Cover tab and Inputs tab header 'Blue cells are the only cells to change', review scored 6.75)*
- The closing cash ties to its own flows and the downside closing cash is checked against base — Tie-outs on the cash build are exactly what a reviewer looks for first; passing all three cleanly is the reason this file can be trusted mechanically. *(Checks tab (3 checks, 0 not OK; MasterCheck = OK), review scored 6.75)*
- Every assumption carries a unit and a named source, and growth/margin/dps inputs carry stated ranges — A reader can see where each number came from and what it can plausibly be — this is the discipline that lets the sensitivity analysis mean something. *(Inputs tab, blue-cell convention and per-line source ranges, review scored 6.75)*
- The model gives a plain Yes/No on whether capex and returns are funded without new debt, in base and downside — That is the actual board question, answered directly and early, rather than left for the reader to infer from the cash line. *(Outputs!B17 and B20, review scored 6.75)*
- Three independent checks with differences of exactly 0: closing cash ties to its flows, the grid centre ties to closing cash less interest, and downside closing cash is not above base. Master status OK. — This is the audit trail a committee partner asks for first. Keep it visible and keep it to these three; do not add checks that no one reads. *(Checks!F3 (MasterCheck) and the three check rows, review scored 6.4)*
- Every driver has a range and a named source (F2, F5, F7, F18, F33, F35, F38, F39, F21, F24). Revenue base $303,150m sourced to the FY2026 10-K, capex $6,500m sourced to the FY2026 10-Q, T-bill yield 4.12% sourced to Yahoo Finance. — An assumption is only a number until it has a source. This is what makes the model defensible. Protect this in the next revision; do not let a client-ready rebuild strip the sources. *(Inputs tab, assumption book with sources, review scored 6.4)*
- Interest income is calculated on average of opening and closing cash without a circular reference, with F11 = 1,267.49 in FY2031. — Many cash-flow models break here or hide it with iterative calculation. Doing it cleanly is a craft sign. *(Model!B11 (interest income, average cash) and the no-circularity note, review scored 6.4)*
- Revenue, OCF, capex, dividends, buyback and shares all trace to inputs. No hard-coded numbers inside formulas on any tab. — The business logic is visible: revenue × margin = OCF, minus capex = FCF, plus opening cash minus uses = closing cash. A client can follow it. That is the spine of the model and it is sound. *(Model rows 5-19 (drivers, not plugs), review scored 6.4)*

## All the large mistakes (blocking issues)

### Deck: MD (171)

- The liquidity floor, which is the entire decision, is sized off one accrual-period cash capex figure with a 1x/2x multiple that is a pure management plug. $6.43B x 2 = $12.86B is arithmetically correct, but nothing ties the floor to what it must cover: debt maturities (slide 2 and 3 both state 'Total debt and lease obligations are not in the material'), working capital swings, or seasonality. A floor set below the debt stack is not a floor. **Fix:** Either obtain total debt, lease obligations and the maturity schedule from the 10-K and build the floor bottom-up (near-term maturities + minimum operating cash + seasonality), or retitle slide 5 row 3 to 'Propose a floor methodology (not a floor level)' and delete the 2x illustration from the action item. Keep the 1x/2x sensitivity in a labelled appendix if the board wants a range, with the footnote already on the page stating the board sets the multiple.
- The recommendation is carried by a 1-2 point spread on equal-weighted management judgment with no economic input. Buybacks only scores 6 while the combined option scores 8, but the two-point gap is entirely the 'funds new warehouses' criterion, which has no number attached. The page states the test labels but not the scoring basis, so the reader cannot audit why growth-only takes 7 rather than 9. The combined option is pre-selected as the winner before the page is read. **Fix:** On slide 4, state the scoring anchor next to each option row — for example, the capex dollars each option leaves unfunded or the cash consumed — and show that the 'Funds new warehouses' score traces to the $6.43B capex requirement versus the $20.21B cash balance. If the grid cannot be tied to dollars, demote it from a score to a labelled qualitative screen and let the quantitative page carry the recommendation.
- The deck recommends a payout form be chosen by the next meeting without a single share price, share count, dividend per share, payout ratio or EPS-accretion figure anywhere in the five pages. A board cannot approve a buyback or special dividend with no per-share math — the recommendation is not supported by any number on the page. **Fix:** Add the dilution and accretion math before slide 5 goes out: current share price and share count, dividend per share and current payout ratio, and the per-share effect of a buyback at the recommended size. If that data is unavailable, change slide 5 row 4 from 'Choose the payout form' to 'Return with payout-form economics' and note that no per-share analysis has been prepared.
- The entire recommendation rests on a 9-point self-scored qualitative grid with hand-assigned weights and scores. Row 'Growth first, then return cash above a floor' scores 3/3/2 = 8, but 'Keeps flexibility' of 2 versus 3 for 'Keep funding growth only' is unsupported, and row 'Buybacks only' scores 'Uses surplus cash' 3 and 'Keeps flexibility' 2 = 6 — the difference between the winner and the last two options is a single judgment point. There is no dollar value, no per-share impact, no cost of capital anywhere in the deck; a board cannot rank capital allocation options on unweighted integers.
- No valuation content exists: no share price, no dividend history, no buyback accretion/dilution math, no ROIC or capex hurdle rate against which 'fund growth first' is justified. Slide 2 states 'Sizing the return needs share price and dividend history' — and then the deck never provides either. A payout recommendation without a per-share value is not actionable.
- The central number is unresolved: '$20.21B gross cash' is stated against unconfirmed debt and leases ('the net of debt and leases is not yet confirmed', 'Treasury to confirm debt, leases and maturities'). Slide 4 then computes '$13.78B of cash sits above one year of capex' and '$20.21B cash covers $6.43B capex 3.1x' — both on gross cash, ignoring debt and leases entirely. Surplus cash cannot be sized before the net position is known; every surplus figure in the deck is provisional.
- The core argument compares 22.2% company-wide after-tax ROIC to 4.12% on a 13-week T-bill and does not bridge to a marginal return on the $6.5B new warehouse capex. The page itself concedes the point in footnote 2 ('22.2% is company-wide after-tax return on capital, not the return on a marginal new warehouse') and in the callout ('Read with care'). As it stands, the '5.4x' multiple on slide 3's title is the ratio of two unlike things. **Fix:** Either (a) replace the 22.2% with a marginal after-tax return on the $6.5B programme, built from incremental warehouse economics (incremental NOPAT / incremental invested capital) with the build and ramp shown, or (b) keep 22.2% as context but retitle slide 3 and slide 2 point 1 to state it as company-wide ROIC versus the marginal cost of leaving cash idle, and add a sensitivity showing what marginal return is needed for the plan to clear the 4.12% (post-tax 3.10%) hurdle. Do not leave '5.4x' standing as the headline comparison.
- No downside or stress case anywhere in the deck. The plan is shown only with $6.43B/$6.5B capex, $9.38B FCF and no new debt; if FCF lands materially below $9.38B or capex overshoots, the $13.3B dividend plus $4B authorization exceed available cash. The 'Headroom $5.79B' on slide 4 is the only cushion and it is not stress-tested. **Fix:** Add a sensitivity on slide 4 or 5: headroom and net cash/debt at FCF −10%, −20% and capex +10% from the FY26 run-rate, keeping dividend and buyback fixed, and state the FCF level at which the plan requires new debt or the buyback is slowed.
- The waterfall mixes a committed outflow ($13.3B special dividend, payable 4 December 2026) with a discretionary authorization ($4B buyback, 'an authorization, not a committed outflow' per footnote 2) as if both were fixed uses, and the 'Capex intention' bar (−6.50) is the FY26 stated intention against $6.43B actually spent (footnote 1). The $5.79B 'Headroom' is therefore a residual of three items of different nature and periods. **Fix:** Split the waterfall into (i) committed uses — special dividend and FY26/FY27 capex — and (ii) the buyback shown separately as a discretionary use with its own schedule; relabel the headline 'Headroom' as 'Headroom before buyback' or 'after dividend and capex only', and state the period each bar covers on the chart itself, not only in the subtitle.
- The waterfall arithmetic does not reconcile with the callout. Chart bars: +20.21, -13.30, -4.00, +9.38, -6.50, ending bar labelled '5.79'. 20.21 - 13.30 - 4.00 + 9.38 - 6.50 = 5.79, so the arithmetic of the bars holds — but the box states 'Headroom left after all three outflows is $5.79B', while slide 2 states 'Covered by $20.21B cash and $9.38B free cash flow ... about $5.8B of headroom' and separately says 'Cash after paying the $30 per share dividend is about $6.9B'. $5.79B and $6.9B are two different headroom numbers on the same deck: $6.9B appears to exclude the $6.5B capex, $5.79B includes it. A director will ask which number is the headroom and get two answers. **Fix:** Pick one definition of headroom and use it on slides 2 and 4. Either state on slide 2 'cash after the $13.3B dividend and $6.5B capex is about $5.8B' and drop the $6.9B, or label the $6.9B as 'cash after dividend, before capex'. Rebuild the chart's final bar to match whichever definition is used.
- The headline economics rest on a number the deck itself disowns in footnote 2 of slide 3: '22.2% is company-wide after-tax return on capital, not the return on a marginal new warehouse investment.' Slide 2 and slide 3 both run 'Growth capital earns 22.2%' as the argument for the $6.5B plan, and slide 2 computes '5.4x the Treasury yield' off it. A company-wide ROIC is not the return on the incremental $6.5B. This is the single number the board is being asked to believe and it is not the number being quoted. **Fix:** Either replace the 22.2% with the incremental after-tax return on the $6.5B plan (requires the project-level return from the model), or retitle slides 2 and 3 to 'Company ROIC of 22.2% versus 3.10% on idle cash' and add a sentence stating the marginal return on the new capex is not disclosed and the board should ask for it. Do not run 'growth capital earns 22.2%' as the headline when the footnote says it does not.
- The central comparison is apples-to-oranges and internally inconsistent: 22.2% is company-wide after-tax ROIC (footnote 2 concedes 'not the return on a marginal new warehouse') versus a 3.10% after-tax holding return on cash. Those are not substitutes. Worse, the arithmetic on the after-tax T-bill does not tie: footnote 1 says 4.12% 'after 24.7% tax are about 3.10% and 1.42% (calcs $3C2, $3C3)' — 4.12% x (1-0.247) = 3.10% is correct, so the '1.42%' for the 5.15% 10-year is wrong arithmetic (1.42% is the 10-year pre-tax 5.15% net of an unstated ~72.4% rate, not 24.7%). One after-tax calculation per instrument. **Fix:** Rebuild slide 3's comparison as like-for-like marginal ROIC on the $6.5B capex versus the after-tax yield on the cash it would otherwise sit in, both at the same tax rate and both stated pre- and after-tax. Fix the 10-year after-tax figure (5.15% x (1-0.247) = 3.88%), and if the 10-year is not the right funding alternative say so without a wrong number.
- The recommendation contradicts the evidence presented. Slides 3 and 5 argue marginal capital earns 22.2% and the board should re-test '22.2% return on capital against the 4.12% earned on idle cash' (slide 5, item 4), yet slides 1-4 ask to distribute $17.3B ($13.3B dividend plus $4B buyback). No page compares returning capital versus deploying the $6.5B capex at the 22.2% rate, and no hurdle rate, WACC or payout-vs-invest trade-off is shown. As drafted, a hostile committee reads this as liquidating a business the deck says earns 22.2%. **Fix:** Insert a page before slide 1 that sizes the growth option at the 22.2% marginal rate against the cost of capital (state the WACC) and shows why the remaining $13.3B plus $4B cannot be redeployed at or above that rate. If it can, the recommendation must change.
- Buyback earnings yield is inconsistent with the numbers shown.
