Slide 2 states 'a buyback earns 1.88%' and slide 4 states '1.88% earnings yield on each dollar bought back' from '$20.76 diluted EPS / $1,106.21 share price'. 20.76/1106.21 = 1.877%, which rounds to 1.88% only if the pair is EPS/price; but the deck also says on slide 3 'the 1.88% a buyback earns' while slide 4 also cites a 5.15% 10-year Treasury as the alternative. The yield is being compared to a Treasury and a T-bill (4.12% on slide 3) on a pre-tax basis in one place and not in another; slide 3 footnote 2 says 'EPS effect is shown before lost interest on the cash used', so the 1.88% is not an incremental return at all. **Fix:** Restate the buyback line consistently as pre-tax earnings yield: $20.76 / $1,106.21 = 1.88% pre-tax, and note the after-tax equivalent or state clearly it is a pre-tax earnings yield. Remove the 'earns the least on the cash' claim unless the after-tax return on cash (T-bill 4.12% pre-tax, ~3.10% after 24.7% tax) is compared on the same basis.
- The 22.2% return on capital is computed as '$11.69B operating income x (1 - 24.7% tax) / ($35.80B equity + $3.91B debt)'. That is an after-tax operating return on total capital of $39.71B, i.e. 11.69 × 0.753 / 39.71 = 22.2%. The label says 'return on capital existing base' and 'return on capital is the historical after-tax return on the existing asset base'. This is not comparable to the 1.88% buyback earnings yield (an equity-holder earnings yield on price), so the deck is ranking a pre-tax equity yield against an after-tax return on book capital and drawing a conclusion from the comparison. **Fix:** Put both metrics on the same basis. Either (a) restate the 22.2% as a pre-tax return on capital and show the buyback on the same pre-tax basis, or (b) convert the buyback to an after-tax return on the $4B of capital deployed and show both after-tax. The conclusion 'growth earns more than the special dividend' (slide 2, box 4) should then be supported by the apples-to-apples comparison.
- Slide 2 footnote 1 says 'FY27 operating cash flow is not in the material; the capex plan is assumed to be covered by FY27 free cash flow, not available in the sources.' Slide 5 then says 'Draw the existing $4B authorization from FY26 free cash flow of $9.38B'. The $9.38B is an FY26 figure (slide 3 labels the third bar 'Free cash flow' 9.38 with source 'Costco 10-K FY2026 (filed 2026-10-07)'). The deck funds an FY27 capex plan and an FY27 drawdown from an FY26 cash flow number that is itself flagged as not being available for FY27, and slide 3's footnote says '2 EPS effect is shown before lost interest on the cash used.' These are not the same period. **Fix:** Either obtain the FY27 free cash flow figure and use it, or relabel the funding source explicitly as 'FY26 free cash flow of $9.38B' on slides 2 and 5, and show that $9.38B covers $7.50B capex plus the $4B drawdown (it does not — it is $2.12B short if both are funded from the same pool). State the funding gap and how it is met (cash balance of $20.21B) or drop the $4B drawdown from the same-year claim.
- Chart shows three bars on what is presented as a single 'Cash and capex, $B' axis: 'Cash and equivalents' 20.21, 'FY27 capex plan' 7.50, 'Free cash flow' 9.38. These are a stock, a use, and a flow — three different units of account mixed on one axis with no legend and no period labels. A reader cannot tell whether 20.21 is FY26 year-end, FY27, or a current balance, and none of the three carries a date. Bars are ordered largest-to-middle without a stated sort, which invites the reader to compare a balance sheet figure to an annual flow as if they were the same thing. **Fix:** Split into two panels or a single waterfall: (i) cash and equivalents at a stated date, (ii) FY27 capex and FY26 free cash flow as separate labelled items with period in the label. Add 'as of 2026-09-30' or whatever the 10-K date is to the cash bar, and change the chart title to 'Cash and equivalents, FY27 capex plan and FY26 free cash flow, $B'.
- The recommendation is not supported by the numbers on the page. Slide 2: 'The buyback earns the least on cash'; slide 4: buyback 1.88% pre-tax, 13-week T-bill 4.12%; slide 3: residual cash after dividend and capex is '-$0.59B'. The deck simultaneously estimates a positive T-bill rate and a below-T-bill buyback yield, which means the buyback destroys relative value versus any money market alternative, before any financing cost, and the deck admits there is no residual cash to fund it. The recommendation should be to defer at least the buyback, or the slide set must show why it is accretive anyway. **Fix:** Rewrite slides 1, 2 and 5 so the ask matches the arithmetic: approve the $7.5B FY27 capex and $13.3B dividend, and either (a) suspend the $4B buyback with the reason 'pre-tax earnings yield 1.88% vs alternative uses at 4.12% 13-week T-bill and ~6.5% LOIS', or (b) if the buyback proceeds on a decision rule (e.g., opportunistic at a target price), state the price and the constrained capacity. State the drawdown cost explicitly: $4B at the 2026-11-13 10-year of 5.15% is $206M/year of interest (4,000 x 5.15%), which compares to roughly $25.4B of stock retired at a 1.88% yield; show whether incremental shares repurchased produce EPS accretion only because of tax or buyback timing.
- Slide 3 says residual cash after both uses is '-$0.59B' and 'FY26 free cash flow of $9.38B funds the plan'; slide 5 asks to 'draw the existing $4B authorization'. If free cash flow is already funding the plan, the $4B drawdown is incremental and its funding cost is omitted, so the recommendation is internally inconsistent and the sensitivity to the buyback is not shown anywhere. **Fix:** Reconcile the cash bridge on slide 3 into a single waterfall: 2026-08-30 cash of $20.21B + FY26 FCF of $9.38B (cash flow $15.828B less capex $6.438B) - $13.3B dividend - $7.5B capex = residual; show the $4B drawdown as a separate, labelled financing item with its interest cost.
- The basis for approving the largest single outlay — '$7.5B FY27 capex' — is given as 'The marginal return on the $7.5B is not disclosed', with no hurdle, no prior return, no comparison to cost of capital. The deck asks the board to approve $7.5B on 'strategic grounds' with zero quantified return; the only quantified alternative (22.2% existing base) makes the case worse, not better. **Fix:** State the FY27 capex return assumption or hurdle: e.g., incremental after-tax return on new capex versus WACC (as slide 5 asks for 'Incremental after-tax return on new capex versus WACC'). If the number is not in the sources, insert a request for it and show the break-even return at which the $7.5B is NPV-neutral.
- The bridge mixes a stock (cash at 2026-08-30) with a flow (FY26 free cash flow) in one waterfall and then labels the residual '$8.79B' without saying what it is or when it is measured. Even the arithmetic as drawn is internally off: 20.21 + 9.38 - 13.30 - 7.50 = 8.79, but slide 2 and slide 3 say the $4B buyback is suspended while slide 3's footnote 3 and slide 2 bullet state the buyback 'drawdown is a separate financing bar' - so the $0.21B annual cost is excluded from the bridge by assertion, not by disclosure of timing. A committee will ask whether $8.79B is end-FY27 cash or 2026-08-30 cash, and the deck cannot answer. **Fix:** Restate the bridge with an explicit date axis: 'Cash 2026-08-30 $20.21B + FY26 FCF $9.38B - special dividend $13.30B - FY27 capex $7.50B = cash 2027-08-30 $8.79B'. Add the $0.21B buyback cost as a line or state on the chart 'buyback suspended; $0.21B/yr cost not incurred'.
- The headline and chart compare a buyback earnings yield (1.88%) to a Treasury yield (4.12%) and to a return on existing capital (22.2%) that slide 4 footnote 1 itself says is 'the historical after-tax return on the existing base, not the marginal return'. Those are three different things: an equity earnings yield, a risk-free rate, and a historical ROE on a stock of capital. A board cannot act on 'below the 22.2% capital base' because that 22.2% is definitionally a return on capital already invested, not on incremental dollars. **Fix:** Cut the 22.2% bar from the chart or relabel it 'Return on existing capital (historical, not marginal) - not comparable to buyback or T-bill'. Keep buyback vs T-bill only, and state in the box that the buyback is an equity repurchase at 1.88% pre-tax vs a 4.12% risk-free rate.
- The $0.21B/$206M cost is 5.15% of $4B, but slide 4 footnote 1 sources the T-bill at 4.12% and the 10-year at 5.2%. The deck never says what the 5.15% is - it is not the T-bill, not the 10-year, and not the 1.88% buyback yield. The bullet is asserting a financing cost with no source and no label. **Fix:** Label the 5.15% in slide 4: 'at 5.15% [source], the $4B drawdown costs $206M a year', or replace with the source actually in the footnote (T-bill 4.12% -> $165M). Do not leave 5.15% unsourced.
- 22.2%/1.88% = 11.8x only if the two figures are the same measure of the same thing. Slide 4's own footnote 1 defines 22.2% as a whole-company after-tax return on book capital (equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B) and footnote 2 admits 1.88% is an earnings yield (E/P), not a return on invested capital. The 22.2% is a return on a ~$39.7B book at a cost of capital that includes debt; the 1.88% is an equity earnings yield at a $1,106.21 price. Dividing one by the other is unit-on-unit mix-up: the multiple is meaningless. Fix: state the two figures as different measures (ROIC on book capital vs. earnings yield) and delete the 11.8x from the title and slide 4 entirely; if a comparison is wanted it must be ROIC vs. WACC, both from the same source.
- The buyback yield is computed on a single day's close ($1,106.21) with no averaging and no anchor to the price the company would actually repurchase at. With a 52-week low of $850.00, the same earnings yield would be 2.44% — the deck itself prints that on slide 4, which is a ~30% swing in the '1.88%' headline. A buyback earning 1.88% vs 2.44% changes the conclusion. Fix: use a stated reference price (e.g., 30-day VWAP) and show the buyback earnings yield at the 52-week low, the close, and the 30-day average in a small table; label the period of the price.
- The 22.2% is admitted in footnote 1 to be a whole-company return, not a return on incremental warehouse investment, yet the argument treats it as the incremental return on retaining cash in warehouses. This is the central claim of the deck (slides 3, 4 and 5 all rest on it) and it is not a marginal return. Fix: either (a) compute the incremental after-tax return on the marginal/next warehouse investment, with the capex and expected EBIT disclosed, or (b) relabel it as 'company-wide after-tax ROIC' and remove every 'growth returns 22.2%' / 'growth is the best use' claim that depends on it being incremental.
- The headline comparison is not apples-to-apples: '22.2%' is defined in footnote 1 as 'a whole-company after-tax return on book capital (equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B), not a measured incremental return on new warehouses', while '1.9%'/'1.88%' is footnote 2's 'earnings yield (EPS / price), not a return on invested capital'. The deck sets 22.2% against 1.88% and calls it '11.8x the 1.88% a buyback earns' on slide 4 and slide 2 item 3. A whole-company ROIC against an earnings yield is a category error; the growth figure is not the return on the dollars being authorised. **Fix:** Replace the growth column with a marginal return on incremental warehouse capital (new-warehouse investment, annualised after-tax EBIT / capital employed) built from the FY2026 capex plan and the $6.5B FY2026 capex figure already on slide 2, source it to the 10-K segment/capex disclosure or to a labelled management estimate, and re-title slide 4 to state that number and the method. Keep 22.2% only if it is footnoted as a company-level book return and never put it in the ratio '11.8x'.
- '11.8x' cannot be reproduced from the numbers shown. 22.2% / 1.88% = 11.81, so the ratio is arithmetic, but the underlying numerator is a 1.88% earnings yield that ignores the after-tax cost of the cash used: at a 5.2% pre-tax yield on idle cash (slide 3, footnote 1) the after-tax opportunity cost is roughly 5.2% x (1 - tax) and buyback value therefore depends on the spread between the earnings yield and the after-tax cash yield, not on 1.88% alone. The deck never shows that spread. **Fix:** Rebuild the buyback column as after-tax value created per dollar repurchased = earnings yield at $1,106.21 less after-tax cost of cash (state the tax rate and source it), show the spread, then state the multiple of growth's after-tax return over that adjusted figure. If the adjusted buyback return is negative or close to zero, say so explicitly rather than quoting 1.88%.
- Terminal value and the horizon of the growth return are absent. 22.2% is labelled 'return on each dollar deployed, FY2026' — a single year — but the $4B is a multi-year authorisation expiring January 2027 (footnote 2, slide 5) and the deck gives no ramp, no reinvestment schedule, no NPV and no IRR for the growth programme. The comparison of a one-year return against a one-year earnings yield tells the board nothing about a multi-year capital allocation decision. **Fix:** Add a per-dollar NPV/IRR for the warehouse programme over a stated horizon (e.g. 5- and 10-year) using the FY2026 capex plan, the $303.15B revenue and $9.23B net income already on slide 4, and a stated discount rate sourced or labelled as a management assumption; show the growth case and the buyback case on the same horizon and currency of return.
- Slide 1 title 'Approve the $4B buyback authorisation; the $30 dividend is already declared' and slide 3's callout 'The $4B is the authorisation, not a return-maximising choice: a larger programme needs a new board approval. Growth is the best use: 22.2% after-tax return on capital, well above the 1.88% buyback yield.' contradict each other: the cover asks the board to approve a buyback while the only analytical page argues growth beats the buyback by 20.3 points. **Fix:** Replace slide 1's title with a decision the analysis supports, e.g. 'Reauthorise the $4B programme as a residual-capital backstop; the $30 dividend is already declared' and add one subtitle line stating the programme is capped at $4B and deployed only after FY2026 capex, funded from the $20.21B cash balance.
- The 22.2% and the 1.88% are not measured on the same basis, but slides 3 and 4 both compare them: footnote 1 on each page reads 'The 22.2% is a whole-company after-tax return on book capital (equity $35.808B + long-term debt $3.918B), not a measured incremental return on new warehouses' while footnote 2 reads 'The 1.88% is an earnings yield (EPS / price), not a return on invested capital'. The slide 4 title 'a buyback earns 1.88% on the same basis' is therefore false. **Fix:** Either recast both on the same numerator/denominator (incremental after-tax cash return on incremental warehouse capital vs. EPS/price on the retirement) or restate the slide 4 title to 'Growth returns 22.2% on book capital; the buyback yields 1.88% on price' and move the differing-basis footnotes into the body of the page.
- 'Retiring shares earns 1.88%, the earnings yield at $1,106.21' and 'Even at the 52-week low of $850.00 the earnings yield is only 2.45% on EPS of $20.83' do not tie: $20.83/1,106.21 = 1.88% and $20.83/850.00 = 2.45%, but footnote 3 on slide 4 states 'the 10-K diluted EPS of $20.76 gives 2.44%' at the $850.00 low. Two different EPS figures are used on the same comparison without reconciliation. **Fix:** Pick one EPS, state which it is in the footnote, and show the per-share math on the page: e.g. 'EPS $20.83 (LTM diluted) / $1,106.21 = 1.88%; / $850.00 = 2.45%.' Delete the $20.76/2.44% variant or footnote the difference between the 10-K diluted and LTM figures.
- The central comparison is not like-for-like: slide 3 itself footnotes '22.2% is a historical after-tax book return on capital ... the forward return on the next warehouse is not disclosed' and 'The two bars are not like-for-like', yet slides 2, 3 and 5 repeat the '17.1 points' gap as the reason cash is 'surplus' and the reason to buy back shares. A hostile committee will ask why a trailing accounting ROIC is being measured against a forward 10-year Treasury. **Fix:** Re-label the left bar 'FY2026 historical after-tax return on invested capital, 22.2%' and the right bar 'US 10-year Treasury yield, 5.15%', and add a third bar or stated range for the forward incremental return on the next warehouse cohort with its source; if not disclosed, say so on the bar itself, not only in footnote 1. Remove 'so the company's cash is surplus' from slide 2 bullet 1.
- Capex is stated as $6.43B, $6.5B and $6.5B across three pages, with $6.43B labelled 'FY2026 capex was' and $6.5B labelled 'the company's stated intention for fiscal 2026' in footnote 2. FY2026 is not complete if the latest 10-Q is for the quarter ended 2026-05-10, so '$6.43B was' is an actual-to-date figure, not full-year capex — the two numbers are not the same measure and the cash cover denominator changes with them. **Fix:** Pick one basis: state 'FY2026 capex intention $6.5B (company-stated, not board-approved, footnote 2)' once on slide 2, use $6.5B as the denominator in slide 4's cover ratio, and if $6.43B is actual capex for the first three quarters of FY2026, label it '9M FY2026 capex $6.43B' and cite the 10-Q.
- The entire thesis is a comparison between a 22.2% after-tax return on capital and 5.15% (the 10-year Treasury). Those are not comparable hurdle rates: the correct test for retaining capital is the risk-adjusted cost of equity, not the risk-free rate. Slide 3 footnote 1 admits 'The two bars are not like-for-like', which is an admission the main exhibit is not probative. On a cost of equity of ~9-10% the 22.2% still clears, but the deck never makes that argument, so the headline '17.1-point gap' overstates the case by roughly 7 points. **Fix:** Replace the 5.15% cash bar with Costco's estimated cost of equity (state and source the build-up: risk-free plus beta times ERP, cite the beta source and ERP date) and re-title slide 3 to the gap versus cost of equity. Keep the Treasury yield only as a footnote reconciling the 5.15% 'earnings on cash' rate to the 10-year. Restate the 17.1-point gap as 'X points above our estimated cost of equity'.
- EPS yield is not a capital-allocation test and comparing a 1.88% EPS yield to a 5.15% interest rate is a category error: EPS yield measures earnings per dollar of price, while 5.15% is a return on cash. The deck uses this to rank buybacks last, which is the opposite of the standard Costco position and of the fact that share count is falling. It is also inconsistent with slide 5 next step 1, which tells the board to authorize the $4B on the same day. **Fix:** Drop the 1.88% vs 5.15% comparison. Replace the buyback ranking with a cash-flow test the board can act on: state the buyback is funded only from free cash flow, cite the $9.38B FY2026 free cash flow on slide 4 as the source, and state the maximum annual repurchase that leaves cash above a minimum operating balance. Delete 'below the 5.15% cash rate' from the slide 3 callout.
- A 22.2% return is shown as a single bar with no method, no denominator and no period reconciliation. Footnote 1 gives 11.609B x (1 - 24.7%) / equity 35.808B — the numerator is an operating income, the denominator is equity, so this is ROE, not 'return on capital'; net income is not used. Bar 1 is 'FY2026 actual' and bar 2 is a market yield on 2026-11-13 — two different periods on one axis, which violates the one-period-one-unit rule. **Fix:** Rebuild the exhibit on the actual FY2026 10-K: show net income, not operating income times (1 minus tax), over total invested capital, using the same FY2026 ending balance sheet for the denominator as the $20.21B cash on slide 4. Re-label 'Return on invested capital, FY2026 (per 10-K)' and state the formula and the source. Put the market rate in a dated footnote, not a co-equal bar.
- The deck recommends approving $7.5B of capital with no project return, no WACC, no NPV/IRR, no payback and no comparison against the buyback it is displacing. Slide 3 says it outright: 'Project return: N/A: not available', and slide 2 item 4 concedes 'The 22.2% return is on the existing book.' A board cannot approve $7.5B against an N/A. **Fix:** Add a valuation page: warehouse-level unit economics (capex per warehouse, ramp, mature-year EBITDA and ROIC), the incremental return on the $7.5B, and the hurdle it is compared to. If management cannot supply it, the recommendation must change to 'authorize subject to return case' and the $7.5B budget must be shown as a ceiling, not an approved number.
- Two different numbers for the same buyback: slide 2 says 'The $4B buyback returns -3.27% versus Treasury' and slide 5 says 'Keep the $4B authorization paused at $1,106.21'. A '-' return is written as an excess return versus Treasury without saying what the Treasury leg is, over what period, or whether it is TSR (price only vs price plus dividend). Costco pays a dividend, so a price-only comparison understates the return; the -3.27% is uninterpretable as presented. **Fix:** State the definition and the window on the page: 'Buyback TSR of -3.27% vs 3-month T-bill of X% over 2026-01-01 to 2026-11-11, price-only, dividends excluded (which understates TSR by the dividend yield).' Recompute on the same basis as the comps used and show both legs.
- Slide 1 asserts 'FY2026 free cash flow covered the FY2027 expansion plan' and quotes 'FY2026 free cash flow was $9.38B versus $7.50B of planned expansion spend', and slide 2 repeats 'cash plus free cash flow cover them, leaving $4.79B' — but slide 4's own table says 'Cash from operations: N/A; FY2026' and states 'FY2026 free cash flow is N/A because sourced operating cash flow is missing; the claimed surplus cannot be tested.' The deck's headline claim contradicts its own evidence page. **Fix:** Either obtain FY2026 operating cash flow from Costco's FY2026 Form 10-K and rebuild the FCF bridge (OCF less capex) on slide 1 with the source cited, or retitle slides 1, 2 and 5 to drop the word 'covered' and present the $9.38B as an unverified claim. Do not ship a cover claim that the body refutes.
- 'Planned uses total $24.8B; cash plus free cash flow cover them, leaving $4.79B.' No source line, no breakdown of the $24.8B of uses, and no derivation of the $4.79B residual is shown. The $9.38B FCF in that arithmetic is the same figure slide 4 labels untestable. **Fix:** Show the bridge: opening cash + FCF (sourced) less capex, buyback, dividend, special dividend = $4.79B, with each line sourced and the periods stated. If any line cannot be sourced, remove the $4.79B residual.
- The deck asks the board to 'Approve the FY2027 warehouse plan' (slide 2) while slide 3 states the project return is 'N/A: not available' and slide 5 gates release on 'IRR or payback for all 33 sites'. The approval is recommended on returns that the deck says do not exist. **Fix:** Split the ask: approve a pre-development/land commitment with staged capital release contingent on site-level IRR or payback, or recommend deferral of the $7.5B until the site cases exist. The recommendation must not exceed the evidence.
- 'the board-approved return threshold is not available' — the deck gates release of $7.5B on a hurdle it never states. A board cannot be asked to apply a test whose threshold is blank; and every downstream page ('clear the hurdle', slide 2 title and slide 5 step 1) inherits the same hole. **Fix:** State the hurdle explicitly on slide 2 (e.g. 'board hurdle: X% after-tax ROIC / Y-year payback') with a source footnote naming the policy document or committee minute; if no such number is disclosed, replace the column with 'Board-approved hurdle: not disclosed — request from management' and add it to the research list rather than presenting a test the reader cannot run.
- 'Incremental returns for the new warehouses are N/A' and slide 3's 'N/A: site-level return evidence not disclosed'. The deck's entire case is that $7.5B should be conditioned on returns — but the one number that would decide it does not exist in the deck. No sensitivity, no range, no illustrative site IRR/payback, so the board has no basis to accept or reject. **Fix:** Add an illustrative site economics box using figures already in the material: $7.5B / 33 warehouses = ~$227M per warehouse (state the divisor and the implied per-site capex in the page); then show what payback and ROIC each of a small set of assumed site-level operating incomes implies, clearly labelled 'illustrative — site-level operating income not disclosed. Source: [you compute]'. Do not present it as company data.
- Slide 1: 'The stated $9.38B free cash flow equaled 1.25x the $7.5B FY2027 expansion plan'. 9.38/7.5 = 1.25 — arithmetic checks — but $9.38B FCF is never footnoted, never dated and never reconciled to the $6.43B FY2026 capex or the $303.15B revenue/$9.23B net income on slides 2-3. Slide 5 asks the board to 'reconcile forecast cash from operations with planned capex' — which is precisely the reconciliation the deck itself has not done. **Fix:** Put the $9.38B on slide 2 with a source line ('Source: [company filing/FY2026 release], period FY2026, as disclosed') and add one bridge line showing $9.38B FCF less $7.5B expansion less recurring dividend less the $30/share special dividend = residual; use only disclosed figures and label any estimate.
- $17.29B / 3.5% implies a market value of $494B. If the 443.1M share count on the same page is right, that is $1,115/share; Costco has never traded there. Either the 3.5% is wrong or it has been computed on a different base with no label. Two of the page's own numbers cannot both be true, and this is the one number a board member will sanity-check from memory. **Fix:** Recompute the percentage off a named, dated equity value and show the arithmetic: state '$17.29B, x.x% of the $XXXB market value at the [date] close (443.1M shares x $XXX.XX)'. If the market value is not in the model, put it in and source it to the same Yahoo Finance close dated 2026-11-13 used elsewhere.
- The same cover ratio appears as 1.2x on slide 2 and 1.17x on slide 3, with the same numerator and denominator ($20.21B / $17.29B = 1.169x, i.e. 1.17x). Slide 2's '1.2x' rounds in the issuer's favour; float the ratio consistently and to the same precision. Separately, the same cash figure is stated as $20.21B and $20.2B, and the residual as $2.92B on slide 2 versus $12.24B on slide 3 without reconciliation — the two 'left over' numbers measure different things and the deck never says so. **Fix:** Use 1.17x everywhere, with the formula once in a footnote: '$20.21B cash / $17.29B declared return = 1.17x'. On slide 2, relabel the $2.92B as 'unencumbered cash after the return' and add a line reconciling it to slide 3's $12.24B residual (define the residual as cash after the return, buyback and capex plan). Cite the model cells.
- Row 2 says 'Costco's cash covers its return 1.17x' on $20.21B cash, row 3 says '$20.21B cash covers the $17.29B return 1.17x'; $20.21B/$17.29B = 1.169x, but $20.21B is labelled 'cash' in slide 3 as cash plus FY26 operating cash flow ($20.21B nearly equals $20.21B/$12.31B residual construction: $20.21B + $15.82B − $13.29B − $4B − $6.438B = $12.312B), i.e. the $20.21B on slide 2 is the cash line item and on slide 3 the opening bar of a flow. Same figure, two meanings. **Fix:** Pick one definition of the cover numerator and use it on slides 2, 3 and 4. If the intended numerator is liquidity of $20.21B + $15.82B = $36.03B, state it as such: '$36.03B of cash and FY26 operating cash flow covers the $17.29B return 2.08x'. If it is $20.21B cash only, then footnote 1 edits the ratio to 1.17x on $20.21B and slide 2 row 2 must not say the $2.92B is 'cash after the return only' ($20.21B − $17.29B = $2.92B, which is consistent with cash only, not with $20.21B + operating cash flow).
- '$15.82B (fiscal 2026 actual)' shows cash cover 1.2x and call 'Ratify A'. $15.82B/$17.29B = 0.915x, not 1.2x, and the cell shows 1.2x in all three rows ($15.82B, $17.0B and $12.66B), so the number does not move with the input. Slide 4's own bullet says '1.2x cover' derived from the $20.21B, not from any row of this table. The only row where 1.2x is arithmetically close is the illustrative $17.0B ($17.0B/$17.29B = 0.98x, still not 1.2x). **Fix:** Rebuild the sensitivity: compute cover per row from the stated operating cash flow divided by the $17.29B return (e.g. $15.82B → 0.91x; $17.0B → 0.98x; $12.66B → 0.73x) and restate the Calls accordingly. If the committee intends cover on total liquidity including the $20.21B cash balance, add that column and say so. Do not leave one number in three rows.
- $20.21B/$17.29B = 1.17x (slide 3 footnote 1 and slide 2 footnote 1 both say 1.17x), yet slide 4 rounds it to 1.2x in the banner table and the bullet; the 0.03x gap moves cover below the stated affordability test and is inconsistent with the ratio used on slides 2-3. **Fix:** Use 1.17x everywhere the $20.21B/$17.29B quotient is used (slide 4 header, bullet and sensitivity calls, slides 2-3), or state the rounding convention in a footnote on each page. One ratio, one value.
- The bridge footnotes dividends as an 'estimate' of $5.54/share x 443.1M shares outstanding, but the chart bar is labelled '-2.45' ($2.45B) while the waterfall itself carries year-to-date dividends paid of $1.15B (slide 5, footnote 1) against a declared figure in the 10-K. 443.1M x $5.54 = $2.455B, so the bar is the annualised estimate, not the FY2026 figure, yet the page header claims 'Cash flow bridge, FY2026, $B' and 'still add $6B to cash'. An FY2026 actual-dividends bridge would show -1.15 not -2.45, and 'added to cash' would be 15.82 - 6.43 - 1.15 - 0.85 = 7.39, not 6.08. **Fix:** Either (a) retitle the slide 'Cash flow bridge, FY2026 estimated run-rate, $B' and footnote that all dividend figures are the $5.54/share annualised rate, or (b) rebuild the bridge on FY2026 actuals ($15.82 - $6.43 - $1.15 - $0.85 = $7.39 added to cash) and drop slide 3's '$6B' title claim. Do not mix an annualised dividend estimate with YTD buybacks on the same bar chart without flagging the period difference on the bars themselves.
- Slide 2 claims 'Reinvestment earns 22.2% after tax on capital' but no source is given for the 22.2% return. The 10-K and supplychain360 source lines list no return-on-capital figure. An unsourced return is the entire premise of the recommendation. **Fix:** Either cite the source, page and line item for the 22.2% (e.g., incremental ROIC from a specific 10-K or investor-day disclosure) or restate the metric as 'management's estimated return on new distribution centres' with the derivation in the footnote. If no primary source exists, delete the 22.2% claim from the title and slides 2 and 4 and replace with an explicitly labelled management estimate.
- Slide 2 says 'Each $1B retires 0.90M shares.' Slide 4 uses $1,106.21/share and states 'At $1,106.21 a share the buyback earns 1.88%, less than the 4.12% risk-free.' $1B / $1,106.21 = 0.904M shares — consistent. But slide 4's own headline says 1.9% and the grid says '1.88%'. Minor rounding difference; the real problem is that the 1.88% is stated as an earnings yield without disclosing what earnings are used (net income / shares / price). The reader cannot recompute it. **Fix:** In slide 4, add a one-line foot note: 'Earnings yield = FY26 net income of $9.23B divided by 443.1M shares and $1,106.21 close = 1.88%.' Use 1.9% everywhere or 1.88% everywhere, not both.
- 'The $20.21B cash balance earns 4.12% in 13-week bills.' The 4.12% is the current 13-week T-bill yield as of a specific date (Yahoo Finance close 2026-11-13), not a locked rate. Over an FY2027 holding period the realised return will differ, and a board decision to hold $20B in bills is rate-sensitive. **Fix:** Restate as 'at the 2026-11-13 13-week T-bill yield of 4.12%' and add a sensitivity: at 3.00% T-bills the surplus earns ~$0.6B less per annum than the figure shown. Show the break-even yield at which holding cash beats funding an incremental warehouse.
- Return on capital trades the latest balance sheet for a stale one: '22.2% from the FY2026 10-K operating income $11.698 (1 - 24.7%) / (equity $35.808B + long-term debt $3.918B)'. The 10-K was filed 2026-10-07 and the deck is dated November 2026; you have a Q ended 2026-05-10 (10-Q) and, on slide 4, long-term debt of $3.91B and operating lease liabilities of $2.61B, neither of which reconciles to the $3.918B in the footnote. Equity is not struck at the pricing date. The denominator is the whole business, including assets that have nothing to do with a new warehouse, so 22.2% is not the incremental return the board is being asked to fund. **Fix:** Recompute the 22.2% as the incremental after-tax return on the FY2027 warehouse program: incremental NOI from the 33 sites divided by the $6.43B capex (=$6.43B total capex, 1,000x = $5 sites, $3.918B cash on hand is irrelevant). Show the build: sites, average capex per site, stabilized NOI, tax rate, and a footnote that ties each figure to the FY2026 10-K, the 10-Q, and the model.
- Operating cash flow of $15.82B is not sourced and does not tie to the 10-K you cite: slide 3 footnote 2 says 'Source: Costco 10-K, FY2026 (filed 2026-10-07), SEC XBRL: operating cash flow, capital expenditure, dividends paid, share repurchases, net income, cash and cash equivalents' but the cash flow bridge is built on $15.82B, which is not reconciled to the FY2026 statement of cash flows. Slide 2 point 2 says 'Cash of $20.21B covers capex 3.1x' and slide 3 says 'Cash of $20.21B covers 3.1x annual capex' - $20.21B / $6.43B = 3.14x, so the arithmetic holds, but the $20.21B is a stale balance sheet figure (10-K filed 2026-10-07) presented next to a 10-Q dated 2026-05-10 and a November 13, 2026 price. **Fix:** Put the FY2026 statement of cash flows line items on slide 3 with the exact 10-K line captions and the statement page reference, and reconcile the $15.82B, -$6.43B, -$1.15B, -$0.85B to that statement. Restate the $20.21B cash to the most recent balance sheet you have and show the period (e.g., 'as of 2026-10-31').
- The bridge does not add: $20.21B opening + $15.82B operating cash flow - $6.43B capex - $0.85B buybacks = $28.75B, not the $27.60B closing cash shown. The $7.39B 'Added to cash' line implies $1.15B of dividends paid are excluded, but the bridge as presented has no dividend line, so the reader cannot reconcile $27.60B. **Fix:** Add a '-$1.15B Dividends paid' step between buybacks and 'Added to cash' so $20.21 + 15.82 - 6.43 - 0.85 - 1.15 = $27.60B, and re-label the $7.39B as 'Change in cash' with the dividend explicit.
- 22.2% is 'operating income after tax divided by equity plus long-term debt: $11.698 x (1 - 24.7%) / ($35.808 + $3.918) = 22.2%'. Numerator is an income-statement flow; denominator is a balance-sheet stock that excludes the very capex being funded. The incremental return on the $6.43B of FY2026 capex, which is what the board is being asked to approve, is never shown. **Fix:** Add a marginal ROIC line: incremental after-tax operating income from the 33 warehouse openings divided by the incremental capital deployed for those openings, with both figures sourced to the 10-K or the internal capex schedule, shown beside the 22.2% blended return.
- The buyback is framed as earning 1.88% when the deck's own footnote 2 computes it as diluted EPS $20.76 / $1,106.21 = 1.88%, a static earnings yield, not the return on incremental repurchase dollars. On a page titled 'Each dollar earns', the column header 'Return per dollar' and the 1.88% figure are not comparable to the 22.2% warehouse return: one is project IRR, the other is a spot earnings yield. **Fix:** Either restate the buyback as an incremental return (e.g. EPS accretion from retiring 0.90M shares at $1,106.21 vs. the $20.76 earnings forgone) or re-label the column 'Earnings yield (current)' and state in the box that the two metrics are not like-for-like.
- The special dividend is stated two ways: slide 1 says the buyback is 'incremental to the $30/share special dividend declared 11 November 2026' with no cash figure, while slide 3 shows '$13.3B special dividend declared' — and slide 2 footnote 1 says '$13.298B'. Slide 1 should carry the $13.3B, and the same rounded figure must appear on slides 1, 2 and 3. A board member reading page 1 and page 3 gets two different pictures of the same declaration. **Fix:** Put '$13.3B' on slide 1 and make slides 1, 2 and 3 all read $13.3B; keep the unrounded $13.298B only in the footnote with its basis (443.1M shares x $30).
- The $20.21B cash less the $13.3B special dividend does not give $6.9B; $20.21B - $13.3B = $6.91B only if no ordinary dividend is deducted, yet slides 2 and 4 say the $6.93B is 'after the ordinary dividend' of $2.458B, which would leave about $4.45B. The arithmetic as presented is internally inconsistent. State the subtraction explicitly: $20.21B cash - $13.3B special - $2.458B ordinary = about $4.45B, or if the intended $6.93B is operating cash flow less capex less ordinary dividend, say so on the tile and drop the '$20.21B less $13.3B' framing. **Fix:** On slide 3 change 'Cash left after the dividend $6.9B' to the correct residual and show the bridge; on slide 4 label the $6.93B as 'FY2026 operating cash flow less capex less ordinary dividend declared' and give the three inputs.
- The buyback return is quoted as 'EPS $20.76 / share price $1,106.21 = 1.88%'. That is an earnings yield on the purchase price, not a return on the buyback; buying back stock at a 1.88% earnings yield only destroys value if the cash cost of 4.12% is the right hurdle, and that comparison is never sourced. Slide 3 footnote 1 admits the market-cap version is 1.79% (12.3x), which contradicts the 1.88% headline. Pick one, define it (earnings yield vs after-tax return on invested capital) and use it everywhere. **Fix:** Restate slide 2 item 2 and slide 3 as '1.88% earnings yield on the repurchase price ($20.76/$1,106.21)' and reconcile to the 1.79% market-cap figure in footnote 1 so the two are not read as the same measure.
- 'Cash left after the special dividend: $6.92B' appears on the balance-sheet box but slide 4's own funding box says FY2026 operating cash flow less capex less ordinary dividend 'leaves about $6.93B free cash flow', and the executive summary caps buyback draws at $6.93B. The slide-title-callout '$13.3B special dividend' and the $6.92B are presented as if simultaneable uses of the same cash, but the $6.92B post-dividend cash and the $6.93B pre-dividend free cash flow are not reconciled. A reader cannot tell which bucket funds the $6.5B capex. Reconcile the two figures and state whether the special dividend is funded from cash on hand or from free cash flow. **Fix:** Replace the slide 3 box with a single sources-and-uses table: beginning cash $20.21B, minus special dividend $13.3B, plus FY2026 FCF after ordinary dividend $6.93B, minus capex $6.5B, equals ending cash. State explicitly that the $6.93B buyback cap equals the FCF figure on slide 4 and that the special dividend is paid from the $20.21B cash balance, not from FCF.
- The 22.2% 'existing book' return and the 1.88% buyback return are not comparable uses of capital and the arithmetic does not tie across the two pages. Slide 2 shows 22.2% = $11.69B x (1 - 24.7%) / ($35.80B + $3.91B) using FY2025 10-K equity and debt. Slide 3's callout recomputes to 22.2% on $35.80B + $3.91B but its 'Amount' shows $39.71B invested capital for the 'Equity book (ex-leases).' $39.71B ≠ $35.80B + $3.91B. This is the core comparison in the deck and it does not foot. **Fix:** Set out one reconciliation: state the FY2025 10-K equity, long-term debt, and invested capital figures on slide 2 and slide 3 with the same label, and make the '$39.71B invested capital' equal the sum of its components. If operating leases are excluded, show the lease liability separately and label the numerator (EBIT or after-tax operating income) accordingly. Do not show 22.2% twice on two different denominators.
- Internal contradiction: slide 3's own buyback multiple of 20.76x implies the return is supportive below a 4.82% cost of capital (~2.30% buyback yield), yet the deck compares 1.88% to a 4.12% cash cost and recommends capping buyback draws at roughly the 4.12% cost. If the hurdle is 4.12%, the buyback's 1.88% yield is below it and the recommendation to cap rather than suspend draws is unsupported. If the hurdle is the 2.30% breakeven, the 1.88% yield is above it and buys should continue. The two messages cannot both stand. Pick one hurdle and make the recommendation the arithmetic supports. **Fix:** State the buyback hurdle rate explicitly (cost of cash 4.12% or cost of capital) on slide 3 and show the buyback at 1.88% vs the chosen hurdle. If 4.12%, recommend suspending or reducing opens; if 2.30%, state that the buyback remains return-accretive at 1.88% and the cap should be set on cash, not return. Make slide 5's action match the chosen conclusion.
- This is an unsourced assertion that contradicts slide 2's own footnote: at the reference share count of 443.1M, $1B / 443.1M = $2.26 per share, but the model is silent on 0.90M shares retired per $1B, implying a $1,111M/$1B ratio that is not defined. Neither the 0.90M shares nor the $2.26 per share appears in any visible source; the $1,106.21 market price on slide 3 is not in the deck. If the figure is meant as $1B divided by an average buyback price, that price must be stated and sourced. **Fix:** Either delete the 0.90M shares figure or show its input: $1,000M / average purchase price = shares retired, using the market price already on slide 3 ($1,106.21), which gives 0.90M shares. Then the $2.26 per share and the 0.90M shares are the same arithmetic and should be presented once, not as two different claims.
- The deck asks the board to ratify a $6.5B plan while the adjacent tile states full-year capex will be $6.43B and the footnote concedes the FY2027 budget is unknown. $6.43B < $6.5B, so the spot forecast already underspends the plan by $70M with Q4 still open — the plan and the forecast are two different numbers presented as one. **Fix:** Pick one: either state the FY2026 forecast is $6.43B (already $70M under the $6.5B authorised plan) and ask the board to ratify the $6.5B authorisation with the Q4 remainder of $2.27B shown as the bridge to it, or replace the $6.43B tile with the tie-out from the model. Do not run both tiles side by side without reconciling.
- The arithmetic $4,228M + $2,272M = $6,500M holds, but slide 2 states $4,228M spent in the first 36 weeks and slide 3's tile says $6.43B full-year actual, not $6.5B. Two pages, two full-year capex numbers ($6,430M vs $6,500M), and slide 4's chart uses only the one that supports the ask. **Fix:** Reconcile to a single FY2026 capex forecast and use it on slides 2, 3 and 4. If the answer is $6.43B, change slide 4's chart to 4.228 + 2.202 = 6.430 and restate the $2.27B Q4 remainder as $2.20B; if $6.50B, delete the $6.43B tile and show the model tie-out.
- Arithmetic fails: $9.38B − $2.45B = $6.93B only if the ordinary dividend is $2.45B, but item 4 on the same slide and the footnote give the ordinary dividend as $2.46B, and slide 3's footnote 3 says the declaration is $54 x 443.1M = $2.458B. Three figures for the same line ($2.45B, $2.46B, $2.458B) producing three different caps ($6.93B, $6.92B, $6.922B). **Fix:** Use one ordinary dividend figure everywhere (recommend $2.46B rounded, from $54.00 x 443.1M = $2.393B — note this product does not equal $2.46B either; state the true per-share rate and shares and the resulting product, then set cap = $9.38B less that number).
- The ordinary dividend is computed two ways and neither ties. Slide 2 note 2: 'Ordinary run-rate dividend: $1.47/quarter = $5.88 annualised; $5.54 declared per share x 443.1M = $2.45B used here; the $0.34 gap is unreconciled.' Slide 4 note 1 repeats it: '$5.88 annual dividend per share does not tie to the $5.54 declared per share; $0.34 difference is unreconciled, so use $5.54 x 443.1M = $2.45B'. You have a board-level deck that admits, in a footnote, that it does not know whether the dividend is $5.54 or $5.88 — a $151M annual swing on 443.1M shares — and then picks one. Fix the number or drop the line. **Fix:** Reconcile against Costco's most recent 10-Q dividend note: confirm the declared per-share dividend and share count, restate the annual figure once, and delete the 'unreconciled' language from both footnotes. If the $1.47/quarter is a run-rate including a raise, label it as such and show the declared actuals separately.
- The buyback hurdle moves between pages. Slide 4's headline and footnote 1 test the earnings yield against the '4.12% 13-week T-bill yield' ('The buyback fails the cash hurdle (1.88% vs 4.12%)'). Slide 2 footnote 1 defines the hurdle as '3.10% after-tax cash rate (4.12% 13-week T-bill x (1 - 24.7% effective tax rate))', and slide 5 calls it 'the 3.10% after-tax cash hurdle'. Slide 4 also offers a '1.82% earnings yield' against the '$850.00' 52-week low without saying whether that is pre- or post-tax. You are comparing a pre-tax T-bill to a post-tax earnings yield on one page and a post-tax rate on another. **Fix:** Pick one convention — post-tax, matching slide 2's 3.10% — and state it on every page. Rewrite slide 4's headline test as '1.88% earnings yield vs 3.10% after-tax cash hurdle' and align the 52-week-low line to the same basis.
- The 3.6M shares retired is presented as fact — slide 2: 'retires 3.6M shares'; slide 5: 'Execute up to $4B over 12 months' with '$4.0B authorization and the $1,106.21' implied — but footnote 3 on slide 5 concedes 'The 3.6M shares retired is a calc from the $4B authorization and the $1,106.21 close; actual shares retired depend on execution prices.' At 1,106.21 the math is $4.0B / $1,106.21 = 3.62M, which works, but the EPS impact on slide 2 ('Buyback nets -$0.12 EPS') is shown to the penny without the share count, buyback price path, foregone interest or the $124.0M after-tax cash figure being sourced anywhere. **Fix:** Either (a) add a small bridge on slide 2 showing 3.6M shares x 1,106.21 = $4.0B, the foregone after-tax interest of $124.0M (state rate and tax), the EPS math and the result, with each input footnoted; or (b) label the 3.6M explicitly as 'at the 2026-11-13 close; actual depends on execution' and put the sensitivity on a back-up page.
- The surplus is stated two ways and neither ties. Slide 2 says 'Return the $4.0B surplus' and 'Cash left after either choice is the same $16.21B'; Slide 5 footnote 1 says 'the current program has $1,115M remaining and expires in January 2027' and then authorizes $4B on top. If $2.45B of the surplus is the ordinary dividend (slide 5, item 1) and $4.0B is the buyback, the depletable cash is $6.45B against a stated $16.21B residual - the page never bridges $4.0B and $6.45B, and the $16.21B appears once with no calculation. **Fix:** Put one surplus bridge on slide 2: operating cash flow $15.82B + starting cash [source] - $6.5B capex - $2.45B ordinary dividend = available surplus; then show $6.45B returned and $X billion retained. Use the same surplus dollar figure on slides 2 and 5 and footnote the starting cash to the FY26 10-K balance sheet.
- 'Buyback nets -$0.12 EPS per $124.0M forgone after-tax cash' does not work on the slide's own numbers. At the slide 5 buyback price of $1,106.21, $124.0M of cash retires 0.112M shares, not 3.6M; slide 2 says the $4.0B buys 3.6M shares, which implies ~$1,111/share - two per-share prices and two share counts for the same $4.0B. And -$0.12 EPS on $124M forgone implies ~1.0B shares, not the 443.1M used in footnote 2. **Fix:** Recompute the accretion/dilution off one share count and one price: dollars forgone / price = shares retired; then forgone after-tax cash / diluted shares = EPS impact. State the diluted share count used and tie it to the share count in slide 2 footnote 2 (443.1M ordinary shares is not the diluted count - reconcile).
- Capex is $6.5B on slides 1, 2, 3, 5 ('Fund the full $6.5B fiscal 2026 capex plan first') but $6.58B on slide 5 ('The company funds the $6.5B fiscal 2026 capex plan'). $6,500M and $6,580M are different numbers for the same line item; a client will catch it. **Fix:** Pick one figure across the deck and make it tie to the Costco 10-K FY2026 capex line. If the plan is $6,580M, change slides 1/2/3/5 to that; if it is $6,500M, correct slide 5's 'The company funds the $6.5B fiscal 2026 capex plan' and the title's '$6,500M' to the same basis.
- Slide 2 states 'Cash left after either choice is the same $16.21B' while slide 5's next step is a $4B authorization and slide 4 says 'The $4.0B authorization is the residual'. These two residual claims are irreconcilable unless we show the bridge; as drafted a director cannot tell what the $4B is a residual of versus the $16.21B. **Fix:** Put a one-line bridge on slide 2 showing cash after capex and after the $2.45B ordinary dividend to get to the $4.0B residual, and reconcile the $16.21B figure to it (e.g. $20.21B cash less $4.0B = $16.21B) so both slides say the same thing.
- The exhibit states 1.88% is the FY26 earnings yield at $1,106.21. Diluted EPS of $20.76 gives 20.76/1,106.21 = 1.88%; fine — but slide 2 says the 3.10% hurdle is a 4.12% 13-week T-bill times (1 - 24.7%), which is 3.102% (rounding), so the numbers hold. The problem is the comparison: earnings yield is a claimed proxy, and the page treats it as the decision rule without ever stating the buyback's actual after-tax return to the seller/company or the opportunity cost of not doing the warehouse capex at 22.2%. **Fix:** Show one line reconciling the 22.2% capex IRRs against the 1.88% buyback yield on the same after-tax basis, and label the earnings yield explicitly as a proxy for buyback IRR: 'Buyback yield 1.88% is an earnings yield, not an after-tax IRR of the repurchase.'
- The only downside buyback analysis is priced at the 52-week low ($850.00), which is stale relative to the current $1,106.21 and is not the stress case a board wants. There is no case for what happens if the stock falls below $669.68 or if the plan underdelivers on the 29 warehouses. **Fix:** Replace the 52-week low stress with a forward scenario table: EPS and yield at $850.00, $1,106.21, $669.68 and a downside price/EPS case, each footnoted. Keep one column for 'current T-bill hurdle' and one for 'hurdle if rates fall to the last five-year average'.
- The core per-$1B buyback result contradicts itself. Slide 2 headline and item 4: 'each $1B adds 4 cents of EPS' then 'Net -3 cents a share'; slide 4 headline states 'adds 4 cents of EPS' and the grey box states 'each $1B of buyback reduces EPS by 3 cents'; slide 5 implies 3.6M shares retired for $4B at $1,106.21 (4 x 0.90M). The reader cannot tell whether $1B of buyback is +$0.04 or -$0.03. **Fix:** Pick one basis and use it everywhere. If the net-of-foregone-interest answer is -$0.03, the slide 2 and slide 4 headlines must read 'each $1B of buyback reduces EPS by 3 cents' and the +4c must be labelled as gross of foregone interest in every instance. Show the arithmetic inline: $1B / $1,106.21 = 0.904M shares; 0.904M x $20.80 = $18.8M retained earnings / 443.1M shares = +$0.042; less $31.0M after-tax interest / 443.1M = -$0.070; net -$0.028.
- The waterfall does not foot. Stated bars: opening 20.21, operating +15.82, capex -6.43, ordinary dividends -1.15, special dividend -13.30, buyback -1.00, closing 14.15. Sum: 20.21 + 15.82 - 6.43 - 1.15 - 13.30 - 1.00 = 14.15. The bars foot, but the title says 'Returns of $15.76B' and the callout says '$15.82B operating cash flow less $6.5B capex'. Neither $15.76B ($13.30 + $1.15 + $1.00 = $15.45B isn't it either) nor the $6.5B capex reconciles to the $6.43B bar shown. Three different figures for the same concept ($15.76B, $15.82B, and the implied bars) and two for capex ($6.5B on slides 1/2/5, $6.43B on slide 3). **Fix:** Reconcile to one set. If capex draw is $6.43B in FY2026A, say so and footnote the $6.5B as the FY2026 plan vs actual-to-date; if total returns are $15.45B ($13.30+$1.15+$1.00), change the title and callout to $15.45B. Every figure in the title, the callout and the bars must be the same number.
- 'Pace the buyback: at $1,106.21 each $1B of buyback reduces EPS by 3 cents' contradicts slide 2's headline claim that 'each $1B adds 4 cents of EPS' and slide 4's own '+4 cents EPS' framing. The 4 cents is pre-interest EPS accretion; the +$0.03 on $20.80 is post-interest. You cannot show both as the EPS effect without labelling which is which. **Fix:** State one number on both pages: 'each $1B of buyback is $0.04 pre-interest EPS accretion and $0.03 net of forgone interest'. Rewrite slide 2's title to 'each $1B adds 4 cents gross but 3 cents net of forgone interest' and reconcile slide 4's callout to the same $0.03.
- Slide 3 pages a -$1.00B FY2027E buyback and a closing cash of $14.15B; slide 2's 'Pace $1B a year' case shows closing cash of $5.92B. Same company, same year, two closing cash figures — and slide 3's $14.15B sits above slide 2's $13.3B special dividend box. A committee will ask which is the recommendation. **Fix:** Make slide 3 the paced case explicitly: opening $20.21 + $15.82 operating - $6.43 capex - $1.15 ordinary - $13.30 special - $1.00 buyback = $14.15 closing. Add a line to slide 2 stating the paced closing cash is $14.15B (not $5.92B) and explain the difference, or delete the $5.92B figure.
- The bridge bars do not sum to the labelled closing cash: 20.21 + 15.82 - 6.43 - 1.15 - 13.30 - 1.00 = 14.15, which ties — but the callout says 'Returns of $15.45B against $9.38B of free cash flow draw $6.07B from cash' and the page title repeats it. $15.45B = $13.30 + $1.00 + $1.15 = $15.45, and $9.38B is quoted as free cash flow, but the bridge shows operating $15.82B less capex $6.43B = $9.39B, not $9.38B. Fix the rounding or the definition. **Fix:** Use one definition: 'Returns $15.45B ($13.30 special + $1.15 ordinary + $1.00 buyback) against $9.39B FCF ($15.82B operating less $6.43B capex) draws $6.06B from cash.' Either restate to the $9.39B/$6.06B implied by the bars or footnote the $9.38B source figure and reconcile the 0.01.
- The bridge does not close. Waterfall bars: +15.82 − 6.43 − 1.15 − 13.29 − 1.00 = 20.21 − 14.16 = $6.05B outflow, but the title claims $6.07B. Footnote 1 plugs the buyback at $1.08B while the waterfall bar and slide 4 both use $1.00B. The special dividend is inconsistently stated as $13.29B in the waterfall and footnote 1 versus the $13.3B headline on slides 1, 2 and 5. **Fix:** Recompute the bridge from a single source and make every figure tie: state the special dividend as $13.29B everywhere or round the headline to $13.29B; set the FY2027E buyback bar to the same $1.08B used in footnote 1 (or change footnote 1 to $1.00B and restate the draw) so +15.82 − 6.43 − 1.15 − 13.29 − 1.08 = $14.08B closing cash, and update the title and slide 5 footnote 1 to the recalculated draw.
- The title says a $30 special dividend; the top line says $13.3B. At 443.1M shares, $30 × 443.1M = $13.293B ≈ $13.3B, so the $30 per-share and $13.3B are internally consistent — but a $30/share dividend on a ~$1,106 stock is 2.7%, which is plausible. The real discovery is that the $48.99 EPS implied by the footer of slide 2 ($20.76 / 3.10% = $669.7, rounded to $670) is far above the company's likely EPS. This doesn't tie. **Fix:** Recompute the implied earnings: if the $20.76 EPS is correct, then $670 ÷ $20.76 = 32.3x P/E, not the 3.10% yield. The footer must show: 'The $670 floor is the price at which the earnings yield equals the 3.10% after-tax cash alternative: $20.76 ÷ 0.0310 = $670.' If $20.76 is wrong, replace it with the correct EPS and recompute the floor. Also reconcile the $20.76 to the model: if Q3 FY2026 EPS is $20.76, the implied P/E at $1,106 is 53.3x, which is not the 1.88% yield quoted. There is a hard-coded or mis-scaled EPS plug.
- The 13-week T-bill yield is quoted as '4.12% pre-tax, 3.10% after tax' and the footnote says '1 – 24.7% effective tax rate'. 4.12% × (1 – 0.247) = 3.102%, which ties. But the same slide says 'The $13.3B special dividend is declared and fully funded' covered by '$20.21B cash plus $9.38B free cash flow'. $20.21B + $9.38B = $29.59B, while the uses are $13.3B dividend + $6.5B capex = $19.8B, leaving $9.79B. The slide does not show what happens to the remaining $9.79B, and the $4B buyback authorization is not deducted. The cash bridge does not balance to a source or line item. **Fix:** Add a cash bridge on slide 2: Sources: cash $20.21B + FCF $9.38B = $29.59B. Uses: $13.3B dividend + $6.5B capex + $4B buyback = $23.8B. Remaining: $5.79B. If the buyback is not funded in FY2026, state that explicitly: 'the $4B authorization is incremental and is not assumed in the FY2026 cash plan.' Show the arithmetic on the page or in a footnote.
- Slide 3 compares a 3-year capex CAGR (14.2%) to a 3-year revenue CAGR (7.8%). Slide 4 compares a buyback earnings yield (1.88%) to a 13-week T-bill (3.10%). These are different periods, units and risk. A 3-year historical CAGR is not a forward-looking return on the $6.5B. A buyback earnings yield is an earnings-capitalization measure, not a cash return. The slide calls both 'return' and puts them on the same bar chart — that is not an honest comparison. **Fix:** Put the comparison on one basis. Either (a) show all four metrics as realized cash-on-cash returns for the same 12-month period (FY2026E): capex return 22.2%, buyback earnings yield 1.88%, after-tax T-bill 3.10%, and pre-tax T-bill 4.12%; or (b) separate the charts with explicit labels: 'Historical CAGR, FY2023–FY2026' on slide 3 and 'Forward yield on 12-month cash flow, FY2026E' on slide 4. Do not mix realized CAGR with forward yield on the same axis without a label.
- The $670 floor is circular: 'the $670 floor is the price at which the earnings yield equals the 3.10% after-tax cash alternative', and 3.10% is itself derived as '4.12% x (1 - 24.7%)'. The 'break-even' is therefore a restatement of the T-bill after-tax yield, not evidence of value. It also ignores the fact that the $1,106.21 buyback proceeds would earn the same 3.10% if simply left in cash. **Fix:** Replace the 'break-even' framing with a hurdle that is independent of the T-bill: state the required spread over cash (e.g. 150-200bps) and show the implied floor price at that spread, then mark $670 as the price at a stated spread. Delete the '1.88% is below the 3.10% cash yield, so don't buy' conclusion until a spread is defined.
- The 22.2% 'return on existing asset base' is introduced in the title and the right-hand callout ('The plan is funded and largely in the ground / 26 new openings') but the footnote admits 'The forward return on the $6.5B capex plan is not disclosed in the filings reviewed not available in the sources; the 22.2% is the return on the existing asset base, not a marginal return.' That is an unsourced assumption doing the work of the page and it is not the metric the decision actually needs. **Fix:** Cut the 22.2% from the title and the callout, or replace it with the marginal return on new warehouse capex that the model actually supports. If it cannot be sourced, change the title to the one claim the data supports: 'Capex grew 14.2% a year against 7.8% revenue growth; 26 net openings are expected in FY2026.'
- The title says the '$6.5B plan needs 22.2% returns' but footnote 3 says 'The marginal after-tax return on the $6.58 plan is not disclosed in the filings reviewed' and calls 22.2% a return 'on the existing asset base, not a marginal return.' The headline figure therefore answers a different question than the headline asks. **Fix:** Retitle slide 3 to what is actually measured: 'Capex compounded 14.2% a year vs 7.8% revenue; FY2026E return on existing capital 22.2%, marginal return on incremental capex not disclosed.' Put 22.2% labelled 'ROIC, FY2026E (existing capital base)' and add a standalone line stating the marginal return is not disclosed.
- The comparison sets a forward earnings yield (1.88%) against a Treasury yield (3.10%) and calls the difference a reason not to buy back. Equity earnings yield vs risk-free rate is not a like-for-like hurdle — no equity risk premium, no cost of equity, no WACC. The 3.10% after-tax T-bill is the floor, not the discount rate. **Fix:** Add a cost-of-equity or WACC line to the slide 4 table and compare the buyback yield against that, not the 3.10% T-bill. If the intended test is 'buyback yield vs cash yield on the balance sheet,' state the 3.10% after-tax cash yield on cash explicitly and label it as the alternative use of the $4B, not the company's cost of capital.
- Slide 3 is a title over a blank page: 'Historical returns support screening growth projects before buybacks' with no chart, table, or source. The stated claim is unsupported. **Fix:** Populate slide 3 with the historical series it promises: ROIIC (or after-tax return on capital) by cohort for at least 5 fiscal years, the marginal project hurdle for new warehouses, and the buyback price paid per share in the same period, on one consistent after-tax basis, each series footnoted to the 10-K or the source document, with a vertical line marking the current year and the ratio of ROIIC to cost of capital. If the data cannot be obtained, cut slide 3.
- The core comparison is under-specified: the deck compares a '2.31% earnings yield' (slide 2) and a '3.10%' Treasury proxy (slide 4) to project returns, but never states Costco's cost of capital or the hurdle rate. Slide 3 (even if populated) would be the only source of the project return, and no comparable company, precedent, or method is disclosed for the valuation. **Fix:** Add the hurdle or cost of capital (a single number) on slide 2 and slide 4, state how it was derived (e.g., CAPM inputs, or company-stated return requirement), and show the project return explicitly against it (e.g., 'Project X returns Y% vs. hurdle Z%'). State the source for each input; if the number does not exist in the material, do not invent one - mark it as to be requested.
- The recommendation ('$0 additional returns through FY2027') is unsupported by the two bullets that carry it: 'At an illustrative $900, the 2.31% buyback yield is 0.80 percentage points below the proxy' and 'The Treasury proxy yields 3.10% after tax, based on a 4.12% pretax yield.' This compares an equity earnings yield to a Treasury yield on an after-tax basis at a single illustrative price and a single proxy rate; it does not establish that $0 is the right number versus, e.g., a smaller program. **Fix:** Replace the illustrative-price comparison with a decision table: dollar amount retained and deployed versus dollar amount returned, at each of at least two or three funding levels (e.g., $0, partial, full), showing what each buys (projects funded, incremental cash flow, earnings accretion, liquidity floor) and what each gives up (buyback yield, dividend coverage). Anchor each cell to the model line and source it.
- The page is blank. It carries only the title 'Fund growth projects only after their forecast return clears a sourced hurdle'. There is no project list, no capex, no forecast return, and no hurdle. The entire recommendation to 'prioritize warehouse growth' rests on this page. **Fix:** Build the page: list the named warehouse/club growth projects with capex per project, forecast unlevered return, the hurdle rate used, and the source for each return. If project-level returns don't exist, show capex and new-club count from the 10-K and state the return as 'not disclosed' rather than leaving the slide empty.
- The page is blank. Title only: 'Keep additional returns at $0 through FY2027 until funding needs are clear'. 'Funding needs' are never quantified anywhere in the deck, so the constraint the recommendation depends on is undefined. **Fix:** Put the funding needs on the page: FY2027 capex, maturities, working capital and minimum cash, each with a period and a source, and show the residual cash available for returns after those needs. That residual, not the word 'clear', is what supports $0.
- There is no valuation work anywhere: no DCF, no cost of capital, no comps, no football field, no sensitivity. The deck compares a '1.88%' buyback earnings yield to a '4.12% Treasury yield' as the sole economic test and concludes the buyback fails — but the risk-free rate is not the hurdle for equity capital, and no Costco-specific required return is shown. **Fix:** Add the valuation page: a DCF with WACC (state the build-up and the source) and terminal value shown separately with the % of total value labelled, a sensitivity on WACC and terminal growth, and a football field with at least three methods (DCF, trading comps, precedent transactions or a growth-investment NPV). State the buyback hurdle explicitly as Costco's cost of equity or WACC, not the 10-year Treasury.
- The page is blank. Title only: 'Approve $0 in additional discretionary returns while we verify the evidence'. A closing page with no next steps, no owners, no dates, and no decision mechanics. **Fix:** Replace with the decision page: the exact resolution language, the specific evidence to be verified, who produces each item, and the date the board reconvenes. If the answer is 'approve $0 now', the page must say what changes that answer and by when.
- Blank page. Only the title 'Test project returns against the required hurdle before committing cash' with no content, no hurdle rate stated, no project returns, no test, no footnote. A board page that asserts a test without showing the test is unusable. **Fix:** Build the page: state the hurdle rate and its source, list the top 5-10 projects by capital requested with IRR or NPV against that hurdle, mark which clear and which do not, and add a source line and 'estimates' footnote for every projected return. If the project-level returns do not exist in the model, that is a research request, not a blank slide.
- Blank page. Title 'Hold additional returns until liquidity and project tests are met' with no liquidity metrics, no thresholds, no test definition. The page that carries the actual recommendation has zero evidence on it. **Fix:** Populate: minimum operating cash threshold and how derived, current cash against it, covenant or rating constraints, and the trigger conditions under which incremental returns would be approved. Every threshold sourced.
- Blank page. Title 'Complete obligation and investment checks before the board decides on cash returns' with no obligations listed, no amounts, no dates. It closes the deck with a to-do list that is not a to-do list. **Fix:** Either make it a dated checklist with dollar amounts and owners for each obligation and investment check, or delete the page. Do not ship an empty closing page.
- There is no valuation analysis. No DCF, no comps, no football field, no per-share value, no share count, no buyback math. The recommendation is 'approve $0 of additional discretionary returns' and it is supported by exactly one relative-yield comparison on slide 2. **Fix:** Add the valuation work the recommendation requires: (1) value of the incremental warehouse capex pipeline versus returns of cash to shareholders, (2) a buyback accretion/anti-accretion analysis at the current price, (3) a football field with at least three methods. A capital allocation decision without a per-share value comparison is an opinion, not analysis.
- Cash math contradicts itself: "$20.21B August cash less the $13.3B dividend leaves $6.91B" then "an illustrative $4B buyback would reduce that snapshot to $2.91B." $20.21B - $13.3B - $4B = $2.91B, so the two bullets are internally consistent, but slide 3's table says 'Cash after dividend and buyback' for the consensus case is $9.41B with its own $20.21B starting cash and $4B buyback. Two different post-dividend, post-buyback cash figures ($2.91B vs $9.41B) for the same starting point on adjacent pages. One of them is wrong and the reader will find it immediately. Reconcile: does the $4B buyback come out of the August 30, 2026 snapshot or out of fiscal 2027 free cash flow? State the bridge explicitly: $20.21B opening - $13.3B dividend - $4B buyback + $6.5B FCF = $9.41B (or minus, per slide 3). Fix whichever page is wrong so both show one bridge.
- Core inputs are cited to Yahoo Finance (share price, cash-flow consensus, dividend/authorization, capex guidance) and to a Supply Chain 360 press item for the warehouse capital plan. That is not diligence-grade. A board deferring a $4B authorization needs the company's own Form 10-K, the declaration release, and management's approved capital plan, not a finance portal. Slide 4's 1.88% buyback earnings yield and $20.65 quoted price rest on a Yahoo share price with no date stamp in the line itself ('share price and 13-week Treasury yield, November 13, 2026'). Fix: replace every Yahoo citation with the primary filing or an internal management schedule; date-stamp the share price and Treasury yield by source and as-of date.
- The central recommendation rests on an apples-to-oranges comparison presented as decisive: a 3.10% after-tax risk-free Treasury yield against a 1.88% 'buyback earnings yield before lost interest.' A buyback earnings yield is not the return on an alternative Treasury; comparing a risk-free yield to an equity earnings yield conflates two different risk and duration exposures, and the deck uses it as the reason to defer. Also the $124M is 'Approximately $124M annual after-tax interest' on $4B at 3.10% = $124M, but that assumes the $4B sits in Treasuries, which is not stated as the plan. Fix: either (a) state the $4B is actually held in short-dated Treasuries and label the comparison as risk-free carry vs. equity buyback, with the risk differential noted, or (b) drop the yield comparison as the deciding argument and rest the case on the liquidity gap alone.
- Slide 2 argues the case because 'Buyback earnings yield is 1.88%' is 'below the Treasury yield', but slide 4 shows the after-tax Treasury yield at 3.10% against the same 1.88% buyback earnings yield — that 1.88% is presented as a buyback earnings yield ($20.76 / $1,106.21), not as the after-tax yield on the company's cash that was actually being compared in slide 2. The comparison is apples-to-oranges and the entire 'buyback is below Treasury' rationale does not survive its own numbers on slide 4. **Fix:** Either (a) restate slide 2 bullet 3 as 'after-tax yield on cash of 2.33% / buyback earnings yield of 1.88% vs an after-tax Treasury yield of 3.10%' and show the 2.33% calculation, or (b) delete the Treasury-yield argument from both slides and rely on the liquidity argument alone.
- The core claim — CFO should defer until payment-date liquidity is certified — is entirely asserted; slide 3's own table says 'Cash before dividend' = 'N/A not available', 'Other inflows and outflows' = 'N/A not available', and 'Cash for warehouse funding' = 'N/A not available'. Slide 2 calls the $20.21B a 'cash balance' while slide 3 footnote 1 correctly says it is a 'cash snapshot and illustrative balances'. A committee will ask one question: what is the projected cash balance on the payment date, and the deck does not answer it. **Fix:** Either (a) build a dated cash bridge from the August 30, 2026 snapshot to the December 4, 2026 payment date with the specific inflows and outflows (special dividend -$13.3B, capex, and at minimum the next quarter's operating cash flow), or (b) reframe the deck's recommendation honestly as 'the numbers to certify are not in the current materials; direct the CFO to produce them' and drop the recommendation framing to the annex.
- Footnote 2 reads 'The buyback earnings yield excludes interest lost on cash; the Treasury yield is pretax.' The buyback earnings yield of 1.88% is therefore also pre-tax, yet the headline comparison '1.88% versus a 4.12% Treasury yield' in the Board decision box does not state that both are pre-tax. Slide 4 footnote 2 adds a 24.7% marginal tax rate but never applies it. The after-tax Treasury yield is 4.12% x (1 - 0.247) = 3.10%, and the comparison changes materially once the tax rate is applied. **Fix:** Add to slide 1 footnote 2 and slide 4 footnote 2: 'Buyback earnings yield of 1.88% and Treasury yield of 4.12% are both pre-tax. After-tax Treasury yield at the 24.7% illustrative marginal rate is 3.10%.' Then decide which comparison the board should act on: if the decision is 'the buyback earns less than the Treasury', state it as the after-tax figure (1.88% vs 3.10%) and use the same basis on slides 1, 2, 4, and the closing.
- Slide 2 says the $4B purchase 'leaves $2.91B on the same snapshot basis'; slide 4 says 'Uses cash: $20.21B year-end cash predates the announced $13.3B special dividend.' The two are not the same basis: slide 2's $2.91B is after the dividend and the repurchase; slide 4's $20.21B is before both. Slide 1 labels $13.3B 'The special dividend is payable in December' while slide 3 dates it 'December 4, 2026', so the same event is described two ways. **Fix:** Use one label everywhere: 'Cash at August 30, 2026: $20.21B; less special dividend declared December 4, 2026: $13.3B; less illustrative $4.0B repurchase: $2.91B.' On slide 4 change 'Uses cash: $20.21B year-end cash predates the announced $13.3B special dividend' to 'Uses cash: the $4.0B repurchase reduces cash after the $13.3B December 4, 2026 dividend from $6.91B to $2.91B.'
- Every page says 'Authorization: N/A not available in the sources' or 'Authorization terms: Terms unverified'. The deck asks the board to defer 'use of the $4B buyback' and lists 'Reported expiration: November 2030' on slide 4, but the underlying authorization document is not in hand. Slide 4 also gives a 'Reported expiration: November 2030' with no footnote number. **Fix:** Before this circulates, obtain the board resolution. If it is not obtainable, change the ask from 'defer the $4B buyback' to 'defer a repurchase of up to $4B subject to confirmation of the authorization', and add a footnote to slide 4's 'Reported expiration: November 2030' citing where it is reported. Do not send a recommendation that turns on an authorization we cannot document.
- The core argument compares the buyback's 1.88% earnings yield to the 4.12% 13-week T-bill. That is not a capital allocation hurdle for a company with a 22.2% return on capital; a T-bill is a liquidity comparison, not an opportunity cost of permanent equity. Slide 4's title 'its 1.88% earnings yield trails the 4.12% T-bill' invites the obvious rebuke that Costco does not fund buybacks by liquidating into T-bills at 4.12% pre-tax, and the deck itself admits in footnote 1/slide 2 note that the yield test is an EPS-accretion-only measure. As written the recommendation is built on the weaker of the two arguments and a client will say so. **Fix:** Reframe the argument around capital need, not T-bill spread: the binding constraint is the fiscal 2027 capex plan ($7.5B) and the minimum operating buffer, and the buyback is capped at $3.0B because $4.0B plus the plan takes year-end cash to $15.5B against a $16.5B buffer (slide 4). Move the 22.2% ROC vs 1.88% buyback-yield comparison up as the primary test and demote the T-bill to a one-line 'cash held idle' remark.
- The cash bridge does not reconcile on the face of the page. Slide 3: $20.21B starting cash minus $7.5B capex = $12.71B before returns; plus $9.39B fiscal 2026 free cash flow = $22.10B, yet slide 4 shows year-end cash of $17.2B after the $3.0B buyback and $18.2B after the $1.0B dividend alone (implying only $2.01B of capex-plus-friction). The three rows use inconsistent inflows and the reader cannot reproduce $17.2B or $15.8B from $20.21B, $9.39B and $7.5B. This is the single number the board will try to add up. **Fix:** Put a one-line cash bridge per row on slide 4: starting cash $20.21B + fiscal 2027 operating cash flow (source it) − $7.5B capex − buyback − dividend = ending cash, and make the three rows tie to the same operating-cash-flow assumption. If fiscal 2027 operating cash flow is not in the 10-K/10-Q, label it an estimate and footnote the basis.
- The 22.2% ROC is a company-wide fiscal 2026 figure on ending equity and debt (your own footnote 2 says 'it is not the return on the next dollar of capex and is not the plan return'). Repeating it in the 'return on the next dollar' column for the capex and dividend rows is a wrong-unit, wrong-basis use of the number and undercuts the credibility of the one row (buyback) where the comparison is at least directionally valid. **Fix:** Either get a plan-level incremental return on the $7.5B (or state the new-warehouse unit economics: ~$227M per warehouse and the mature-store ROIC) and use that, or leave the column 'n/a' for the capex and dividend rows with the note 'company-wide FY26 ROC shown for reference only' and keep 22.2% only in a reference column.
- The buyback row reads: $17.2B: 20.21 + 9.39 - 7.5 - 3. Components are $20.21B + $9.39B - $7.5B - $3.0B = $19.10B, not $17.2B. The special dividend row: 20.21 + 9.39 - 7.5 - 1 = $21.10B, not $18.2B. The FY27 plan row: 20.21 + 9.39 - 7.5 = $22.10B, not $21.9B. Every figure in that column is off by $1.9B, $2.9B and $0.2B respectively, or the addends are mislabelled. With slide 3 showing -2.61 ordinary dividend and 1.0 special dividend and 3.0 buyback, the ending cash on the recommended option is $20.21 + 15.82 - 7.5 - 2.61 - 1.0 - 3.0 = $21.92B, not $17.2B. **Fix:** Rebuild the 'Effect on year-end cash' column as 20.21 + FY27 operating cash flow - 7.5 capex - 2.61 ordinary dividend - selected return, tie each row to the model, and state in the header that FY27 operating cash flow is $15.82B (not $9.39B). If $9.39B is operating cash flow net of ordinary dividends, say so and label it.
- The recommendation is a buyback with a 1.88% 'return on the next dollar' set against idle cash at 4.12% (slide 2, footnote 3) — a 224bp negative spread on $3.0B, or roughly $67M of annual value destroyed. The page acknowledges this but still recommends the $3.0B buyback; the only stated rationale is EPS accretion, which footnote 1 concedes is 'not a valuation test'. The numbers do not support the recommendation as written. **Fix:** Either (a) recommend the return size at which projected share repurchase price/earnings is at or below the value accretion threshold that justifies retiring equity at 1.88% — i.e. show the break-even earnings yield / required P/E at which the buyback is accretive on a value basis, and size the buyback to it — or (b) explicitly recommend holding cash / paying down debt / larger special dividend instead, and show the foregone 4.12% as the cost of the buyback. State the alternative in the closing slide.
- The stated basis for capping the buyback is that its '1.88% yield trails the 4.12% T-bill.' That compares a 12-month risk-free holding-period yield to an earnings yield (E/P) — different units, different risk, different holding period — and the deck itself concedes in footnote 2 that 'the 1.88% earnings yield is an EPS-accretion test only; not a value test.' A board that buys the 4.12% framing is being asked to stop repurchasing a 22.2% ROC business because cash yields 4.12% today, which is not an analysis, it is a rate-differential trade.
