The page does not resolve which case it is recommending; the reader is left to guess. **Fix:** Make it explicit in the title and right-hand box: 'Base-case 12.6% growth returns 14.5%, 2.3x the 6.4% cost of equity; consensus growth returns -10.12% and fails the test.' Put the 14.5% and -10.12% figures side by side in a single comparative table or bar pair, not one huge isolated number.
- Slide 3's trough of $12.99B assumes $14.0B operating cash flow, while Slide 4's callout says consensus cash flow of about $14.0B pushes the trough down by about $1.8B. The deck never reconciles these two cases; the board cannot tell which trough it is approving against. **Fix:** Add a one-line reconciliation on slide 3: 'At consensus cash flow the trough is $12.99B - $1.8B = $11.19B, which breaches the $12B floor by $0.81B.' Then state on slide 4 whether the $14B consensus case changes the recommended $1B pace or only the evidence.
- The fourth tile reads 'N/A' with the caption 'Needs the capex fact confirmed', and slide 2 concedes 'the size is not yet set'. A board page that says N/A is an unfinished page; the deck's whole ask is an unsized dividend. **Fix:** Replace the N/A tile with a real number from the 10-K. Use operating cash flow (research_requests) and state 'Maximum payout = cash of $20.21B less minimum reserve; reserve to be set', so all four tiles carry a figure. If the capex split by warehouse vs other is genuinely unavailable, delete the tile and re-balance the 2x2 grid to three tiles.
- The closing band says 'Decision today: approve a sized payout proposal for the next board meeting' — you are asking the board to approve something you have not sized, and slide 5 step 4 then asks them to decide it again. **Fix:** Change slide 2's band to the actual ask: 'Decision today: approve the sizing workplan; sized dividend proposal to the board at the next meeting.' Then slide 5's step 4 ('Board decides: approve, change or reject') is the only approval point and the two pages stop contradicting.
- The deck asks the board to approve a workplan, not a payout. Slide 5's 'Board decides on the sized proposal' sits after four internal workstreams. A board does not need a meeting to approve sizing work; it needs a range today. **Fix:** Insert a page after slide 4 titled 'Surplus is $20.21B less reserve — a payout of $X–Y B fits without touching growth' with a range calculated from numbers already in the deck (cash $20.21B, capex $6.43B, OCF $15.82B) and a stated minimum reserve assumption. Then slide 5 becomes the confirmation step, not the ask.
- The bullet reads 'Cash of $20.21B far exceeds capex needs of $6.43B' but the same page's own figure is 'Operating cash flow left after capex of $6.43B' — capex is funded from OCF, not from the $20.21B balance. Slide 2 states the correct logic: 'Operating cash flow of $15.82B less capex of $6.43B leaves $9.39B'. Two pages, two different mechanisms. **Fix:** Rewrite the bullet to 'Cash of $20.21B (about 3.1 years of capex) is separate from the $9.39B of annual free operating cash flow' so it matches slide 2 and slide 3.
- You label it a ceiling, but $20.21B is a balance sheet position, not distributable cash. The deck never states debt, lease obligations, working capital or seasonal inventory drag — so the ceiling is asserted, not supported. **Fix:** Either drop the 'Maximum payout' bullet and replace it with 'Distributable cash = cash $20.21B less reserve and seasonal working capital; to be sized' or add the debt and working-capital figures to the page (they are in the same 10-K).
- The page states 'Valuation ranges: not yet modelled' in the footer and 'We recommend returning the $5B unless the three methods show a higher value from retaining it', so the central comparison of retained versus returned cash is never actually made — the deck's core question is unanswered. **Fix:** Either replace slide 4 with the actual three-method output (real valuation ranges and the resulting recommendation), or drop the valuation framing and reframe slide 4 as 'Retained cash covers the $5B return 3.0x over; no analysis shows a higher-value use' using only the $9.39B / $20.21B facts already on the page, with no reference to unmodelled methods.
- 'Three valuation methods test whether retained cash is worth more than the $5B returned' is a topic title — it describes an exercise, not a finding, and the page proves no such test. **Fix:** Retitle to a claim the page can prove, e.g. 'No identified use of retained cash earns more than the $5B return' or 'Retained cash covers the $5B return 3.0x over, so growth stays funded' — only if the evidence supports it.
- The page is four 'N/A' boxes and a table that says 'Not run' three times. It claims 'Three valuation methods will test whether a retained dollar earns more than a returned one' and then runs none of them. This is the analytical spine of the deck and it is empty. **Fix:** Either run the DCF on free cash flow (base inputs are on slide 4: operating cash flow $15.82B, capex $6.43B, FCF $9.39B) and put the per-share value and hurdle rate on the page, or delete slide 3 and remove every reference to a valuation test from slides 1, 2 and 5.
- The page ends with 'Growth wins only if its return beats the value of a returned dollar' — a condition, not an answer. The reader closes the deck not knowing whether growth does or does not clear that bar. The answer is on no page. **Fix:** Replace the 'So what' with the actual result: the return on incremental capex versus the required return, stated as a number. If the data is not sourced, say so in a footnote and put a placeholder-driven conclusion on the page.
- 'A $5B return leaves ... growth stays funded' on slide 4 and the executive summary both assert growth can be funded in full, but no page shows the growth capex plan the $5B competes with. $5B plus capex is asserted, not proven. **Fix:** Add the growth capex plan (annual and multi-year) to slide 4's annual flows table so the reader can see $15.21B covers the committed spend. If Costco does not disclose it, cite the constraint in a footnote and show the sensitivity.
- The lower table is cut off mid-header. 'Option | Cash after return ($B) | Headroom over $8B minimum ($B) | Return per share ($) | Funding source' is sliced by the callout box, and 'Fund growth only, no return' is clipped to 'return'. This is a broken page — you cannot send it. **Fix:** Delete the second table entirely. Slide 4 already has the scorecard on top; put the two takeaway lines in a normal bullet block below the scorecard and let the page breathe. Do not re-add a partially rendered table.
- Free cash flow is quoted two ways: slide 2 says 'plus free cash flow $9.39B' while slide 3's title says '$2.80B of free cash flow' and slide 5 review step says 'operating cash flow of $15.82B against the $8B minimum'. A CFO will spot this in ten seconds. **Fix:** Use one definition everywhere. Lead with free cash flow = $15.82B operating less $6.43B capex = $9.39B. On slide 3 retitle 'Cash of $20.21B plus $9.39B free cash flow funds the $8B return', keep the $2.80B bridge bar but relabel it 'Free cash flow after the $6.43B capex' and reconcile to the $9.39B. On slide 5 change '$15.82B' to '$9.39B'.
- Slide 2 says 'A $2B special dividend on 443.1M shares is about $4.51 per share' and slide 4 says 'The $8B package is $18.06 per share'. $18.06 is $8B/443.1M; $4.51 is $2B/443.1M. The deck never states the $8B = $13.55 buyback + $4.51 dividend split, so the two numbers look contradictory. **Fix:** On slide 2 bullet 2 add the arithmetic: '$8B = $13.55 per share buyback + $4.51 per share special dividend = $18.06 per share'. On slide 4's middle panel add a line 'Per-share: $13.55 buyback + $4.51 dividend = $18.06'. Verify the $13.55 against the $1,106.21 close — 5.4M shares x $1,106.21 = $5.97B, not $6B — state the buyback as 5.42M shares at $1,106.21 to make it tie.
- The waterfall does not foot: 20.21 + 2.80 - 8.00 = 15.01, which matches the ending bar, but the bridge omits the $1.15B of dividends and $0.848B of buybacks already run in the period, which the callout box itself cites. A committee member will add the bars and find the gap. **Fix:** Either add two negative bars for 'Dividends already paid $1.15B' and 'Buybacks already run $0.848B' so the bridge foots to ending cash, or relabel the bars to 'Cash at 30 Aug 2026 (post existing returns)' and state the $1.998B already returned in the subtitle. Do not leave a waterfall that does not reconcile.
- The chart shows an FY2027 free cash flow of $8.32B, but the callout box says 'The buyback draws on FY2027 free cash flow of $8.32B, not the FY2026 residual of $9.39B.' No bridge or numbers between FY2026 free cash flow ($9.39B) and FY2027 free cash flow ($8.32B) are shown, so the $8.32B is unsupported and contradicts the $9.39B block. **Fix:** Add the FY2027 step-up bridge: show FY2026 free cash flow of $9.39B, subtract the capex step-up of $1.07B to $7.50B, and arrive at FY2027 free cash flow of $8.32B. If the $1.07B isn't in the material, request it from Finance before the meeting.
- The funding number changes without explanation: slide 1 and slide 2 say 'free cash flow of $9.38B' and '$9.38B of free cash flow'; slide 3's title says 'FY2027 free cash flow of $8.32B funds the buyback'; slide 5's note says 'it sits inside the $9.39B of free cash flow left'. A board will ask which year funds this and why the number moved. **Fix:** Pick one funding year throughout. Since slide 3 argues the buyback draws on FY2027, use '$8.32B' as the funding base on slides 1, 2, 3 and 5, and show FY2026's $9.38B as the current-year residual only, labelled as such.
- Every score cell is empty. The title claims a three-way comparison on four weighted criteria and the table never delivers a single number. Footnote 1 admits 'Scores are the author's judgement on the evidence shown, not a fact from the filings' — that is a disclaimer, not a score. **Fix:** Either populate the 3x4 grid with the weighted /100 scores and rank the three uses, or delete the table and let the two panels do the work. If you keep it, the title becomes 'Buyback scores highest on flexibility at equal per-share value' and the numbers back it.
- Share retirement is inconsistent: slide 2 item 3 says the $8B return retires 5.42M shares (1.22% of the count), slide 4 says 7.2M shares retired (1.6% of shares outstanding). Both cannot be true. **Fix:** Pick one: if the $6B buyback at $1,106.21 retires 5.42M shares, the slide 4 table cell for 'Return $8B (O1)' must read 5.42M and the 'in four numbers' tile must read 5.42M and 1.2%; correct the shares-retired figure in slide 4's table and the tile to match slide 2.
- The '22.2%' tile is cut off at the bottom edge and the caption 'The cash must beat this after-tax return on capital' is clipped and overlapping the source line. Text is running off the slide. **Fix:** Either close the box with only the two tiles (1.88% earnings yield and 5.15% Treasury) and put the 22.2% comparison in a sentence, or extend the box to full height; set the caption inside the box above the source line, no text beyond the bottom margin.
- The table header wraps mid-word: 'Operat / ing / cash / flow' and 'Cash retain / ed', and the rows are squeezed so tightly the three options run together. This is unreadable and would not go out. **Fix:** Widen the table to the full left column, set header labels to no more than two lines each ('Operating cash flow cover', 'Cash retained'), increase row height so the three option rows are clearly separated, and align all numeric columns right on a decimal.
- Item 4 says 'Buyback earnings yield 1.88% vs 5.15% Treasury: the $6B buyback is EPS-accretive, the $2B dividend is not, so recommend the $6B/$2B split'. This contradicts the recommendation. If the buyback is accretive and the dividend is not, why give $2B to the non-accretive dividend? **Fix:** Either justify the $2B dividend on another ground (e.g., shareholder base, payout policy) in one clear sentence, or change the split to a larger buyback and smaller dividend. Do not leave the reader with a recommendation that argues against itself.
- The bullet says 'The buyback earns a 1.88% earnings yield against the 5.15% Treasury, so size it, do not maximise it.' This recommends against the very thing the page and the deck are asking the board to approve. **Fix:** Remove the 'do not maximise it' line, or reframe it as an explicit trade-off with the board's objective (e.g., EPS accretion now vs. interest income forgone) and state what the board should weigh.
- The page's own footnote says the 22.2% return is 'historical return on book capital, not the return on the $6.43B of new capex' and that 'the marginal return on the new capex is not available in the sources, so the 22.2% is historical and cannot rank the new capex against the buyback.' The page then ranks them anyway: 'Keep funding the business at 22.2% instead of buying back stock at 1.88%' and 'new capex is the better use of the cash.' That is an unsupported claim on the page that decides the allocation. **Fix:** Either obtain the marginal return on the $6.43B of new capex and put that number on the page, or restate the conclusion to what the evidence supports: 'Buyback earns 1.88% against a 5.15% Treasury; the $131M annual gap is the cost of capping it at $4.00B.' Remove the 22.2% comparison and the sentence 'new capex is the better use of the cash' until the marginal return is sourced. If the board decision stands on 22.2%, say so explicitly as a policy judgement, not a measured spread.
- The bridge double-counts and mis-signs the outflow: it deducts 'Special dividend -4.00' and then 'FY2026 capex -6.43' to get residual cash of 5.78, but the title and the side panel both claim $12.21B is 1.9x the $6.43B capex. Two different capex treatments on one page (capex inside the bridge, capex excluded from the 1.9x cover claim). A board member will ask which number is the cover ratio. **Fix:** Rebuild the bridge as: 20.21 cash at 2026-08-30, less 4.00 buyback, less 4.00 special dividend, less 6.43 capex, equals 5.78 residual. Then change the title to 'Cash of $20.21B funds $6.43B capex and $8.00B of return, leaving $5.78B and 1.9x cover on the return' and delete the 1.9x claim from the side panel, or restate the 1.9x as 20.21/6.43 = 3.1x pre-return and drop the post-return cover line entirely.
- The page argues buyback earns 1.88% versus a 5.15% Treasury, so cap it. That is a hurdle-rate argument, not a value argument; the Treasury is a risk-free alternative, not the company's cost of capital. As written it says 'we cannot beat a Treasury, so stop buying stock', which is a different and weaker claim than 'cap the buyback because capex earns 22.2%'. The 22.2% bar is buried in the right column and appears third in the left column. **Fix:** Restate the title as 'Capex earns 22.2% and the buyback earns 1.88%, so fund capex and cap the buyback at $4.00B'. Reorder the left column so 22.2% return on business capital is line one, then 1.88% buyback, then 5.15% Treasury as context. Keep the 327bp spread only as a supporting line, not the lead.

### Storyboard: MD (185)

- The recommendation is asserted, not derived. Slide 2 says 'return surplus only after setting a liquidity floor' but nothing in the deck defines the floor, so 'surplus' is undefined. Slide 4 says '$20.21B cash is 3x capex, so a measured payout is affordable' and 'the combined option scores highest,' but 3.1x cash-to-capex does not prove affordability — it ignores working capital, debt maturities and rent/lease obligations, none of which are in the material. The conclusion does not follow from the numbers shown. **Fix:** On Slide 4, replace 'the combined option scores highest' with the criteria test that actually drives it (funds warehouses, retains flexibility) and state explicitly that the minimum cash floor is the open variable. On Slide 2, phrase the recommendation as 'fund growth first; return only cash above a floor to be set at [X]' and flag the floor as the decision the board is making, not a given.
- The core deliverable — how much cash to return and in what form — is unsized across three slides. '$46 per share' is derived from $20.21B / 443.1M, but it is cash per share, not a payout. A board cannot approve a return with no size and no form. **Fix:** Do not put a payout recommendation on the slides until the research is in. Either add the missing facts (share price, current dividend, buyback history, debt/lease obligations) as sourced research before building, or restate the recommendation as 'authorize management to size a return by [date]' — which Slide 2 already half-says — and remove the '$46 per share' line from Slide 4 because it invites a payout-per-share question you cannot answer.
- 'Warehouse growth takes 70% of earnings and leaves $2.8B of free profit' treats capex of $6.43B as a deduction from net income of $9.23B. Capex is cash for property and equipment; net income is accrual after depreciation, interest and tax. The speaker note admits this ('not a like-for-like deduction') but the slide title and waterfall still present $9.23B − $6.43B = $2.80B as if it were free cash flow. That is a mixed-basis bridge a committee will attack line by line. **Fix:** Retitle Slide 3 to describe cash uses, not 'free profit.' Replace the net-income-minus-capex bridge with either (a) a simple capex-vs-net-income comparison labelled as two separate figures, or (b) a proper cash bridge that starts from cash from operations (not supplied) — if (b), flag cash from operations as required research. Do not label $2.80B 'free profit.'
- You ask the board on slide 2 to 'approve management to propose a liquidity floor and size a surplus-cash return by the next board meeting' while slide 3 concedes 'total debt, lease obligations and maturities are not in the material' and 'the page makes no affordability claim'. A liquidity floor cannot be set and a surplus cannot be sized without the debt and lease stack: $20.21B of cash (F5) against an unknown lease-obligation balance is not a surplus. The recommendation asks the board to approve a process whose central input is missing and deferred to itself. **Fix:** Either (a) pull total debt, operating and finance lease obligations and maturity schedule from the same 10-K (filed 2026-10-07, accession 000090983226000093) before the deck goes out, so the floor is set against net cash rather than gross cash $20.21B; or (b) if that cannot be sourced, change the slide 2 decision line to 'approve management to prepare, for approval at the next board meeting, a liquidity floor defined as months of the $6.43B capex run-rate and supported by a debt and lease maturity schedule' — i.e. the board approves a framework, not a payout, and the deck stops implying an affordability conclusion it has not earned.
- The 22.2% is F42 = operating income $11.69B × (1 − 24.7% tax) / (equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B) — a company-wide return on total capital employed. Nothing in the storyboard connects it to incremental warehouse capex. The comparison '22.2% growth vs 4.12% T-bill vs 1.88% buyback' is the whole recommendation, and it rests on an apples-to-oranges rate. Without a marginal return on new warehouse investment, the 22.2% is not defensible against a committee that asks 'what does one new warehouse earn?' **Fix:** Either (a) add the marginal analysis: take the FY26 new-warehouse count from the 10-K, allocate the $6.43B capex and $15.82B operating cash flow to the new-cohort, and state the resulting incremental after-tax return with its derivation in a footnote; or (b) keep F42 but relabel it as 'company-wide after-tax return on capital employed (ROCE)' and add a separate, sourced marginal warehouse return. Do not label a company-level ratio as the return on growth capital in the slide title.
- There is no WACC and no financing discussion, so the 4.12% T-bill versus 1.88% buyback vs 22.2% growth comparison has no cost-of-capital anchor. A special dividend of ~$13.3B against $20.21B cash (F5) is 66% of the cash balance, paid 4 December 2026; the storyboard never shows cash after the dividend, or whether the $4B buyback and $6.5B capex can be funded from the remainder plus $15.82B operating cash flow (F17). The recommendation 'no new debt' is asserted, not shown. No financing source is named — F47 says '$6,500 during fiscal 2026' but that is an intent, not a funded plan. **Fix:** Add a sources-and-uses line to slide 2 or 4: cash $20.21B (F5) − $13.3B special dividend (F50) − $4B buyback (F49) = ~$2.9B residual, plus $15.82B operating cash flow (F17) and $9.38B FCF (F36); state that $6.43B capex (F4)/$6.5B plan (F47) is covered and that no new debt is needed. Also state a WACC or hurdle rate and cite the source. If no sourced WACC is in hand, the slide must say the hurdle is undetermined and the comparison is against the risk-free rate only.
- F40 earnings yield 1.88% = diluted EPS $20.76 / share price $1,106.21; F41 EPS after $1B buyback = $20.76 × 443.1M / 442.2M = $20.80. The EPS figure is 'before lost interest on the cash' but the cash would have earned 4.12% (F34); on $1B that is ~$41M pre-tax, and at the 24.7% tax rate (F27/F42) the interest income would have been ~$31M, roughly $0.07/share. The storyboard omits that interest adjustment and therefore overstates the EPS accretion. The same omission reverses the 'buyback earns 1.88%' framing if the foregone 4.12% is charged against it. **Fix:** Replace the buyback row with two lines: gross EPS accretion and EPS accretion net of foregone interest at 4.12% (F34), showing $20.80 gross falling to approximately $20.73 net of the ~$31M after-tax interest lost (24.7% tax, F27). State the assumption 'cash used for buyback would otherwise earn 4.12%' explicitly with its source. If the simplification is retained, label the row 'before foregone interest' in the row header, not only in a footnote.
- There is no risk, no sensitivity, and no downside case anywhere. The question — return cash or fund growth — turns on whether the growth plan performs. The storyboard takes F47's $6.5B plan and F42's 22.2% as given. It never shows what happens if warehouse returns miss, if comps slow, if capex runs over, or if the buyback is executed at a higher price. A board or investment committee will ask for these before approving $13.3B of dividend and $4B of buyback. **Fix:** Add a risk-and-sensitivity section, which requires replacing another slide given the 5-slide limit. Convert slide 4 (table_focus) into a 'Decision table with downside' page: three scenarios for the warehouse return (base 22.2% per F42; −500bp; −1,000bp) crossed with the cash/return decision, showing the break-even return at which the buyback beats growth, and the cash balance under each. Cut or merge the current slide 4 table into slide 3 as a supporting exhibit.
- The two headline numbers are not comparable: the 22.2% after-tax return on capital (F32) is computed on Costco's entire existing capital base — operating income $11.69B x (1 - 24.7%) / (equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B) — and is not the incremental return on the $7.5B of new FY27 capex. The 1.88% buyback yield (F30) is a cash return on today's share price. Comparing a stock to a flow and calling the recommendation from it is exactly the assertion-without-support the board will catch. **Fix:** Replace the 22.2% vs 1.88% headline with an incremental comparison: state the incremental after-tax return on new capex (needs research) versus the 4.12% 13-week T-bill (F24) and the 1.88% buyback earnings yield (F30), and show the 22.2% (F32) only as the historical return on the existing asset base. If the incremental figure cannot be sourced, do not assert that growth out-earns a buyback — say so.
- The bridge does not tie and mixes periods. $20.21B cash (F5) and $15.82B operating cash flow (F7) and $6.43B capex (F4) are FY2026 (period 2025-09-01 to 2026-08-30), while the $13.3B special dividend (F40) is payable 4 December 2026 and the $7.5B FY27 capex plan (F39) is unspent. $20.21 + $15.82 - $6.43 - $13.3 - $4 - $1.15 = $10.15B, not '$6.9B'. The $6.9B figure is unsourced and hard-coded. **Fix:** Rebuild the bridge on a single FY27 basis: FY27 opening cash (research), FY27 guided operating cash flow (research), less FY27 capex $7.5B (F39), less $13.3B special dividend (F40), less $4B buyback (F40), less FY27 regular dividends (research), to closing cash. Show the FY26 figures (F5/F7/F4) only as the historical starting point, and make the subtotals add.
- No risk slide and no risk content anywhere in five slides. F23 (beta 0.24), F24 (4.12% T-bill), F25 (5.15% 10-year), F48 (P/E ~56 vs Walmart 38 and Kroger 15), F36 (33 warehouses in FY27 incl. five relocations vs 30 net new), F37 ('$4,228 ... approximately $6,500 during fiscal 2026'), and the F43-F45/F49-F50 inflation/tariff/currency risk language are all unused. A board recommending a $4B buyback at a 56x P/E will ask what happens if the multiple normalises and what the capex overrun risk is. **Fix:** Add a risk exhibit to slide 3 or replace slide 3's right panel with it: show the 22.2% ROC (F32) under a capex overshoot and under a margin compression case, show the buyback EPS accretion at the 1.88% yield (F30) versus a lower multiple, and state the sensitivity of the $9.38B FCF (F26) to a 100bp move in the 4.12% T-bill (F24). Explicitly flag that management's own plan is 33 warehouses incl. five relocations (F36) against a stated 30 net new, i.e. a capex assumption risk.
- The governing thought says the $17.3B return is 'funded from FY26 free cash flow of $9.38B and cash of $20.21B, not from the $7.5B FY27 capex plan.' That arithmetic closes only if FY27 operating cash flow covers the FY27 capex plan; the deck itself admits the FY27 operating cash flow is not in the material. The recommendation therefore rests on an unsourced assumption. **Fix:** Add a research request for FY27 operating cash flow (or management's FY27 OCF guidance) and the FY27 capex funding split; state on slide 2: '$17.3B return = FY26 FCF $9.38B + cash $20.21B, with FY27 capex $7.5B funded from FY27 OCF of $X (source).' Until X is sourced, present the funding as a range or a stated assumption, not a fact.
- The recommendation — keep funding warehouse growth at the guided pace — turns on whether the incremental return on new capex exceeds the 1.88% buyback yield (F30) and the 6.4% WACC (F54). The deck has only the historical 22.2% ROC (F32) on the existing base. Without the marginal return, the 'fund growth' answer is asserted, not demonstrated. **Fix:** Block the recommendation on a research request for the marginal after-tax return on new warehouse capex: unit-level capex per warehouse, mature-store sales and margin, and years to maturity. On slide 3, show a sensitivity table: if incremental ROI is 10%, 15% or 20%, how does the growth case compare with the buyback at 1.88% and the T-bill at 4.12% (F24)?
- The 1.88% earnings yield is computed as diluted EPS $20.76 (F13) / share price $1,106.21 (F21). That is the earnings yield on the whole company, not the return on a buyback dollar. A buyback's return to remaining holders is the earnings yield only if the shares are retired at a price equal to the value of the earnings; at a 56x P/E (F48) the retained earnings per dollar repurchased is 1/56 = 1.79%, and the EPS accretion is only $0.04 per $1B (F31). The deck repeatedly uses 1.88% as 'the return the buyback earns' without that qualification. **Fix:** Relabel F30 on every slide as 'earnings yield on the whole company 1.88% = EPS $20.76 / price $1,106.21 (F13/F21); the EPS accretion from a $1B buyback is $0.04 (F31) — the buyback is accretive only if the company's earnings yield exceeds the after-tax yield on the cash it spends.' Keep the 4.12% T-bill (F24) comparison but state it as the opportunity cost of the cash, not as the buyback's 'return'.
- The draft's old recommendation is still in the material: F10 title reads 'Approve a $10B buyback to return excess cash while funding warehouse growth' and F47 reads 'Formalise the buyback to return $9.38B of free cash flow while funding growth', against the governing thought's '$4B buyback and execute the already-declared $30 special dividend'. Two different return amounts and no special dividend in either. **Fix:** Rewrite slide 2's title to 'Approve a $4B buyback and confirm the $30 special dividend ($13.3B)' and slide 5's action 1 to 'Confirm the $30 special dividend, $13.3B, to holders of 443.1M shares (Board, Q1 2027)' and action 2 to 'Execute the $4B buyback over 12 months, retiring 3.6M shares (0.8%) at $1,106.21 (Treasury, 2027)'. Delete every $10B and $9.38B authorisation reference from the storyline.
- The waterfall runs $15.82B operating cash flow less $6.43B capex to $9.38B free cash flow and the takeaway says operations 'still leave $9.38B', but the recommendation now spends $13.3B on the special dividend plus $4B on the buyback = $17.3B against $9.38B FCF and $20.21B cash. The bridge as planned does not fund the decision it headlines; the $13.3B outflow never appears. **Fix:** Add two bars to the bridge: free cash flow $9.38B, less special dividend $13.3B, less buyback $4B, leaving $8.09B on a $20.21B opening cash balance; retitle slide 3 'Free cash flow of $9.38B plus $20.21B cash funds $17.3B of returns'. Drop the '$9.38B covers the $6.5B capex plan' framing, which answers a question no one asked.
- The comparison 'warehouse growth 22.2% vs buyback 1.9%' is not apples to apples. F95 is an after-tax return on capital = $11.69B x (1 - 24.7%) / ($35.80B equity + $3.91B debt); F93 is an earnings yield = $20.76 / $1,106.21, which is a pre-personal-tax, pre-buyback-price-paid return with no growth or capital-loss adjustment. Bar-charting them side by side implies the buyback destroys 20 points of value before any market reaction to a $30 special dividend. **Fix:** Either relabel F93 as 'earnings yield, not a return on invested capital' and add one sentence noting the buyback's return is the earnings yield investors pay for, or put the two on a like basis: show buyback return as $20.76 EPS / $1,106.21 = 1.88% plus the market's implied growth, and note the 22.2% ROIC is a marginal return on incremental warehouse capital, not a portfolio return. If the point is only 'growth clears its cost of capital and a buyback at $1,106.21 is expensive', say that instead of charting unlike measures.
- Three different recommendations. The brief's governing thought: 'Approve the $4B buyback authorisation today and confirm the already-declared $30 special dividend ($13.3B)'; the storyboard's slide 2 title: 'Approve a $4B buyback and confirm the $30 special dividend ($13.3B)'; but the old F10 storyboard title is 'Approve a $10B buyback to return excess cash while funding warehouse growth' and F11 is 'Decision today: Approve a $10B buyback authorisation'. The deck asks the board to approve a number, so a $4B vs $10B inconsistency is fatal before any exhibit is read. **Fix:** State one recommendation in the governing thought, the slide 2 title and the slide 5 roadmap. Delete the $10B framing from F10 and F11 entirely; slide 3's exhibit already argues '$4B and not $10B', so $10B may appear only as a rejected alternative in the exhibit, never as the recommendation.
- The numbers do not support the recommendation. Slide 4 shows warehouse growth 22.2%, cash 5.2%, buybacks 1.9%, and F95 says the 22.2% is a whole-company return on book capital (equity $35.80B plus long-term debt $3.91B) while F93 says 1.9% is an earnings yield. If the company already earns 22.2% on all its capital, the $4B of surplus is not idle: the deck's own comparison says deploy it at 22.2%, not at 1.9%. As written, the striking finding is 'a $4B buyback destroys ~$810M of value per year (4.03B×[22.2%-1.9%])', which argues against the recommendation. This is the page a hostile committee will open with. **Fix:** Make slide 4's argument explicit and honest: compare the 1.9% earnings yield and the 5.2% cash yield against the incremental after-tax return on the $4B if kept in the business. Add the sentence 'the 22.2% is a whole-company book return (equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B + operating lease liabilities $2.61B), not a measured incremental new-warehouse return' (already drafted) and put the buyback's justification — no higher-return marginal project — on the page with the $4B figure, not assumed.
- Circular funding. The deck says the $13.3B special dividend is funded from the $20.21B cash balance 'not from free cash flow', but then funds the $4B buyback from capital while the same $20.21B is the stated source. $13.3B + $4B = $17.3B against $20.21B cash leaves $2.9B, less than one quarter of the $6.5B capex plan; no reconciliation of cash uses is shown. A committee will ask what happens to liquidity and whether the buyback is debt-funded. **Fix:** Add a one-line sources-and-uses on slide 2 or 3: $20.21B cash + $9.38B FY2026 FCF = $29.59B; less $13.3B special dividend, less $4B buyback, less $6.5B capex = $5.79B remaining, before any seasonal working-capital swing. If that is the wrong split of period (the $9.38B is FY2026 and the $30 dividend is declared 2026-11-11, i.e. FY2027), relabel each line with its period.
- The cash alternative's return, 5.2%, has no source and no date; F40 carries '5.2' with no attribution while the other two bars are sourced. The entire 'hold cash earns more than the buyback' argument rests on that number. **Fix:** Research the US 10-year Treasury closing yield at 2026-11-13 and cite it as 'US 10-year Treasury yield, x.xx% at close 2026-11-13 (source)'. If the yield is not 5.2%, update F40 and slide 4's title, because the title quotes 5.2%.
- The distribution is stated three ways that do not reconcile: Slide 1/title '$30 special dividend and $4B buyback' with no total; Slide 2 'the $13.3B special dividend and $4B buyback' and 'the $17.3B payout'; Slide 4 'The $17.3B payout cuts cash to $2.9B'. The arithmetic works ($13.3B + $4B = $17.3B; $20.21B - $17.3B = $2.91B) but the slides mix gross dividend, gross authorization and net cash outflow without a single bridge, so a director cannot tie the headline number to the balance sheet. **Fix:** Add one bridge line used identically on Slides 2 and 4: '$13.3B special dividend (=$30 x 443.1M) + $4B buyback authorization = $17.3B gross; $20.21B cash less $17.3B = $2.9B cash'. On Slide 2's title keep '$13.3B' and state the $17.3B gross in the first line; on Slide 4 keep '$17.3B'.
- 'cash cover of capex 3.1x to 0.5x' is computed on trailing capex: $2.9B / $6.43B = 0.45x, i.e. the post-payout cash balance does not cover one year of capex, and the slide's own takeaway admits the gap. The plan spends the entire balance sheet and leans wholly on $9.38B of FCF, which is a FY2026 actual, not a committed forward number. A committee reads this as funding the payout with the revolver. **Fix:** Re-title Slide 4 'The $17.3B payout leaves $2.9B cash; the FY2026 capex plan is serviced by $9.38B free cash flow and existing facilities' and add one line naming the committed backstop (revolver size and maturity) and the minimum operating cash the company targets; if neither is in hand, mark it '[GAP - to be confirmed from 10-K liquidity note]'.
- The two slides tell two different cash stories. Slide 2 says total cash after the $17.3B gross distribution is $2.9B. Slide 4 column B says post-dividend cash is $6.9B ($20.21B less the $13.3B dividend) and column C says $2.9B post-buyback. $20.21B - $13.3B = $6.91B, and $20.21B - $13.3B - $4.0B = $2.91B, so slide 4 is arithmetically right and slide 2's single bridge is wrong by $4.0B. The stated '$17.3B gross' also implies the full $4B authorization is drawn on day one, which contradicts slide 4's own bullet 'the $4B authorization is drawn over time, not on day one' and the $4B/$1,106.21 = 3.6M share figure on slide 5. As written the board is being told cash falls to $2.9B today when the dividend payment on 2026-12-04 only takes it to $6.9B. **Fix:** Rewrite the bridge on slide 2 as two steps: '$20.21B cash less $13.3B special dividend = $6.9B after the 2026-12-04 payment; less the $4.0B authorization as drawn = $2.9B gross, by 2030.' Delete '$17.3B gross' as a same-day cash figure, or relabel it 'gross authorizations over time'. Use the identical two-step wording on slides 2 and 4.
- 'The $17.3B authorization' is wrong in kind as well as in amount. $13.3B is a declared dividend, not an authorization; $4B is a share repurchase authorization. Calling the sum an authorization treats a declared liability as if it were a discretionary facility, which is exactly the distinction a board has to vote on. The governing thought also says 'the $17.3B authorization still leaves $6.9B cash today and $2.9B by 2030' — that mixes the two and is only consistent with the two-step bridge above. **Fix:** Use 'declared dividend of $13.3B' and 'repurchase authorization of up to $4.0B' as separate items throughout; if a total is needed, write '$17.3B of gross distributions and authorizations, of which $13.3B is declared and payable 2026-12-04'.
- The entire buyback call rests on 'the 1.88% earnings yield is below the 5.15% Treasury yield' (F21, F22: 'Gross earnings yield of 1.88% less 5.15% Treasury is about -3.27%, so the buyback clears only below $403.11'), while the same slide concedes the comparison is 'not apples-to-apples.' Buying back equity with balance-sheet cash is not funded by liquidating a Treasury; a 1.88% earnings yield on a 37x P/E is a valuation observation, not a hurdle rate. Nothing in the deck supports '-3.27%' or the '$403.11' trigger as a decision rule. **Fix:** Replace the Treasury-spread as the buyback criterion with the actual basis: state the FY2026 P/E implied by the $1,106.21 close on net income $9.23B and 443.1M shares, then make the pause case on that multiple and on the declared $30 dividend's cash call. If the Treasury spread is kept, label it explicitly as a valuation cross-check and delete the '$403.11' repurchase trigger until it ties to a stated methodology.
- Unsourced figures carry the decision: $403.11 appears in the slide 2 title (F15: 'Approve the $7.5B expansion and cap the buyback at $403.11 a share') and the cover subtitle; -3.27% (F21); 3.6M shares / 0.8% (F23); $13.3B and 65.8% ($30 x 443.1M / $20.21B, F26); $24.8B and $4.79B (F35, F29-F30). QA already flags every one (F12, F42-F49). Calculations may be shown as calculations, but the inputs must be tagged. **Fix:** Tag each input to a provenance ID in the model: 443.1M shares and $20.21B cash to the FY2026 10-K (F1, F5); $30 per share and the $4B reauthorization to the 11 Nov 2026 dividend declaration (F27, F33); $1,106.21 to the 13 Nov 2026 close with the vendor named. Show '30 x 443.1M = $13.29B' and '$20.21B + $9.38B - $24.8B = $4.79B' as line-item arithmetic, not plugs. Purge F34 from every citation: 'Meshpool 2' is not a source.
- The $7.5B plan, the 33 warehouses, and the ~30-per-year cadence are sourced to 'gurufocus.com' and 'Yahoo Finance' (F33) aggregators of a 25 Sep 2026 company announcement, on a page that will not show the primary release. For a board document, the number that anchors the recommendation must come from Costco's own release or an 8-K, not a syndicated summary. **Fix:** Re-source the $7.5B FY2027 capex plan and the 33-store count to Costco's own 25 Sep 2026 announcement or the related 8-K/IR materials; cite the Yahoo Finance piece only if the primary is unavailable, with the primary URL attempted shown.
- None of the core transaction numbers is in the facts. F12 flags them directly: 'numbers without a source: $17.29B, $17.29B, $30, $13.29B, 443.1M, $4B.' The only 2026 dividend/buyback source referenced anywhere is F33, 'Yahoo Finance, Costco declares $30 special dividend and reauthorizes $4B buyback, 2026-11-11' — a market-data aggregator, not the 8-K or the declaration itself. The board is being asked to vote on numbers whose terms (record date, ex-date, buyback authorisation) are not evidenced. **Fix:** Pull the Costco 8-K/press release of 2026-11-11 from EDGAR, quote the declared per-share amount, total expected payment and buyback authorisation; if the 8-K is not retrievable, insert [GAP: declared special dividend per share and buyback authorisation] in slides 1, 2 and 5 and do not state $17.29B as fact. Replace the Yahoo Finance citation with the filing.
- The market cap that drives '3.5% of the $490.19B market cap' has no source in the packet and no as-of date. Using facts in hand it also does not tie: $490.19B / 443.1M shares = $1,106.27, against the $1,106.21 used on slide 4 — a 6-cent difference that implies a different share count or a different date. A committee will price-check that against the screen. **Fix:** Source the market cap and share price to a dated market data pull (same date for both), state the date and the source on slides 1 and 4, and reconcile the implied per-share price to the $1,106.21 used in the buyback maths; if they cannot be reconciled, use one share count and one price throughout and label it.
- The recommendation rests on 22.2%, and slide 4's own footnote concedes it 'is an average historical return on existing capital, not the incremental return on the next $1B of capex.' The board is not voting on the historical book; it is voting on the fiscal 2026 $6.5B. As drafted the governing thought is asserted, not demonstrated: there is no marginal ROIC or NPV on the plan under review, so the 1.2x cash cover is what actually supports the approval and the return ranking does not. **Fix:** Restate the recommendation on the coverage arithmetic that is evidenced (cash $20.21B covers the $17.29B return 1.2x, residual $2.92B; capex $6.5B covered 3.1x by cash and 2.4x by $9.38B free cash flow) and demote 22.2% to a stated ceiling with the marginal return shown as [GAP: expected after-tax return on the fiscal 2026 $6.5B capex plan] until the company provides new-warehouse unit economics.
- The page compares existing capital at 22.2% to idle cash at 3.88% and a buyback at 1.42%, but the number that decides the vote — the marginal after-tax return on the incremental $6.5B — is a [GAP]. Slide 4's own footnote concedes '22.2% is an average historical return on existing capital, not the incremental return on the next $1B of capex'. The recommendation to hold the $6.5B is therefore asserted, not supported by a number. **Fix:** Either source a marginal-return figure for the fiscal 2026 capex plan (new-warehouse unit economics: average sales per warehouse, four-wall margin, capex per opening) and populate the [GAP], or rewrite slide 4's title to 'Existing capital earns 22.2% after tax, 5.7x the 3.88% cash yield; the marginal return on the $6.5B is not yet evidenced' and convert the hold recommendation to conditional on the November 2027 test.
- None of the return/hurdle figures on the deciding page carry a cited source in the material. The only sourced inputs are F4 ($6.43B capex), F5 ($20.21B cash) and F1 (443.1M shares). No fact in hand gives diluted EPS $20.76, the $1,106.21 close (only F24's $490.19B cap and prose references), the tax rate, the Treasury yield, or the 22.2%/1.42%/3.88% returns. **Fix:** Attach a provenance ID to each: diluted EPS $20.76 and the $1,106.21 close (10-K or market data as of 2026-11-13), the $9.38B FCF and $39.71B equity-plus-long-term-debt (10-K), the tax rate and Treasury yield (defined source, dated). Replace any figure without a source with a [GAP] marker.
- The deck recommends the board 'approve the declared $30/share special dividend' but slide 1 footnote and F231 state the dividend was declared 2026-11-10, before the November 2026 board meeting; a board cannot approve what the company has already announced. The recommendation is logically dead on arrival. **Fix:** Recast the ask as a decision the board can still take: 'Ratify the declared $30.00/share special dividend (payable 2026-12-04) and set the fiscal 2026 growth budget at approximately $6.5B.' Change slide 2 title to 'The declared $30.00/share dividend is funded 1.2x from the $20.21B cash balance; hold the $6.5B growth plan' and slide 5 step 1 owner/action accordingly.
- $15.82B operating cash flow is used in slides 2, 3 and 4 ('return plus capex totals $23.79B against $20.21B cash, a $3.58B shortfall funded from the $15.82B operating cash flow'; '2.4x' cover) but no fact in the input set or the cited 10-K facts supports it. $20.21B cash less $13.29B less $4B = $2.92B ties, but the $15.82B does not appear anywhere. **Fix:** Pull FY2026 net cash provided by operating activities from the 10-K (SEC XBRL NetCashProvidedByUsedInOperatingActivities, period 2025-09-01 to 2026-08-30) and cite it as a new fact with the filing URL; if it differs from $15.82B, rebuild slides 2, 3 and 4 to the sourced figure. Do not carry $15.82B forward unsourced.
- The 3.88% cash hurdle — the single test the whole recommendation turns on ('must clear the 3.88% cash hurdle at the November 2027 review') — has no derivation, no source and no period. A committee will ask what 3.88% is: cost of capital, cash yield, or the return on the $20.21B. **Fix:** Add a line in slide 3 or 4 footnote defining the hurdle: state the input and the calculation (e.g. short-term treasury / cash yield at the stated date with the source, or weighted average cost of capital with components). If the input does not exist in the material, raise it as a research request and mark it [GAP] in the interim.
- The $15.82B operating cash flow that funds the $3.58B shortfall appears on slides 2 and 3 with no fact in the material: F5 is cash $20.21B, F4 is capex $6.43B, F3 is net income $9.23B. No CF-operating fact is sourced, and there is no citation for the $15.82B. Every downstream ratio (2.4x, the $12.66B downside trigger, the $3.58B residual) inherits the hole. **Fix:** Add a research request for Costco's net cash provided by operating activities for 2025-09-01 to 2026-08-30 (10-K, SEC XBRL NetCashProvidedByUsedInOperatingActivities) and cite it like F5; until it lands, mark $15.82B as [GAP] and label the ratios as provisional.
- F32 states 'after-tax return on capital 22.2% = operating income $11.69B x (1 - 24.7% effective tax rate) / (equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B)'. The arithmetic does not work: $11.69B x (1 - 0.247) = $8.80B; $8.80B / ($35.80B + $3.91B) = 22.2%. But the tax rate call: $3.02B / $11.69B = 25.8%, not 24.7%. At 25.8% the return is $11.69B x (1 - 0.258) / $39.71B = 21.8%, not 22.2%. Neither the numerator nor the denominator reconciles to the cited source facts (F14, F17, F15, F10). **Fix:** Recompute F32 from the sourced figures only. Either show operating income $11.69B x (1 - $3.02B/$11.69B, i.e. 25.8%) = $8.68B over invested capital $39.71B (= $35.80B + $3.91B) = 21.8%, and restate the number everywhere it appears (slides 2 and 4); or state explicitly which tax rate and which capital base produce 22.2% and footnote them. Do not carry 22.2% onto the slide until the calculation ties to the XBRL tags named.
- The special-dividend rejection compares a stock yield (1.88%, F30) with the 13-week T-bill yield (4.12%, F24) and states the cash 'already earns 4.12% in Treasuries'. But cash and cash equivalents of $20.21B (F5) are not all invested at 13-week T-bill; the 4.12% is the current bill yield, not Costco's realised yield on that balance. The whole 'skip the special dividend' conclusion rests on swapping a realised earnings yield for an unrealised market yield on a different instrument. The committee will ask this. **Fix:** State the actual yield Costco earns on its cash (interest income and average cash balances from the 10-Q/10-K) or reframe the comparison as 'cash held earns the current risk-free rate if invested at 4.12%' and flag that $20.21B is not all invested in bills. If realised interest income is not available in hand, add it as a research request and title the slide accordingly.
- The story ignores its own most recent fact. F38 declares a $30 per share special dividend on 11 Nov 2026. On 443.1M shares that is ~$13.29B — more than double the $6B the deck asks the board to approve — and per F9 Costco repurchased only $848.0M in FY26, with nothing shown against the $4B authorisation (F39). The slide titles read as a live capital allocation decision when the decisions have effectively already been taken. **Fix:** Rebuild slides 2, 4 and 5 around the actual position: state the $30 per share special dividend (~$13.29B on 443.1M shares) and the $4B buyback authorisation, size total committed returns, and deduct them from the $20.21B cash and $9.38B FCF. Then state the residual decision: how much of the ~$6.5B capex plan is funded from residual cash after the $13.29B special dividend, and how the $4B buyback is paced under the January 2030 authorisation.
- The $2B special dividend is subsumed by the announced $30 per share dividend and the $4B buyback by the announced $4B authorisation, so the board is being asked to approve something already announced or already authorised. The $6B figure is neither the current run-rate nor the incremental ask, and total FCF still does not cover $13.29B plus $4B. **Fix:** Restate the recommendation in incremental terms and show the arithmetic: total returns committed = $13.29B special dividend + $4B buyback = $17.29B; funding sources = $20.21B cash + $9.38B FCF − $6.5B capex. If a $6B ask is still intended, define it as incremental to the announced $13.29B, and show how the remaining cash and any revolver draw fund it.
- The same quantity appears as $6.5B (plan, F36 'approximately $6,500 during fiscal 2026') and $6.43B (actual, F4) with no reconciliation. '$9.38B free cash flow' (F26) = OCF $15.82B less capex $6.43B, so it is actual-to-actual. Slide 5 step 1 says 'Confirm the $6.5B fiscal 2026 capex plan', which reads as endorsing a spend already incurred, and 'cash cover of capex held above 2x' is measured against which number? At $20.21B/$6.43B cover is 3.1x (F27); against the $6.5B plan it is 3.1x too, but the deck never says the basis. **Fix:** Pick one convention and state it everywhere: '$6.43B FY2026 capex actual, $6.5B FY2026 plan (Q4 to go)'. On slide 2 lead with $6.43B actual and show FCF $9.38B as computed; on slide 5 say 'ratify the $6.5B FY2026 plan' and define the cover test as cash $20.21B / FY2027 budgeted capex, with the FY2027 budget flagged as a research request (below). Add a one-line bridge: 36-week spend $4.228B (F36) + remainder = $6.5B.
- The deck states two different expiry dates for the same authorisation. F39 says January 2030; F49 says November 2030. Both cite the 11/13 Nov Yahoo pieces. A committee member who has the press release will catch this in ten seconds, and the pacing schedule in slide 5 depends on which is right. **Fix:** Pull the 8-K (Item 8.01) filed around 11 Nov 2026 and quote one date. Until then, slide 4's F49 line and slide 5's 'January 2030 authorisation' cannot both stand; delete the loser and footnote the source.
- The governing thought calls the dividend 'already committed' and the incremental ask 'approve the $6.5B capex plan and pace the $4B buyback', but slide 5 step 3 asks the board to 'fund' the dividend and set a payment date. A special dividend of this size is declared and paid on board approval; it is not a fact until the record and payment dates are set. As planned, the deck tells the board the decision is smaller than it is. **Fix:** State the three items as separate board actions with their cash timing: (1) special dividend $30/share x 443.1M = $13.3B, payable on a record date set at the November 2026 board; (2) capex plan $6.43B–$6.5B; (3) buyback authorisation $4B through the date you confirm from the 8-K. Show the cash ladder: $20.21B cash (F5) less $13.3B dividend = $6.9B pro forma, before $9.38B FCF (F26).
- The headline return (22.2%) is computed on existing book capital — operating income $11.69B x (1 - 24.7%) / (equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B) — but the slide is titled as if it justifies the $6.5B of NEW capex. Slide 4 itself concedes 'no marginal return on the $6.5B plan is in the facts.' A board member will immediately ask: is the next $6.5B earning 22.2% or something less? If we cannot answer, the recommendation to approve the capex plan is asserted, not demonstrated. The same figures also show the buyback at 1.88% vs a 4.12% T-bill — so on its own numbers, the deck argues cash is better than buybacks AND better than the marginal project unless the marginal project returns >4.12%. That comparison is the whole decision and it is absent.
- Two dates for the same buyback authorisation: F39 says January 2030, F52 says November 2030, and the storyboard's closing slide ('$4B buyback under the authorisation running to November 2030') picks November without flagging the conflict. Slide 5 lists 'the authorisation expiry date from the 8-K' as an open item, which is correct — but then slides 2, 3 and 5 all quote a date anyway. You cannot cite a date you have not sourced. F39 is from a Yahoo Finance recap, not the 8-K.
- The $165M lost pre-tax interest is a hard-coded plug: it is not derived from F6 (interest expense $145.0M) or from any funding rate in the facts, and the math only works if $4B x 4.12% = $164.8M — that is, the T-bill yield applied to the full $4B as if drawn on day one and held for a year. But the $4B is drawn over four years out of ~$6.93B/yr of post-ordinary-dividend FCF (slide 4). Interest foregone on a drawn-down balance is not $4B x 4.12% for one year. As written, the $20.67 post-buyback EPS is a plug that manufactures the 'dilutive' conclusion.
- $20.76 / 0.0412 = $503.88 is arithmetic, but the slide calls 4.12% 'cash cost' in the title and 'opportunity cost of cash' in the row — then uses it as the hurdle in the price sensitivity. Pick one. If 4.12% is the opportunity cost of idle cash, the correct comparison for the BUYBACK is Costco's cost of equity (F23 beta 0.24 against a 5.15% 10-year, F25 — not yet used anywhere), which would put the buyback hurdle materially below 4.12% and weaken the 'does not clear anywhere in the observed range' claim. If 4.12% is the hurdle, say why a 13-week T-bill is the right discount rate for a perpetual equity retirement. A committee will ask this in the first two minutes.
- Confirm the arithmetic and label the period. $22.85/$1,106.21 = 2.066%; $25.01/$1,106.21 = 2.261% — the figures tie, but F50 is labelled 'current full-year earnings' in the fact file and research shows it is the FY2027 consensus, while F51 is FY2028. Sitting a FY2027 number next to a trailing 1.88% (FY2026 EPS $20.76) without labelling the year mixes periods and a committee will catch it. Both are TickerReport consensus, not company guidance; state that on the page. **Fix:** Relabel as 'FY2027E EPS $22.85 → 2.07% (consensus, TickerReport 13 Oct 2026; F50)' and 'FY2028E EPS $25.01 → 2.26% (F51)', and add 'consensus estimates, not company guidance' to the footnote. Keep the trailing 1.88% on FY2026 EPS $20.76/$1,106.21 as the headline number.
- The two anchor numbers of the whole deck — the $30/share dividend (about $13.3B) and the $4B buyback authorisation — rest on a secondary aggregator. Slide 3 already half-admits this: the expiry is cited as 'January 2030' in F39 and 'November 2030' in F49/F52, an unresolved contradiction. You cannot put 'about $13.3B' in front of a board on a Yahoo article. **Fix:** Pull the 8-K announcing the special dividend and the repurchase authorisation from EDGAR (CIK 0000909832). Replace F38/F39/F52 with the 8-K exhibit and its filing date. Resolve the expiry to one date and cite the 8-K paragraph; until that lands, keep the 'open item' footnote already drafted on slide 3 and flag the same on slide 5.
- The headline comparison is 22.2% (after-tax ROIC) against 1.88% (earnings yield), but those are not comparable returns. 22.2% is a return on the capital already invested in warehouses; 1.88% is the earnings yield on a new dollar spent buying back stock at $1,106.21. A buyback does not 'earn' 1.88% — it retires shares at a P/E of 53.3x. The correct comparison is the earnings yield 1.88% against the after-tax cost of the cash (4.12% T-bill x (1-24.7%) = 3.10%, or the 5.15% 10-year x (1-24.7%) = 3.88%) plus the 22.2% marginal ROIC on reinvested capital. As written, the deck asserts a conclusion the math does not support. **Fix:** Rewrite the governing thought and slide 4 title to compare the $4B buyback's 1.88% earnings yield against the 3.10% after-tax cash yield it forgoes and the 22.2% after-tax ROIC on the marginal warehouse dollar. State the buyback hurdle explicitly: 'A buyback only creates value if the earnings yield exceeds the after-tax cost of the cash; at $1,106.21 the earnings yield is 1.88% versus 3.10% after-tax on a 4.12% T-bill, so the surplus — not the core — is what we return.'
- F32 says after-tax ROIC is 22.2% = operating income $11.69B x (1 - 24.7%) / (equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B). Two problems: (a) 24.7% is not sourced anywhere in the storyboard — it is the effective tax rate implied by $3.02B / $12.22B pre-tax, but that derivation is not shown and the number is hard-coded; (b) the denominator omits operating lease liabilities (F11, $2.61B), which is material for a company with Costco's lease profile. Adding leases drops ROIC to roughly 20.6%. A committee will catch both. **Fix:** On slide 4, show the ROIC build in a footnote table: operating income $11.69B, effective tax rate 24.7% = income tax expense $3.02B / pre-tax income $12.22B (both F17 and the implied pre-tax figure from F2 net income), NOPAT $8.80B, invested capital $39.71B = equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B + operating lease liabilities $2.61B, ROIC 22.2% (or 20.6% including leases — pick one and label it). Do not leave 24.7% as an unsourced plug.
- There is no slide testing what happens if the key assumptions are wrong. The recommendation rests on two numbers — the 22.2% ROIC on warehouses and the 1.88% earnings yield — and a board will ask: what if the marginal warehouse ROIC is 15% instead of 22.2%? What if the share price is $950 or $1,250 instead of $1,106.21? What if capex runs to $7.5B instead of $6.5B? None of this is planned, and the deck has no room for it because slide 5 is a next-steps page. **Fix:** Add a sensitivity strip to slide 3 (waterfall_bridge) or slide 4: a 3x3 table of buyback value creation (dollars of value created or destroyed) across share price $950 / $1,106.21 / $1,250 and after-tax cost of cash 3.10% / 3.88% / 4.12%. Use only the F21 $1,106.21, F24 4.12%, F25 5.15%, F30 1.88%, F32 22.2% already in the material. Label the base case cell.
- The governing thought asserts the buyback 'is below the 3.10% after-tax cash yield it forgoes' — that is a reason NOT to buy back — yet the recommendation is 'Approve a $4B buyback'. Slide 4 states 'net -$0.12' EPS. A buyback that destroys EPS and earns 1.88% against a 3.10% opportunity cost cannot be recommended without an explicit, stated rationale (e.g., no incremental warehouse IRR above the hurdle for the marginal $4B, excess capital has no better use). The recommendation does not follow from the numbers as presented. **Fix:** Decide and state the logic on slide 2: either (a) 'Fund the full $6.5B plan; the remaining $4B has no project above the 3.10% cash hurdle, so return it — accepting -$0.12 EPS in exchange for removing the low-return capital', or (b) recommend returning less / none and hold cash at 3.10%. Whichever is chosen, the slide 2 title and the closing ask must match it.
- Unit mismatch: F29 scaling rule is 'every $1B... retires 0.90M shares' and F31 is 'diluted EPS after a $1B buyback $20.80'. Slide 2 states 'buyback retires 3.6M shares... and lifts gross EPS +$0.16' — +$0.16 is the 4x of the +$0.04 implied by F31 ($20.80 − $20.76). The +$0.16 and the 3.6M both assume 4x the stated $1B unit, but the slide never says the EPS uplift is the 4x of a $1B figure; a committee reading F31 (a $1B, +$0.04 number) against '+$0.16' will call it a plug. **Fix:** State on slide 2: '$4B buyback = 4 x the F29 $1B unit: retires 3.4M shares (4 x 0.90M... recompute at 4x) and lifts gross EPS +$0.16 (4 x +$0.04)'. Note 4 x 0.90M = 3.6M shares, not 3.4M; verify the 3.6M against 443.1M × (1 − 442.2/443.1) = 0.90M per $1B — the arithmetic is internally consistent, but the slide omits the 4x.
- Contradiction. Slide 5 says 'the buyback is accretive on this test only below about $1,150 where the yield crosses the 3.10% cost' — but 'accretive' means the earnings yield exceeds what the cash earns; that crosses at $669 ($20.76 / 3.10%), not $1,150. $20.76 / $1,150 = 1.81%, which is below 3.10%, not above. Slide 4's own return chart (growth 22.2%, buyback 1.88%, cash 3.10%) says the opposite of Slide 5's parenthetical. A committee member who multiplies $20.76 by 55x and gets 3.10% kills the slide in the room. **Fix:** Delete 'accretive' from Slide 5 and replace with one sentence in the same words as Slide 4: 'At $1,106.21 the buyback returns 1.88% versus 3.10% on cash — a $48.9M annual give-up; the gap closes only if the price falls below $669.00 where the earnings yield equals the 3.10% cash rate, or if the cash rate falls below 1.88%.' If the intent was a $1,150 ceiling, say it as 'the board has chosen to cap execution at $1,150 regardless of the arithmetic', not as an accretion test.
- The 3.10% hurdle is asserted, defined nowhere. The material supports exactly one cost of capital: a 4.12% 13-week T-bill after tax (4.12% x (1-24.7%)). The deck never states whether 3.10% is Costco's after-tax WACC, an after-tax cash rate, or a board policy floor, and never shows the derivation. Slide 3 then proves the $6.5B capex plan earns 22.2% yet Slide 2/heading says 'no project clears' the hurdle — the reader cannot tell whether you mean no project has been identified in the remaining $4B, or that the whole plan fails, which 22.2% contradicts. **Fix:** Add a labelled definition box to Slide 2: 'Hurdle = 3.10% after-tax cash rate (4.12% 13-week T-bill x (1 - 24.7% effective tax rate)).' Then rewrite the Slide 2/Slide 4 sentences to 'no project in the remaining $4B clears 3.10%; the funded $6.5B plan clears it at 22.2%'. If the board uses a formal WACC, state it as a separate labelled line so the two rates are not conflated.
- No comps, no precedents, no DCF. Every multiple is self-referential (own earnings yield, own ROIC). A board asked to authorise $4B will ask (a) what peers trade at and how peers return cash (Walmart, Target, BJ's, Dollar General; Sam's Club dividend and buyback cadence), and (b) whether there is any recent precedent of a large-cap retailer/special-dividend capital return. None is planned. The recommendation does not follow from the numbers alone — a board cannot price a buyback without knowing where the stock trades versus its own history and its peers. **Fix:** Add a 1x1 research request (see research_requests). Use two of the four content columns on Slide 4 or a compact table on Slide 2 to show peers' P/E, FCF yield, buyback yield and dividend yield next to COST's 1.88% / 0.53%. If a DCF is added, it must be a short sensitivity table on terminal growth and WACC, not a point estimate.
- 'the gap closes only if the price falls below $669.00 where the earnings yield equals the 3.10% cash rate' — this figure is not computed from any disclosed formula and cannot be verified. At $669.00, earnings yield is $20.76/$669.00 = 3.10%, which ties to 3.10% after-tax cash. But the yield must be compared to the after-tax cash yield, and the crossover on the 4.01% 10-year is never given. The board will ask 'what price clears the 4.01% hurdle?' and the storyboard is silent. **Fix:** Add the 10-year crossover: at $20.76 / 4.01% = $517.70. State both: 'the buyback clears the 3.10% cash hurdle below $669.00 and the 4.01% 10-year hurdle below $517.70.' Then state the buyback fails both at $1,106.21. If the board sets a $1,150 cap, it is a price cap, not an accretion test — say so.
- '22.2% FY26 actual after-tax return on capital' is built on F32 but the denominators mix sources: equity $35.80B (F15) + long-term debt $3.91B (F10) = $39.71B, and the tax rate 24.7% is not sourced on the slide. F17 gives tax expense $3.02B, F14 gives operating income $11.69B and F3 gives net income $9.23B; the effective rate implied by F17/F14 is 25.8%, not 24.7%. The 24.7% plug is unsourced and the 22.2% does not tie. **Fix:** Recompute the effective tax rate from F17/F14: $3.02B/$11.69B = 25.8%. Rebuild ROIC: NOPAT = $11.69B x (1 - 25.8%) = $8.68B; invested capital $39.71B; ROIC = 21.9%. Update all slides (2, 3, 4) to 21.9%, or source 24.7% to a specific filing line and show the arithmetic. Do not carry two different ROIC numbers for the same measure.
- The recommendation is stale on arrival. F40 says the $30 special dividend was 'declared' on 2026-11-11 and F41 says the $4B buyback was 'reauthorized' with an expiration of January 2030. A board briefing dated November 2026 cannot 'recommend' approving a dividend that has already been declared and a buyback that has already been reauthorized — the decision is behind us. Every action title ('Approve the $13.3B...', 'Fund the $13.3B...') is answering a question that no longer exists. **Fix:** Reframe the decision to what is still open as of November 2026: (a) confirm the funding plan for the already-declared $13.3B dividend, (b) set the pace of execution of the $4B reauthorized buyback given its 1.9% earnings yield, and (c) confirm the $6.5B FY2026 capex plan under the $6.43B FY2025 run-rate. Rewrite the governing thought and all five action titles to 'confirm/pace/hold' rather than 'approve/fund'. If the intent is genuinely to recommend the declaration pre-announcement, the deck must be re-dated ahead of the 2026-11-11 declaration and the source F40 restated as a proposal, not a declaration — the current date makes the current framing unrecoverable.
- The two load-bearing facts — the $30 special dividend ($13.3B) and the $4B buyback reauthorization — are sourced only to finance.yahoo.com ('Costco (COST) Declares $30 Special Dividend And Reauthorizes $4 Billion Buyback, 2026-11-11'). Yahoo is a secondary aggregator; for a board document the primary sources are the company's 8-K and press release, which exist if the declaration is real. Shipping a $13.3B capital action sourced to a Yahoo headline is exactly what a committee will challenge. **Fix:** Source F40/F41 to the Costco 8-K (item 8.01 / 2.03) and the company press release, with the accession number and filing date. If the 8-K cannot be located for 2026-11-11, the fact is not verifiable and the deck must not present it as declared.
- The buyback math ignores the cash cost of the buyback itself. F31 shows EPS rising to $20.80 'before lost interest on the cash', but the $4B buyback is funded by drawing down $20.21B cash yielding ~4.12% (F24) — the lost interest is ~$165M pre-tax, ~$124M after tax at 24.7%, which is ~$0.28/share. Against a buyback EPS accretion of only $0.04 (F31), the correct conclusion is that the buyback is dilutive to EPS once the cash cost is charged. The deck's central argument (buyback = weakest use of cash) is right, but the numbers shown make it look slightly accretive rather than destructive, which understates the case and will be caught. **Fix:** Add to slide 4 the after-interest adjusted EPS: $20.80 − $0.28 = $20.52, versus $20.76 pre-buyback, i.e. −$0.24/share. State that the $4B buyback is EPS-dilutive after funding cost and therefore should be paced, not front-loaded. Use only F13, F21, F24, F30, F31 and the 24.7% tax rate already in F32.
- The $1.15B in F8 is dividends paid for the period 2025-09-01 to 2026-05-10, a nine-month figure, while F12 gives dividends declared per share of $5.54 for the full year 2025-09-01 to 2026-08-30. At 443.1M shares (F1) the full-year ordinary dividend is roughly $2.46B, not $1.15B. The slide folds a nine-month number into a full-year bridge and then uses it to assert returns exceed FCF 'in year one'. The direction holds ($15.4B of returns against $9.38B of FCF) but the closing cash of $12.30B and the 'draw on the cash balance' line are struck on a mismatched period. **Fix:** Rebuild the bridge on one period basis: cash $20.21B + OCF $15.82B − capex $6.43B − special dividend $13.3B − buyback $4B − ordinary dividend $2.46B (= $5.54 × 443.1M) = $6.84B closing cash, not $12.30B. Restate the takeaway as '$15.76B of returns against $9.38B of free cash flow, a $6.38B draw on the cash balance'. If the board wants to show the nine-month figure, label it 'dividends paid 9M to 2026-05-10' and keep it out of the full-year bridge.
- Option (A)'s $12.30B closing cash ties to the slide 3 bridge, which carries the same period error as above, so the size of the recommended plan is understated by roughly $5.5B (the ordinary dividend and the special dividend are not the same thing). Option (C) 'retain $30.4B' has no arithmetic anywhere in the storyboard: $20.21B cash (F5) plus $9.38B FCF (F26) is $29.59B, and nothing produces $30.4B. The two options a board member will compare are both built on figures that do not tie to a source. **Fix:** Set (A) closing cash to $6.84B (per the corrected bridge) and recompute it on a single period basis. Either show (C) as $29.59B = $20.21B + $9.38B and footnote the convention, or state the $30.4B build explicitly; do not show a number the deck cannot re-derive.
- The +$1.05 EPS figure has no arithmetic on the page and does not reconcile to the deck's own rates. $30.4B at 4.12% for one year is $1.25B pre-tax; on 443.1M shares that is $2.82 per share pre-tax. At the deck's 24.7% effective tax rate (F32) it is $2.13 after tax, or $1.89 if you use the $29.59B build. Nothing in the material produces $1.05. A committee will ask where $1.05 came from and the page cannot answer. **Fix:** Either show the calculation on the page and make it tie, or cut the number. If retained at the stated build, present it as '$29.59B at 4.12% = $1.22B pre-tax / $0.92B after tax at 24.7% = +$2.08 EPS' and label it as gross interest income, not incremental EPS, since it ignores any lost operating return.
- The governing thought says 'Confirm the declared $13.3B special dividend from the $20.21B cash balance,' and slide 2 sizes a 'front-load' option with 'closing cash $6.84B'. But a declared special dividend is a legal obligation, not an option to be modelled for a board that must 'confirm' it. The deck treats a done fact as a decision variable, and no slide states the actual payment date or whether it is payable before FY2026 capex spend. Slide 3's bridge ('less the $13.3B special dividend less the $4B buyback less the $2.46B ordinary dividend') assumes all outflows in one fiscal year on one opening cash balance of $20.21B. **Fix:** Make slide 1's third open item 'set the funding and payment timing of the declared dividend' rather than 'confirm dividend funding.' On slide 2, replace the A/B/C buyback framing with the single decision the board actually has — the $4B buyback pace — and state the $13.3B as a fixed, already-declared outflow. On slide 3, add the payment date to the $13.3B bar label and, if the dividend and the $4B buyback and the $2.46B ordinary dividend do not all fall in the same 12 months, split the bridge by period instead of one waterfall.
- The headline figure '$13.3B' is nowhere sourced. The material gives a $30 per share special dividend (F40) and 443.1M shares (F1); 443.1M x $30 = $13.293B, but no cell in the storyboard shows that multiplication or that the $13.3B ties to $30 x 443.1M. **Fix:** Add a cell 'Special dividend $13.3B = $30.00 (F40) x 443.1M shares (F1)' and cite it on slides 1, 2, 3 and 5. State whether 443.1M is the right share count on the record date (it is the 2026-09-29 count, F1) or whether shares outstanding at record date will differ.
- The cost of equity of 'about 5.4%' is built as '4.12% risk-free plus beta 0.24 times a 5.15% equity risk premium.' This mixes an ERP of 5.15% (the 10-year Treasury yield, F25) with a risk-free of 4.12% (the 13-week T-bill, F24) — the 10-year Treasury is the standard risk-free rate in CAPM, and here the same number is used twice under two different labels. 4.12% + 0.24 x (5.15% - 4.12%) = 4.37%, not 5.4%; 4.12% + 0.24 x 5.15% = 5.36% only if the 5.15% is the ERP, which the storyboard never says it is. The deck's own arithmetic works only on one reading and is inconsistent with the sources it cites. **Fix:** Rewrite the valuation leg with labelled inputs: risk-free = US 10-year Treasury 5.15% (F25); ERP = [sourced]; beta = 0.24 (F23). Then cost of equity = 5.15% + 0.24 x ERP. If the ERP is 5.15% (F25 reused as ERP), say so explicitly and rename F25 in the cell; if it is not, research the ERP. Show the resulting number to two decimals and tie it to the 'about 5.4%' on the governing thought.
- The valuation leg cites '22.4x LTM EPS ($1,106.21 / $20.76)' — that is consistent with F21 and F13 — but the storyboard also carries F34 ($309B LTM revenue, $15B EBITDA, multiples.vc 2025-10-26) and F51 (EPS $20.82, also 2025-10-26) in the source pack. These are stale (FY2025, not FY2026), and F51's $20.82 conflicts with F13's $20.76 diluted EPS for the period 2025-09-01 to 2026-08-30. If the exhibit or any slide uses the multiples.vc figures, the deck shows two different EPS and two different LTM revenues on the same board page. **Fix:** Strip F34, F35, F45, F46, F51 from the deck. Use only FY2026 10-K figures: revenue $303.15B (F2), diluted EPS $20.76 (F13), operating income $11.69B (F14), net income $9.23B (F3). If a trading multiple is needed, build it from F21 / F13 and label it '22.4x FY2026 diluted EPS (F21/F13).'
- The bridge mixes a nine-month FY2026A ordinary dividend paid of $1.15B (period 2025-09-01 to 2026-05-10, F8) with a full-year FY2026A operating cash flow of $15.82B and capex of $6.43B (2025-09-01 to 2026-08-30, F7/F4), and then subtracts FY2027E items (the declared $13.3B special dividend and the $1B paced buyback). The arithmetic $20.21 + 15.82 - 6.43 - 1.15 - 13.3 - 1.0 = $14.15B, not the stated $9.84B; the gap is $4.31B and would not survive a committee reading the page. Mixing nine months of dividend with twelve months of cash flow inside one bridge means the 'closing cash' bar is unsupportable. **Fix:** Move to a clean cash roll-forward on one basis. EITHER (a) show FY2026A ending cash $20.21B, then a full-year ordinary dividend at the run-rate of $5.54 x 443.1M = $2.46B (state the period once: 'FY2026 declared rate annualised; FY2026A full-year dividend paid not separately disclosed'), then FY2027E special dividend $13.3B and FY2027E buyback $1.0B, closing cash $9.84B (reconciles with 20.21 + 15.82 - 6.43 - 2.46 - 13.3 - 1.0 = $12.84B only if FY2027E operating cash flow is added); OR (b) leave the bridge as pure one-year and add a clearly labelled 'FY2027E' bar row with FY2027E operating cash flow. Put the reconciliation math on the page: starting cash $20.21B, plus/minus the operating cash flow for the same period, less capex for the same period, less dividends for the same period, less special and buyback, equals $9.84B. The ordinary dividend run-rate $2.46B is currently a footnote, not an in-line subtraction, and the stated $9.84B cannot be derived from what is shown.
- Slide 2's deciding number applies a $1B-buyback EPS and interest calculation (4 cents / 7 cents / -3 cents) to a $4B front-load option (slide 2 option A: 'incremental buyback effect $4B, closing cash $6.84B, EPS -$0.24') as if the per-$1B economics scaled linearly, while slide 4 shows the $4B program 'across four years, not a single year'. A $4B buyback executed in four equal tranches of $1B each year at $1,106.21 share price would not have the same annual EPS impact as a front-loaded $4B in year one: shares retired in year 1 earn the full-year EPS benefit and the funding cost is on the full $4B for the full year, while tranches in years 2-4 leave cash sitting on the balance sheet in year 1. Option A's headline '-$0.24 EPS' in year 1 is $0.06 x 4, which silently assumes the shares are all retired at the start of year 1 and the cash is all spent on day one, but does not say so. **Fix:** On slide 2, keep the option columns on a per-year basis and state the timing explicitly: 'Option A: $4.0B retired in year 1, EPS impact in year 1 (full-year effect) -$0.24; Option B: $1.0B retired each year, EPS impact in year 1 -$0.06, $3.0B undrawn'. On slide 4, replace the four-year $4B row with a table showing each $1B tranche in its year of execution and the EPS effect only in the year it is retired, or explicitly foot the number: 'illustrative; assumes each $1B tranche retired at the start of the FY and held for the full year; actual annual EPS impact depends on execution timing and share price at purchase.' If the $0.24 in slide 2 and the '$4B across four years' row on slide 4 are intended to say different things, reconcile them on the page; if they are intended to say the same thing, one of them is wrong.
- The recommendation to hold the $6.5B FY2026 capex plan rests entirely on the after-tax return on capital of 22.2% (F32) clearing the cost of equity of 6.11%-6.59% (slide 4, 5.15% 10-year + 0.24 beta x 4.0-6.0% ERP). Slide 3 has a sensitivity on closing cash to a 10% capex overshoot ($7.15B) but no sensitivity on the returns side: what does ROCE look like if incremental capex earns anything less than the 22.2% historical average? A committee will ask this in the first minute and the answer is not on the page. The 22.2% is a stock, backward-looking ROCE, not the marginal return on new warehouses. **Fix:** Add a small grid on slide 4 (or slide 3): after-tax return on incremental capex at 3 scenarios, e.g. 22.2% (historic average, F32), 15.0%, 10.0%, and the resulting ROCE versus the 6.11%-6.59% hurdle and versus the 1.88% buyback yield. State on the slide: 'Hold-capex case fails if incremental ROCE falls below 6.59% (the top of the cost-of-equity range)'. If you cannot source an incremental return, request it (see research requests) and show the breakeven: at what incremental ROCE on the FY2027 capex does the buyback become the better use of $1B? The answer is roughly the 6.6% hurdle, but show the number.
- Net income is not the funding source. Capex is funded from operating cash flow, and cash at 2026-08-30 ($20.21B) is a point-in-time balance, not a fiscal-year flow. Comparing a balance sheet stock to a P&L flow and to a cash flow item mixes periods and bases; $9.23B less $6.43B = $2.80B is an accrual residual, not surplus cash, and the deck itself concedes 'operating cash flow is not in the facts [GAP]'. If the true OCF is, say, $11B, the surplus is nearer $4.6B, not $2.8B — the special dividend is sized off the wrong number. **Fix:** Rewrite the executive summary point (1) and slide 3's bullet as: 'Fiscal 2026 capex of $6.43B equals X% of operating cash flow of $Y.YYB [research]'; delete 'about 70% of earnings' and 'about $2.8B' until the operating cash flow figure is sourced from the same FY2026 10-K.
- The recommendation is 'return only surplus cash as a one-off special dividend sized after the board confirms a cash buffer' — but every input needed to size the dividend (free cash flow, minimum cash buffer, existing dividend commitment, debt maturity wall, D&A and maintenance capex within the $6.43B growth figure) is unsourced. Slide 4's 'special dividend returns surplus cash and keeps more flexibility' is asserted, not evidenced.
