A board cannot vote on a number the deck does not contain. **Fix:** Add a sourcing column to slide 5: for each of operating cash flow, dividends paid, total debt and maturities, D&A, maintenance vs growth capex, cash and equivalents plus short-term investments, name the 10-K line item and page. Do not declare a special dividend recommendation until the FY2026 statement of cash flows is in hand.
- The $30 special dividend and the $4B buyback authorization — the two facts the entire recommendation rests on — are sourced only to 'finance.yahoo.com, Costco (COST) Declares $30 Special Dividend And Reauthorizes $4 Billion Buyback, 2026-11-11.' A board cannot approve a $13.3B payment on a secondary aggregator headline; we need the company's own 8-K/press release with declaration date, record date, payment date, and the buyback authorization terms. **Fix:** Replace the Yahoo source on F37/F38 with the primary filing: Costco's 8-K (Item 8.01/2.03) or the company press release announcing the special dividend and repurchase authorization. Cite accession number and URL. State the declaration, record, and payment dates and the exact authorization amount and expiry in the slide footnote.
- The funding math mixes periods. F26/F4/F7 are fiscal 2026 (2025-09-01 to 2026-08-30): OCF $15.82B, capex $6.43B, FCF $9.38B. But F35 says FY2026 *intention* of '$6,500' (i.e., $6.5B) of which only $4,228M spent in the first 36 weeks — so the 'keep the $6.5B capex plan' and the '$9.4B FCF' are on different capex bases ($6.43B actual vs $6.5B plan). Combined with a $13.3B dividend against $20.21B cash, the deck must show the pro-forma cash bridge, not just assert coverage. **Fix:** Add a one-line funding bridge on Slide 2 or 3: cash $20.21B + FCF $9.38B − special dividend $13.3B = ~$16.3B pro-forma cash (before buybacks and ordinary dividends). Label every figure with its period; footnote the $6.5B as the FY2027/next-year capex intention distinct from the FY2026 $6.43B actual, and show both.
- There is no sensitivity, no downside scenario, and no answer to the obvious challenge: if COST's earnings yield is 1.88% and warehouse ROIC is 22.2%, why does a buyback exist at all, and what happens if the stock falls to the 52-week low $850 (F22) or the $4B is fully deployed? The 'cap buybacks at $4B' recommendation is asserted, not shown to be the constrained-optimal use. **Fix:** Rework Slide 4 from a static ranked table into 'Use of $1B of capital' with a sensitivity band: warehouse ROIC 22.2% (F32) vs buyback earnings yield 1.88% (F30) vs T-bill 4.12% (F24) vs 10-year 5.15% (F25), and add a break-even line: at what buyback price does EPS accretion exceed the 4.12% cash return? Use F29/F31 ($1B retires 0.90M shares; +$0.04 EPS). Show the pro-forma cash and leverage after the $13.3B dividend and at full $4B buyback.
- The comparison is not like-for-like and is weighted heavily in favour of the recommendation. 22.2% is a trailing after-tax return on operating income over a book capital denominator of equity $35.80B plus long-term debt $3.91B; $35.63B of that denominator is net PP&E at historical cost (F16) and excludes operating lease liabilities of $2.61B (F11). The other three rows are current market returns on cash. A board member will say the 22.2% includes returns on warehouses bought decades ago, the marginal new warehouse does not earn 22.2%, and the correct comparison is the return on new capex versus the 1.88% buyback. Nothing in the storyboard answers that. **Fix:** Retitle slide 4 to state the method honestly: 'Existing base earns 22.2% after tax on book capital; new capex is underwritten against a buyback at 1.88%'. Add the forward test to the slide: the $6.5B fiscal 2026 capex plan implies roughly $1.65B of incremental operating income at the existing 25.4% after-tax margin ($6.5B x 25.4%, from F32), which is the number that has to clear the 1.88% buyback hurdle — and flag it as the open item to be underwritten by the finance team. If it cannot be evidenced, do not make the recommendation rest on it.
- Two problems. First, the break-even prices ignore tax on the foregone interest: the after-tax cash alternative is 4.12% x (1 - 24.7%) = 3.10%, so the break-even is roughly $670, not $504, and the delivered number overstates the case by about 25%. Second, the $1,000 threshold has no derivation anywhere — $20.76 / $1,000 = 2.08%, so the 2.1% in the decision line is wrong, and no argument is given for why the hurdle is 2.1% rather than the 3.10% after-tax cash yield. As written the recommendation asserts a number the model does not produce. **Fix:** Restate the sensitivity on an after-tax basis: buyback break-even against the after-tax 13-week T-bill yield is EPS $20.76 / 3.10% = about $670 per share, against the after-tax 10-year yield $20.76 / 3.88% = about $535. Then anchor the threshold: either set the buyback price discipline at the 3.10% after-tax cash alternative, EPS $20.76 / 3.10% = $670 per share, or state plainly that the board is accepting a lower return than cash to retire stock at $1,000 and say why. Fix the arithmetic in the decision line — at $1,000 the earnings yield is 2.08%, not 2.1%.
- The 3.10% after-tax cash yield is the single load-bearing number for the entire recommendation — it sets the $670 floor — and it appears with no derivation. Fact F24 gives the 4.12% pre-tax 13-week T-bill yield; Fact F32 uses a 24.7% effective tax rate; but nothing in the material states '3.10% = 4.12% x (1 - 24.7%)'. As drafted, 3.10% is a hard-coded plug and the board will ask for it before they ask anything else. **Fix:** On Slide 4, replace the callout with: 'Set the buyback floor at $670 per share, where the earnings yield equals the 3.10% after-tax cash alternative (3.10% = 4.12% US 13-week T-bill, F24, x (1 - 24.7% effective tax rate, F32)).' Add the arithmetic as a footnote to the exhibit. If a different tax rate is intended for the cash alternative, source it and restate.
- The deck treats the $30 special dividend as declared and the board's decision as one of funding ('Confirm funding for the declared $30 special dividend (~$13.3B)'), while the brief asks whether Costco should 'return more cash to shareholders (buybacks or a special dividend) or keep funding warehouse growth'. The C-suite brief governs over the brief.md question, but the storyboard does not reconcile the two: a board member reading the cover against the question will ask whether the dividend is a done deal or still open. **Fix:** On Slide 1, add one line under the subtitle: 'The $30 per share special dividend was declared on 11 November 2026 (F37); this briefing addresses funding, the capex plan, and the $4B buyback authorization, not whether to declare.' Then align Slide 2 metric (1) to say 'declared $13.3B special dividend' rather than implying a fresh decision.
- The entire buyback discipline rests on $20.76, which is fiscal 2026 diluted EPS from the 10-K. The vote happens in November 2026, but the decision prices a fiscal 2027 earnings stream. It is a valuation base mismatch, and the board will not miss it. **Fix:** Add a research request for consensus FY2027 diluted EPS and restate the crossing as EPS_FY27 / 3.10% on Slide 4, with the $20.76-based $670 shown only as the fiscal 2026 reference. State explicitly: 'The $670 floor prices fiscal 2027 EPS, not the fiscal 2026 10-K figure; we refresh it before execution.' If no FY2027 EPS is obtainable, cap the recommendation at a price/NTM-EPS framework and say so.
- The '$6.5B capex plan holds' argument and the 22.2% ROC are the two pillars of the recommendation, but the 26-opening figure and the revision from 28 are sourced to app.ultralayer.ai, an unofficial transcript aggregator. A board cannot sign a capex plan against a secondary source. **Fix:** Source the transcript to the official Costco investor-relations webcast/transcript on investors.costco.com, or confirm the 26 net openings against the 10-K/10-Q warehouse count. If neither is available, change the reference to 'approximately 26 net new openings (management guidance)' and footnote the source limitation on the slide.
- The single load-bearing fact of the entire deck — the $30/share special dividend (F38) and the $4B buyback reauthorization (F39) — is sourced only to 'finance.yahoo.com, ... 2026-11-11'. Every downstream number ($13.29B outflow, $6.92B pro forma cash, $2.92B post-payout, $9.03/share) inherits this. For a board document that is the definition of an unsourced assumption. **Fix:** Re-source F38/F39 to Costco's own 8-K press release and the declaration/record/payment dates on the company IR page and in the SEC filing (EDGAR CIK 0000909832). Cite the 8-K accession and date on slides 1, 2, 3 and 5. If the declared amount or timing differs, rebuild the $13.29B and $6.92B.
- The governing thought asserts 'up to $4B' with no basis for the quantum. Nothing in the facts ties $4B to capex, to the FY2026 plan (~$6.5B per F36), to the backlog of 13 remaining warehouse openings (F37), or to any project pipeline. A committee will ask 'why $4B and not $6B or $2B' and the deck has no answer. It reads as a plug sized to match the coincidentally-equal $4B buyback authorization in F39. **Fix:** Either (a) tie the reserve to evidence: FY2026 capex intention ~$6.5B (F36), 36-week spend $4,228 (F36), 13 remaining openings (F37), or (b) relabel it explicitly as an illustrative placeholder equal to the authorized buyback and say in the box 'reserve quantum to be set by Finance after the costed project pipeline is delivered.' Do not present $4B as if it were derived.
- The entire deal premise — the $30/share special dividend and the $4B reauthorization — is sourced only to finance.yahoo.com (F38: 'declared a special cash dividend of $30 per share'; F39: 'reauthorized a $4b share repurchase program'; F47: 'payable December 4, 2026'), with a Google News aggregation URL (F48) as corroboration. No primary source, no 8-K, no press release text, no ex-date/pay-date cross-check. This is the number the whole deck hangs on. **Fix:** Pull the issuer's 8-K/press release from SEC EDGAR (CIK 909832) and cite the accession number and exhibit for the declaration, the per-share amount, the record and payment dates and the authorization size; if EDGAR does not yet carry it, label every instance 'unverified — press report' and hold board distribution. Strip the Google News URL as a citation on any slide.
- $30 x 443.1M = $13.29B, not $6.92B. As titled and framed, the ending bar does not tie to the inputs on the slide: starting cash $20.21B (F5) less a $13.29B declared outflow is $6.92B, so the $6.92B is the post-dividend balance, not the dividend. A hostile committee reads that title as the cash cost of the dividend and it is wrong by ~$6.4B. **Fix:** Retitle to state both legs explicitly: 'Starting cash $20.21B less the $13.29B implied dividend ($30 x 443.1M shares) leaves an illustrative $6.92B before all intervening flows.' Label the $13.29B bar as the outflow and the $6.92B bar as the ending balance; keep the 'not a forecast of the actual payment' caveat.
- The governing thought says defer and size the reserve 'only after liquidity and project returns are verified.' Slide 4 already highlights 'holding cash pending review' and shows the $4B 'as incremental to the reported $13.29B dividend' — i.e., it recommends $4B before the gates that slides 1-3 say are unverified. The number is doing the recommending and the caveat is doing the hedging. **Fix:** Make every $4B instance read 'illustrative comparison, not a proposed amount' in the same sentence, not a footnote, and change slide 4's highlight to a decision rule: 'Hold capital until Finance delivers (a) the costed incremental warehouse pipeline and (b) the minimum operating cash floor; then size the return.' Delete any chart, bar or score that implies $4B is the answer.
- The bridge opens on cash and cash equivalents $20.21B at 2026-08-30 (F5) — a balance-sheet date two months and a full quarter before today — and closes on an 'illustrative ending balance' that ignores the intervening $15.82B operating cash flow (F7), the $6.43B capex (F4), the $848M repurchases (F9) and the $1.15B dividends already paid (F8). Labelling it 'illustrative' does not fix it: it will be read as a post-dividend cash position, and it is not one. Worse, the $13.29B payable is larger than 65% of the $20.21B cash balance, so the chart's central message ('we can afford to hold') visually contradicts the numbers it is built from. **Fix:** Rebuild slide 2 as a labelled sources-and-uses on a stated as-of date, with every line tied: opening cash $20.21B at 2026-08-30 (F5) + reported FY26 operating cash flow $15.82B (F7) − reported capex $6.43B (F4) − reported dividends paid $1.15B (F8) − reported repurchases $848M (F9) − illustrative $13.29B special dividend (F38/F1) = stated ending balance. Show the $13.29B as a memo line, not as the endpoint, and label the ending balance 'illustrative; excludes Q1 FY27 flows and any buyback'. If the $30 special dividend is confirmed, reconcile the $13.29B to the actual declared amount and payment date before it goes to the board.
- The deck's operative fact — a $30 special dividend, a $4B buyback reauthorization, a 2026-11-24 record date and a 2026-12-04 payment date — rests on one secondary aggregator ('finance.yahoo.com, Costco (COST) Declares $30 Special Dividend...') and a Google News topic page (F48). A board briefing cannot recommend a hold on a distribution whose existence, size, record date and payment date are unconfirmed. The $4B 'reauthorization' is doubly weak: the number itself is not sourced to the company, and the deck never says what the existing authorization was or how much of it remained. **Fix:** Before the board sees this: pull the company's own 8-K announcing the special dividend and the buyback authorization from EDGAR (CIK 0000909832), which will give the declared per-share amount, the aggregate, the record date, the payment date and the authorization size. Replace every F38/F39/F47 reference with the 8-K URL and exhibit number. If no 8-K exists by the time of the meeting, the cover stamp ('Unverified press reports, hold distribution pending primary-source confirmation') is doing exactly the right job — keep it and keep the deck on hold.
- The recommendation is asserted, not derived. Nothing in the storyboard connects 'prioritize growth' to a number: the after-tax return on capital 22.2% (F32) is a historical, company-wide figure and the deck itself says on slide 4 that 'historical company return on capital is not a marginal warehouse return.' The marginal return on the next warehouse is never estimated, so the comparison that would justify directing capital to growth — marginal warehouse return versus the 1.88% earnings yield on a buyback (F30) — is missing. A committee will ask 'at what marginal return?' and the storyboard has no answer. **Fix:** Add to slide 4 an explicit marginal-return test: state that the company does not disclose a marginal warehouse return, and present the decision rule instead — fund growth while expected marginal returns exceed the 1.88% earnings yield (F30) net of the 4.12% short-rate opportunity cost (F24); the mechanism is already laid out as −1.22pp (F30 minus F24 × (1 − 24.7%)). Then state what would change the answer: if the board's estimate of marginal warehouse returns falls below the after-tax cost of capital, the hold-and-test rule reverses. That is the comparison that makes the recommendation follow from the numbers rather than precede them.
- The deck asserts a $0 incremental return policy while carrying a reported $30 special dividend (443.1M shares × $30 = $13.293B) plus a $4B buyback reauthorization. The word "additional" does all the work and is never defined, so the recommendation reads as inconsistent with the transaction the board is actually being asked to approve. A committee will ask 'so are we approving $0 or $17.3B?' and the storyboard has no answer. **Fix:** On slide 2, restate the recommendation as: 'Approve no incremental return beyond the reported $30 special dividend (~$13.3B; 443.1M shares × $30) and $4B buyback reauthorization, subject to verification.' Add the reconciliation sentence: reported $30 × 443.1M shares = $13.3B; $13.3B + $4.0B = $17.3B; the $0 refers only to the incremental amount above these reported actions.
- The $30 dividend and $4B buyback rest on aggregators (finance.yahoo.com, Simply Wall St., capitaldigest.com). F51 itself flags unresolved terms (January vs. November 2030; payment timing), and F47 gives a payment date of December 4, 2026 while F50 says payment timing is unspecified. That is a contradiction inside the evidence set, not merely a sourcing gap. **Fix:** Before distribution, obtain the primary source (Costco press release or 8-K) and reconcile: dividend amount, record date November 24, 2026, payment date December 4, 2026, and buyback authorization amount/expiry. Tag every slide figure to the primary filing, not the aggregator. If unresolved, hold distribution as slide 5 already provides.
- The two numbers the entire recommendation hinges on are sourced to supplychain360.io ('Costco plans to add approximately 30 net new warehouses annually for the next five to 10 years, but expects capital expenditure growth to slow after fiscal 2027' and 'Costco plans 33 warehouse openings in fiscal 2027, including five relocations'). F41, the $30/share special dividend, is quoted with the source field truncated ('Source:') so the reader cannot see where $13.3B comes from at all. A board will ask 'says who' on both, and a blog is not an answer. **Fix:** Replace F38/F39 with the primary source before this slide is built: the 10-K (filed 2026-10-07) or the earnings release for the warehouse-opening plan, and the 8-K/press release for the $30/share special dividend and the $4B authorization. Show as a footnote on slides 1, 2, 3 and 5: 'Source: COST 8-K dated [date], $30.00/share special dividend, $13.3B aggregate; COST 10-K FY2026, [section] for warehouse count and capex plan.' Where the plan is only management commentary on the call, label it 'management guidance, [date] call' — not 'plans'.
- The recommendation to defer further distributions asserts one number against another without a valuation: the buyback is judged on 1.88% earnings yield (F30, $20.76/$1,106.21) versus 22.2% after-tax return on capital (F32) versus a 4.12% T-bill (F24). These are not comparable — F32 is a historical book ROIC on equity $35.80B plus debt $3.91B, not the prospective, incremental, after-tax return on the next $4B of warehouses. The slide claims the distinction ('distinguish historical company returns from prospective warehouse returns') but no slide carries a prospective number. On slide 2's own math the $4B is 0.8% of the $490.19B market cap (F28), so the choice is materially immaterial and the deck never says so. **Fix:** On slide 4, put the three options on the same basis: incremental after-tax return on the marginal $4B, not the historical 22.2% (F32). Add a small sensitivity table on slide 4: (a) $4B buyback = 3.6M shares retired (F29: 0.90M per $1B) vs 443.1M, EPS $20.76 to $20.83 (F31: $20.80 at $1B, so 4x the effect less lost interest on $4B), return = 1.88% earnings yield (F30); (b) $4B held at the 4.12% T-bill (F24) = 4.12% pre-tax; (c) $4B into warehouses at an assumed incremental return — ask management for the number and show it as a range, e.g. 'at the FY2026 ROIC of 22.2% (F32) the marginal $4B compounds at 22.2%; at half that, 11.1%; at Costco's 1.88% earnings yield it is value-neutral.' State the break-even: warehouses need only beat 1.88% pre-tax to be the better use of the $4B, which is a low bar F32 clears easily — use that as the argument, not the assertion.
- The recommendation defers an 'additional $4B distribution' that no evidence shows was ever proposed. The declared $30/share special ($13.3B) and the $4B authorization (F41) are already board-approved facts. A board recommendation must answer a live question; the deck never states what incremental $4B is on the table or why it would be tabled now. **Fix:** Recast the decision as the actual live question: whether to accelerate or size up deployment under the existing $4B authorization (F41) ahead of the FY2027 capex ramp from $6.43B (F4) to ~$7.5B (F51). Either name the proposal on the table or state explicitly that no additional distribution is being proposed and the recommendation is a monitoring stance with triggers.
- The $10.80B end balance cannot be reproduced from the figures cited on the slide. From $20.21B cash (F5) less $13.3B special (F41) plus FY2027 OCF $14.0B (F52) less capex $7.5B (F51) the balance is approximately $13.4B, not $10.80B. If annual dividends paid at $5.88/share on 443.1M shares (~$2.6B) are also deducted, the balance is approximately $10.8B — but that line item (F42) is not shown as a bridge step. A committee will start here and stop reading. **Fix:** Show every bridge step as a labelled bar with its source: opening cash $20.21B (F5), less special dividend $13.3B (F41), plus FY2027E operating cash flow $14.0B (F52), less FY2027E capex $7.5B (F51), less annual common dividend ~$2.6B (F42 x F1). Recompute the closing balance from those bars and update the slide title to match; if F52 is consensus and not company guidance, label it 'consensus estimate' on the bar.
- The central comparison is missing a number. The grid shows a 1.88% earnings yield on a buyback (F30) and no return on the $4B retained in cash. The 13-week T-bill yield of 4.12% (F24) and the 10-year yield of 5.15% (F25) are cited on Slides 4 and 5 but not deployed as the opportunity cost of holding cash, so 'holding preserves cash' overstates the case. The preferred option must win on a number, not an adjective. **Fix:** Add two columns to the grid: 'retain in cash' with an illustrative $4B x 4.12% (F24) = $165M annual interest, and 'buyback' with $4B x 1.88% (F30) = $75M of attributable earnings plus 3.6M shares retired at $1,106.21 (F29). State the trade that makes holding defensible for one year: the 4.12% cash yield versus 1.88% earnings yield plus an option on warehouse returns to be quantified by the CFO (F32).
- The load-bearing fact of the entire deck — the $30/share special dividend, ~$13.3B total, December 4, 2026 payable date, and the new $4B authorization (F41) — has a blank source field: '(Source: ' — no URL, no press release date, no filing. The same is true of F51 (CFO capex guidance) and F52 (consensus operating cash flow). I will not take a $13.3B number to a board with an empty source. **Fix:** Attach the primary document to F41: Costco's dividend declaration press release (date and URL) or the 8-K, and reconcile $30 x 443.1M = $13.29B against any board-approved figure. For F51, cite the Q4 FY2026 earnings call transcript and the exact speaker; for F52, name the consensus provider, the number of contributing analysts, and the as-of date.
- The stated logic is wrong and it undercuts the recommendation. The earnings yield on a buyback is F30 = 1.88% ($20.76 / $1,106.21). The return on the cash retained is the 13-week T-bill of 4.12% (F24), which is AFTER the special dividend is paid. So retaining the $4B earns 4.12% pretax vs 1.88% on a buyback — that is a cash-yield argument, not an 'income' argument, and the comparison is apples-to-oranges: pre-tax cash on cash vs after-tax EPS accretion. The recommendation to pause is being justified by a metric that favors the buyback on a genuine economic basis once you use the right comparison. **Fix:** Retitle to 'Holding $4B earns 4.12% on cash vs 1.88% earnings yield on a buyback' and replace the 'income' framing with a like-for-like comparison: state the after-tax cash return (4.12% × (1 − 24.7%) = 3.10%) alongside the after-tax earnings yield (1.88%). Flag that neither captures warehouse returns and that the pause is about optionality, not about income.
- The bridge does not close against the F41/F51 facts. F52 says consensus FY2027 operating cash flow is ~$14.0B (a 12% decline). F51 says FY2027 capex is guided to ~$7.5B. $14.0B − $7.5B = $6.5B free cash flow, not $10.80B. The $10.80B only makes sense if you are adding back the special dividend of $13.3B (F41) — but the dividend is payable 4 Dec 2026, i.e., inside FY2027. If the dividend is already deducted, the slide's title is wrong; if it is not, the title is wrong. Either way the number as stated does not reconcile. This is exactly the bridge a committee will re-derive in the room. **Fix:** Retype the slide title to state the bridge unambiguously: 'FY27 FCF of $6.5B ($14.0B op cash flow − $7.5B capex) covers the $13.3B special dividend with $0B to spare'. If the $10.80B is meant to be 'cash available before the dividend', show it as a line item and delete the title. Do not use $10.80B as a headline number without showing the arithmetic that gets there.
- The storyboard requires a four-case sensitivity but does not name the four cases, the range of the operating cash flow assumption, or the outcome under each. This is the single most important number in the deck — the entire 'pause' recommendation depends on whether FY27 FCF covers a $4B buyback (F41) — and it is left unspecified. F52 gives one point estimate (~$14.0B), not a range. **Fix:** Specify the four cases by name and number: e.g. Base $14.0B, Down 10% $12.6B, Down 20% $11.2B, Severe $9.0B, each crossed with capex of $7.5B (guidance, F51) to show FY27 FCF of $6.5B / $5.1B / $3.7B / $1.5B, and in each case show the $4B buyback draw and remaining cash vs cash of $20.21B (F5).
- The arithmetic does not tie. 443.1M × 0.20% = 0.886M shares, i.e., 0.89M, not 0.90M. More importantly, F29 is a RATE — 0.20% of shares retired per $1B — not a proposed amount, and it is being used as if it were the economics of the buyback. The buyback economic case is F31: EPS rises from $20.76 to $20.80 on the first $1B, i.e., +0.04, or +0.19%. The after-tax cash cost of that buyback is the lost T-bill interest: $1B × 4.12% × (1 − 24.7%) = $31.0M per year, or $0.07/share at 443.1M shares. Net of lost interest, a $1B buyback is EPS-dilutive, not accretive. This is the only buyback math that matters and it is not on the slide. **Fix:** Put F31 on the slide verbatim ($20.76 → $20.80 on the first $1B) and add the interest cost line ($31.0M pre-tax, $0.07/share) so the committee sees that a $1B buyback is EPS-negative once the 4.12% cash cost is charged. Correct 0.90M to 0.89M or restate F29 as '~0.9M shares' with the 0.20% figure shown.
- The slide cannot be built to the required standard because the source for the after-tax rate is not in the fact set: a tax rate on the retained cash is not given. F17 gives income tax expense of $3.02B, from which an effective rate can be derived (24.7% on operating income of $11.69B, F14) but that is the OPERATING effective rate, not the rate applicable to interest income on idle cash, which for a domestic corporate is the 21% federal statutory rate. Using 24.7% understates the after-tax cash return. This is a hard-coded plug waiting to happen. **Fix:** State the assumption explicitly on the slide: 'After-tax cash return = 4.12% × (1 − 21% statutory) = 3.25%' and footnote that 24.7% is the company's operating effective rate (F17/F14), not the rate on interest income. If you prefer 24.7%, say so — but say which and why.
- The deck asserts 'Pause the $4B buyback' but never quantifies the counterfactual it is overriding. It compares the buyback's 1.88% earnings yield (F30) against an after-tax cash yield of ~3.1% and a 22.2% company return on capital (F32) — but F32 is operating income / (equity + debt), a whole-company figure, not an incremental warehouse return, and the board cannot defend a $4B decision on a proxy. **Fix:** On slide 4, split the counterfactual into three columns and label the third explicitly as unavailable: (a) Hold $4B — 3.1% after tax at the 4.12% T-bill (F24); (b) Buy back $4B — 0.90M shares retired per $1B (F29), 3.6M shares at $4B, 1.88% earnings yield (F30) before lost interest; (c) Build warehouses — blank, to be filled by the gate in slide 5. Then state on slide 2 that the recommendation is a pause for evidence, not a verdict that (c) beats (b), and cite the 22.2% figure only as context with its formula shown.
- Two load-bearing numbers come from press aggregators, not primary sources. F39 (33 openings in fiscal 2027, ~30 net additions) is sourced to 'supplychain360.io' — adjacent to F38, which is the same outlet. F51 ($7.5B capex guide) is sourced to 'Costco investor relations materials and the fourth-quarter results call' with no URL or date. A committee will ask for the transcript and the filing; neither is in the pack. **Fix:** Replace F39 with the 10-K's warehouse count disclosure and the capital-expenditure discussion (CIK 0000909832, filed 2026-10-07), and replace F51 with the Q4 FY2026 earnings call transcript from Costco IR. Also fix the hard contradiction: F40 says 'approximately $6,500' for fiscal 2026 while the actual spend is $6.43B (F4); make sure slide 3 shows the actual, not the guide, for fiscal 2026.
- The title says the pile covers the $7.5B plan 3.1x, but F27 defines the ratio as cash $20.21B / capital expenditure $6.43B (the FY26 actual), not the $7.5B FY27 guidance. On the number in the title it is 2.7x. The same mismatch runs through the slide: the sources-and-uses subtracts $7.5B (F36) while the cover ratio uses $6.43B (F4). This is the single number the whole slide rests on and it does not tie. **Fix:** Recompute everywhere on one denominator and label it: FY27 guidance $7.5B. Cash cover = $20.21B / $7.5B = 2.7x on F5/F36. Change the Slide 3 title to "The $20.21B cash pile covers the $7.5B fiscal 2027 capex plan 2.7x" and change F27 to the same basis.
- The governing thought says the plan "still leaves a $5.6B trough against a $4.0B minimum," but Slide 3's schedule states a "$16.5B trough." Both cannot be right, and the two directions of the deal test are opposite: $16.5B remaining means the $4.0B return is trivially affordable; $5.6B means the return is what creates the headroom constraint. The board cannot approve a number it cannot reconcile, and the binding constraint is the second one. **Fix:** State one trough and derive it on-page from the same rows: opening $20.21B (F5) + operating cash flow $15.82B (F7) - capex $7.5B (F36) - dividends and buybacks actually paid. If the $5.6B figure is the one you want, show the full-year fiscal 2027 outflows that consume the difference (existing buyback $0.85B, dividends above the $1.15B 10-Q run-rate, plus the proposed $4.0B); if you cannot source those, drop the $5.6B claim and use the number the schedule supports. Do not carry both.
- Both are stale or wrong-period. F8, dividends paid $1.15B, is a 10-Q figure "for the period 2025-09-01 to 2026-05-10" (nine months, filed 2026-06-03), being inserted into a full-fiscal-2027 schedule next to a full-year $15.82B cash flow. F9, $848.0M, is labelled "share repurchases" for FY26 and is carried as the FY27 run-rate at the same $0.85B. Nine months of FY26 dividends are not twelve months of FY27 dividends, and the annual dividend has already stepped up ($1.47/quarter per F39 versus $1.30 prior). **Fix:** Rebuild the outflows on a consistent fiscal-2027 basis and label each period. Dividend: use $1.47/quarter x 4 x 443.1M shares = ~$2.6B/yr, sourced to F39 and F1, and say so. Buyback: carry only the $3.0B proposed, or restate the $848.0M (F9) explicitly as 'proposed $3.0B' rather than leaving a $0.85B placeholder. F8 and F9 leave the schedule.
- The $4.0B minimum operating cash is the single number that makes the $16.5B year-end balance look safe, and it is asserted as a management assumption with no source. Slide 3 tells the reader to 'confirm against the 10-K liquidity discussion' in the deck itself. A committee will read that as 'we did not check.' **Fix:** Pull the 10-K liquidity and capital resources discussion and quote the actual minimum operating cash or cash-plus-availability threshold. If the 10-K states a figure (e.g., 'we maintain cash and availability to fund operations'), cite it to the page and replace '$4.0B (management assumption)' with the sourced figure plus the page reference. If no figure exists, recast the $4.0B as a calculation: three months of operating cash outflow ($15.82B / 4 = $3.96B ≈ $4.0B) and label it 'analyst estimate, 3 months of operating cash flow, vs. no disclosed minimum.'
- The $1,200 trigger has no method behind it. At $1,200 the earnings yield is 1.73% versus 1.88% at $1,106 — that is a 15bp difference, which does not justify a hard stop. The real logic is the spread to the risk-free rate: at $1,106 the spread to T-bills is negative 224bp (1.88% - 4.12%); at $1,200 it is negative 239bp. The deck switches from a spread argument on slides 2 and 4 to a level argument on slide 5, and the two do not connect. **Fix:** Reframe the cap as a spread rule and state it on every slide the same way: 'buyback earns 1.88% vs. 4.12% on T-bills — a negative 224bp spread; cap the program at $3.0B so no dollar is repurchased below a zero spread. Pause entirely if the spread widens past negative 250bp, which occurs at approximately $1,245 ($20.76 / (0.0412 - 0.0025)).' Use $1,245 or whatever the deck's own arithmetic supports; do not keep $1,200 without showing the yield it implies.
- The sizing logic mixes units and horizons. 1.88% is a perpetual earnings yield on a share; 4.12% is F24, a 13-week T-bill yield at close on 2026-11-13. Comparing a stock's earnings yield to a three-month bill rate, with no risk premium or duration term, is not a defensible cost of capital and does not justify capping the buyback. The deck itself on slide 5 prices the company at 53.3x trailing earnings, which is itself evidence the market does not use 4.12% to discount COST's earnings. **Fix:** Restate the cap on a cash-cost basis with numbers already in the deck, not a T-bill rate: run a one-year, $1,106.21/share buyback versus holding the cash, with foregone after-tax interest at 4.12% and the EPS accretion from slide 2 (2.7M shares, EPS $20.76 to $20.90). If the cash-cost test says $3.0B is worse than holding cash, present it as a cash-cost test; if not, you have no cap and the recommendation must change. Separate the two concepts explicitly on the slide rather than fusing them.
- Recommendation does not follow from the numbers. A $3.0B buyback is proposed; $1,115M (F50) remains available under the existing authorization through January 2027; the increment that actually needs board action is $1,885M, not $4,000M. As drafted the board is asked to renew $4.0B more than it will use, and the two program figures do not tie. **Fix:** Set the renewal size to what the program needs and say so: renew approximately $2.0B (or state the exact figure equal to $3.0B less the $1,115M remaining amount) before the January 2027 expiry, and show the reconciliation on the slide: existing authorization $1,115M + new approval $1,885M = $3.0B program.
- The schedule mixes periods: it starts with cash $20.21B at 2026-08-30 (FY26 balance sheet), adds FY26 actual operating cash flow $15.82B (2025-09-01 to 2026-08-30), then subtracts FY27 guidance capex $7.5B, FY27e dividends $2.6B, and a proposed FY27 $3.0B buyback + $1.0B special dividend. You are adding a full prior-year cash inflow to a balance-sheet date that already reflects the cash generated through that date, then netting forward-year outflows against it. The arithmetic may close at $21.93B but the period logic double-counts FY26 operating cash flow inside the FY26 closing cash and then spends it on FY27 items. A client treasurer will catch this. **Fix:** Relabel every row with its period and rebuild the bridge on one basis: either (a) reconcile FY26 closing cash $20.21B forward using only FY27 items — FY27 operating cash flow (research), FY27 capex $7.5B, FY27 dividends $2.6B, $3.0B buyback, $1.0B special — and state the FY27 operating cash flow assumption and its source; or (b) present the FY26 actual bridge as a historical sources-and-uses (cash at 2025-09-01 + $15.82B opex cash flow − $6.43B capex FY26 − dividends paid) and show the FY27 plan as a separate forward schedule starting from $20.21B. Do not put FY26 actual operating cash flow and FY27 uses in the same column without a reconciling opening-balance line.
- The 'leaving ~$8.6B cash and 3.1x capex cover' does not tie to the facts. F5 cash $20.21B less $6B return = $14.21B; less $7.5B FY2027 capex = $6.71B, not $8.6B. 3.1x is F27 = cash $20.21B / capex $6.43B (FY2026 capex), but the slide pairs it with $7.5B FY2027 capex ($20.21B/$7.5B = 2.7x, and the post-return ratio is 0.9x). Either the $8.6B is a post-FCF figure or the 3.1x is on the wrong denominator; both are stated as if they follow from $6B returned. **Fix:** Put a one-line pro forma cash walk on slide 2: 'Cash 2026-08-30 $20.21B − $6.0B return = $14.21B; + FY2027 OCF (state assumption) − $7.5B capex = $X'. Quote the cover ratio on a labelled denominator: 3.1x on FY2026 capex $6.43B (F27) or 2.7x on FY2027 capex $7.5B. Do not show both unlabelled.
- The slide compares a 1.88% buyback earnings yield (F30) and a 4.12% T-bill (F24) against a '22.2% after-tax return on capital' (F32) that is not marginal. F32 is operating income $11.69B × (1−24.7%) / (equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B), the return on the existing capital base, not the return on the next $1B of warehouse capex. It omits operating lease liabilities $2.61B (F11) from the capital base and carries no sales/EBIT forecast for the 33 planned 2027 openings (F37). A committee will tear the 22.2% apart on sight. **Fix:** Relabel F32 as 'return on existing invested capital (equity + LT debt only)' and add the invested-capital definition in a footnote including operating leases (F11). Add a marginal-return line: expected steady-state sales and after-tax operating margin per new warehouse from F37's 33 openings, sourced from the 10-K. If marginal data cannot be sourced, drop the 22.2% row and let the argument rest on capex cover, not on a return comparison.
- The dividend row shows '$2.26/share' while the board recommendation on slide 2 is a $2B special dividend at $4.51/share. $2.26 is the $1B row, $4.51 is the $2B recommendation. The slide will be read as contradicting the executive summary. **Fix:** Show the $1B row as $2.26/share and add the recommended $2B column as $4.51/share, or note '$2.26 per $1B'. Keep F29 and the slide 2 $4.51/share consistent.
- EPS accretion is shown at $20.80 (F31) with no financing cost. The $1B buyback would consume cash earning 4.12% (F24) or the 5.15% 10-year (F25); on ~$1B that is ~$41M pre-tax, ~$31M after tax at the 24.7% rate used in F32 ($3.02B/$?? — the 24.7% is itself unsourced). The 1.88% earnings yield is a return metric, but the table is an EPS comparison; without the lost interest the $20.80 is overstated by roughly $0.07. **Fix:** Add a financing line to the buyback row: lost after-tax interest at 4.12% (F24) and the resulting EPS accretion, or label the $20.80 explicitly as 'before lost interest'. Source the effective tax rate (F17 income tax $3.02B / pre-tax income from F14 and F6) or show it as a single sourced figure.
- 'Hold buybacks above $1,150 a share' and 'pause if cash falls below $8B' are asserted thresholds with no derivation. $1,150 is only 4.0% above the $1,106.21 close (F21); no value, comp or historical multiple supports either number. The $8B floor does not tie to the cash walk in slide 2 and is probably an artifact of the unsupported $8.6B. **Fix:** Derive the thresholds: state the price at which the earnings yield falls below the T-bill (breakeven price = EPS $20.76 / 4.12% = $504, so the $1,150 cap needs a different logic — e.g. a stated multiple of the 52-week range high $1,106.21, F22) and the minimum cash floor as the lower of 12 months' capex or a stated months-of-capex rule. Replace 'above $1,150' with a formula or a sourced multiple, and tie the cash floor to the pro forma walk.
- The governing thought endorses a $4B buyback at a 1.88% earnings yield when the same book shows the 13-week T-bill at 4.12% (F24) and the 10-year at 5.15% (F25). Slide 2 asserts the return; it does not state the give-up, nor the rate at which the $20.21B is currently invested. A committee will ask 'why buy back stock at 1.88% when cash earns 4.12%' before it reads slide 3. **Fix:** On slide 2, add a labelled line: 'Cash earns 4.12% (13-week T-bill, 13 Nov 2026) vs 1.88% earnings yield on buyback; post-tax spread -X bps.' Compute X from the two rates already in the material and the 24.7% effective tax rate, and state plainly that the buyback is EPS-accretive (F31: $20.76 to $20.80 per $1B) but returns less than holding the cash. Slide 4's buyback row already carries the 4.12% comparison; slide 2 must not omit it.
- 22.2% (F32) is operating income $11.69B x (1 - tax) over average invested capital, an entire-company ROIC on stock built over decades. It is being used to justify the FY2027 marginal $7.5B capex step-up. The two numbers do not measure the same thing, and slide 3's 'on the $7.5B plan FY2026 OCF $15.82B implies $8.32B of FCF' is a restatement of FCF, not a return on the increment. **Fix:** Relabel the capex row and slide 4's title to 'Return on existing invested capital 22.2% (F32), not the marginal return on the FY2027 $7.5B step-up.' If the marginal return cannot be sourced, say so on the slide and drop the implied comparison to the 1.88% buyback yield, which is a marginal number. If the argument is that maintenance capex is covered and the surplus is discretionary, make that the argument in words, not a 22.2% figure.
- Two tax rates in one book. Slide 3 footnote states 'income tax $3.02B / (operating income $11.69B less interest expense $145.0M) = 26.2%'. Slide 4's F32 formula uses '24.7% effective tax rate' with no derivation shown. Same company, same fiscal year, two rates; the client's tax director will catch it in the room. **Fix:** Pick one rate and show the build. Either recompute F32 with 26.2% (F17/(F14-F6)) and restate the 22.2% accordingly, or derive 24.7% explicitly from net income $9.23B (F3) / pre-tax income, cite F52's $83M and $72M discrete benefits as the bridge from the statutory rate, and put that derivation on slide 4 next to the 22.2%. Do not carry both.
- The two numbers the decision turns on are the worst-sourced items in the deck. The $7.5B FY2027 capex comes only from F36, financialfilings.com re-typing 'spend approximately $7,500 du ring fiscal 2027' from an annual report; no cost structure, no SI&O or pilot cost for the step-up, no post-2027 capex path. The 22.2% return comes from F32, an undefined invested-capital denominator. (Note: F32 as written is arithmetically impossible: $11.69B x (1 - 24.7%) = $8.802B; $8.802B / ($35.80B + $3.91B = $39.71B) = 22.2%. The '24.7%' keyed into F32 is a rounding of the 26.2% effective rate derived in F52's bridge, so the same workbook uses two tax rates for the same return. That is exactly the kind of internal inconsistency a committee catches.) A board is being asked to commit $7.5B against a re-typed press number and a historical average that the slide itself admits is not the marginal return. **Fix:** Replace F36 with the primary source: Costco FY2026 10-K, Item 7 MD&A capital expenditure section, and the 4Q26 earnings release/call (Sept-Oct 2026). Quote the $7.5B (and the 'up to 33 new warehouses' from F37) with its page reference. If primary does not confirm $7.5B, rebuild the slide-3 walk on the confirmed number. On slide 4, either source the actual FY2027 capex economics (warehouse count x build cost x ramp) or relabel the row unambiguously as a historical company average and state that no marginal return is sourced, not buried in a note. Footnote F32's 24.7% <-> F17 26.2% discrepancy and use one rate everywhere.
- There is no share price before the 2026-11-13 Yahoo close (F21). We are deciding on a $4B buyback and a $2B special dividend priced off one day's close, at the top of a 52-week range ($850.00-$1,106.21, F22), with no 30-day VWAP, no average price of the existing program, and no sensitivity to COST trading 10-20% lower at execution. At $1,106.21, $4B retires 3.62M shares (0.8% of 443.1M); at $850, 4.71M shares (1.1%). The recommendation does not move, but the share count and EPS accretion do, and the board will ask. **Fix:** Add a 30-day and 90-day VWAP and the year-to-date range to F21, sourced from a market data terminal, and put one line on slide 4 or 5: at the 52-week low of $850.00, $4B retires 4.71M shares, not 3.62M. State the reference price used for the $4.51/share dividend is the 2026-11-13 close and footnote that the record-date price will differ.
- Unsupported figures inside the two tables. Slide 3's opening cash and OCF sensitivities are explicitly assumed, but the table has no haircut to the $7.5B capex and no working capital/seasonality note, even though Costco's cash swings materially intra-year. Slide 4 keys 24.7% from F52 while the bridge elsewhere is 26.2% (F17 $3.02B / ($11.69B - $145.0M)). F52's $83M and $72M discrete benefits are benchmarked only against the statutory rate, which is asserted, not sourced. The numbers do not tie to one rate. **Fix:** Slide 4: derive the tax rate once, at 26.2% as F17/$11.69B-$145.0M, use it in F32, and footnote F52 explicitly: R&D credit $83M and stock comp $72M, and the statutory rate and source. Slide 3: add a capex overrun column (+10%, capex $8.25B) and a note that working capital and seasonality are excluded; recompute closing cash and cover on each column, and label all ratios x.
- The walk ignores dividends. F12 shows dividends declared of $5.54/share in FY2026, and F8 shows $1.15B dividends paid through 2026-05-10 (a 10-Q stub). At 443.1M shares, the annualised dividend at $1.47/share is roughly $2.6B. The model adds a full year of OCF but subtracts no regular dividend, which overstates closing cash by about $2.6B. A board member will catch it in ten seconds. **Fix:** Add a 'regular dividends' line to the walk: opening cash $20.21B + OCF $15.82B - capex $7.5B - regular dividends ~$2.6B (443.1M x $1.47 x 4) - return $6B = closing cash ~$19.9B. If you keep $22.53B, state explicitly that the walk excludes the regular dividend and label it a gross-of-dividend walk. Do not carry a $22.53B cover headline while omitting a known cash use.
- Every operating number on slides 2-5 (the cash walk, the 2.1x OCF cover, the 2.7x cash cover, the sensitivity table, the $8.25B stack test, the closing) rests on the $7.5B FY2027 capex figure. That figure's only source is financialfilings.com, a secondary aggregator. If it is wrong or if it is a gross number from a different period, the entire recommendation resizes. This is the single most load-bearing unsourced number in the deck and it is not on the SEC audit trail. **Fix:** Pull the FY2027 capex statement from the primary 10-K or the FY2026 Q4 earnings release/transcript on sec.gov or the company IR page. If the primary source gives a range or a different figure, rebuild the walk and the sensitivity table on the confirmed number and re-label every ratio.
- 'Assumed flat' is an unsourced plug, and it is not conservative: it omits any working-capital build (the brief says so) and any interest income on the cash being returned. More importantly, Costco's FY2027 OCF will be affected by the F49 tariff-refund gross-margin tailwind and by the F48/F50 price reinvestment, both sourced from Yahoo Finance, but neither is reflected. The sensitivity table then treats the plug as a base and calls the -10% case a downside, which it is not — the downside is already inside the flat assumption. **Fix:** Either (a) source an FY2027 OCF estimate from consensus or the company's own guidance, or (b) label the $15.82B as an unsourced flat assumption and add a working-capital sensitivity row: 0 / -$1B / -$2B working-capital build, showing closing cash $22.53B / $21.53B / $20.53B on the base. Add the F49/F48/F50 gross-margin and reinvestment notes to slide 3 as the operating evidence behind any OCF path.
- The comparison is incomplete. The exhibit sets 22.2% ROIC (F32) against the 13-week T-bill 4.12% (F24) and a 1.88% earnings yield (F30), but never states a cost of capital. In a $490.19B market-cap company with beta 0.24 (F23) and a 5.15% 10-year (F25), the 13-week bill is the wrong hurdle and a committee will say so. The word 'before' in the title asserts a ranking the slide does not yet prove. **Fix:** State the cost of capital explicitly: because beta is 0.24 (F23), use a debt-like cost of equity; show WACC as the 4.12% 13-week T-bill (F24) as the risk-free proxy, or disclose that if COST's cost of equity is equity-risk-premium times 0.24 beta plus 4.12%, it lands under 6%, which is still materially below 22.2% (F32). Add '22.2% vs. a cost of capital under 6%' as the slide's comparison line and change the title to 'Growth earns 22.2%, buybacks earn 1.88%: fund growth first'.
- The buyback pace of $1.0B a year and the $13.3B dividend are stated as a decision but not derived. The only scaling facts are F29 ($1B retires 0.90M shares at $1,106.21, 0.20% of 443.1M) and F31 (EPS rises from $20.76 to $20.80 after a $1B buyback, before lost interest). Nothing in the storyboard says why $1.0B a year rather than the full $4B authorization (F42) or zero. The number is a plug. **Fix:** On slide 2, show the test behind the pace: at $1,106.21 (F21), $4B retires 3.6M shares and adds $0.17 to EPS (from $20.76 to $20.93, using F29 and F31); at $1.0B it adds $0.04. Justify $1.0B as the amount that keeps the buyback's cash return (1.88% earnings yield, F30) above the 4.12% lost on cash (F24) only if funded from surplus cash, and state the condition: if the share price falls below the level where the earnings yield exceeds the T-bill yield, accelerate toward $4B; if it rises, stop. Put that condition on slide 5.
- The trough of $6.9B and the $4.9B minimum are not supported by any fact on the slide. F5 gives cash $20.21B at 2026-08-30; F7 gives operating cash flow $15.82B; F4 gives capex $6.43B; F41 gives the $30 dividend; F42 gives the $4B repurchase; F12 gives dividends declared per share $5.54. No source or arithmetic produces $6.9B or $4.9B, and the Q1 2027 timing is asserted. **Fix:** Build the bridge from the numbered facts: start cash $20.21B (F5), add operating cash flow $15.82B (F7), subtract capital expenditure $6.43B (F4), subtract the regular dividend. For the special dividend, use $30 x 443.1M = $13.293B (F41, F1); for the buyback use the stated $1.0B pace. Show the trough and its date. Source the minimum operating cash or label it an assumption with the basis: cash cover of capex is 3.1x (F27); state whether the minimum is one quarter of capex, a weeks-of-cash figure, or the $4.9B you intend.
- The governing thought asserts growth earns 22.2% on capital and the buyback earns 1.88%, but 22.2% is a current-period, balance-sheet-based ROIC on total capital (F32), not the return on incremental capital. The deck's own evidence contradicts the marginal argument: revenue has compounded at 7.8% (F18) while capex has compounded at 14.2% (F19), so incremental capex is running roughly two-for-one against incremental revenue. A committee will ask what the last $1.0B of capex earns, and the storyboard has no number for it. **Fix:** On slide 4, relabel the 22.2% as 'after-tax return on total capital' and add a third bar for return on incremental capital: compute it as the change in after-tax operating income over the change in invested capital using F14, F17, F15 and F10 across the three-year period ending 2026-08-30 (the 2023 comparatives are cited in F18, F19, F20), or, if that cannot be computed from the facts in hand, drop the 'fund growth first' conclusion to 'growth earns a positive spread over the 4.12% cost of cash; incremental returns require confirmation'.
- The math in the governing thought and in F29 does not work. F29 says 'every $1B spent on a buyback retires 0.90M shares (0.20% of 443.1M) at $1,106.21'; 0.90M / 443.1M = 0.20%, correct, but the phrasing '$1B ... retires 0.90M shares (0.20% of 443.1M)' plus the EPS math on F31 implies a 20% accretion. Check: $1.0B / $1,106.21 = 0.90M shares, 0.20% of the base. At $20.76 EPS, retiring 0.90M of 443.1M shares lifts EPS to $20.76 x 443.1/442.2 = $20.80 (F31), i.e. +0.19%. That is the number the slide must show, not 0.20% accretion. **Fix:** Rewrite F29 in the deck and slide 1 to read 'every $1.0B of buyback at $1,106.21 retires 0.90M shares, 0.20% of 443.1M shares outstanding, lifting diluted EPS to $20.80 from $20.76 (+0.2%)'; delete any percentage that reads as accretion of the EPS.
- The slide title asserts a trough above a stated minimum operating cash, but the storyboard never states the minimum and never dates the schedule. F42 gives the buyback authorization ($4B, expiring January 2030); the board call is a pace (governing thought: $1.0B a year). Uses implied: $13.3B dividend (443.1M x $30) plus $1.0B a year of buybacks plus capex at F4 $6.43B a year, against starting cash F5 $20.21B and F26 FCF $9.38B. Without a minimum, the slide is an assertion. **Fix:** Add a stated minimum operating cash to slide 3, e.g. 'minimum = 1.0x next-twelve-month capex of $6.43B plus seasonal working capital', and show the year-end trough against it: starting cash $20.21B (F5) less $13.3B dividend (F41 x F1) less $1.0B buyback pace, plus $9.38B FCF (F26) less $6.43B capex (F4), with the trough figure and the date. If the trough goes below the minimum, the buyback pace must be cut; state that.
- The deck lists no risks and no sensitivity anywhere, and no source other than SEC XBRL and Yahoo Finance for the core recommendation. Missing, and a committee will ask: (1) how much of the $4.0B authorization (F42) is already used or how much of the $848.0M repurchased in FY2026 (F9) counts against it; (2) what happens if the F25 10-year yield of 5.15% or the F24 13-week bill yield of 4.12% move against the 1.88% earnings yield; (3) the dilutive/antidilutive crossover, i.e. the price at which the earnings yield equals the cash yield (F30 1.88% vs F24 4.12%), which is roughly $494; (4) how the F52 52.0x P/E and F53 32.4x EV/EBITDA (multiples.vc, dated 2025-10-26) relate to the decision, since a buyback above intrinsic value destroys value. **Fix:** Replace slide 4's 'success' exhibit with two panels: (a) the return comparison of the 22.2% total return (F32) against the 4.12% cash yield (F24) and the 1.88% earnings yield (F30), and (b) a simple sensitivity table showing the earnings yield at three share prices ($900, $1,106.21 per F21, $1,300) against the 4.12% cash yield, with the crossover price stated. Add a risks bullet list on slide 4: buyback authorization usage, rate move, and the multiple (F52/F53).
- The recommendation compares the 1.88% earnings yield to the 4.12% 13-week T-bill but never nets the lost interest on the cash spent on the buyback, and F31 says so explicitly ('before lost interest on the cash'). At $1.0B/yr, the lost T-bill interest is ~$41M against the ~$18M of EPS accretion from retiring 0.90M shares, so the buyback is EPS-dilutive, not accretive, on the deck's own numbers. The analysis as planned supports the opposite of the recommendation. **Fix:** On slide 2 and slide 3, restate F31 as a net EPS effect: EPS accretion from retiring 0.90M shares is ~$0.04 (= $20.76 x 443.1/442.2 - $20.76), against lost interest on $1.0B at 4.12% of ~$41M, or ~$0.09/share, for a net ~-$0.05/share. The buyback case must be argued on capital-return grounds, not accretion, or the recommendation changes to 'pause the buyback entirely until the earnings yield exceeds the cash yield.'
- The deck compares a 1.88% earnings yield to a 4.12% cash yield, but simultaneously carries F32 (after-tax return on capital 22.2%) and F52 (P/E 52.0x). A 52x P/E and a 22.2% ROIC are inconsistent with a 1.88% earnings yield being the right hurdle for growth capex; the marginal return on the growth program is the number the board needs and it is not in the deck. As drafted, slide 3 answers the wrong question. **Fix:** Replace F52 with a marginal-return figure: compute the after-tax return on incremental capital deployed, e.g., incremental operating income per incremental dollar of net PP&E using F14/F16 and the prior-year 10-K, or state the comparable. If the marginal ROIC is not available in the materials, add a research request; do not put a stale P/E from a 2025-10-26 source next to a 2026-11-13 price.
- The $6.4B minimum operating cash has no source in the materials; the slide shows F5 $20.21B, F7 $15.82B, F4 $6.43B, F8 $1.15B, F9 $848.0M, F41 $30/share, but nothing that establishes why $6.4B is the floor. The $9.9B trough also cannot be reproduced from the listed inputs without a period assumption: $20.21B - $13.3B - $1.15B - $0.848B - $6.5B + $15.82B is not $9.9B in any single period. A committee will ask, and there is no cell to point to. **Fix:** Put the trough bridge on the slide as a dated, tie-out schedule: beginning cash $20.21B at 2026-08-30, less special dividend $13.3B (= $30 x 443.1M), less regular dividends at the F43 run-rate ($1.47/qtr x 443.1M x 4), less capex $6.5B (F40), less buyback $1.0B, plus operating cash flow $15.82B (F7), equals trough. Label every period. Source the $6.4B minimum to the company's stated minimum operating cash or change the hurdle to a stated, sourced figure.
- Dates contradict. Slide 2 title says 'Fund the declared $13.29B dividend' (already declared), but the governing thought says 'the board's open decisions are to fund it.' A declared dividend payable 4 Dec 2026 to holders of record 24 Nov 2026 is a legal obligation, not an open decision; F54/F56 fix the record and payment dates. Presenting funding as a decision the board takes on 16 Nov is wrong as a matter of corporate action and invites the exact question 'isn't it already declared?' at the table. **Fix:** Reframe Slide 2 as 'Funding the already-declared $13.29B dividend from the $20.21B cash balance, and setting the ongoing $4B authorization pace' and Slide 5 as 'Confirm funding sources for the 4 Dec payment and approve the buyback pace and capex envelope'; do not put 'ratify the dividend' as an ask.
- The buyback pace premise does not tie. The governing thought says 'pace the $4B authorization at fiscal 2026's own $848M a year rather than a round $1.0B,' but F9 ($848.0M fiscal 2026 repurchases) is past spend, not an authorization pace, and the $4B authorization runs to January 2030 (F42) — at $848M/yr that is ~4.7 years, ~$4.0B, which happens to fit, but at $1.0B/yr it is 4.0 years, also fits. Nothing in the material explains why $1.0B is wrong or why $848M is the right glide path; it is a hard-coded plug dressed as policy. **Fix:** Either (a) state the binding constraint explicitly — e.g., authorization term to Jan 2030 (F42) implies no more than ~$4B/3.2 years ≈ $1.25B/yr to consume it, so $848M/yr would leave capacity unused — or (b) drop the '$848M vs $1.0B' framing and replace it with 'fund the authorization at a pace the board sets; the earnings-yield test below applies to any pace.' Do not present $848M as 'the pace' without the arithmetic.
- $13.29B is asserted in the title and strip but the source facts give two different figures: F55 says '$13.3 billion' (approximately) and F56 says 'about $13.3 billion'; the deck's $13.29B is a recomputation (443.1M x $30.00) that is not shown and is presented to two decimals when the underlying inputs are one-decimal and 'approximately.' A committee member will ask 'where does 13.29 come from and why is it not 13.3?' **Fix:** Show the arithmetic on the slide: '$30.00 x 443.1M shares (F1) = $13.29B; company release states approximately $13.3B (F55/F56).' If the strip must be one figure, use $13.3B to match the source and footnote the 443.1M x $30.00 calc.
- Internal contradiction that a committee will catch: 3.6M shares equals 0.8% of the 443.1M outstanding, but slide 2 also cites F29, 'every $1B spent on a buyback retires 0.90M shares (0.20% of 443.1M)', which implies 0.8% of shares for the full $4B (or 0.2% per $1B year). The '$4B = 3.6M shares' phrase conflates the authorization with shares actually retired and should be struck or restated. **Fix:** Replace the phrase with: '$4B authorization; at $1B/yr and $1,106, every $1B retires ~0.90M shares (0.20% of 443.1M); full $4B at today's price is ~3.6M shares if drawn.' Do not show 3.6M shares next to the authorization without the pace and timing.
- The bridge is self-contradictory: opening cash $20.21B + operating cash flow $15.82B − capex $6.43B − dividends paid $1.15B − buybacks $848M − the special dividend $13.3B = $14.15B, with no minimum operating cash identified. Slide 2 claims the dividend is funded from 'cash $20.21B + FCF $9.38B less capex $6.43B' but never states the trough or the floor; the numbers only work because of the unstated operating cash the business needs. **Fix:** On slide 3, state the ending cash line: '$20.21B + $15.82B − $6.43B − $1.15B − $0.848B − $13.3B = $14.15B' and add a defined minimum operating cash floor with its basis, showing $14.15B above it. If the $4B buyback is also drawn in the period, show the lower trough and say so.
- The cost of equity is doing all the work and is effectively unsourced: F31 is '6.4% = 10-year Treasury 5.15% (F25) + beta 0.24 x 5.0% equity risk premium (the premium is an assumption ...)'. Beta 0.24 (2 years of weekly returns) and a 5.0% ERP yield 6.4%; the ERP is an assumption with no source or range. The 14.5% implied return also hinges on a single three-year operating-cash-flow growth rate of 12.6%. **Fix:** Show the cost-of-equity build with the ERP sourced or ranged (e.g. Damodaran implied ERP, Kroll recommended range) and show cost of equity at beta 0.24 and at beta 0.8–1.0. Show the buyback implied return at three-year OCF growth of 5%, 8%, 12.6% and state the price below which it stops clearing 6.4%.
- The 14.5% implied return is inconsistent with holding capex at $6.43B: 3-year OCF growth of 12.6% was achieved with capex growing 14.2% a year (F19), and F38 says Costco expects capex growth to slow only after fiscal 2027, with F39 citing 33 openings in fiscal 2027. Buybacks fund from operating cash flow, not free cash flow after growth capex, so the growth assumption needs a capex line. **Fix:** Add a sensitivity that shows OCF growth as capex rises: operating cash flow $15.82B, capex $6.43B to, say, $8.0–$9.0B, and the resulting FCF and buyback capacity. Either reduce the buyback pace in years when capex rises, or state that growth capex is funded from cash on hand.
- The '$12B operating cash floor' is a board-set policy number the briefing invents; there is no company-stated minimum, and the deck admits this in a footnote. A board cannot be asked to 'set the operating cash floor at $12B' when the number is not derived from Costco's own liquidity policy, debt covenants, or working-capital seasonality. Slide 3 then draws a dashed line at $12B and claims a '$2.15B cushion' against an undefined floor. This is an unsourced plug dressed as policy. **Fix:** Either (a) derive the floor: state minimum operating cash = e.g. 3 weeks of cost of goods / peak seasonal working-capital build, and show the number in the liquid assets section; or (b) if it must remain a board-set number, label it 'proposed $12B floor — board-set policy, no company-stated minimum; benchmarked to [three named peers' minimum cash]' and add the peer benchmarks as a data point. Do not present $12B as if it were a found fact. Until sourced, downgrade the claim from 'set' to 'propose'.
- Two different market caps are in play: F28 gives $490.19B (=$1,106.21 × 443.1M) and F48 (the 11 Nov article) says 'about $472.4b'. The storyboard tells the designer to 'Do not show the $472.4B' but never reconciles the $17.8B difference (which is the special dividend itself — $13.3B — plus the price move between 11 and 13 Nov). A director who saw the 11 Nov article will notice the gap and ask. **Fix:** Add one footnote to Slide 1: 'Market cap $490.19B at 13 Nov 2026 close = $1,106.21 × 443.1M. The 11 Nov article's $472.4B reflects the pre-announcement price; the $17.8B difference is price appreciation plus the $13.3B dividend, not a change in share count.' Then use only F28 throughout, as the storyboard already instructs.
- The 14.5% implied return is built on a 12.6% operating-cash-flow growth rate that is a three-year historical CAGR (F20, $11.07B FY23 → $15.82B FY26). Your own footnote (F50) says consensus expects operating cash flow to FALL ~12% to ~$14.0B next year. Slide 4 says 'which would put the implied return near the cost of equity' but never shows the number. In the bear case, implied return = 1.88% + (-12%) = roughly -10%, not 'near' the cost of equity. The recommendation to 'draw the $4B buyback at $1B a year' is therefore not supported by the briefing's own consensus data, and the committee will ask. This is the single largest hole in the deck. **Fix:** On Slide 4, add a third bar: 'Consensus case: operating cash flow -12% to $14.0B, implied return ≈ -10%; buyback does not clear the 6.4% cost of equity in the near term.' Then anchor the recommendation on a longer horizon: 'Buyback pace is justified only if three-year operating cash flow growth resumes above 4.6%; if consensus holds, the buyback fails its own test and the $1B should be redeployed to the dividend or held.' If the recommendation is to draw regardless, state the reason — e.g. share-count retirement at a 22.2% return on capital — and stop calling it a 14.5% return.
- The headline says 'Cash trough of $12.99B ... a $0.99B cushion over the proposed floor' and the body says at $1B/yr the trough is 'about $11.99B, just below the floor', but the headline presents the cushion without the buyback draw. The slide as titled therefore overstates the cushion the recommendation actually produces. The footnote (consensus OCF ~$14.0B, trough ~$11.19B) is below the floor too, so under the deck's own consensus case the recommendation breaches the proposed $12B floor before any buyback. **Fix:** Rewrite the slide 3 headline to state both states: 'Cash trough of $12.99B after the special dividend and capex, $11.99B after a $1B buyback draw — $0.99B above, $0.01B below the proposed $12B floor; consensus OCF (~$14.0B) takes it to ~$11.19B.' Then make the first line of the takeaway the draw-pause rule, not the dividend-paid-from-cash point.
- The break-even price is described as 'a research request, not yet computed, and the treasurer will present it at the next board.' A board approving a buyback pace at $1,106 is being asked to approve the draw without the price at which the buyback stops creating value. The slide even instructs 'buy back below $1,106', which is a direction, not a break-even. **Fix:** Compute the break-even price before the deck goes out, using the numbers already in the material: break-even = the price at which the implied return equals the cost of equity. With the 9.2%-10.2% committee cost of equity and the 12.6% growth case, the buyback clears at $1,106 by 1.4x; show the price (as computed) at which 14.5% falls to 9.2% and at which it falls to 10.2%. If the treasurer cannot supply it in time, change the callout to state the break-even as a gating condition of the authorization: 'authorization drawn on a pace basis only at or below the computed break-even price; treasurer to present the break-even at the Q1 2027 board before the first tranche.'
- The $848M of FY26 repurchases (F9, PaymentsForRepurchaseOfCommonStock) is already deducted inside the FY26 ending cash of $20.21B at 2026-08-30. Subtracting it again in the bridge double-counts it and understates the trough by $848M. The tie-out is internally consistent only because the plug was added twice. **Fix:** Drop the -$0.85B line from the bridge. Recompute ending cash as $20.21B + $15.82B - $6.43B - $2.46B - $13.30B = $13.84B, restate the trough as $13.84B (not $12.99B), the cushion vs the $12B floor as $1.84B, the post-$1B-draw trough as $12.84B ($0.84B above the floor), and change slide 3's title and slide 2's bold $12.99B/$14.15B references accordingly. If the intent was to show a forward-year use of cash, label it FY27 pro forma, date it, and state that FY26 ending cash is the opening balance.
- Two problems. (a) 5.54 x 443.1 = $2,454.8M = $2.45B, not $2.46B; the bridge uses $2.46B, so the arithmetic does not tie to the stated inputs. (b) F12 says dividends declared per share were $5.54 for FY26, but F43 says the annual dividend is $5.88. Which rate applies to FY27 is not stated, and the two are 6% apart. **Fix:** Recompute the regular dividend as 5.54 x 443.1 = $2.45B and change every reference from $2.46B to $2.45B, or, if forward-year, use 5.88 x 443.1 = $2.61B and label it as such. Add one line on the chart: "FY26 declared $5.54/sh (F12); current annualized rate $5.88/sh (F43); FY27 run-rate to be confirmed by the treasurer." Pick one rate for the bridge and footnote the other.
- The bridge presents FY26 actual OCF ($15.82B, period ended 2026-08-30) as the cash available alongside the special dividend that is paid in Q1 FY27 (4 December 2026). The FY27 OCF the dividend actually competes with is not sourced anywhere in this storyboard. Slide 3 itself concedes consensus OCF falls about 12% to $14.0B but then says "it lowers the trough by about $1.8B" — if consensus is the forward view, the -$1.8B cannot be a clause, it is the case. **Fix:** Restate the bridge as pro forma FY27: opening cash $20.21B at 2026-08-30, plus management's FY27 OCF (to be sourced), less FY27 capex, less regular dividends, less the $13.30B special dividend. Show both cases on the page: FY26 actual OCF $15.82B and consensus FY27 OCF $14.0B (F50), with troughs of $X and $X-1.8B against the $12B floor. If the FY26 actual is retained as the primary bridge, retitle the page "FY26 pro forma" and move the consensus case to slide 4 as the downside, not a clause.
- The bridge subtracts $6.43B as the FY27 outflow, but F19 says capex grew 14.2% a year over three years (from $4.32B). The governing thought says "hold FY27 capex to the FY26 actual of $6.43B" — that is a policy, but nothing on slide 3 shows what holding looks like next to the trend, and the only forward capex marker in the facts is F40 ("approximately $6,500" for fiscal 2026, a $6.5B figure that is neither the FY26 actual $6.43B nor a FY27 number). A committee will ask what the do-nothing capex path is. **Fix:** Add two bars to the bridge or a two-line callout: FY26 actual $6.43B (F4) and a trend-line FY27 at +14.2% p.a. = about $7.34B (from F19), showing the $0.91B of capex avoided by holding. Reconcile F40's "approximately $6,500" to $6.43B on the page (rounding of a company intention, not the actual) so no one in the room catches the two numbers as inconsistent.
- The recommendation is 'one-time special dividend' but the one number that determines whether cash is surplus — operating cash flow — is a gap. Slide 2 says 'Gaps shown as N/A: operating cash flow, dividends and buybacks paid, minimum cash reserve' and slide 3 says 'Operating cash flow is [GAP], so the earnings-based view is approximate'. Net income of $9.23B is not cash; without OCF and the FCF after dividends a payout size cannot be derived. **Fix:** Pull operating cash flow, dividends paid and buybacks paid for FY2026 from the 10-K (central index for accession 000090983226000093) into slide 2, and replace the slide 3 '[GAP]' with OCF and FCF-after-capex. Do not size the dividend until the slide 3 figure is real.
- 'a buyback at 443.1M shares needs a price view that is not in the facts [GAP: share price and valuation]' — the deck compares a special dividend against a buyback with no share price, no valuation and no return metric. That is an unsupported choice. **Fix:** Add current share price as of the cover date and show dollars returned per $1B under each option at 443.1M shares, so the dividend-vs-buyback comparison is numeric. If price cannot be sourced, delete the buyback comparison from slide 4 rather than leave the claim.
- The slide compares options the board is choosing between, but two of the three criteria ('certainty for shareholders', 'flexibility') are scored qualitatively with no basis, and the buyback row is scored N/A on share count ($5B could not be sized for share retirement without the share price — [GAP] is acknowledged). A board deciding form of return cannot score options it cannot size. The recommendation 'special dividend is a one-off' is asserted, not derived from any number on the slide. **Fix:** State the decision test explicitly on slide 4: rank options on (a) cash left / capex coverage (computed: $15.2B / $6.43B = 2.4x; $10.2B / $6.43B = 1.6x; growth-only 3.1x), (b) per-share amount ($5B / 443.1M = $11.28; $10B / 443.1M = $22.57), (c) reversibility (special dividend = one-off; buyback = programme you can pause). Leave the buyback row blank on share count until the price is pulled; do not score it qualitatively in the meantime. Move the 'growth stays funded' conclusion from the title into the takeaway with the 2.4x arithmetic shown.
- The brief's chosen answer is 'special dividend', and slide 4 admits 'Buyback share count effect is N/A [GAP: share price]'. Without the price you cannot say the $5B special dividend is better or worse than the $5B buyback, cannot state the buyback's accretion, and cannot state what the $11.28 per share represents as a yield or as a % of price. The special-dividend recommendation is therefore unsupported relative to its own stated alternative. **Fix:** Research and insert: (1) Costco close price on a dated pre-board date; (2) current regular quarterly/special dividend and total paid in FY2026; (3) existing buyback authorization remaining; (4) debt maturity schedule from the FY2026 10-K. Then restate slide 4 with the buyback row sized: shares retired = $5B / price, pro-forma share count and accretion. If you cannot get the price from the 10-K, say the source and date you will use (e.g., a specific 10-K cover-page or market close date).
- The funding math does not tie. Cash is $20.21B (F5). Hold $8B minimum + fund $6.43B capex + return $8B = $22.43B of uses against $20.21B of cash — a $2.22B shortfall. The deck never shows the sources of the $8B return (free cash flow? revolver? which period?). **Fix:** Add a one-line sources-and-uses on slide 2: beginning cash $20.21B, plus FCF (net income $9.23B less capex $6.43B = $2.80B, sourced), less minimum cash $8B, equals returnable ~$15.0B, of which you return $8B. State the period for each figure and reconcile to $20.21B.
- $12B is asserted as a scored option but never bounded against cash. $12B return + $8B minimum + $6.43B capex = $26.43B uses vs $20.21B cash — a $6.22B hole and it requires new debt, which contradicts holding 'minimum operating cash'. The option is not credible as drawn. **Fix:** Either size the large-return option to what cash+fiscal-2027 FCF can actually fund, or add the debt line and the interest cost of the incremental borrowing so the option is scored on a real balance-sheet effect. Use only $20.21B, $9.23B, $6.43B, $8B and the new debt figure you source.
- The central recommendation is asserted, not evidenced. No numbers show what the $6.43B of capex earns (new-warehouse ROI, incremental revenue per warehouse, comp sales, capital intensity of a new club) or what holding $8B costs (interest income forgone, currently zero — interest expense F6 is only $145.0M, so the 'cost to hold' is largely opportunity cost, not interest). The comparison cannot be made with the figures given. **Fix:** Add a method slide or a half-slide with sourced return on new clubs (revenue per new warehouse, capex per new warehouse, payback) vs the after-tax cost of the $8B idle cash, both from SEC filings/company disclosure. If the return figure cannot be sourced, change the governing thought to the funding-capacity argument, which the numbers do support.
- Hard-coded plug: '$20.21B of cash plus FCF $2.80B less $8B minimum... plus interest expense of about $0.15B overlooked... $22.99B of reachable cash less $8B minimum... equals $15.0B returnable' does not tie. $20.21 + $2.80 = $23.01B, not $22.99B; subtract $8B gives $15.01B, not $15.0B. More importantly, the bridge ignores the $1.15B of dividends paid and $848.0M of buybacks already executed during FY2026, which are cash outflows already reflected in the $20.21B cash at 2026-08-30. Double-counting the $8B return as if it is the only use of cash misstates the decision. **Fix:** Rebuild the bridge on Slide 3 to start from $20.21B cash at 2026-08-30, add only incremental FCF, and deduct ONLY the proposed $8B return. Change the 'returnable' figure from $15.0B to $8.01B. Adjust Slide 2's 'about $15.0B returnable' to 'about $8.0B available for incremental return after maintaining the $8B minimum'.
- Inconsistent and unsupported: ending cash after a $12B return cannot be $11.0B when beginning cash is $20.21B and FCF is $2.80B. $20.21B + $2.80B - $12B = $11.01B, which is arithmetically correct only if the $8B minimum is not deducted. But the slide claims '1.4x minimum' on $11.0B, meaning minimum is ~$7.86B, not $8B. And the statement 'it needs about $0.0B of new debt' is an assertion with no source — the fact gives $3.91B long-term debt at 2026-08-30, not a debt capacity or covenant. **Fix:** Correct the $12B option row: ending cash = $11.01B; ratio to $8B minimum = 1.38x. Delete the '$0.0B of new debt' claim or replace with 'No incremental debt assumed; check covenants on the $3.91B long-term debt at 2026-08-30.' Add a research request for debt covenant headroom.
- Free cash flow is not net income less capex. The standard definition is operating cash flow less capex. Facts give operating cash flow $15.82B (F7) and capex $6.43B (F4); FCF = $9.39B, not $2.80B. Using net income understates FCF by $6.59B and renders the entire bridge wrong. **Fix:** Replace 'free cash flow $2.80B (net income $9.23B less capex $6.43B)' with 'free cash flow $9.39B (operating cash flow $15.82B less capex $6.43B)'. Rebuild the bridge with $9.39B, and re-evaluate the $8B return and $8B minimum against the larger figure.
- The $9.39B FCF figure is FY2026 actual (OCF $15.82B less capex $6.43B, item F130). That figure is spent once: it funds FY2026 capex of $6.43B and leaves $9.39B — but the FY2027 guided capex step-up to $7.5B (F140) has to come out of FY2027 cash flow, not out of the FY2026 residual. The slide as worded implies one pool funds both the $6.43B and the $7.5B, which double-counts the same cash. If FY2027 FCF is only ~$8.3-9.4B and capex steps to $7.5B, the residual pool is closer to $1-2B, not $8-10B. The recommendation size, as constructed, is not supportable as stated. **Fix:** Split the slide into two periods: FY2026 actual (OCF $15.82B less capex $6.43B = $9.39B) and FY2027 guided (OCF ~$15.82B growing 12.6% (F126) less capex $7.5B = ~$10.3B residual). State explicitly that the $8-10B buyback is funded from FY2027 free cash flow, and show the FY2027 residual after the $7.5B step-up ($10.3B), not the FY2026 $9.39B. Label the $9.39B as FY2026 and the buyback pool as FY2027.
- Authorization headroom is $1,115M and FY2026 repurchases were $848.0M (F155, F115) — so the new $8-10B authorization is not incremental spend; it replaces a program with $1.12B remaining. The board decision is not 'approve $8-10B on top of $1.12B', it is 'increase from the $4,000M/$1,115M remaining to $8-10B'.
