The slide as written leaves the reader to do this subtraction themselves, which is exactly the arithmetic a committee will ask for and a hostile press will frame as 'buyback doubles' when it does not. **Fix:** State plainly: 'Current program $4,000M with $1,115M unspent (F145, F155); new authorization of $8-10B replaces it; incremental capacity above the rollover is $6.9-8.9B.' Put that incremental number in the title of Point 3 on slide 2 and in the closing footnote.
- The comparison is 1.88% earnings yield on a buyback (F134) vs 5.15% on idle cash. On its face, holding cash beats buying back stock 5.15% to 1.88%. The slide asserts buyback wins because it is 'flexible', but the number goes the other way. A committee will not accept a recommendation whose own figures say the status quo earns 2.7x what the proposed action earns. Either the comparison is wrong (5.15% is nominal, pre-tax, and after 24.7% effective tax rate (F123) is ~3.88% versus 1.88% pre-tax return on buyback) or the buyback case needs a different justification (per-share accretion, return on capital 22.2% (F136) deployed into new stores vs cash drag, or the assumption that cash is earmarked for capex so is not truly idle). **Fix:** On slide 4, replace the '1.88% vs 5.15%' headline comparison with the after-tax cash yield (~3.88% using 24.7% effective tax rate, F123) against the buyback earnings yield (1.88%, F134), and add the growth case: capital deployed into warehouses earns 22.2% after tax (F136), which is the actual alternative to cash, not Treasury bills. If the buyback recovers less than 3.88%, say so and justify the buyback on EPS accretion and the board's stated capital-return policy.
- The math holds ($8B / $1,106.21 = 7.23M shares = 1.63%; $10B / $1,106.21 = 9.04M shares = 2.04%) but the deck uses the 16 November 2026 close, and repurchases will execute over quarters at unknown prices. The MD review note already flags 0.9M shares per $1B and 7.2-9.0M across $8-10B as a discrepancy (F133, F135). If the stock moves, the share retirement and the EPS accretion change materially: at $1,300, $10B retires 7.7M not 9.0M; at $900, 11.1M. The deck shows a point estimate at a single-day close that will be wrong by the time of purchase. **Fix:** Run the share retirement and EPS accretion at three prices ($900, $1,106.21, $1,300) as a sensitivity table on slide 4, and label the current figures as 'at the 2026-11-13 close only'. Add a line: '$8-10B retires 6.2-11.1M shares (1.39-2.50% of 443.1M) across an $900-1,300 share price range.'
- Free cash flow is stated as $9.39B on slide 2 ('$9.39B free cash flow') and as '$9.38B' in F130 and in the slide 2 footnote construction; the FY2027 residual is '$10.3B' on slide 3 and 'about $10.3B' on slide 5, but the arithmetic '$15.82B growing 12.6% less $7.5B capex' gives $15.82 x 1.126 - 7.5 = $10.31B only if the 12.6% is applied to OCF, not to FCF. State one number and one derivation; the same figure appears as both $9.38B and $9.39B in the same source fact and the deck carries both. **Fix:** Pick one basis. F130 says '$9.38B = operating cash flow $15.82B less capital expenditure $6.43B', which computes to $9.39B on the stated inputs; correct F130 to $9.39B and make slide 2, slide 3 and slide 5 read $9.39B, with the FY2027 pool shown as $10.31B (rounded to $10.3B) and the derivation 'OCF $15.82B x 1.126 = $17.81B, less $7.5B capex = $10.31B' printed on the chart.
- The FY2027 pool of $10.3B rests entirely on an unsourced 12.6% OCF growth rate (F126 is a historical three-year CAGR from $11.07B to $15.82B, not a forecast). Applying a historical growth rate to a forward year and then sizing a board authorization off it is a hard-coded assumption presented as arithmetic; the 0% growth sensitivity is buried in a footnote ('FY2027 residual falls to $8.3B and the safe ceiling is $6B, not $10B') and contradicts the $8-10B recommendation on the face of the slide. **Fix:** Move the 0% growth case out of the footnote and onto slide 3 as a second scenario column: 'FY2027 OCF $15.82B flat, less $7.5B capex = $8.3B pool; buyback ceiling covered by FCF is $8.3B, with $1.7B above that funded from the $20.21B cash balance.' Title slide 3 to the range, not the point estimate: 'FY2027 free cash flow of $8.3-10.3B funds the buyback; $10B draws $1.7B on cash.'
- The deck admits 'incremental warehouse ROIC is not disclosed and is a research gap' and then proceeds to score 'capex safe' at 10% weight and conclude 'buyback wins' on arithmetic. A board deciding whether to fund warehouses or buy back stock is being asked to accept a 22.2% *consolidated* ROIC (F136, existing business, not incremental) as a proxy for new-warehouse returns, with no sourced figure for the thing being decided. The weighted total is therefore an asserted recommendation dressed as a grid. **Fix:** Do not build the recommendation on the 22.2% consolidated ROIC as if it were incremental. Either research a defensible incremental return (see research requests) and disclose the method, or restate slide 4 to show the decision turns on the $8.3-10.3B FY2027 FCF pool and the flexibility of a pausable authorization, and demote the grid to a secondary exhibit.
- 1.88% = $9.23B / $490.2B is the earnings yield of the whole company, not the return the $8B earns. Applying it to $8B gives $150.4M, which is not an incremental earnings figure: a buyback does not create earnings, it reduces shares; the only P&L effect is forgone interest on the $8B spent. The slide sells this as 'the return a buyback earns on each dollar.' That is a category error and a committee member will catch it in the first minute. **Fix:** Replace the 1.88% 'return' framing on slide 2 with a buyback accretion calc that is actually computable: $6B buyback / $1,106.21 = 5.42M shares (already on slide 2 as F23), reducing 443.1M to 437.7M, EPS $20.76 x 443.1M / 437.7M = $21.02, about 1.2% accretion; then show the offset — forgone interest on $8B at a stated rate — and the net effect. If the incremental-earnings number cannot be sourced, delete '$150.4M a year' and the '$150M' bullet rather than assert it.
- F166's earnings yield uses 'diluted EPS $20.76' from F151, and F167's post-buyback EPS uses '$20.80 = EPS $20.76 x 443.1M / 442.2M shares' from a $1B buyback. Neither F151 (diluted EPS) nor F151's source is in the brief's facts; F151 is cited only inside F166/F167/F31. The deck's central accretion number is sourced to a fact that is not itself sourced. **Fix:** Get diluted EPS directly from the FY2026 10-K income statement (SEC XBRL, EarningsPerShareDiluted, period 2025-09-01 to 2026-08-30, accession 000090983226000093) and cite the accession and line. Confirm $20.76 before any EPS math is built on it; if it differs, recompute F166, F167 and the slide 2 accretion.
- The $2B special dividend at $4.51/share is asserted (F24) but no dividend-per-share figure beyond the routine $30 special dividend (F175) is provided, and no cost-of-equity or discount rate is anywhere in the material. Without a rate, 'return on cash' cannot be scored and the 'cash earns nothing' argument is an assertion, not a test. **Fix:** Research: Costco's current quarterly dividend per share and its cost of equity / WACC (or at minimum the risk-free rate from the 10-K or Treasury data) so the $8B return can be compared against the 4-5% opportunity cost of idle cash. Until then, mark the slide 4 'return on cash' scores as [GAP] with the rate to be applied.
- The thesis 'the plan is already covered 2.5x and the retained $12.21B still funds it 3.1x' conflates two different covers: 2.5x is operating cash flow $15.82B / capex $6.43B; 3.1x is cash $20.21B / capex $6.43B (F163). Saying 'covered 2.5x' to justify not touching the balance sheet, then returning $8B of the balance sheet, is internally contradictory — F162 free cash flow is only $9.38B, so the $8B return is ~85% of FY26 FCF, not 'genuinely spare'. **Fix:** On slide 2 and the governing thought, state both covers with their different numerators and labels: 'FY26 operating cash flow $15.82B covers the $6.43B capex 2.5x; cash on hand covers it 3.1x; FY26 free cash flow is $9.38B, so the $8B return equals 85% of FY26 FCF and is funded from cash on hand, with $12.21B retained.' Add the FCF-coverage ratio explicitly.
- The $8B sizing, the 5.42M share retirement and the 1.22% figure all rest on a single Yahoo daily close of $1,106.21 at 2026-11-13. That is stale relative to a November 2026 board meeting, is a researched fact not a given, and the brief says 'research the company's SEC filings and market data (share price, valuation)'. No 52-week range, no average volume — a $6B buyback's market impact cannot be sized off one closing print. **Fix:** Add a research line for COST's 30-day VWAP and 52-week range as of the board date, and show the share-retirement calc at both the 2026-11-13 close ($1,106.21) and the VWAP: the F165 rate of 0.90M shares per $1B is a rate to recompute, not a fixed input.
- The deck's comparison row rests on two secondary aggregators (capitaldigest.com and a Yahoo Finance article) both dated 2026-11-11/12, with no company filing or press release cited. It does not quantify the reconciliation: a $30/share special dividend on 443.1M shares is $13.29B, plus the $4B buyback authorization = $17.29B announced, which is larger than the proposed $8B — the two cannot simply sit in a 'comparison row' unquantified. **Fix:** Cite Costco's own press release or 8-K for the $30 dividend and $4B authorization, then put the arithmetic in the row: '$30 x 443.1M = $13.29B dividend; $4B authorization; combined announced $17.29B versus the incremental $8B proposal; pro-forma cash $20.21B - $17.29B - $8B = negative, so the package must be sized net of announced actions or sequenced.' Do not show the row until that math is reconciled.
- The accretion case is asserted before it is calculated. Slide 3 asks to 'show the accretion as a calc from diluted EPS and the share count' but F167 only gives the $1B case ($20.80 = $20.76 x 443.1M / 442.2M). The deck's own 1.2% figure for $8B has no calc fact behind it; F166 earnings yield is 1.88% ($20.76/$1,106.21), which is well below any plausible November 2026 short-term rate, so a buyback funded from cash is EPS- and value-dilutive once forgone interest is charged. That flips the recommendation. **Fix:** Add calc facts before the slide is built: (a) full $8B buyback at the F165 rate — shares retired and the $20.76 EPS restated; (b) forgone interest = $8B x the short-term risk-free rate [GAP: rate as of November 2026]; (c) net EPS effect = buyback accretion less forgone interest. If net is negative, the recommendation on slides 2 and 5 changes to a smaller return or a dividend-only structure — the arithmetic decides, not the prose.
- The buyback/dividend split does not reconcile across slides. The governing thought says 'cap the buyback at $4.0B' and pay $4.0B as a special dividend; slide 4 says 'Cap the buyback at $4.0B' and '$4.0B as a special dividend ($9.03/share)'; slide 5 says 'size the buyback at $4.0B' and 'declare the $4.0B special dividend ($9.03/share)'. But slide 2 title says 'cap buyback at $4.0B, dividend $4.0B' while its body says 'A $6.0B buyback and a $2.0B special dividend'. In the legacy content, slide 2 reads 'buyback tranche sized at $6.0B' and '$2.26 per share'. A committee will not know which number is the recommendation. **Fix:** Pick one split and use it on every slide. The governing thought is $4.0B buyback / $4.0B dividend ($9.03/share), which ties to the 1.88% vs 5.15% logic; overwrite slide 2's body to '$4.0B buyback and a $4.0B special dividend ($9.03/share)' and delete the $6.0B/$2.0B/$2.26 per share references and the stale F9 text
- The $403 break-even price appears in no fact. No cited fact gives a break-even price, and $403 does not reconcile to the sourced $1,106.21 close, $20.76 EPS or 1.88% earnings yield. As written it is an unsourced hard-coded plug on the success test of the whole recommendation. **Fix:** Either delete the $403 test and replace the measure of success with a sourced metric (cash stays above 1.5x the $6.43B capex), or show the break-even calculation explicitly: state the assumed reinvestment rate and horizon used to get $403 and cite the input. Do not ship the number until it is derived.
- The 5.15% Treasury yield is not in any fact and has no source. The 327bp spread and the '$131M a year' forgone on slide 4 are computed from it, so one unsourced input drives the entire buyback-vs-dividend recommendation. It is also the sole basis for the $4.0B cap. **Fix:** Source a dated Treasury yield (specify 10-year, and the close date) from a named source, or if none is available add it as a research gap and hold the spread, the $131M and the '327bp' out of the slides until it is sourced.
- Two different splits of the same $8.0B are live in the material: $4.0B/$4.0B in the governing thought and slides 1-2, $6.0B/$2.0B in the cover-line variant. The special dividend per share is $9.03 in one place and $2.26 in the other. The board cannot vote an $8.0B return it cannot state. **Fix:** Pick one split and make it the only split in the deck: $4.0B buyback + $4.0B special dividend ($9.03/share at 443.1M shares). Delete the $6.0B/$2.0B cover variant and the $2.26/share line from slide 2.
- The single biggest decision number, the $4.0B tranche cap, does not follow from the numbers shown. At $1,106.21 a $4.0B buyback retires 3.62M shares (0.8% of 443.1M); the same $4.0B returned as a special dividend buys the shareholder nothing in yield. Nothing on slides 3-4 sizes the tranche: the 1.88% vs 5.15% spread argues for $0 to the buyback, not $4.0B, and the only offset offered is 'dilution' with no share issuance number in the material. **Fix:** Put the sizing on the page: state the dilution being offset (shares issued under equity plans, in M, from the 10-K) and the price ceiling above which $4.0B of buyback is value-dilutive, then show the arithmetic that selects $4.0B. If the offset is annual dilution of roughly 0.9M-1.0M shares, say whether $1.0B, $4.0B or $0 clears it.
- A live instruction to the board not to use a number, with the number never documented and never explained. The break-even price is the price at which the 1.88% earnings yield equals the 5.15% Treasury (approx. $20.76/0.0515 = $403), and slide 5 cites it as a bare fact. A director will ask what $403 is and why it is excluded. **Fix:** Either delete the line or make it a stated test: 'the buyback is value-accretive versus the 5.15% Treasury only below a $403 break-even price (EPS $20.76 / 5.15%); at $1,106.21 it is not'. If it stays, it belongs on slide 4 next to the two-bar chart, not buried in the closing.

### Storyboard: Senior partner (87)

- The deck recommends sizing a buyback or special dividend without the share price or payout history. Without them you cannot state a payout, a yield or a per-share figure, so the recommendation is not actionable for a board. The $46/share line is cash per share, not a return figure. **Fix:** Pull share price as of a dated close and the annual dividend per share ($/share) and buyback history ($ and shares) from Costco's 10-K or press releases, and state the liquidity floor ($B) management would set. Until then, slide 4's title must not claim affordability; make it "Cash of $20.2B funds growth and leaves room for a measured payout, subject to pricing".
- Both slides make the same argument: that growth capex is the priority and cash is the source of any return. Slide 3 shows capex at 70% of net income. Slide 4 scores 'funds new warehouses' at 3 for growth-first options. The board reads the same point twice and learns nothing new on slide 4. This is a MECE violation on the spine of the deck. **Fix:** Cut the 'funds new warehouses' argument from slide 4's framing. Make slide 4 purely about the choice between keep-funding-growth-only and growth-plus-a-floor-based-return, against two criteria: uses surplus cash and keeps flexibility. Slide 3 already proves growth is the first call. Slide 4 should answer 'what do we do with what's left'.
- The deck admits it cannot show whether capex is rising, flat or falling, then builds the entire recommendation on 'growth comes first'. If capex is flat or declining, the case for holding back returns weakens. The board will ask this question in the first thirty seconds. **Fix:** Add research: get prior-year capex (FY2025 and FY2024) and prior-year net income from the same 10-K or prior filings. Put a three-year capex trend column on slide 3. If capex has grown, the growth-first answer gets stronger. If it has not, the answer needs to change.
- This contradicts slide 2's title 'let the declared ... $4B buyback run'. Slide 2 says run the $4B; slide 4 says hold it modest. A board cannot act on two opposite asks in the same deck. Either the buyback is approved and runs, or it should be trimmed — pick one. **Fix:** Make both pages say the same thing. If the recommendation is to let the declared program run, retitle slide 4 to 'The declared $4B buyback is the right size: growth still returns 22.2% vs 1.88% on repurchases' and drop the word 'modest' and any implication of trimming. If you genuinely want to trim it, change slide 2 to say so and size the trim.
- The comparison is apples to oranges and reads as a trap. F32 computes return on the existing capital base (operating income $11.69B x (1-24.7%) / (equity $35.80B + LT debt $3.91B)). That is the average return on capital already deployed, not the incremental after-tax return on the next dollar of FY27 capex. F30 is a forward earnings yield COST paid $1,106.21 for against a 1.88% yield. Setting 22.2% (a backward-looking average return on capital) next to a 4.12% money-market rate tells a director you are comparing a return on capital that is not the return on the marginal investment against a short-term risk-free rate. One question kills the whole page. **Fix:** Replace the 22.2% bar with the incremental after-tax return on new warehouse capex, benchmarked against the 4.12% risk-free (F24). If the incremental number is not in the filings, say so and make it a research request, do not put the 22.2% average on the same axis as 1.88% and 4.12%.
- The bridge does not tie and the closing number is derived from facts that live in two different periods. F5 is cash at 2026-08-30, F7 ($15.82B operating cash flow) and F4 ($6.43B capex) are for FY ended 2026-08-30, F8 (dividends paid $1.15B) and F12 (dividends declared per share $5.54) are for the nine months to 2026-05-10, and F40 (the $13.3B dividend and $4B buyback) falls in FY27. Adding a full-year cash flow and a nine-month dividend to a period-end cash balance and calling the residual "$6.9B closing cash" is a number a committee will re-derive and get something else. An untying bridge on a board page is not survivable. **Fix:** Rebuild the bridge on one basis and label the period on every bar: closing cash FY26 $20.21B (F5), plus FY26 operating cash flow $15.82B (F7), less FY26 capex $6.43B (F4), less the special dividend $13.3B (F40), less the $4B buyback (F40), less the FY26 regular dividend actually paid (F8, $1.15B for the nine months, flag the period). Show what closing cash the residual implies and state the assumption. If the three of F4, F5, F7 and F8 cannot be put on one basis, drop the closing-cash claim and title the slide on the point the filings do support.
- Topic title that defeats the page's job. The page is a decision grid of three options against four criteria, but the title states the conclusion of slide 2 again, so the grid proves 'funded from cash' rather than letting the board choose. It also front-runs the recommendation, and if the grid scored option A and option B equal on capex-funded and balance-sheet risk (they are: growth-only funds $7.5B from $9.38B FCF and $20.21B cash; growth-plus-return also funds $7.5B and draws down $17.3B of the same cash), the title is misleading. **Fix:** Retitle slide 3 as a comparison, e.g. 'Growth-only funds $7.5B capex from $9.38B FCF; growth plus the $17.3B return draws cash to $2.9B — both keep capex whole'. Make the grid's callout the scoring result, not a restatement of the recommendation, and let slide 2 own the endorsement.
- The storyboard answers a different question than an earlier draft still asserts: F10 "Approve a $10B buyback to return excess cash while funding warehouse growth", F11 "Decision today: Approve a $10B buyback authorisation", F47 "return $9.38B of free cash flow", F51 "Retire 8.4M shares", F57 "The $9.38B authorisation". If any of these strings survive, the deck contradicts itself on its recommendation. **Fix:** Delete every residual $10B and $9.38B authorisation reference from the working file. The only buyback number on any page is $4B and the only special dividend is $30/share ($13.3B). Re-state slide 5's step 1 exactly as the storyboard does: $4B buyback confirmed, $30 dividend at Q1 2027.
- The comparison is not apples to apples and an investment committee will call it out. 22.2% is an after-tax return on capital (operating income $11.69B x (1-24.7%) / equity $35.80B + long-term debt $3.91B). 1.88% is an earnings yield against a $1,106.21 share price, which embeds a multiple. Comparing an ROIC to an earnings yield implicitly assumes you could buy Costco's whole capital base at book, which you cannot. As written, the page is the whole argument for the recommendation and it is the weakest link. **Fix:** Keep the three bars (growth 22.2%, cash 5.2%, buyback 1.9%) but change slide 4's title to state the real test: "Growth returns 22.2% after tax; a buyback returns 1.9% and cash 5.2%". In the right-hand argument, replace the word 'prioritise' with an explicit statement that the board is not being asked to weigh a book-return hurdle against a market price; it is being asked whether $4B of surplus is better deployed in warehouses (22.2%) or in retiring shares at a 1.9% earnings yield. That framing survives scrutiny; the current one does not.
- The arithmetic is never shown and does not close on its face. $13.3B special dividend + $4B buyback = $17.3B against $9.38B free cash flow. Every committee member will ask the same question in the room and slide 2 has no answer. **Fix:** Add one line to slide 2's point 2, using facts already in hand: the special dividend is funded by the $20.21B of cash and equivalents on the balance sheet (F5), with free cash flow of $9.38B funding the $6.5B fiscal 2026 capex plan (F98) and the regular dividend. Do not put a number on the page that is not already sourced; if you want to show post-dividend cash cover, compute it from F5 and F90 and label it as computed on the slide.
- The title is a question-plus-straw-man, not a claim. It names a $10B option that the board never asked for, and a reader skimming the deck will wonder who put $10B on the table. The governing thought is $4B; the deck should defend $4B against 'more' generically, not against a phantom programme. **Fix:** Retitle slide 3: 'A $4B return consumes 31% of the $13.3B $30 dividend is paid from; $10B would breach the $6.5B capex plan.' If you keep the $10B comparison, label it once as a boundary case in the exhibit subtitle, not in the slide title.
- The 5.2% is the pivot of the 'holding cash beats a buyback' argument, and it is the only number in the deck with no source. A board or investment committee will ask, and 'the 10-year Treasury' without a date is not an answer. **Fix:** Add the source and exact observation date (e.g. U.S. Department of the Treasury daily yield curve, 2026-11-13 close). If it cannot be sourced to that date, replace 5.2% with a sourced figure or delete the cash bar and the sentence it supports.
- The $2.9B cash figure does not reconcile. Cash $20.21B less the $17.3B distribution is $2.91B, but the $4B buyback runs to January 2030 and is not funded on day one; only the $13.3B special dividend is paid now, leaving roughly $6.9B. The page presents the full $17.3B as if spent in one instant. **Fix:** Split the table into two dated columns: 'pro forma post-dividend (Nov 2026)' with cash $20.21B less $13.3B = $6.9B, and 'post-buyback (by Jan 2030)' with cash $2.9B. Restate the title as 'The $13.3B dividend cuts cash to $6.9B today; the $4B buyback draws it to $2.9B by 2030'.
- The verdict is 'growth earns 22.2% on capital against 5.15% on cash', but slide 2 only asserts the 22.2%, slide 3 argues the buyback is dilutive, slide 4 covers the balance sheet, and slide 5 is process. Nothing shows the return on new warehouse capital, the 29 openings, or why capex should be held rather than cut. The case for growth is stated, not shown. **Fix:** Rebuild slide 3 as a two-panel exhibit: left, the after-tax return per $1 of capital (growth 22.2%, cash 5.15%, buyback 1.88%) as the bar chart; right, the new-warehouse economics (29 planned for FY2026, 16 opened by week 36, capex $6.43B actual vs $6.5B intention, revenue $303.15B and net income $9.23B). Title it 'Growth at 22.2% beats cash at 5.15% by 17.1 points; hold the $6.5B capex plan'.
- The $6.9B post-dividend cash number carries no source. $20.21B cash less the $13.3B special dividend = $6.9B is your own arithmetic on F226 and F5, but the board cannot approve a plan whose headroom figure is unstated. The $2.9B is worse: it assumes the full $4B buyback is spent, and slide 4 itself says 'the $4B authorization is drawn over time, not on day one'. **Fix:** Label the $6.9B on slide 2 and slide 4 as 'company calculation: $20.21B 10-K cash less $13.3B declared dividend', and change the slide 4 'post-buyback (by 2030)' column header to 'cash floor if the full $4B is drawn by 2030' so the $2.9B is a floor, not a forecast.
- Slide 2 calls the buyback 'drawn opportunistically' and a use of cash; slide 3 says 'A buyback at 1.88% is dilutive' and 'Buyback last'. That is a real contradiction: the deck recommends approving a $4B authorization while arguing the spend destroys value against holding cash at 5.15%. **Fix:** Reconcile on slide 2 in one line: 'Approve the $4B authorization as optionality, not a commitment; the board should not draw it while the cash earns 5.15% and the marginal warehouse earns 22.2%.' Carry that exact framing to slide 3's callout and slide 5 step 2.
- The $30/share special dividend and $4B buyback reauthorization are dated 2026-11-11 (F32, F33) but the storyboard's own provenance flags (F12, F42, F43, F44) show the $30, $4B, $13.29B and $17.29B are not tied to any cited fact. For a board vote, the declared terms are the entire subject of the deck. **Fix:** Tie the $30/share, $4B and 2026-11-11 declaration date to a named primary source (the Costco press release) in every slide that uses them, or replace with a [GAP] marker and a research request. Do not let slide 1's cover carry the vote numbers without a source.
- Title reads 'Growth capital earns 22.2% after tax; a buyback nets 1.42%'. The 22.2% is, by the slide's own footnote, a historical average on existing capital, not the incremental return on the $6.5B. The board is being asked to hold the $6.5B on the strength of a number that does not describe that $6.5B. A committee member will catch this in ten seconds. **Fix:** Either (a) change the title to 'Existing capital earns 22.2% after tax, 5.7x the 3.88% cash yield; the marginal return on the $6.5B is [GAP]' and put the marginal-return research request front and centre, or (b) obtain the incremental after-tax return on the fiscal 2026 capex plan and use it in the title. The current title overclaims and undercuts the recommendation.
- The $490.19B market cap and $1,106.21 share price appear in slides 1 and 4 but are not in the cited facts in hand. Every percentage that depends on them (3.5% of market cap, 1.42% buyback return, 0.20% share retirement per $1B) is unsupported. F13 also flags the 3.527 vs 3.53% rounding. **Fix:** Add the market-data provenance (share price date source, e.g. Yahoo Finance close on a stated date, and the resulting market cap) as a cited fact, and recompute 3.53% consistently across slides. If the market-cap source cannot be pinned to a date, footnote the date and state the price used.
- Both pages answer the same question: can we afford it. Slide 3 covers the return 1.2x by cash; slide 4's option A states the same cash left $2.92B, cover 1.2x, growth funded no. The same computation and the same conclusion appear on two of the four content pages. That is not MECE and it burns a page the board does not have. **Fix:** Keep the waterfall on slide 3 as the affordability proof and rewrite slide 4 as a pure decision page: make the rows the decision criteria (affordability already shown on slide 3, growth funded, return to holders, reversibility) and the columns the three options, with no re-derivation of the 1.2x. Cut every cell that repeats a slide 3 number.
- The governing thought tells the board to hold the $6.5B growth plan and review it against a 3.88% hurdle at November 2027, but no slide contains the actual or expected return on that $6.5B. The board would be asked to fund the plan and defer the only question that matters to a year out. This gap controls the answer to the brief and it is open on slide 5. **Fix:** Either fill the gap with a sourced number and cite it, or, if it cannot be sourced in time, change the recommendation to approve the dividend, hold growth capex only to the FY2027 review date already stated, and make the 3.88% hurdle the explicit condition on the next tranche. Do not ship slide 5 with a [GAP] footnote on the page that carries the call to action.
- The deck simultaneously recommends returning $6B ($4B buyback + $2B special dividend) and references, as the 'frame', the $30/share special dividend plus $4B buyback already announced on 11 Nov 2026. Slide 4's must-show says 'the $30 special dividend and $4B reauthorisation already announced on 11 Nov 2026 set the frame', while Slide 2 says 'Decision today: approve the $6B return'. At $1,106.21 and 443.1M shares, $30/share is roughly $13.3B — six times the proposed $2B dividend. Two incompatible recommendations sit in the same deck. **Fix:** Pick one. Either recast the recommendation around the already-declared transaction ($30/share dividend is ~$13.3B at 443.1M shares = 6.7x the $2B proposed; the $4B reauthorisation is already in place, so the incremental decision is whether to draw on it), or move the 11 Nov announcement to a single 'recent context' bullet on Slide 4 with the explicit sentence: 'The board's decision today is incremental to the $30/share dividend and $4B authorisation announced on 11 Nov 2026.' Do not let Slide 2's '$2B special dividend' and Slide 4's '$30 special dividend' appear in the same 5-page deck without reconciliation.
- Internal contradiction on the buyback size and horizon. Slide 4 uses a '$4B at $1,106.21 retires 3.6M shares (0.8%)' table, but exactly $4B of buyback cash at $1,106.21 retires about 3.62M shares, which is 0.82% of 443.1M. The slide's own EPS maths (cash cost for 0.9M shares equals about $996M, not $1B) compounds the inconsistency and undermines the dilutive conclusion. **Fix:** Set one basis: say 'a $4B authorisation retires 3.62M shares (0.82% of shares outstanding) at $1,106.21' and restate EPS on that basis: EPS accretion $20.76 to $20.92 before lost interest; $20.76 to $20.55 after $165M lost interest at 4.12%, so dilutive. Use the same numbers on slides 3 and 4.
- The storyboard carries two contradictory expiration dates for the same $4B authorisation: F39 says 'January 2030' and F49 says 'through November 2030.' A board deck cannot show both. **Fix:** Resolve to a single date before build. Default to F39 ('January 2030') only if the 11 Nov 2026 release confirms it; otherwise use F49's 'November 2030' and remove the January 2030 reference from slides 2, 4 and 5.
- You asked for a page that answers the question and instead wrote the whole deck on it. The source URLs do not belong on the page. A board will not read it; they will ask the person next to them what it says. This is the one page that must survive being read for fifteen seconds. **Fix:** Rebuild slide 2 as five elements only. Title: 'Approve the $6.5B fiscal 2026 capex plan; pace the $4B buyback; the $13.3B special dividend is declared'. Element 1, three big numbers: '$13.3B declared', '$4B authorised, not spent', '$6.5B capex to approve'. Element 2, one line: 'Not $17.3B of cash paid: $13.3B declared, $4B authorised.' Element 3, the funding line as a single sentence: '$20.21B cash + $9.38B free cash flow - $2.45B ordinary dividends = $6.93B before the special.' Element 4, the comparison as three chips: 'Growth 22.2% on book capital | Buyback 1.88% at $1,106.21 | Cash 4.12%'. Element 5, one decision sentence: 'Approve the $6.5B plan and the buyback pacing, incremental to the 11 November 2026 announcement.' Strip every URL; put all sources on slide 3 footnotes.
- You are asking the board to approve $6.5B of capex and the only return number on the page is the return on the existing $35.80B of book capital. The facts contain no marginal return on the plan, and the deck admits it only in a footnote. A board member will ask 'what do the thirteen new warehouses earn?' and the page has no answer. You are approving spend on average economics, not incremental economics. **Fix:** Keep 22.2% but relabel it on the page, not in a footnote: '22.2% existing book return, not the return on the $6.5B plan'. Then put one row in the table titled 'Return on the $6.5B plan' and fill it, at minimum, with revenue per net new unit times the 13 openings, or with the 3-year revenue growth of 7.8% a year against capex growth of 14.2% a year as the honest placeholder. If the marginal figure cannot be sourced, say so in the row in bold and list it as the open research item. Do not present 22.2% as if it were the return on the money being asked for.
- The title 'cash still covers 3.1x capex' ($20.21B cash / $6.43B capex) uses a stock-over-flow ratio, then the same slide instructs 'Flow-based coverage of 2.5x ($15.82B / $6.43B) replaces the stock-over-flow 3.1x.' A reader sees a headline 3.1x and a body 2.5x for the same claim. Worse, $20.21B cash / the $6.5B plan is 3.11x while $20.21B / $6.43B is 3.14x — the '3.1x' is only exact if you imply the plan, so the two numbers are not even the same calculation. **Fix:** Pick one measure and make it flow-based. Title slide 3: 'FCF of $9.38B covers the $6.5B plan 1.4x' ($9.38B / $6.5B). Drop the 3.1x entirely. If you want the balance-sheet cushion, put it in a footnote: 'cash of $20.21B equals 3.1x the $6.5B plan.' Never show two coverage ratios on one page.
- Step 1 says 'approve the $4B authorization'; step 3 says 'execute the buyback over 12 months ... at no higher than $1,106.21.' Step 1 must name the actual authorization. Today's program is $4,000 with $1,359 remaining (F46) and $848.0M already repurchased in FY26 (F9). So $1,359 + $848M = $2.207B already used of $4B; $4B − $2.207B = $1.793B remains. Approving '$4B' without topping up the authorization is internally inconsistent with the remaining capacity. **Fix:** Restate step 1: 'Approve a $4B authorization, replacing the current $4B program with $1.359B remaining.' Step 3 then reads 'execute up to $4B' — the $4B is new authorization, the $1.359B is what remains of the old. Closes the capacity gap.
- Every number in the deck — market cap $490.19B, earnings yield 1.88%, buyback shares 3.6M, the EPS bridge, the $1,150 crossover — rests on a share price of $1,106.21 from the 2026-11-13 close. The cover itself says 'confirm the 2026-11-16 close before the pack is finalised.' A board paper that prices $4B of buyback off a four-day-old print will be challenged on the first question. **Fix:** Refresh to the 2026-11-16 close and recompute: market cap = refreshed price x 443.1M; earnings yield = $20.76 / refreshed price; shares retired per $1B = $1B / refreshed price. If the refreshed price differs by more than ~1%, restate the $4B/$0.16/$0.12/-$0.04 bridge on slide 2 and the $1,150 crossover on slide 5.
- You are using the 13-week T-bill yield as the cost of capital for a multi-year warehouse decision. The 10-year is 5.15% (F25) — you have it and you footnote it, but the governing thought does not use it. On the board's actual reinvestment horizon the hurdle is higher than 3.10%, which makes the 'return only the surplus' conclusion stronger, not weaker, and you are leaving that on the table while exposing yourself to the obvious challenge: 'you priced a 20-year asset against a 13-week bill.' **Fix:** Slide 2 title becomes: 'Fund the $6.5B plan, then return $4B: no project clears the after-tax cash hurdle (3.10% short-duration, 4.01% on the 10-year 5.15%)'. Present both hurdles in the returns chart on slide 4 — two thin reference lines labelled '3.10% after-tax 13-week T-bill' and '4.01% after-tax 10-year (5.15%)' — so the buyback at 1.88% fails both, and the conclusion is hurdle-insensitive.
- Slide 2 must show 'the three options and what each implies', but no slide in the storyboard ever names the three options. The deck jumps straight to the recommendation on slide 2 without framing the choice for the board. A board briefing that argues for one path without showing the alternatives it was weighed against is an advocacy memo, not a decision document. **Fix:** On slide 2, add a compact three-row options table before the recommendation: (A) Keep funding growth only — implies capex stays at $6.5B planning level and no additional return; (B) Return cash via buyback only — implies every $1B retires 0.90M shares (0.20% of 443.1M) at $1,106.21, and earns 1.88% against 22.2% return on capital; (C) Declared special dividend plus $4B buyback, capex held (recommendation) — implies funding the $30 per share special dividend ($13.3B) and the $4B buyback from $20.21B cash and $9.38B free cash flow. Title the page as a claim: 'Return cash and hold capex: fund the $13.3B special dividend and $4B buyback, keep the $6.5B plan'.
- Option A, 'front-load $4B in year one, closing cash $6.84B, EPS -$0.24', reports closing cash of $6.84B, which per slide 3's arithmetic is what remains after returning the full $13.3B dividend and the full $4B buyback. The presentation labels the cash blow as a property of the buyback, but $13.3B of dividend is already committed and common to all three options in slide 2. A director comparing options will attribute the wrong cause. **Fix:** On slide 2, label the $6.84B as cash after dividend and full $4B buyback in one line, and add a shared baseline row: closing cash after the declared $13.3B dividend and before any buyback, around $10.84B. In the option-A text, write 'incremental buyback effect: $4B, closing cash $6.84B', not 'front-load $4B in year one, closing cash $6.84B'.
- Slide 4 reads, 'A paced $1B buyback costs $0.06 a share; the front-loaded $4B costs $0.24'. But $0.06 is the cost of the paced $1B annual buyback, while $0.24 is the cost of the full $4B front-loaded program. The comparison is not like-for-like; it compares one year against four years of buyback. A board member will catch this. **Fix:** Rewrite the slide 4 title and the bridge to compare cost per $1B of buyback: '$1B of buyback adds 4 cents of EPS to $20.80 — but the interest forgone is 6 cents, so the net is negative 1.5 cents'; then add one row showing the $4B front-loaded program across four years, not a single year.
- The buyback pacing argument is made three times: slide 2 states 'net -3 cents a share', slide 3's bridge ends at 'closing cash $9.84B on the recommended paced path', and slide 4 is by your own spec 'an EPS bridge per $1B of buyback... then down to $20.73 after the $0.07 a share of after-tax interest forgone, a net -3 cents a share'. Two of these pages make the same point; the deck has no room to spare at five slides. **Fix:** Make slide 4 the single buyback-arithmetic page and strip every buyback-EPS calculation out of slide 3. Slide 3's title becomes the balance-sheet claim only ('Returns of $15.76B exceed $9.38B of free cash flow by $6.38B, drawing cash to $9.84B') and its body carries only the bridge, the three-year capex/OCF table and the sensitivity. Slide 4 keeps the $20.76 -> $20.80 -> $20.73 bridge, the $31.0M after-tax funding cost and the three returns on one basis. No number appears on both pages.
- The board is being asked to confirm the funding and timing of a dividend it has already declared, and the page intended to close the meeting carries three of these placeholders including the payment date itself. A closing roadmap with three GAPs is not a closing page. The brief asked for payment timing and the material does not have it. **Fix:** Before this goes out, pull the declaration press release and the 8-K for the record date, payment date and the buyback authorisation effective date, and the FY2026 earnings date from the company's IR calendar. Replace all three GAPs with dated milestones carrying the number the board measures: payment date and $13.3B outflow; buyback start date and the first $1.0B tranche; four-week cadence to the audit committee against the $9.84B closing-cash path.
- Both the 24.7% rate and the 22.2% return on capital rest on the same derived number, and you have already flagged it: 'pretax income is not in the fact pack'. The whole recommendation turns on comparing a 22.2% return on capital against a 6.11% to 6.59% cost of equity. If the tax rate is wrong, the conclusion moves. **Fix:** Get pretax income from the FY2026 10-K income statement and recompute: 24.7% must equal $3.02B income tax expense / pretax income. If it does not tie, correct the rate, the $31.0M after-tax funding cost, the $0.07 a share and the 22.2%. Put the recomputed rate in one footnote cell on slide 4 and carry the same figure into slide 5. Delete the [GAP] flag only after the rate ties to a filed statement.
- The single load-bearing claim against buybacks — that a buyback commits the board to ongoing repurchases while a special dividend does not — is a judgment with no evidence. The page itself admits share price and repurchase history are missing. A board member will ask 'why does a buyback commit us to anything?' and there is no answer on the page. This is the argument the recommendation stands on, and it is currently empty. **Fix:** Research Costco's authorization structure and repurchase history from the 10-K and prior filings: any stated buyback authorization size, shares repurchased in fiscal 2026 and the prior two years, and average price paid. Replace the 'keeps flexibility' judgment with a fact: 'The board's existing authorization is $X and Costco repurchased $Y of stock in fiscal 2026; a special dividend carries no recurring commitment, while a buyback authorization is standing.' If the filings show Costco has a large open authorization, say so and address it head-on.
- The two content slides do not build: slide 3 shows capital intensity and slide 4 shows the after-tax return by use of capital. They're different arguments (capital intensity vs. funding) but they get to the same recommendation. Slide 4 does not need to re-derive that $6.5B capex is the highest-return use, since slide 3's cash bridge already establishes the plan is funded and the argument is already made. The two content slides should be: (a) capital intensity is structural, and (b) funding the return is the answer. The pacing is odd: the question is asked on the cover and answered on slide 2, then the deck asks whether capex can be maintained, then returns to the original question on slide 4. The logic finishes where it should have started. **Fix:** Restructure the two content slides to be in service of the recommendation: slide 3 should establish the capital intensity constraint (capex growing 14.2% a year vs. revenue 7.8%, net PP&E $35.63B, capex guided to $6.5B) and conclude that the plan is already sized to the returns; slide 4 should be the funding argument (cash $20.21B + FCF $9.38B + buyback authorization $4B vs. the $13.3B dividend, with the buyback price discipline at $1,000/share where the earnings yield exceeds 2.1%). If you keep the capital intensity comparison on slide 4, it must be the buyback-versus-capex question on the same page — not a re-derivation of the recommendation.
- The board question is 'should Costco return more cash or keep funding warehouse growth?' The storyboard never actually says what 'more cash' would mean, because the $30 dividend is already declared. The decision as framed (approve the dividend vs. fund capex) is a false choice — the dividend is declared and the cash is already there. If the board question is 'should we return more cash', the deck must state the marginal alternatives: what else could be done with the $4B buyback authorization? And what would 'more cash' actually be, beyond the declared dividend? The deck answers a question the board already answered. **Fix:** Reframe slide 2's decision line and slide 1's subtitle to the actual open question: 'the dividend is declared; the remaining choice is how to deploy the $4B buyback authorization and what to do with any additional cash.' Slide 2 title should become, e.g., 'Approve the declared $13.3B dividend and the $6.5B capex; use the $4B buyback only below $1,000 per share.'
- Slide 2's title is 'Defer further returns until liquidity and growth returns are verified'. The brief asks a binary question — return more cash or fund growth — and the governing thought names a third path ('defer…pending review'). That is a process recommendation, not an answer to the question. A board cannot vote on 'review'. **Fix:** Make the page-1 answer a single sentence with the amount and the two options sized: e.g. 'Fund the $6.5B FY2026 growth plan from the $9.38B FCF and hold the incremental $4B authorization in reserve; only $X (if any) returns to shareholders in FY2027.' Put the governance wording ('primary-source verification') in the next-steps slide, not the headline.
- Slide 3's job is to show the $30 special dividend implies $6.92B of illustrative cash. That is just $20.21B cash minus $13.29B dividend. It is one subtraction; it does not need a full waterfall page, and it does not answer whether the board should return more. It also leaves the slide with almost no analytical content beyond the arithmetic — a wall of text and a two-bar chart. **Fix:** Kill slide 3. Fold the $20.21B cash, $13.29B dividend and $6.92B remainder into a single 'liquidity bridge' panel on slide 2 or slide 4. Replace slide 3 with the slide the board actually needs: 'FY2027–FY2028 growth capex, if funded at the 3-year trend of +14.2% p.a., consumes ~$X B ($6.43B grown at 14.2% for 2 years = $8.4B), leaving $Y B of the $20.21B cash for returns.'
- The cover title reads "Costco should put growth ahead of additional shareholder returns" but the stamp says "Unverified press reports, hold distribution pending primary-source confirmation." A board cover cannot simultaneously be a recommendation and a hold notice. It reads as if the board is being asked to act on a rumor. **Fix:** Retitle the cover "Costco should prioritize warehouse growth and approve $0 of additional discretionary returns through FY2027." Move the verification caveat to a single line at the bottom: "Reported special dividend per press reports dated 2026-11-11; primary-source confirmation pending." Delete the word 'hold' from the cover.
- The slide shows a "labelled liquidity bridge: starting cash, unverified reported dividend outflow, illustrative ending balance" but never states the three option outcomes the brief requires (growth-only, buyback, special dividend). The recommendation is $0 incremental, but the reader cannot see the alternative choices or their dollar implications. **Fix:** Replace the bridge with three labelled options: (A) growth-only, $0 incremental return; (B) buyback $1B increments, 0.90M shares retired per $1B, $0.04 EPS accretion per $1B; (C) special dividend, $1B equals $2.26 per share. State the recommendation above the options. Keep the $30 unverified dividend as a footnote, not the central visual.
- The closing titled "Confirm the dividend and return to the board before December 4" contradicts the governing thought. The board is being asked to approve $0 incremental returns, not to confirm a dividend. The decision slide has been replaced by a process slide. **Fix:** Retitle slide 4 "Condemn additional returns, approve the December 4 dividend if verified, and re-review in Q1 FY2027." Put the four numbered steps in a box below the recommendation. The first step should be board approval of the $0 incremental-return position, not dividend verification.
- Governing thought — 'Prioritize warehouse growth and approve $0 of additional discretionary returns through FY2027' — conflicts with fact F38 (and F47, F49, F39…) that a $30/share special dividend and a $4B buyback reauthorization were declared on 2026-11-11. A board briefing dated November 2026 cannot lead with a recommendation to approve $0 of discretionary returns when the company has already announced $13.29B of them. **Fix:** Rewrite the governing thought to reconcile: 'The $13.29B special dividend and $4B reauthorization are already declared; fund them from the $20.21B cash balance and $9.38B free cash flow, pause incremental discretionary returns, and gate FY2027 warehouse commits on cash-floor and marginal-return tests.' Update the slide 1 cover sentence so it no longer reads '$0 of additional discretionary returns'.
- The cover's italic line says 'Reported special dividend per press reports dated November 11, 2026; primary-source confirmation pending.' But F38, F39, F47 and F51 are press reports; no primary source (8-K, SEC filing, company release) is listed anywhere in the brief. That line concedes the story's central fact is unverified on the deck's own face. **Fix:** Insert a 'primary-source verification' step into slide 5 before 'Approve'; replace the cover disclaimer with: 'Source: Yahoo Finance / Simply Wall St. reporting dated 2026-11-11; company confirmation pending.' Add a research request to pull the 8-K from EDGAR and, if confirmed, update the cover to cite it.
- The bridge does not tie and the title even admits it ('before other movements'). The evidence supplied is cash $20.21B, the $13.3B dividend and a $4B buyback; $20.21B less $13.3B less $4B is $2.91B, not $6.91B. The $6.91B figure has no fact behind it anywhere in the material, and the page omits the one movement that actually determines whether liquidity covers growth funding: the $6.43B of capex. As written this is the deck's proof page and it does not prove anything. **Fix:** Rebuild the bridge on supplied facts only: start $20.21B cash at 2026-08-30, less $13.3B declared special dividend, less $6.43B fiscal 2026 capex, less any remaining fiscal 2026 return (dividends paid $1.15B, repurchases $848.0M), equals illustrative cash; then show the hypothetical $4B buyback as a separate bar. Re-title to the number the rebuilt bridge produces, and add a one-line footnote: 'Illustrative, not a forecast; excludes operating cash inflow and other movements.' If the intent was 20.21 - 13.3 = 6.91, say so in the title ('$6.91B of cash remains after the declared dividend') and drop the $4B from the same page.
- The $10.80B does not reconcile with the inputs a board member will reach for. Cash $20.21B (F5) less $13.3B special dividend (F41) is $6.91B; add FY27 operating cash flow ~$14.0B (F52) less ~$7.5B capex (F51) is $13.41B. I cannot get $10.80B from any combination of the facts on the page. An unexplained headline number on the cash bridge is the first thing that gets killed in a board meeting. **Fix:** Put the arithmetic on the page. Either label every bar with its source fact (F5, F41, F52, F51, F12) and show opening cash + FY27 OCF - FY27 capex - regular dividends = ending cash, or change the title to the number that arithmetic actually produces. Do not ship a bridge whose total a director cannot reproduce on the back of the envelope.
- The title claims 'The $20.21B cash pile covers the $7.5B fiscal 2027 capex plan 3.1x,' but 3.1x is cash/2026 capex (F27, $20.21B/$6.43B); against the $7.5B plan it is 2.7x. Worse, the schedule on the same slide subtracts the $7.5B from cash, so the 3.1x claim and the schedule prove different things. A CFO will catch this in ten seconds. **Fix:** Set the title to a claim the schedule proves and keep one cover metric. Two options: (a) 'Cash of $20.21B and FY27 operating cash flow of $15.82B fund the $7.5B capex plan and still leave a $16.5B trough' — or (b) keep '3.1x' but define it on the slide as cash/FY26 capex, state it against the $6.43B actual, and put the $7.5B plan in the schedule below. Do not let both uses of '3.1x'/'2.7x' stand in the same deck without a label.
- The governing thought says fund the plan first, then return $4.0B; slide 2's decision line says 'approve the $4.0B return and renew the authorization'; slide 4's title says the buyback earns 1.88% versus 22.2% on warehouse capital and is sized at $3.0B 'not the full authorization.' Are the three options mutually exclusive, or is the recommendation fund growth AND return $4.0B? As written, a board could read slide 2 as choosing returns over growth, then slide 4 as choosing growth over returns. The recommendation is not a choice and the options table presents a choice. **Fix:** Rewrite slide 4's title to state the actual decision: 'Fund the $7.5B plan first; the incremental $4.0B return is EPS-accretive but earns 1.88% versus 22.2% on warehouse capital.' Then add a line on slide 2: 'This is fund growth and return cash, not either/or — $4.0B is incremental to the plan, covered by the $16.5B trough.' The board must be able to read slide 2 alone and know no growth is being cut.
- The argument hinges on a $16.5B year-end cash balance and a stated management assumption of a '$4.0B minimum operating cash,' but F5 only gives cash at 2026-08-30 and F24 only gives the T-bill yield; no fact in hand establishes the $4.0B minimum. The page says to 'note the intra-year low point is a research item,' which means the governance test is not actually proven. **Fix:** Add a research request to pull the 10-K liquidity and capital resources discussion for Costco's minimum operating cash guidance. If the 10-K does not state $4.0B, either label the $4.0B as an estimate derived from FY26 net cash or replace it with the lowest monthly cash balance disclosed for FY26; in any event, do not leave 'research item' visible on a board exhibit.
- The argument for capping the buyback uses a negative 224bp spread against T-bills, but T-bills are not the right opportunity cost when the alternative is the $7.5B warehouse plan. The deck concedes on slide 4 that 'the incremental return on the FY27 plan is not derivable from the 10-K', so the pivotal number behind the recommendation has no evidence, and the page argues the second-best comparison instead. **Fix:** Rewrite slide 4's takeaway title to name the choice and the hurdle: 'Fund the $7.5B plan first; cap the buyback at $3.0B because its 1.88% earnings yield is below the 4.12% T-bill yield and far below the ~22% company-wide ROC'. Add a research request for the incremental return on the FY27 plan and label the 22.2% ROC explicitly as a ceiling, not the plan return.
- F31 and the slide 4 table disagree. F31 says $1B in buybacks lifts EPS to $20.80; slide 4 says $3.0B lifts EPS to $20.90 and $4.0B to $20.93. You cannot tell the board the buyback is accretive with two different accretion numbers in the same deck. **Fix:** Drop F31 from the storyboard and present one mechanical series, from slide 4: $20.76 at $0B, $20.90 at $3.0B, $20.93 at $4.0B, all 'before foregone interest on the cash.' If you want an intermediate, derive it explicitly from the F29 scaling rule and state the arithmetic on the page.
- The spine of slide 4 is the 22.2% after-tax return on warehouse capital, and it is built on an effective tax rate of 24.7% that appears nowhere in the facts. The table's whole argument rests on this cell. **Fix:** Either compute the tax rate from the facts in hand — income tax expense $3.02B / pretax income (operating income $11.69B less interest expense $145.0M) — and state that formula in the source line, or drop the tax-rate input and show the return as operating return on capital. Do not publish 24.7% until it is either derived on the page or sourced.
- The ~$8.6B residual cash balance is the number that proves the recommendation is not starving the growth plan. $20.21B cash - $4B - $2B = $14.21B, not $8.6B. No fact in the material explains the difference. **Fix:** Add the bridge in the facts or on slide 2 as one line: $20.21B beginning cash less $6B returned less the planned FY2027 capex or dividend commitments that bring it to $8.6B. If the missing outflow cannot be sourced, change the residual to $14.2B and rebuild the cover and 3.1x statements around it.
- The cash walk does not reconcile. 'Opening cash $20.21B less the $6B return = $14.21B; plus FY2027 OCF $15.82B less $7.5B capex = $6.71B closing cash.' $14.21B + $15.82B - $7.5B = $22.53B, not $6.71B. The number the governing thought rests on is wrong on the page that states the governing thought. A board member will do this in their head before you finish the sentence. **Fix:** Rewrite the walk so it foots: Opening cash 30 Aug 2026 $20.21B + FY2027 OCF $15.82B - FY2027 capex $7.5B - return $6B = closing cash $22.53B. If the intended closing figure is $6.71B, state the bridge that gets there (e.g. other uses or timing) or restate the governing thought. Do not ship two incompatible closing-cash numbers on the same deck.
- The table treats the buyback as a fresh $1B-for-$1B use of capital and headlines it against capex, but slide 2 already says $4B of the $6B return is a renewal of the existing $4B authorization. Renewing an authorization is not new cash out the door in FY2027 unless you also state how much will actually be repurchased. As written, slide 4 argues against $4B that slide 2 says is already authorized. **Fix:** Change slide 4's buyback row to the actual incremental spend (e.g. '$848.0M repurchased in FY2026; $XB assumed in FY2027 under the renewed $4B authorization') and add one line making clear the authorization is a ceiling, not a commitment. If the FY2027 repurchase assumption is not in the materials, ask for it before this slide is built.
- The walk does not foot. Opening cash $20.21B + OCF $15.82B - capex $7.5B - return $6B = $22.53B. But the deck also argues FY2026 FCF is $9.38B ($15.82B - $6.43B). If FY2027 OCF is flat and capex rises to $7.5B, incremental cash generation is only $8.32B, not $15.82B. The $22.53B closing cash implies $28.53B of inflows against $13.5B of outflows, which does not reconcile to the stated FY2027 capex step-up. **Fix:** Rebuild the walk so the change in cash is internally consistent: opening $20.21B + FY2027 OCF $15.82B - FY2027 capex $7.5B - return $6B = $22.53B. Then explain why the $7.5B capex does not reduce cash by the full amount — it is an outflow, but the walk double-counts if OCF already nets the prior year's capex. State the FY2027 OCF assumes flat operations with no working-capital build and no interest income on the returned cash. Label the assumed flat OCF clearly on the slide and in a footnote. If the arithmetic still cannot be made to foot, change the headline to the number that does foot.
- The slide carries five separate asks: a three-row history table with two CAGRs, a capacity argument, a four-column OCF/capex sensitivity table with a recomputed cover ratio per line, a break-even OCF, and a working-capital caveat. That is three pages of content on one. The sensitivity table is decision-grade work and deserves room; right now it will be an illegible footnote or it will kill the history table. **Fix:** Cut the sensitivity table and the break-even line from slide 3 and move them to slide 4, which has space, or drop the break-even and working-capital caveat to a footnote. Slide 3 becomes: history table left, capacity argument right, two lines of text. Keep the ratios 3.1x and 2.7x and the one-line denominator explanation.
- Neither $6.9B nor $4.9B appears anywhere in the facts. The title asserts a specific trough and a specific minimum with no source and no build, on the one slide that is supposed to de-risk the whole recommendation. A committee will ask for the arithmetic and there is none. **Fix:** Either build the trough from named facts — opening cash $20.21B (F5), plus operating cash flow $15.82B (F7), less capex $6.43B (F4), less the $30 special dividend on 443.1M shares (F41/F1), less regular dividends $1.15B (F8) and buyback cash paced at $1.0B — and state the resulting figure on the page; or delete the numbers from the title, retitle it "Cash stays above the operating minimum through the dividend and the buyback", and table the schedule underneath. Do not ship an unsourced trough on the decision slide.
- There is no stated minimum operating cash anywhere in the storyboard or the facts. You cannot claim to clear a bar you never set. Worse, the trough is never computed: a $13.3B special dividend ($30 x 443.1M, F41/F1) plus FY27 capex of ~$6.5B (F40) against opening cash of $20.21B (F5) plus FCF mechanics already puts the company through the floor. As drafted the page proves the opposite of what the title says. **Fix:** Set the minimum explicitly and compute the trough before rebuilding this page. Two candidate anchors are already in the facts: working capital plus one quarter of capex, or the $13.3B dividend measured against FY free cash flow of $9.38B (F26). The trough computation has to be shown on the slide. If the arithmetic does not clear the bar, the title and the recommendation both change; that is a decision, not a formatting choice.
- The comparison that does the work is stated wrong. The 22.2% is return on invested capital (F32), not return on incremental capital; the buyback return is 1.88% (F30) on today's price. Those are not like with like, and no committee will let it slide. Two facts that would carry the argument are sitting unused: capex grew 14.2% a year for three years while revenue grew 7.8% (F19 vs F18), so incremental returns are already fading; and P/E is 52.0x (F52), which is what makes the 1.88% earnings yield what it is. **Fix:** Retitle slide 4 to compare the two decisions on the same basis. Lead with P/E 52.0x (F52) and earnings yield 1.88% (F30) as the price of buying back stock today, then show the marginal return on the growth program, not the blended ROIC. Add the capex vs revenue growth gap (14.2% vs 7.8% a year, F19/F18) as the tension the page is resolving. If the marginal return cannot be sourced, say so and label the 22.2% as blended ROIC.
- The brief asks what each option implies, including doing nothing. The slide names the recommendation and one pace, but the three alternatives (dividend alone, buyback alone, neither) have no stated consequence. The $13.3B is arithmetically $30 x 443.1M (F41/F1) and the $1.0B pace is not a fact in the material at all - it is the author's proposal, presented as if sized. **Fix:** Show the three options as three lines with three consequences, using the facts in hand. Dividend alone: $13.3B out (F41/F1), cash falls from $20.21B (F5) by roughly a year of FCF of $9.38B (F26). Buyback at $1.0B a year: 0.9M shares retired, 0.20% of the float (F29), EPS $20.76 to $20.80 (F31). Neither: $20.21B (F5) earning 4.12% (F24), versus 1.88% if deployed into the buyback (F30). State the decision requested of the board on this page.
- The arithmetic does not close: 443.1M shares x $30 = $13.293B, not $13.3B, and the brief's own F29 scaling rule ("every $1B paid as a speci...") is truncated mid-sentence so no one can verify the special dividend implies any share count. A board member will do the multiplication in the room and find the page wrong. **Fix:** State the dividend as $13.29B (443.1M x $30) on slide 2 and in the governing thought, and complete the F29 special-dividend leg before sign-off so the $13.3B figure has a source. If it cannot be tied, label it 'approximately $13.3B' and show the calculation in a footnote.
- Neither number is sourced. F29 gives a scaling rule only; there is no fact anywhere in the brief that states a $6.4B minimum operating cash, and no fact that produces a $9.9B trough. The slide asserts a test it cannot show. **Fix:** Either derive the trough on the page from F5 + F26 + F41 + F8 + F9 with the dates, and label the $6.4B as 'management's stated minimum operating cash (source: ___)', or replace the title with a claim the facts support, e.g. 'Cash of $20.21B covers the $13.3B dividend 1.5x and frees $6.9B of FCF for capex'. Do not ship an unsourced minimum.
- $1.0B a year is asserted as the recommendation but nothing in the brief sizes it: F9 shows $848.0M repurchased last year, F42 shows the $4B authorization runs to January 2030, and the 1.88% vs 4.12% comparison supports slowing, not the specific $1.0B. The recommendation is therefore not sized, which the brief explicitly requires. **Fix:** Make the buyback pace a derived number, not a round number: e.g. 'Pace the buyback at $0.85B a year, matching fiscal 2026's $848.0M (F9), and leave the $4B authorization undrawn to January 2030 (F42)'. Or, if $1.0B is the intent, add the sentence 'a $1.0B annual pace retires 0.90M shares a year (F29), 0.20% of shares, at the 4.12% cash yield foregone'. Either way the slide must show the trade.
- The cover asks 'return cash or fund growth?' and the executive summary answers 'both' without acknowledging the trade-off. A board cannot approve a recommendation that says 'do two opposite things' without a capital structure framework. **Fix:** Retitle slide 2: 'Return $17.3B of the $20.21B cash pile, hold capex at $6.43B, and set an operating cash floor of $12B' — the floor number must come from research, not invented. Then make slide 3 the fundability test: does $20.21B + $9.38B FCF - $13.3B dividend - $6.43B capex - $1.15B regular dividend leave enough operating cash?
- The visual is a metrics panel ($13.3B, $4B, 14.5%) with no decision. The brief asked for 'a clear, sized recommendation (state the amounts and what each option implies)'. The options — $13.3B dividend + $4B buyback vs. $13.3B dividend + $0 buyback vs. $0/$0 and full growth capex — are never laid out side by side. Without the options, the board cannot see what each implies. **Fix:** Replace the metrics panel with a three-column options table: Option A (dividend + buyback) / Option B (dividend only) / Option C (hold cash, fund growth). Rows: cash returned, remaining cash, implied return on buyback, capex held at $6.43B, impact on 30 net new warehouses per year. Keep $13.3B, $4B, 14.5% as bolded numbers inside the table.
- The cost of equity is stated two ways. The committee case is 9.2%-10.2% (Treasury 5.15% + beta 0.8-1.0 x 5.0% ERP); slide 4's title tests the buyback against 9.2%-10.2% ('clears at 1.4x'). But slide 2's Option A compares the 14.5% implied return with the same committee band, while slide 4's callout says 'at beta 0.24 the cost of equity is 6.4%'. Which number governs the approve/reject decision is not resolved on the page. **Fix:** Pick one decision hurdle and use it everywhere. Make slide 4's title: 'At the committee's 9.2%-10.2% cost of equity, the 14.5% base-case buyback clears; consensus growth (-12% to $14.0B) takes it negative.' Put the 6.4% beta-0.24 build in the footnote only, labelled 'Yahoo Finance beta, not the committee case', and delete any 14.5%-vs-6.4% comparison.
- The governing thought says 'return surplus cash through a one-time special dividend, sized once the cash-flow and payout gaps are closed', but three of the numbers needed to close those gaps do not exist anywhere in the deck: operating cash flow, dividends and buybacks paid, and a minimum cash reserve. The board cannot act on 'surplus' if surplus is undefined. The GAP placeholders are honest but they turn slides 2, 3 and 5 into promises, not evidence. **Fix:** Pull operating cash flow, dividends paid and share repurchases from the same 10-K (CashFlowStatement, 'NetCashProvidedByUsedInOperatingActivities', 'PaymentsOfDividends', 'PaymentsForRepurchaseOfCommonStock'), and share price/valuation from a market source. Then put the payout capacity number on slide 2, not slide 5.
- The buyback alternative reads 'Its share count effect cannot be sized without the share price' and is scored 'N/A'. The board is being asked to 'choose its form' between a special dividend and a buyback, but one of the two options is blank. You cannot present an options grid where a row has no numbers next to a row with $11.28/share. **Fix:** Research the 10-K (FY2026, filed 2026-10-07): closing share price, current regular dividend per share, remaining buyback authorisation, and debt maturities. If found, populate the buyback row with shares retired at $5B / price and re-score it against the special dividend. If not found by press time, cut the buyback row from slide 4 and reframe the decision as 'special dividend $5B vs larger return', rather than shipping a two-option grid with one option empty.
- The $8B return has no source. The board will do the arithmetic out loud: $20.21B cash minus $6.4B capex minus $8B return leaves $5.8B, but the recommendation says 'hold $8B minimum operating cash'. The page cannot have it both ways and nothing in the fact base reconciles it. **Fix:** Reconcile the cash bridge before this goes near a board: state opening cash $20.21B, less capex $6.43B, less the $8B return, equals ending cash — and show whether that clears the $8B minimum. If it does not, the return size or the minimum has to change. Pull the FY26 cash flow statement (operating cash flow, dividends paid, buybacks already executed) from the 10-K to build the bridge.
- Every score in that Harvey grid is invented. The fact base contains no cost of capital, no return on new warehouses, no buyback accretion math, no dividend precedent. A board member will ask 'beat on what basis?' and there is no answer on the page. **Fix:** Either build the grid from named inputs — return on invested capital for new warehouses vs cost of equity, EPS accretion from $6B of buybacks on 443.1M shares — or cut the grid to two columns. Do not ship a scoring grid whose scores came from nowhere.
- Same fact, three times. Slide 2 says '$6.4B capex, $8B return'. Slide 3 says '$20.2B vs $6.4B capex'. Slide 5 says '$6.43B capex plan on track'. The deck is 3 content pages and two of them re-say the recommendation instead of supporting it. **Fix:** Slide 3 must be the cash bridge and the surplus calculation — the one thing slide 2 cannot carry. Slide 5 must be owners, dates and triggers only; delete the capex restatement.
- Same arithmetic, two answers. 0.9M per $1B x $8-10B = 7.2-9.0M, so the slides agree only if a reader interpolates; taken at face value slide 2 reads as if the buyback retires 0.9M shares total. The exec summary is the one page a board member reads if he reads one, and it is wrong as written. **Fix:** Rewrite slide 2 bullet 2 as 'At the $1,106.21 close, the $8-10B authorization retires 7.2-9.0M shares (1.63-2.03% of 443.1M); each $1B buys 0.90M shares'. Keep the 0.90M/$1B rate in a footnote in the notes, not the headline bullet.
- The 2.5x is 15.82/6.43 using FY2026 capex. The FY2027 plan is $7.5B, and 15.82/7.5 = 2.1x. The sentence claims to cover both the current and the step-up and quietly uses the smaller denominator. On a page where the whole claim is 'we can fund growth and buy back', that is a selectivity the board will catch. **Fix:** State both: 'operating cash flow of $15.82B covers the $6.43B FY2026 plan 2.5x and the $7.5B FY2027 step-up 2.1x'. If the point is that headroom exists, say what is left: 15.82 - 6.43 = $9.39B in FY2026 and 15.82 - 7.5 = $8.32B against the FY2027 plan.
- The deck names two different buyback pools. Slide 2 title and body: 'Approve an $8-10B buyback and fund the $6.43B capital plan' and 'funded from FY2026 free cash flow of $9.39B'. Slide 3 title: 'FY2027 free cash flow of $10.3B funds an $8B buyback'; slide 3 takeaway: 'the buyback draws on FY2027 free cash flow, not the FY2026 residual'. Slide 5 footnote and governing thought also say FY2027. The board cannot approve a mandate against two different sources of cash. **Fix:** Pick FY2027 as the funding year and make it consistent everywhere. Set slide 2 title to 'Approve an $8-10B buyback funded from FY2027 free cash flow of about $10.3B after the $7.5B capex step-up, and reject the special dividend' and rewrite point 1 so $9.39B FY2026 free cash flow is presented only as the opening balance that rolls into FY2027, not as the pool the buyback draws on.
- The $10.3B FY2027 pool is built by applying a trailing three-year 12.6% growth rate (F126) to FY2026 operating cash flow. No source supports FY2027 operating cash flow, and no source supports the $10.3B or the '$1.7B excess over FY2027 free cash flow' language. The whole sizing of the recommendation rests on an unsourced growth extrapolation presented as a fact on a board chart. **Fix:** Do one of two things. Either relabel the $10.3B as a scenario (base case = 12.6% OCF growth, sensitivity = 0% growth, giving $8.3B) with both cases printed on the waterfall as separate columns, and move the $10.3B out of the slide title into the chart labels; or commission the research and cite the driver before the pool is used to size the authorization. Do not let a computed growth rate and an unsourced operating-cash-flow projection appear in the same claim without one of the two labels.
- Slide 4 argues 'Returning $8B beats holding all the cash or returning all $20.21B', but F175 says Costco 'declared a special cash dividend of $30 per share' and F173 says it 'reauthorized a $4 billion buyback'. A $30 dividend on 443.1M shares is roughly $13.3B, plus a $4B authorization is $17B+ — not $8B. The board has already acted, and the deck's central option does not match the announced facts. This is the one thing a board will not forgive. **Fix:** Either reframe the recommendation as 'the declared $30 special dividend plus the $4B buyback return roughly $17B; hold the remaining $3B as buffer' — which ties to F173/F175 — or add a line on slide 2 stating explicitly that the $8B is a counterfactual the board is being asked to consider against the $13.3B+$4B already announced, with the reason. Pick one; do not ship both silently.
- The option grid scores three options on 'return on cash' and 'EPS effect' but the only rate given is the 1.88% earnings yield (F166) computed from diluted EPS $20.76 (F151) and share price $1,106.21. The diluted EPS itself (F151) is not in the facts list shown to me, and no interest rate on idle cash is given. The grid cannot be scored with in-hand figures. **Fix:** Pull Costco's diluted EPS from the 10-K (referenced as F151 but not in the fact list provided) and confirm $20.76. Add a short-term rate (e.g. the 3-month T-bill from F33's TNX/finance.yahoo source) so 'hold cash' has a real number rather than 'earns nothing'. Then the 1.88% vs. the risk-free rate comparison is defensible.
- Slide 3 ('The $6.43B plan is funded 2.5x by cash flow, so $12.21B of cash sits idle') and Slide 4 ('The $8B return forgoes interest but lifts EPS about 1.2% by retiring 5.42M shares') both prove the same point: the $8B is spare and accretive. Two of four content slides make the identical argument. **Fix:** Keep Slide 3 as the funding argument with the waterfall. Replace Slide 4 with the missing analysis: the full cash-out reconciliation. Title Slide 4 '$30 dividend ($13.3B) plus $4B buyback plus the $8B proposal = $25.3B returned, and it fails the test' and show the arithmetic against the $20.21B cash and $15.82B operating cash flow.
- The $30 per share special dividend and $4B buyback announcement is the most material fact in the story and it is cited only to aggregators (capitaldigest.com, a Yahoo Finance article). A board paper cannot rest on a blog. **Fix:** Pull the primary source: Costco's 8-K filed 2026-11-11 on EDGAR (CIK 0000909832) and the press release. Cite the 8-K accession number, not capitaldigest.com. Do the same for the $4B authorization.
- Both the cover and slide 2 still split the return '$6.0B buyback and a $2.0B special dividend' and slide 2 sizes the buyback tranche at $6.0B, but slide 4 caps the buyback at $4.0B with a $4.0B dividend. The deck cannot tell two different answers. **Fix:** Change slide 1's context line and slide 2's decision line to '$4.0B buyback and $4.0B special dividend'; replace F9's '$6.0B buyback and a $2.0B special dividend' everywhere it appears.
- The $2.0B/$6.0B split drives the $2.26/share dividend figure and the 'returned' base for the buyback spread; both are now wrong. Keeping them miscalculates the dividend per share and the forgone return. **Fix:** Rebuild slide 2 point 2 as '$4.0B special dividend pays $9.03 per share' and slide 2 point 3 as 'the $4.0B buyback earns 1.88%'; delete the $2.26 figure.
- The $403 break-even price appears nowhere in the brief or facts and cannot be derived from what is in hand. A board will ask for the derivation and there is none. **Fix:** Either delete the $403 break-even metric and replace with 'executed at a price below the 5.15% Treasury-equivalent earnings yield' or add a research request to define the break-even price and its inputs before this slide is built.
- The split is stated two different ways. Slide 1's one context line says 'proposed $8.0B return ($4.0B buyback, $4.0B special dividend)'. Slide 2's can_do and node-1 must-show in the storyboard body differ from the QA artefacts: F9 shows '$8.0B ... through a $6.0B buyback and a $2.0B special dividend', F16 shows 'buyback tranche sized at $6.0B', F22 shows 'A $6.0B buyback and a $2.0B special dividend leave cash of $12.2B'. A board cannot approve a number the deck states two ways. **Fix:** Lock the split at $4.0B buyback / $4.0B special dividend everywhere. Slide 1 context line, slide 2 title, slide 2 bullet 3, slide 2 decision line, slide 4 right-hand argument and slide 5 action row 2 all read $4.0B. Delete every '$6.0B' and '$2.0B' and the '$2.26 per share' dividend-per-share figure that follows from the wrong split.
- The already-announced 11 Nov 2026 events are moved to slide 2 but never reconciled to the recommendation. F174 says Costco 'declared a special cash dividend of $30 per share' and F175 says it 'reauthorized a $4b share repurchase program'. The deck then asks the board to approve a $4.0B buyback and a $4.0B special dividend of $9.03 per share without ever saying whether the proposal is incremental to, replaces, or is the same as the $30 and the $4B. Slide 2 has one reconciliation line in the must-show ('consistent with the 11 Nov 2026 reauthorization, this is an incremental special dividend of $4.0B') but nothing in the evidence supports the word 'incremental' and the $4B reauthorization is not netted. As written the board may be voting on money it already approved. **Fix:** Add to slide 2 a two-column reconciliation: announced 11 Nov 2026 ($30/share special dividend, $4B buyback reauthorization) versus proposed today ($4.0B incremental special dividend at $9.03/share, $4.0B buyback tranche), with the net additional cash out shown on one line. If the proposal is in fact the same $4B, stop calling it new and retitle slide 2 around the incremental dividend only.

### Workbook: MD (79)

- Every formula in column B (the FY2026 actuals column) is broken and returns #N/A.
